[p0009-l0001] [28.8pt] Preface
[p0009-l0002] [14.4pt] Communication underlies pretty much everything you do, from your facial
[p0009-l0003] [14.4pt] expression showing disapproval or enjoyment, to your email about the latest
[p0009-l0004] [14.4pt] project update, to what you say in a meeting or presentation. But what is
[p0009-l0005] [14.4pt] communication, and how do you make it successful?
[p0009-l0006] [14.4pt] The Oxford English Dictionary defines communication as “the activity or
[p0009-l0007] [14.4pt] process of expressing ideas and feelings or of giving people information.” This
[p0009-l0008] [14.4pt] encompasses many of the important aspects of communication but doesn’t
[p0009-l0009] [14.4pt] indicate what would make it successful. The same dictionary defines
[p0009-l0010] [14.4pt] communicate as “to share or exchange information, news, ideas, feelings, etc.”
[p0009-l0011] [14.4pt] This definition provides more detail, but we still need to go further to work out
[p0009-l0012] [14.4pt] how to make communication successful.
[p0009-l0013] [14.4pt] The Merriam-Webster Dictionary adds that communication is “through a
[p0009-l0014] [14.4pt] common system of symbols, signs, or behavior,” introducing the idea of
[p0009-l0015] [14.4pt] commonality and how the ideas or information is conveyed.
[p0009-l0016] [14.4pt] Here’s what we have so far:
[p0009-l0017] [14.4pt] Expresses ideas and feelings
[p0009-l0018] [14.4pt] Gives people information
[p0009-l0019] [14.4pt] Shares or exchanges information, news, and so on
[p0009-l0020] [14.4pt] Uses a common system of symbols, signs, or behavior
[p0009-l0021] [14.4pt] That gives a good idea of what communication is, but what makes
[p0009-l0022] [14.4pt] communication successful?
[p0009-l0023] [14.4pt] The polyglot linguist Michel Thomas put it simply when he said the aim of
[p0009-l0024] [14.4pt] communication is to “get the ball over the net.” None of the definitions we’ve
[p0009-l0025] [14.4pt] seen so far cover this critical element of understanding.
[p0009-l0026] [14.4pt] Let me take a stab, then:
[p0009-l0027] [14.4pt] Successful communication is the art and science of sharing or exchanging

=== PDF page 10 / printed label 10 ===
[p0010-l0001] [14.4pt] ideas and information, using a common set of symbols, signs, or behaviors,
[p0010-l0002] [14.4pt] resulting in shared understanding.
[p0010-l0003] [14.4pt] The cost of miscommunication is high, whether it’s cumulative wasted time or
[p0010-l0004] [14.4pt] the price of putting things right. So why is there not more emphasis on making
[p0010-l0005] [14.4pt] communication successful, or at least improving it? That is the focus of this
[p0010-l0006] [14.4pt] book.
[p0010-l0007] [14.4pt] Software development and architecture have patterns and antipatterns that can
[p0010-l0008] [14.4pt] be applied (or recognized) in writing code and architecting systems. A pattern is
[p0010-l0009] [14.4pt] a reusable solution that has been shown to be effective when used to solve a
[p0010-l0010] [14.4pt] problem. The biggest benefit is that someone else has done the hard work for
[p0010-l0011] [14.4pt] you, and you just need to apply the solution to your particular situation and
[p0010-l0012] [14.4pt] problem.
[p0010-l0013] [14.4pt] Antipatterns are not the direct opposite of patterns. They are solutions that look
[p0010-l0014] [14.4pt] like they solve a problem but have consequences that outweigh any potential
[p0010-l0015] [14.4pt] benefits. Learning about antipatterns means that you can recognize them in
[p0010-l0016] [14.4pt] designs or existing systems, or recognize situations where they might occur so
[p0010-l0017] [14.4pt] that you can avoid or mitigate them.
[p0010-l0018] [14.4pt] This book applies the concept of patterns and antipatterns to communication.
[p0010-l0019] [14.4pt] People often quote Brian Foote and Joseph Yoder’s 1997 paper “Big Ball of
[p0010-l0020] [14.4pt] Mud” (and for good reason): “If you think good architecture is expensive, try
[p0010-l0021] [14.4pt] bad architecture.” It means that creating good architecture requires an
[p0010-l0022] [14.4pt] investment, but not investing will result in bad architecture that costs more in the
[p0010-l0023] [14.4pt] long run. The same thing should be said for communication: If you think good
[p0010-l0024] [14.4pt] communication is expensive, try bad communication. Investing in good
[p0010-l0025] [14.4pt] (successful) communication is less expensive than bearing the costs of bad
[p0010-l0026] [14.4pt] (unsuccessful) communication.
[p0010-l0027] [20.16pt] Why I Wrote This Book
[p0010-l0028] [14.4pt] Throughout my career in software development and software architecture, I have
[p0010-l0029] [14.4pt] frequently discovered that the principles and techniques I apply naturally do not
[p0010-l0030] [14.4pt] come naturally to others. In some cases, I have applied knowledge I learned
[p0010-l0031] [14.4pt] from somewhere else to the technology domain, and in others, my approach just

=== PDF page 11 / printed label 11 ===
[p0011-l0001] [14.4pt] seemed to me the right way to do it.
[p0011-l0002] [14.4pt] I realized that I had built up many patterns and antipatterns in my toolbox, and
[p0011-l0003] [14.4pt] not all were the type that can be applied to code or architecture. Some were
[p0011-l0004] [14.4pt] applicable to what many would describe as soft skills, like creating diagrams and
[p0011-l0005] [14.4pt] documentation. Even some that were designed to be used in code or architecture,
[p0011-l0006] [14.4pt] I was applying outside of their intended use.
[p0011-l0007] [14.4pt] It turned out that these soft patterns and antipatterns could all be categorized as
[p0011-l0008] [14.4pt] communication patterns and, recognizing that my tool kit was not widely
[p0011-l0009] [14.4pt] accessible to others, I determined to make it available. The result is this book
[p0011-l0010] [14.4pt] and the training courses that I provide through O’Reilly and privately (along
[p0011-l0011] [14.4pt] with other architectural courses and consulting).
[p0011-l0012] [14.4pt] My intention in writing this book is to improve communication of teams and
[p0011-l0013] [14.4pt] organizations within the technology sector so that individuals can increase their
[p0011-l0014] [14.4pt] productivity and general happiness, and organizations see an improvement in
[p0011-l0015] [14.4pt] their return on investment (ROI) and even their bottom line.
[p0011-l0016] [14.4pt] Investing in your soft skills will enhance your technical skills and make you a
[p0011-l0017] [14.4pt] stand-out technical star.
[p0011-l0018] [14.4pt] I consider myself to be a lifelong learner and would love to hear your
[p0011-l0019] [14.4pt] experiences of applying the patterns and antipatterns in this book, and of any
[p0011-l0020] [14.4pt] other methods you use to optimize the way you and your colleagues
[p0011-l0021] [14.4pt] communicate. You can contact me via O’Reilly (“How to Contact Us”), my
[p0011-l0022] [14.4pt] website, or social media.
[p0011-l0023] [20.16pt] Who Should Read This Book
[p0011-l0024] [14.4pt] This book is intended for developers, engineers, and all types of architects
[p0011-l0025] [14.4pt] (solution, software, data, enterprise, and so forth) at any point in their careers.
[p0011-l0026] [14.4pt] Because the skills this book presents are not formally or traditionally taught,
[p0011-l0027] [14.4pt] even the most seasoned technologist can benefit.
[p0011-l0028] [14.4pt] Applying this book’s patterns to your communication will set you apart as
[p0011-l0029] [14.4pt] someone who not only has the technical skills but also the soft skills to get things
[p0011-l0030] [14.4pt] done and be understood by technical and nontechnical audiences alike. For those
[p0011-l0031] [14.4pt] aspiring to move from development to architecture, or into a senior or tech-lead

=== PDF page 12 / printed label 12 ===
[p0012-l0001] [14.4pt] role, improving your communication will remove at least some of the hurdles
[p0012-l0002] [14.4pt] between you and the role you desire.
[p0012-l0003] [14.4pt] Although principally tailored to developers and architects, the patterns in this
[p0012-l0004] [14.4pt] book can be applied by, and provide benefits to, anyone in the software and
[p0012-l0005] [14.4pt] technology industries (and other industries besides). The relevancy of each
[p0012-l0006] [14.4pt] pattern and antipattern will depend on your role.
[p0012-l0007] [14.4pt] For example, Part I will be useful to business analysts (BAs), and Part IV will be
[p0012-l0008] [14.4pt] useful to anyone working in a remote or hybrid environment, or with customers
[p0012-l0009] [14.4pt] in another time zone. Managers and leaders will greatly benefit from Parts II, III,
[p0012-l0010] [14.4pt] and IV, with the added benefit of being able to disseminate the techniques and
[p0012-l0011] [14.4pt] principles to their reports and teams.
[p0012-l0012] [14.4pt] Many patterns and principles in this book come from domains very different
[p0012-l0013] [14.4pt] from software, and I wouldn’t be surprised if they can be applied in still more
[p0012-l0014] [14.4pt] domains.
[p0012-l0015] [20.16pt] How to Read This Book
[p0012-l0016] [14.4pt] The book is structured into four parts, each covering one major aspect of
[p0012-l0017] [14.4pt] communication in the software and technology domain. You are free to start
[p0012-l0018] [14.4pt] wherever you feel would most benefit you, or with whatever piques your interest
[p0012-l0019] [14.4pt] the most. Otherwise, Part I is the place to start.
[p0012-l0020] [14.4pt] Part I covers patterns and antipatterns for diagrams and other visuals. Chapter 1
[p0012-l0021] [14.4pt] lays the foundations that the other chapters in Part I build upon. Ensure you
[p0012-l0022] [14.4pt] understand and are applying the patterns in Chapter 1 before you start adding the
[p0012-l0023] [14.4pt] other patterns from Part I to your tool kit.
[p0012-l0024] [14.4pt] Part II includes patterns and techniques for written, verbal (spoken), and
[p0012-l0025] [14.4pt] nonverbal communication, which you can apply to remote and in-person
[p0012-l0026] [14.4pt] interactions. Part III contains principles, practices, and patterns to improve
[p0012-l0027] [14.4pt] knowledge management and sharing, including documentation. Part IV offers
[p0012-l0028] [14.4pt] many strategies and patterns for you to use when communicating with people in
[p0012-l0029] [14.4pt] other time zones and with different working hours, particularly in hybrid and
[p0012-l0030] [14.4pt] remote environments.

=== PDF page 13 / printed label 13 ===
[p0013-l0001] [20.16pt] Images and Color
[p0013-l0002] [14.4pt] Some of the figures in this book need to be viewed in color. In the printed
[p0013-l0003] [14.4pt] version, all images are grayscale, so we’ve included links to color versions of
[p0013-l0004] [14.4pt] any images that need to be viewed in color. All images are available on the
[p0013-l0005] [14.4pt] accompanying website.
[p0013-l0006] [20.16pt] Software Tools
[p0013-l0007] [14.4pt] You do not need to use any specific software tools to put the patterns and
[p0013-l0008] [14.4pt] techniques in this book into practice, but I do mention various tools. When citing
[p0013-l0009] [14.4pt] tools that could be used for a particular purpose, I usually refer to those that are
[p0013-l0010] [14.4pt] well-known. The recommendations I make are mostly open source options.
[p0013-l0011] [14.4pt] Remember to check the license required for your situation and ensure that the
[p0013-l0012] [14.4pt] terms of service meet your needs.
[p0013-l0013] [14.4pt] To create my original diagrams and illustrations for this book, I used draw.io, for
[p0013-l0014] [14.4pt] a time known as Diagrams.net. I encourage participants to use draw.io when
[p0013-l0015] [14.4pt] completing exercises in the workshops I run. It is free, open source, requires no
[p0013-l0016] [14.4pt] login, and can be used as a desktop application or via a web browser. Draw.io
[p0013-l0017] [14.4pt] has many integrations for other applications, and the desktop version is available
[p0013-l0018] [14.4pt] for Windows, macOS, Linux, and ChromeOS.
[p0013-l0019] [20.16pt] Polyglot Media
[p0013-l0020] [14.4pt] Polyglot Media is a fictitious company used to create examples for this book.
[p0013-l0021] [14.4pt] The company has around 150 employees, who are spread across several
[p0013-l0022] [14.4pt] countries, and an international customer base. Polyglot Media provides
[p0013-l0023] [14.4pt] customers with access to various digital media (ebooks, audiobooks, and videos)
[p0013-l0024] [14.4pt] on a subscription and pay-as-you-go basis, and it also offers hard copies of
[p0013-l0025] [14.4pt] books. Some media is stored in-house, and some is provided by partners. The
[p0013-l0026] [14.4pt] Polyglot Media system is also used by authors to update and create publications
[p0013-l0027] [14.4pt] and by editors (employed by Polyglot Media) to access and edit the authors’
[p0013-l0028] [14.4pt] publications.
[p0013-l0029] [10.8pt] EXAMPLE

=== PDF page 14 / printed label 14 ===
[p0014-l0001] [14.4pt] POLYGLOT MEDIA EXAMPLES
[p0014-l0002] [14.4pt] Throughout the book, I used Polyglot Media in examples. You will see that
[p0014-l0003] [14.4pt] lots of the diagrams are based on Polyglot Media systems, and other
[p0014-l0004] [14.4pt] examples are presented in a box just like this one. All examples are fictitious
[p0014-l0005] [14.4pt] but based on my own or others’ experiences and learning.
[p0014-l0006] [20.16pt] Conventions Used in This Book
[p0014-l0007] [14.4pt] The following typographical conventions are used in this book:
[p0014-l0008] [14.4pt] Italic
[p0014-l0009] [14.4pt] Indicates new terms, URLs, email addresses, filenames, file extensions, and
[p0014-l0010] [14.4pt] pattern or antipattern names.
[p0014-l0011] [14.4pt] Constant width
[p0014-l0012] [14.4pt] Used for program listings, as well as within paragraphs to refer to program
[p0014-l0013] [14.4pt] elements such as variable or function names, databases, data types,
[p0014-l0014] [14.4pt] environment variables, statements, and keywords.
[p0014-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0014-l0016] [10.8pt] This element signifies a tip or suggestion.
[p0014-l0017] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0014-l0018] [10.8pt] This element signifies a general note.
[p0014-l0019] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0014-l0020] [10.8pt] This element indicates a warning or caution.

[p0019-l0001] [28.8pt] Part I. Visual Communication
[p0019-l0002] [14.4pt] Visual elements in software architecture and design communicate key
[p0019-l0003] [14.4pt] information. Your audience’s eyes are naturally drawn to these elements, which
[p0019-l0004] [14.4pt] may be the only thing they look at in any detail. Despite this, little guidance or
[p0019-l0005] [14.4pt] training is available on creating diagrams and visuals (except notation-specific
[p0019-l0006] [14.4pt] courses, such as ArchiMate), especially on how to effectively and successfully
[p0019-l0007] [14.4pt] communicate your message to your audience.
[p0019-l0008] [14.4pt] Visual literacy, the ability to understand and create visuals, is a skill architecture
[p0019-l0009] [14.4pt] and software training courses are missing. I wrote Part I to fill that knowledge
[p0019-l0010] [14.4pt] gap. These patterns and antipatterns guide you to create diagrams that serve your
[p0019-l0011] [14.4pt] audience’s needs and produce the outcomes you want. You will learn to strike a
[p0019-l0012] [14.4pt] balance between the need for information and for accessibility.
[p0019-l0013] [14.4pt] For any diagram or visual you create, Part I offers many patterns that you can
[p0019-l0014] [14.4pt] apply to successfully convey your message to your audience and get what you
[p0019-l0015] [14.4pt] need in return.

=== PDF page 20 / printed label 20 ===
[p0020-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 1. Communication
[p0020-l0002] [28.8pt] Essentials
[p0020-l0003] [14.4pt] This chapter provides the foundation that you will build upon with the other
[p0020-l0004] [14.4pt] patterns in Part I. When I refer to patterns and antipatterns, I mean the following:
[p0020-l0005] [14.4pt] Pattern
[p0020-l0006] [14.4pt] A reusable solution known to be effective when used to solve a specific or
[p0020-l0007] [14.4pt] more general problem, which may otherwise be known as techniques,
[p0020-l0008] [14.4pt] practices, methods, or rules.
[p0020-l0009] [14.4pt] Antipattern
[p0020-l0010] [14.4pt] A solution that is not recommended. It looks like it is the right way to solve a
[p0020-l0011] [14.4pt] problem when in reality its consequences outweigh any benefits.
[p0020-l0012] [14.4pt] I highly recommend making sure you are employing the patterns and
[p0020-l0013] [14.4pt] antipatterns in this chapter before building on them with all the others. Think of
[p0020-l0014] [14.4pt] it the same way as architecting a building: you need to get the foundation right
[p0020-l0015] [14.4pt] before you can build the walls, floors, and roof. Don’t start building on sand, or
[p0020-l0016] [14.4pt] your construction will sink. Get this groundwork right first.
[p0020-l0017] [20.16pt] Know Your Audience
[p0020-l0018] [14.4pt] The know your audience pattern is also known as understanding your customer.
[p0020-l0019] [14.4pt] One of the essential factors to keep in mind when creating and editing a diagram
[p0020-l0020] [14.4pt] is who is going to be viewing and reading it. Your diagram’s purpose is to
[p0020-l0021] [14.4pt] communicate successfully with this audience. Knowing who they are and
[p0020-l0022] [14.4pt] designing the diagram to their needs are key to this purpose.
[p0020-l0023] [14.4pt] People who view your diagram could have the following roles:
[p0020-l0024] [14.4pt] Developers (full-stack, frontend, backend…)

=== PDF page 21 / printed label 21 ===
[p0021-l0001] [14.4pt] Architects (technical, solution, security…)
[p0021-l0002] [14.4pt] Business analysts
[p0021-l0003] [14.4pt] Product owners
[p0021-l0004] [14.4pt] Project managers
[p0021-l0005] [14.4pt] Customers
[p0021-l0006] [14.4pt] Support teams
[p0021-l0007] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0021-l0008] [10.8pt] Make a list of the roles that view your diagrams and then group the roles based on the types of
[p0021-l0009] [10.8pt] diagrams you create. You will likely find that you have a different audience mix for different
[p0021-l0010] [10.8pt] diagrams. Use these lists with the questions near the end of this section.
[p0021-l0011] [14.4pt] The following diagrams have been created for different audiences, and the type
[p0021-l0012] [14.4pt] of diagram and notation have been chosen to suit the audience.
[p0021-l0013] [14.4pt] Figure 1-1 shows a Unified Modeling Language (UML) class diagram aimed at a
[p0021-l0014] [14.4pt] technical audience, including developers, architects, and database administrators.
[p0021-l0015] [14.4pt] A product owner or project manager would be unlikely to need the information
[p0021-l0016] [14.4pt] in this diagram or be able to understand it without help.

=== PDF page 22 / printed label 22 ===
[p0022-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 1-1. UML class diagram, aimed at a technical audience
[p0022-l0002] [14.4pt] Figure 1-2 is a C4 context diagram. This is a versatile diagram, useful for both
[p0022-l0003] [14.4pt] technical and business audiences to get an overview of a system. This diagram is
[p0022-l0004] [14.4pt] equally readable and useful to roles such as product owner, project manager,
[p0022-l0005] [14.4pt] architect, developer, and business analyst.

=== PDF page 23 / printed label 23 ===
[p0023-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 1-2. C4 context diagram, useful to most audiences
[p0023-l0002] [14.4pt] The domain story shown in Figure 1-3 is aimed more at business roles, but also
[p0023-l0003] [14.4pt] those technical roles that are involved in translating business needs into technical
[p0023-l0004] [14.4pt] solutions. Domain stories are created to improve communication between
[p0023-l0005] [14.4pt] business stakeholders and technical roles so that the solution meets the needs of
[p0023-l0006] [14.4pt] the business and users. Domain or subject matter experts (SMEs) would be
[p0023-l0007] [14.4pt] involved in creating and verifying a domain story, along with roles such as
[p0023-l0008] [14.4pt] product owner (if not the SME), business analyst, and architect.

=== PDF page 24 / printed label 24 ===
[p0024-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 1-3. Domain story diagram, aimed at business roles and technical translator roles such as architects
[p0024-l0002] [10.8pt] and business analysts
[p0024-l0003] [14.4pt] After identifying your audience, ask yourself the following about them:
[p0024-l0004] [14.4pt] What do they want from you?
[p0024-l0005] [14.4pt] Consider your audience’s expectations and needs. Meeting their needs is key
[p0024-l0006] [14.4pt] to successful communication. Is your audience looking for specific
[p0024-l0007] [14.4pt] information so they can make a decision or report back to someone else?
[p0024-l0008] [14.4pt] Keep your audience happy by sending them away with the information and
[p0024-l0009] [14.4pt] understanding they need to do their jobs.
[p0024-l0010] [14.4pt] What do you want from them?
[p0024-l0011] [14.4pt] This question is often missed: what you need from your audience. Do you
[p0024-l0012] [14.4pt] need agreement or sign-off on your design decisions? Do you need them to
[p0024-l0013] [14.4pt] make a decision based on your diagrams? Make sure the audience
[p0024-l0014] [14.4pt] understands what you need from them and by when, and has all they need in
[p0024-l0015] [14.4pt] order to meet your expectations.
[p0024-l0016] [14.4pt] What is their technical understanding?

=== PDF page 25 / printed label 25 ===
[p0025-l0001] [14.4pt] Your audience’s technical understanding will determine the type of diagram
[p0025-l0002] [14.4pt] they will benefit from. Consider how technical your project manager is. Does
[p0025-l0003] [14.4pt] your product owner want to know about the selected technology, or just
[p0025-l0004] [14.4pt] about how well the selection meets the requirements?
[p0025-l0005] [14.4pt] What level of detail do they need?
[p0025-l0006] [14.4pt] Whether the content is technical or not, you need to consider the appropriate
[p0025-l0007] [14.4pt] level of detail. Is this diagram for an architectural review board that will
[p0025-l0008] [14.4pt] expect a lot of detail? Does the development team need implementation
[p0025-l0009] [14.4pt] details, or is determining those details part of their job?1 Ask teams what
[p0025-l0010] [14.4pt] they want, rather than going by any written or unwritten guides the company
[p0025-l0011] [14.4pt] gives you. If there are rules that don’t meet the team’s needs, bring it up with
[p0025-l0012] [14.4pt] the appropriate person.
[p0025-l0013] [14.4pt] Besides these questions, consider that members of your audience may not be
[p0025-l0014] [14.4pt] native speakers of the language you are using and may have a different cultural
[p0025-l0015] [14.4pt] background from you (see “Simple Language”).
[p0025-l0016] [14.4pt] Once you’ve identified your audience and their needs, you can begin your
[p0025-l0017] [14.4pt] diagram.
[p0025-l0018] [20.16pt] Mixing Levels of Abstraction
[p0025-l0019] [14.4pt] Mixing levels of abstraction is a communication antipattern that has a
[p0025-l0020] [14.4pt] counterpart in the coding world. If you have ever coded, you will likely know
[p0025-l0021] [14.4pt] mixing levels of abstraction as a sin or a code smell.2 Although putting all the
[p0025-l0022] [14.4pt] information someone could need into one diagram might seem appropriate, this
[p0025-l0023] [14.4pt] leads to clutter and confusion from the audience’s perspective.
[p0025-l0024] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0025-l0025] [10.8pt] Levels of abstraction refers to the granularity or generality of the information you present in a
[p0025-l0026] [10.8pt] diagram. Abstraction levels range from high-level views showing the major components of a
[p0025-l0027] [10.8pt] system and their relationships to one another down to low-level diagrams detailing the code’s
[p0025-l0028] [10.8pt] structure.
[p0025-l0029] [10.8pt] Using different levels of abstraction across multiple diagrams allows you to communicate

=== PDF page 26 / printed label 26 ===
[p0026-l0001] [10.8pt] appropriately for the audience, while still ensuring that all relevant information is captured.
[p0026-l0002] [14.4pt] All software is an abstraction, but in essence, levels of abstraction let you hide
[p0026-l0003] [14.4pt] low-level details from high-level concepts. Developers do not write software
[p0026-l0004] [14.4pt] using ones and zeros (binary or machine code); they develop by writing in
[p0026-l0005] [14.4pt] higher-level languages that abstract away the complexities of machine code and
[p0026-l0006] [14.4pt] all the levels between (interpreters, compilers, and so forth).
[p0026-l0007] [14.4pt] Take a look at Figure 1-4. If you think of the process of going to work as one
[p0026-l0008] [14.4pt] level of abstraction, the next level could contain the concepts of getting up,
[p0026-l0009] [14.4pt] having a shower, getting dressed, having breakfast, leaving the house, and so on.
[p0026-l0010] [14.4pt] The next level for getting up would contain pushing back the covers, sitting up,
[p0026-l0011] [14.4pt] and standing up. There you have three levels of abstraction (going to work,
[p0026-l0012] [14.4pt] getting up, pushing back the covers), but in software terms, these should all be
[p0026-l0013] [14.4pt] separate (such as in different methods or classes) to avoid confusion and
[p0026-l0014] [14.4pt] unneeded complexity, and to aid readability.
[p0026-l0015] [10.8pt] Figure 1-4. Levels of abstraction in everyday life
[p0026-l0016] [14.4pt] Using levels of abstraction applies to software architecture and diagrams in the
[p0026-l0017] [14.4pt] same way. In code, you apply this principle to methods, classes, layers in a
[p0026-l0018] [14.4pt] layered application (such as a presentation layer, the business logic, a persistence
[p0026-l0019] [14.4pt] layer, and the data store), and more. In diagrams and software architecture, you
[p0026-l0020] [14.4pt] apply this principle to the content of diagrams and to the structure of services,
[p0026-l0021] [14.4pt] microservices, and so forth.
[p0026-l0022] [14.4pt] The C4 model is a hierarchy of abstractions. It uses an abstraction first approach
[p0026-l0023] [14.4pt] (prioritizing abstraction and building everything else around it). Its four
[p0026-l0024] [14.4pt] abstraction layers define its core diagrams:3
[p0026-l0025] [14.4pt] 1. The system context shows an overview of the system and how it fits into its

=== PDF page 27 / printed label 27 ===
[p0027-l0001] [14.4pt] environment, including interactions between the system and other entities.
[p0027-l0002] [14.4pt] 2. The container level zooms in on the software system in scope, showing the
[p0027-l0003] [14.4pt] high-level components or building blocks and how they interact with each
[p0027-l0004] [14.4pt] other and external entities.
[p0027-l0005] [14.4pt] 3. The component level zooms in further on an individual container from the
[p0027-l0006] [14.4pt] previous level. It shows the components inside the container and their
[p0027-l0007] [14.4pt] interactions with each other and with external entities.
[p0027-l0008] [14.4pt] 4. The code level zooms in still further on a component from the previous
[p0027-l0009] [14.4pt] level. It shows how the component is implemented. (This level is often
[p0027-l0010] [14.4pt] more detail than is necessary in your documentation).
[p0027-l0011] [14.4pt] You can think of these four levels as maps that you can zoom in on to reveal
[p0027-l0012] [14.4pt] increasing levels of detail. The C4 abstraction layers can illustrate the need for
[p0027-l0013] [14.4pt] varying levels of detail.
[p0027-l0014] [14.4pt] Figure 1-5 isn’t a context diagram and it isn’t a container diagram. It is a mix of
[p0027-l0015] [14.4pt] two levels of abstraction. If you look at the diagram closely, it doesn’t make
[p0027-l0016] [14.4pt] sense. The system in focus (Polyglot Media’s software system) seems to have
[p0027-l0017] [14.4pt] been partially divided into containers, and the relationship between the software
[p0027-l0018] [14.4pt] system and containers doesn’t make sense. The software system and the
[p0027-l0019] [14.4pt] containers belong in different conceptual levels. In reality, containers within the
[p0027-l0020] [14.4pt] software system will be interacting with the containers shown.

=== PDF page 28 / printed label 28 ===
[p0028-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 1-5. C4 diagram showing both context and container levels of abstraction (antipattern)
[p0028-l0002] [14.4pt] Figure 1-6 shows how the context diagram for Figure 1-5 should look. This is
[p0028-l0003] [14.4pt] where the software system in focus (Polyglot Media) belongs, with its related
[p0028-l0004] [14.4pt] external systems and actors.

=== PDF page 29 / printed label 29 ===
[p0029-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 1-6. C4 context diagram
[p0029-l0002] [14.4pt] Figure 1-7 shows how the container abstraction layer information in Figure 1-5
[p0029-l0003] [14.4pt] should be shown in a C4 container diagram. The system in focus (Polyglot
[p0029-l0004] [14.4pt] Media) is shown as a dashed box with the containers inside it.
[p0029-l0005] [14.4pt] C4 models, based around a hierarchy of abstractions, are an excellent way to
[p0029-l0006] [14.4pt] illustrate the need to keep levels of abstraction separate in diagrams. This
[p0029-l0007] [14.4pt] separation rule applies to all types of diagrams. Apply it to your sequence
[p0029-l0008] [14.4pt] diagrams, data flow diagrams, diagrams with no formal notation, and all the
[p0029-l0009] [14.4pt] other types of diagrams you use. All your diagrams should follow this rule; it is
[p0029-l0010] [14.4pt] essential for communication.
[p0029-l0011] [14.4pt] Once you have split up any mixed diagrams so that each has only one level of
[p0029-l0012] [14.4pt] abstraction, read on to find out how to make them easily navigable.

=== PDF page 30 / printed label 30 ===
[p0030-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 1-7. C4 container diagram
[p0030-l0002] [20.16pt] Representational Consistency
[p0030-l0003] [14.4pt] Using the representational consistency pattern is the next step after checking
[p0030-l0004] [14.4pt] levels of abstraction: linking discrete diagrams together so that your audience
[p0030-l0005] [14.4pt] can navigate between them easily and see how they fit together. Understanding

=== PDF page 31 / printed label 31 ===
[p0031-l0001] [14.4pt] how your diagrams relate to one another should be easy for your audience. You
[p0031-l0002] [14.4pt] risk unsuccessful communication if your audience has to think too much, or
[p0031-l0003] [14.4pt] remember a key or pivotal detail, to understand relationships between diagrams
[p0031-l0004] [14.4pt] (each an individual level of abstraction).
[p0031-l0005] [14.4pt] Many notations, such as C4 and data flow diagrams, have formal and explicit
[p0031-l0006] [14.4pt] ways of communicating representational consistency. As noted previously, C4
[p0031-l0007] [14.4pt] diagrams also have explicit levels of abstraction (context, container, component,
[p0031-l0008] [14.4pt] and code). Take a look at Figure 1-8, which shows the system in scope (Polyglot
[p0031-l0009] [14.4pt] Media) in the center of the diagram. This context level is the highest in C4
[p0031-l0010] [14.4pt] diagrams.
[p0031-l0011] [10.8pt] Figure 1-8. C4 context diagram
[p0031-l0012] [14.4pt] The next level down is a C4 container diagram (Figure 1-9). The method of
[p0031-l0013] [14.4pt] connecting these diagrams is a dashed box in Figure 1-9, which is labeled (in the

=== PDF page 32 / printed label 32 ===
[p0032-l0001] [14.4pt] bottom left) the same as the central box (Polyglot Media) in Figure 1-8. This
[p0032-l0002] [14.4pt] allows the audience to see the connection between these two diagrams,
[p0032-l0003] [14.4pt] whichever one they see first.

=== PDF page 33 / printed label 33 ===
[p0033-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 1-9. C4 container diagram for Polyglot Media (showing the high-level interactions within the
[p0033-l0002] [10.8pt] Polyglot Media system)
[p0033-l0003] [14.4pt] In a data flow diagram, you use numbers and letters to indicate the identity of its
[p0033-l0004] [14.4pt] elements, and then you can use those same numbers and letters to guide your
[p0033-l0005] [14.4pt] audience through different levels (shown in different diagrams). For example, in
[p0033-l0006] [14.4pt] Figure 1-10 the processes are numbered 1 through 3 to indicate the order in
[p0033-l0007] [14.4pt] which they occur, and if those processes are further divided in another diagram,
[p0033-l0008] [14.4pt] they can be identified by that number.
[p0033-l0009] [10.8pt] Figure 1-10. Level 1 data flow diagram
[p0033-l0010] [14.4pt] In Figure 1-11, process 2 from Figure 1-10 is divided into three subprocesses.
[p0033-l0011] [14.4pt] You can tell this because they are numbered 2.1 through 2.3. The processes are
[p0033-l0012] [14.4pt] again ordered, but relative to the higher-level diagram in Figure 1-10.

=== PDF page 34 / printed label 34 ===
[p0034-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 1-11. Level 2 data flow diagram
[p0034-l0002] [14.4pt] If you compare the data stores in Figures 1-10 and 1-11, you will notice that the
[p0034-l0003] [14.4pt] data stores labeled A and B in Figure 1-10 also appear in Figure 1-11 with the
[p0034-l0004] [14.4pt] same identities.4
[p0034-l0005] [14.4pt] If the notation you’re using doesn’t provide a formal way of connecting
[p0034-l0006] [14.4pt] diagrams, you will need to make the connection explicit yourself. For example,
[p0034-l0007] [14.4pt] Figure 1-12 shows another way to connect process 2 (Fetch media) in Figure 1-
[p0034-l0008] [14.4pt] 10 and its subprocesses 2.1 through 2.3 in Figure 1-11 using a similar method to
[p0034-l0009] [14.4pt] the one employed by C4 diagrams (shown previously in Figure 1-9). You could
[p0034-l0010] [14.4pt] use a similar technique in many types of diagrams.
[p0034-l0011] [10.8pt] Figure 1-12. Level 2 data flow diagram with explicit representational consistency
[p0034-l0012] [14.4pt] TIP

=== PDF page 35 / printed label 35 ===
[p0035-l0001] [10.8pt] When including diagrams in documentation, refer to them in the text of that documentation.
[p0035-l0002] [10.8pt] Use hyperlinks if possible, and label your diagrams (for example, “Figure 1: System X context
[p0035-l0003] [10.8pt] diagram”) and then reference that label explicitly.
[p0035-l0004] [14.4pt] Make representational consistency explicit in your diagrams and documentation
[p0035-l0005] [14.4pt] to reduce your audience’s cognitive load.5
[p0035-l0006] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0035-l0007] [14.4pt] This chapter covered the essentials of visual communication, giving you a
[p0035-l0008] [14.4pt] foundation on which to build with the remaining patterns and antipatterns in
[p0035-l0009] [14.4pt] Part I. As you continue through the book, think about how you could apply these
[p0035-l0010] [14.4pt] essentials along with the other patterns and antipatterns explored.
[p0035-l0011] [14.4pt] Having thought about what your audience needs from your diagram, it is time to
[p0035-l0012] [14.4pt] consider the amount of information that you are presenting in it. Keeping this to
[p0035-l0013] [14.4pt] the bare minimum needed to communicate your message will improve your
[p0035-l0014] [14.4pt] audience’s understanding.
[p0035-l0015] [10.8pt] 1 The level of detail in designs for development teams can be controversial, leading to clashes
[p0035-l0016] [10.8pt] between development and those creating the designs.
[p0035-l0017] [10.8pt] 2 A characteristic of the code that possibly indicates a deeper problem.
[p0035-l0018] [10.8pt] 3 C4 also has some supplementary diagrams such as a deployment diagram.
[p0035-l0019] [10.8pt] 4 Notice that the data store identities (A, B, and so on) are ordered in the same sequence as the data
[p0035-l0020] [10.8pt] stores are accessed in the flow of the diagram.
[p0035-l0021] [10.8pt] 5 Cognitive load is the amount of effort a person has to exert to reason or think about something.

=== PDF page 36 / printed label 36 ===
[p0036-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 2. Clarify the Clutter
[p0036-l0002] [14.4pt] Your chances of communicating successfully are drastically reduced if an
[p0036-l0003] [14.4pt] audience has to work hard to understand your message. This chapter explores the
[p0036-l0004] [14.4pt] patterns and antipatterns that will help you reduce your audience’s cognitive
[p0036-l0005] [14.4pt] load. You will identify and eliminate elements of your diagram that obscure your
[p0036-l0006] [14.4pt] message, and split that message across multiple diagrams where needed.
[p0036-l0007] [20.16pt] Color Overload
[p0036-l0008] [14.4pt] When talking about the color overload antipattern, I often refer to it as an
[p0036-l0009] [14.4pt] explosion of unicorns. In most cases, the colors used in diagrams are not given
[p0036-l0010] [14.4pt] much, if any, thought at all. When too many colors are used, the audience may
[p0036-l0011] [14.4pt] have difficulty matching them to their meaning without a lot of mental effort
[p0036-l0012] [14.4pt] (even if a legend is included). Even worse, colors that are used without any
[p0036-l0013] [14.4pt] meaning at all cause the audience to waste mental energy deciphering a detail
[p0036-l0014] [14.4pt] that’s irrelevant to your intended message.
[p0036-l0015] [14.4pt] This antipattern usually occurs because the diagram’s author had no motivation
[p0036-l0016] [14.4pt] to consider color or no idea that color is important in visual communication. The
[p0036-l0017] [14.4pt] author might use default colors or randomly selected colors in a diagramming
[p0036-l0018] [14.4pt] application because that is quicker than taking the time to think about color
[p0036-l0019] [14.4pt] selection or because that individual or business has always used those colors.
[p0036-l0020] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0036-l0021] [10.8pt] Failing to consider color in a visual presentation is an example of being “penny-wise but
[p0036-l0022] [10.8pt] pound-foolish.” You may save time creating the diagram but will spend more time (and
[p0036-l0023] [10.8pt] therefore money) later by having to explain to audiences, redo the diagram, or even clear up a
[p0036-l0024] [10.8pt] mess resulting from miscommunication. Just as fixing bugs in code early saves money and
[p0036-l0025] [10.8pt] time in the long run, so does getting communication right early.
[p0036-l0026] [14.4pt] This antipattern applies not just to diagrams but to all visuals, except those
[p0036-l0027] [14.4pt] purely in black-and-white (that is black-and-white only, and not grayscale). But

=== PDF page 37 / printed label 37 ===
[p0037-l0001] [14.4pt] don’t think the answer is to create all visuals in black-and-white. The way to
[p0037-l0002] [14.4pt] combat this antipattern is to consider color and use it to communicate.
[p0037-l0003] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0037-l0004] [10.8pt] If you’re reading this book in print, all the images are in grayscale. Where color is important,
[p0037-l0005] [10.8pt] the image caption includes a link to the book’s website, which contains all color versions.
[p0037-l0006] [14.4pt] In Figure 2-1, an overpowering array of bright colors shows that each
[p0037-l0007] [14.4pt] component in the diagram is different, but without any consideration for
[p0037-l0008] [14.4pt] communicating any further details. Think about how you would fix this before
[p0037-l0009] [14.4pt] reading on.
[p0037-l0010] [10.8pt] Figure 2-1. Rainbow sequence diagram (https://communicationpatternsbook.com)
[p0037-l0011] [14.4pt] To fix Figure 2-1, you must minimize the color palette, using only the number of
[p0037-l0012] [14.4pt] colors needed to convey your message. You don’t need a different color for
[p0037-l0013] [14.4pt] every component. When selecting colors, consider which colors go well together
[p0037-l0014] [14.4pt] (that is, they don’t clash) as well as the luminosity of the colors; too many bright
[p0037-l0015] [14.4pt] colors may overwhelm your audience.
[p0037-l0016] [14.4pt] Next you need to consider what you are trying to communicate to your audience
[p0037-l0017] [14.4pt] via these selected colors. You can use them to communicate aspects of the
[p0037-l0018] [14.4pt] components like function or type by using the same color for each category. For

=== PDF page 38 / printed label 38 ===
[p0038-l0001] [14.4pt] example, Figure 2-2 uses four colors to differentiate the UI, data store, APIs, and
[p0038-l0002] [14.4pt] service, and a key communicates the meaning of the colors.
[p0038-l0003] [10.8pt] Figure 2-2. Colors used to group by type (https://communicationpatternsbook.com)
[p0038-l0004] [14.4pt] Grouping colors by type isn’t the only option for improving Figure 2-1, but it
[p0038-l0005] [14.4pt] implements the fixes just mentioned to minimize distraction from the message of
[p0038-l0006] [14.4pt] the diagram. Avoiding distractions from your message is key to successful
[p0038-l0007] [14.4pt] communication.
[p0038-l0008] [20.16pt] Boxes in Boxes in Boxes
[p0038-l0009] [14.4pt] Boxes are often used to communicate where a component in the diagram is
[p0038-l0010] [14.4pt] situated (conceptually, logically, physically…) as well as to group components.
[p0038-l0011] [14.4pt] When you have too many boxes, their many lines become visually confusing,
[p0038-l0012] [14.4pt] meaning the audience must spend precious attention and brainpower working out
[p0038-l0013] [14.4pt] which line belongs to which box. You need a lot of whitespace to make a
[p0038-l0014] [14.4pt] diagram with many boxes legible, leaving less space for what you actually want
[p0038-l0015] [14.4pt] to communicate.
[p0038-l0016] [14.4pt] The boxes in boxes in boxes antipattern emerges as the diagram author uses the
[p0038-l0017] [14.4pt] same form of delineation to represent different meanings in a diagram. Maybe
[p0038-l0018] [14.4pt] the author is unaware of other ways of expressing meaning or ends up trapped in

=== PDF page 39 / printed label 39 ===
[p0039-l0001] [14.4pt] the this is the way it has always been done way of thinking.
[p0039-l0002] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0039-l0003] [10.8pt] Whitespace is just as important as the content of your diagram. It gives the eye a place to rest,
[p0039-l0004] [10.8pt] reducing cognitive load, as well as making the diagram easier to scan and more legible.
[p0039-l0005] [14.4pt] Whatever the reason, this antipattern disrupts communication and usually wastes
[p0039-l0006] [14.4pt] time or money. Your audience will not appreciate having to expend effort on
[p0039-l0007] [14.4pt] deciphering your diagram and will either not expend this effort or do so but still
[p0039-l0008] [14.4pt] not receive the message you are trying to communicate.
[p0039-l0009] [14.4pt] Many types of diagrams can fall prey to this antipattern, but structural diagrams
[p0039-l0010] [14.4pt] that convey situation or location (for example, logical location) are the most
[p0039-l0011] [14.4pt] common victims. Data flow or sequence diagrams can end up with lines and
[p0039-l0012] [14.4pt] boxes too close together, and the fixes discussed in this section will still apply.
[p0039-l0013] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0039-l0014] [10.8pt] Keep your background color subtle compared to the colors used for borders and text. You must
[p0039-l0015] [10.8pt] create high contrast between elements in your diagram and the background so that your
[p0039-l0016] [10.8pt] audience can clearly differentiate everything in the diagram.
[p0039-l0017] [14.4pt] Figure 2-3 is a cloud resources diagram that uses dashed-line boxes to indicate
[p0039-l0018] [14.4pt] various logical constructs like virtual network boundaries, storage accounts, and
[p0039-l0019] [14.4pt] policy application. The difference in line type (dashed versus solid) between
[p0039-l0020] [14.4pt] boxes and relationships is good, but a lot more can be done to make this diagram
[p0039-l0021] [14.4pt] more readable. How could you improve Figure 2-3?

=== PDF page 40 / printed label 40 ===
[p0040-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 2-3. Boxes in boxes in boxes cloud resources diagram (antipattern)
[p0040-l0002] [14.4pt] When you create diagrams, consider alternatives to boxes.1 Labels on
[p0040-l0003] [14.4pt] components are a good alternative. If you do use boxes, differentiate between
[p0040-l0004] [14.4pt] them by using color and pattern of both the box outline and background.
[p0040-l0005] [14.4pt] Removing unnecessary detail and splitting the diagram into multiple diagrams
[p0040-l0006] [14.4pt] are also good techniques.
[p0040-l0007] [14.4pt] Figure 2-4 shows one example of how the diagram in Figure 2-3 can be
[p0040-l0008] [14.4pt] improved:
[p0040-l0009] [14.4pt] Some boxes have been replaced with labels or notes, reducing the number
[p0040-l0010] [14.4pt] of meanings boxes are used to communicate.
[p0040-l0011] [14.4pt] Other boxes have been merged because their separation was not required to
[p0040-l0012] [14.4pt] convey the message.
[p0040-l0013] [14.4pt] Those boxes that remain have been differentiated using color and pattern
[p0040-l0014] [14.4pt] (solid line and a shaded background).

=== PDF page 41 / printed label 41 ===
[p0041-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 2-4. Decluttered cloud resources diagram
[p0041-l0002] [14.4pt] Remember that boxes are just one way to communicate meaning in a diagram,
[p0041-l0003] [14.4pt] and the nesting of boxes is often visually confusing. Whitespace is your friend,
[p0041-l0004] [14.4pt] along with the other strategies discussed in this antipattern.
[p0041-l0005] [20.16pt] Relationship Spiderweb
[p0041-l0006] [14.4pt] Connections (or relationships) in diagrams are typically shown as lines, but the
[p0041-l0007] [14.4pt] way those lines are styled and arranged determines whether the relationships are
[p0041-l0008] [14.4pt] clear.
[p0041-l0009] [14.4pt] When relationships cross over each other or even cross over other components in
[p0041-l0010] [14.4pt] the diagram, you end up with a spiderweb of confusion. It is not clear whether
[p0041-l0011] [14.4pt] this crossing has meaning. If relationships are labeled, it is not clear which line
[p0041-l0012] [14.4pt] the label applies to as there are so many lines and labels close together. A spider
[p0041-l0013] [14.4pt] would feel at home and catch a good dinner if it lived in a diagram suffering
[p0041-l0014] [14.4pt] from this antipattern.
[p0041-l0015] [14.4pt] The relationship spiderweb antipattern often occurs when not much thought, if
[p0041-l0016] [14.4pt] any, has been given to the layout of the components in a diagram or their
[p0041-l0017] [14.4pt] relationships. Some diagramming applications provide unhelpful defaults, such
[p0041-l0018] [14.4pt] as arrows with unclear line crossings. However, as the saying goes, “It’s a bad

=== PDF page 42 / printed label 42 ===
[p0042-l0001] [14.4pt] craftsperson who blames their tools.” Change those defaults or change the tools
[p0042-l0002] [14.4pt] you use.2
[p0042-l0003] [14.4pt] Any diagram that shows relationships can suffer from this antipattern, but
[p0042-l0004] [14.4pt] structural diagrams are more prone to it than behavioral ones. Even C4 diagrams,
[p0042-l0005] [14.4pt] which promote many good practices in creating diagrams, can fall foul of the
[p0042-l0006] [14.4pt] relationship spiderweb antipattern.
[p0042-l0007] [14.4pt] In Figure 2-5, relationships cross each other and even over containers, adding to
[p0042-l0008] [14.4pt] the audience’s mental load. To add further burden, it is not clear to which
[p0042-l0009] [14.4pt] relationship some of the labels apply.
[p0042-l0010] [14.4pt] Figure 2-6 shows an example of how Figure 2-5 can be improved. The arrows
[p0042-l0011] [14.4pt] (relationships) have been made orthogonal (using right angles) instead of
[p0042-l0012] [14.4pt] straight. This allows the diagram’s author to manipulate the relationship much
[p0042-l0013] [14.4pt] more easily to ensure higher clarity. It is now obvious to the audience which
[p0042-l0014] [14.4pt] label applies to each relationship. If required for further clarity, you can use
[p0042-l0015] [14.4pt] pattern or color to distinguish between different types of relationships.
[p0042-l0016] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0042-l0017] [10.8pt] The position of labels indicating relationships should be standardized in a diagram—for
[p0042-l0018] [10.8pt] example, close to the beginning of the relationship or in the middle of the line. Make
[p0042-l0019] [10.8pt] exceptions to this rule when moving the label would clarify the relationship or label, such as
[p0042-l0020] [10.8pt] away from a right angle or farther away from another label.
[p0042-l0021] [14.4pt] None of the components in Figure 2-5 had to be removed when creating
[p0042-l0022] [14.4pt] Figure 2-6, but if components such as a logging service were present, they could
[p0042-l0023] [14.4pt] be moved into a separate diagram so that its relationships would be clear in that
[p0042-l0024] [14.4pt] separate diagram.
[p0042-l0025] [14.4pt] When two lines cross in a diagram, it is ambiguous whether they are right angled
[p0042-l0026] [14.4pt] or straight. Make it clear that they do not intersect by using a line jump (typically
[p0042-l0027] [14.4pt] an arc that jumps the line it crosses). No line jumps are required in Figure 2-6,
[p0042-l0028] [14.4pt] but Figure 15-2 has one.
[p0042-l0029] [14.4pt] NOTE

=== PDF page 43 / printed label 43 ===
[p0043-l0001] [10.8pt] Remember that diagrams are free; you should use as many as you need to successfully
[p0043-l0002] [10.8pt] communicate your message to the audience. Multiple diagrams, each with a single purpose, are
[p0043-l0003] [10.8pt] much more effective than one diagram with multiple purposes (as discussed in “Mixing Levels
[p0043-l0004] [10.8pt] of Abstraction”).

=== PDF page 44 / printed label 44 ===
[p0044-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 2-5. Spiderweb C4 container diagram for Polyglot Media (antipattern)

=== PDF page 45 / printed label 45 ===
[p0045-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 2-6. Untangled C4 diagram
[p0045-l0002] [20.16pt] Balance Text

=== PDF page 46 / printed label 46 ===
[p0046-l0001] [14.4pt] Having too much information in a diagram obfuscates the intended message but
[p0046-l0002] [14.4pt] sometimes you really need to add extra information to completely convey the
[p0046-l0003] [14.4pt] message. In that case, you can apply the balance text pattern. You are balancing
[p0046-l0004] [14.4pt] having enough information for the audience to understand the message against
[p0046-l0005] [14.4pt] having so much clutter that the message is lost or only partially understood.
[p0046-l0006] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0046-l0007] [10.8pt] Information that isn’t best displayed in a diagram should be moved to text or tabular form
[p0046-l0008] [10.8pt] alongside the diagram. Information written as sentences is a good candidate to abridge or
[p0046-l0009] [10.8pt] remove from a diagram into separate text. Relational data is a good candidate to be displayed
[p0046-l0010] [10.8pt] in table form.
[p0046-l0011] [14.4pt] Some notations and types of diagrams, such as flow diagrams, have built-in
[p0046-l0012] [14.4pt] ways of adding more information—a standard format to add extra text without
[p0046-l0013] [14.4pt] becoming visually confusing. For other diagrams, notes or footnotes can add
[p0046-l0014] [14.4pt] more information without cluttering the main content.
[p0046-l0015] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0046-l0016] [10.8pt] If you do use notes, be wary of separating them entirely (for example, as text alongside the
[p0046-l0017] [10.8pt] diagram), because if the notes get lost or the diagram is viewed without them, the diagram
[p0046-l0018] [10.8pt] could be misunderstood.
[p0046-l0019] [14.4pt] The flow diagram in Figure 2-7 has too much text. The word customer, which
[p0046-l0020] [14.4pt] has been introduced through the subtitle, is repeated unnecessarily in the
[p0046-l0021] [14.4pt] diagram boxes. Note that some of the text doesn’t even fit within the flowchart
[p0046-l0022] [14.4pt] components.
[p0046-l0023] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0046-l0024] [10.8pt] In general, it is better to follow an existing convention for adding extra information to a
[p0046-l0025] [10.8pt] diagram, such as the annotation shown in Figure 2-8 (rather than separate notes). Using a
[p0046-l0026] [10.8pt] common or expected approach will aid the audience’s understanding, although exceptions will
[p0046-l0027] [10.8pt] exist.

=== PDF page 47 / printed label 47 ===
[p0047-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 2-7. Flow diagram with too much text (antipattern)
[p0047-l0002] [14.4pt] Look to Figure 2-8 for an example of how to apply this pattern and two ways to
[p0047-l0003] [14.4pt] include extra text in the diagram (one method is usually enough, but two are
[p0047-l0004] [14.4pt] illustrated here). Excess text, like the repetition of customer, has been
[p0047-l0005] [14.4pt] completely removed or shortened. Information that is required has been placed
[p0047-l0006] [14.4pt] into a flowchart annotation (explaining “Select consumer mode”) and into four

=== PDF page 48 / printed label 48 ===
[p0048-l0001] [14.4pt] numbered notes (with superscript references in the diagram), making the main
[p0048-l0002] [14.4pt] flow much clearer.
[p0048-l0003] [10.8pt] Figure 2-8. Flow diagram with excess text removed or moved to annotations and references
[p0048-l0004] [14.4pt] Besides removing the repeated word customer, the text has been changed from
[p0048-l0005] [14.4pt] full sentences to short phrases. The subject, customer, can be inferred from the
[p0048-l0006] [14.4pt] title and context. You are not repeating information that can be deduced from the
[p0048-l0007] [14.4pt] structure of the diagram itself.

=== PDF page 49 / printed label 49 ===
[p0049-l0001] [14.4pt] Deciding how to display information stripped from the main diagram will
[p0049-l0002] [14.4pt] depend on the type of diagram, your message, and the format of the delivery
[p0049-l0003] [14.4pt] (presentation, documentation, or other forms).
[p0049-l0004] [14.4pt] You may be tempted to move all excess information into notes or footnotes, but
[p0049-l0005] [14.4pt] this just moves the problem rather than solving it. The audience will still suffer
[p0049-l0006] [14.4pt] from having to wade through excess information. Make sure to remove any
[p0049-l0007] [14.4pt] information that is not required to convey your message. Move it to another
[p0049-l0008] [14.4pt] diagram if needed.
[p0049-l0009] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0049-l0010] [14.4pt] Your diagrams should now be free of clutter and have a single purpose. Look out
[p0049-l0011] [14.4pt] for scope-creep (where the scope of your diagram grows and grows), which will
[p0049-l0012] [14.4pt] likely make your diagram multipurpose and allow clutter to creep back in. Don’t
[p0049-l0013] [14.4pt] be afraid to split a diagram into multiple diagrams to clarify your message.
[p0049-l0014] [14.4pt] Your next stop on this diagramming journey is accessibility. In the following
[p0049-l0015] [14.4pt] chapter, you will see how default or common practices can make it much harder
[p0049-l0016] [14.4pt] for some of your audience to understand your visuals. You will find that what
[p0049-l0017] [14.4pt] you have learned about decluttering your diagrams has begun the process of
[p0049-l0018] [14.4pt] making them more accessible.
[p0049-l0019] [10.8pt] 1 Boxes hark back to the Windows 3.1 era when they are used by themselves. Far fewer colors could
[p0049-l0020] [10.8pt] be displayed on a screen.
[p0049-l0021] [10.8pt] 2 Most diagramming applications will remember your updated default settings, so you should need to
[p0049-l0022] [10.8pt] set your defaults only once.

=== PDF page 50 / printed label 50 ===
[p0050-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 3. Accessibility
[p0050-l0002] [14.4pt] When most people hear the word accessibility in the world of software, they
[p0050-l0003] [14.4pt] immediately think of screen readers (for those who are blind or have reduced
[p0050-l0004] [14.4pt] vision), but they don’t think much further than this. Accessibility in the
[p0050-l0005] [14.4pt] technology arena encompasses much more.
[p0050-l0006] [14.4pt] When you create a diagram, you should consider whether your audience can
[p0050-l0007] [14.4pt] fully access it. If a stakeholder doesn’t fully understand your message, you both
[p0050-l0008] [14.4pt] lose out.
[p0050-l0009] [14.4pt] Accessibility isn’t just for those with temporary or permanent disabilities or
[p0050-l0010] [14.4pt] special needs. Your diagrams and visuals need to be accessible to people with
[p0050-l0011] [14.4pt] different levels of knowledge, business functions, and familiarity with your
[p0050-l0012] [14.4pt] product or domain. Your audience’s environment also affects the level to which
[p0050-l0013] [14.4pt] they can access your diagram, including the screen size they are using and
[p0050-l0014] [14.4pt] amount of time they have to consume your information.
[p0050-l0015] [14.4pt] This chapter will help you consider accessibility beyond screen readers and
[p0050-l0016] [14.4pt] produce diagrams that can be accessed by a much wider audience.
[p0050-l0017] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0050-l0018] [10.8pt] Anyone can be disabled. It is their environment that disables them. Your aim is to put everyone
[p0050-l0019] [10.8pt] on an equal footing.
[p0050-l0020] [20.16pt] Relying on Color to Communicate
[p0050-l0021] [14.4pt] You are relying on color to communicate when you use color alone to represent
[p0050-l0022] [14.4pt] meaning, such as different colored boxes to represent new or changed items in a
[p0050-l0023] [14.4pt] diagram. The most obvious and common form of the relying on color to
[p0050-l0024] [14.4pt] communicate antipattern is to use color to represent positive and negative
[p0050-l0025] [14.4pt] without providing any other indication of meaning (such as a legend) for those
[p0050-l0026] [14.4pt] who can’t distinguish the colors. For example, green is used to show good or go,

=== PDF page 51 / printed label 51 ===
[p0051-l0001] [14.4pt] and red for bad or stop. A traffic light at least uses position as an indicator of
[p0051-l0002] [14.4pt] color meaning, but red and green are often used in diagrams without even this
[p0051-l0003] [14.4pt] sort of cue.
[p0051-l0004] [14.4pt] Having said that, there has been a movement away from using just the colors red
[p0051-l0005] [14.4pt] and green. For example, a diff in GitHub’s UI shows additions in green with plus
[p0051-l0006] [14.4pt] signs, and deletions in red with minus signs.1 That hasn’t happened for other
[p0051-l0007] [14.4pt] colors, though, and this is affecting the accessibility of diagrams and other
[p0051-l0008] [14.4pt] visuals.
[p0051-l0009] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0051-l0010] [10.8pt] EventStorming (a collaborative modeling technique) uses colored sticky notes to represent
[p0051-l0011] [10.8pt] different elements. The notes also have different shapes to ensure that everyone can see the
[p0051-l0012] [10.8pt] difference between any two elements.
[p0051-l0013] [14.4pt] You cannot rely on color to be perceived the same way by everyone for several
[p0051-l0014] [14.4pt] reasons. Of the total population, 4.5% have some form of color blindness (color
[p0051-l0015] [14.4pt] vision deficiency). It is more prevalent in men (affecting 1 in 12 men) than
[p0051-l0016] [14.4pt] women (1 in 200). So, sadly, as the tech industry is still dominated by men, more
[p0051-l0017] [14.4pt] than 4.5% of your audience likely cannot perceive the difference between two or
[p0051-l0018] [14.4pt] more colors in your visual.
[p0051-l0019] [14.4pt] Along with color vision deficiency, some people struggle to perceive low
[p0051-l0020] [14.4pt] contrast—for example, those who are partially sighted. Other conditions that
[p0051-l0021] [14.4pt] might affect how well your audience can view your diagram and its colors
[p0051-l0022] [14.4pt] include photophobia, keratoconus, and glaucoma.2 In addition to color, you need
[p0051-l0023] [14.4pt] to consider contrast to make your diagrams accessible.
[p0051-l0024] [14.4pt] CONTRAST
[p0051-l0025] [14.4pt] All of your audience needs to be able to easily perceive the content of your
[p0051-l0026] [14.4pt] diagram, and this doesn’t just include text. Arrows, icons, and patterns also
[p0051-l0027] [14.4pt] need to have adequate contrast.
[p0051-l0028] [14.4pt] You can calculate a contrast ratio, the perceived difference in luminance
[p0051-l0029] [14.4pt] between a foreground and background color; for example, pure red (hex

=== PDF page 52 / printed label 52 ===
[p0052-l0001] [14.4pt] value #FF0000) on a white background has a contrast ratio of 4:1. The ratio
[p0052-l0002] [14.4pt] remains the same even if the foreground and background colors are
[p0052-l0003] [14.4pt] swapped. The ratio required changes depending on the size of the text, icon,
[p0052-l0004] [14.4pt] pattern, and so on (smaller requires a higher ratio). WhoCanUse is one tool
[p0052-l0005] [14.4pt] you can use to check the contrast ratio between two colors.
[p0052-l0006] [14.4pt] The Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG) provide best practices
[p0052-l0007] [14.4pt] and requirements, along with success criteria, for making digital content
[p0052-l0008] [14.4pt] accessible to people with disabilities. For more information see the WCAG
[p0052-l0009] [14.4pt] website, and WebAIM specifically for contrast.
[p0052-l0010] [14.4pt] Another factor that affects the perception of colors in a diagram is format. The
[p0052-l0011] [14.4pt] monitor or projector your audience is using may be calibrated very differently
[p0052-l0012] [14.4pt] from the monitor the diagram was prepared on. Similar colors may end up fairly
[p0052-l0013] [14.4pt] indistinguishable or unidentifiable. If a green color looks yellow to the audience,
[p0052-l0014] [14.4pt] but you refer to a “green” component, for example, the audience will be
[p0052-l0015] [14.4pt] confused.
[p0052-l0016] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0052-l0017] [10.8pt] When using diagrams in multiple mediums—for example, a wiki/web page or a presentation
[p0052-l0018] [10.8pt] using a projector—you should consider using different color palettes. This is especially true if
[p0052-l0019] [10.8pt] the background color will be different across mediums (for example, white in a web page and
[p0052-l0020] [10.8pt] black in a slide deck). Consider how the audience will perceive the colors and how the
[p0052-l0021] [10.8pt] background color will change the contrast ratio of elements and labels.
[p0052-l0022] [14.4pt] Diagrams or visuals may be viewed in printed form, such as in a document
[p0052-l0023] [14.4pt] (paper, pamphlet, and so on) or a book, and could be printed in grayscale (a book
[p0052-l0024] [14.4pt] is likely to be printed in grayscale). All the colors are lost in grayscale, and only
[p0052-l0025] [14.4pt] the saturation (lightness/darkness) remains. In this case, all the colors in a
[p0052-l0026] [14.4pt] diagram could appear to be the same shade of gray.
[p0052-l0027] [14.4pt] If colors are indistinguishable, any meaning ascribed to them is lost. A legend is
[p0052-l0028] [14.4pt] meaningless if the audience cannot tell one color from another. When presenting
[p0052-l0029] [14.4pt] a diagram and referring to a component by color, part of your audience is likely
[p0052-l0030] [14.4pt] to be unable to tell which component you are referring to, whether the diagram is
[p0052-l0031] [14.4pt] in color or grayscale. Don’t think because it looks good to you that your message

=== PDF page 53 / printed label 53 ===
[p0053-l0001] [14.4pt] will be successfully communicated to the audience.
[p0053-l0002] [14.4pt] You have several options for avoiding the relying on color to communicate
[p0053-l0003] [14.4pt] antipattern and giving audiences a cue besides color. One method is to use
[p0053-l0004] [14.4pt] patterns. By using a suitably contrasting pattern, like the one shown in Figure 3-
[p0053-l0005] [14.4pt] 1, colors that may look similar in grayscale or to someone with color vision
[p0053-l0006] [14.4pt] deficiency can still be differentiated easily. This is a valuable technique when
[p0053-l0007] [14.4pt] you can’t vary the color palette in your diagram.
[p0053-l0008] [10.8pt] Figure 3-1. Even in grayscale, pattern can differentiate two colors
[p0053-l0009] [14.4pt] Don’t refer to components of a diagram, either in speech or text, using only color
[p0053-l0010] [14.4pt] as a reference, such as “The red boxes in the diagram show…” This is the sort of
[p0053-l0011] [14.4pt] situation that can be solved by using patterns: “The red boxes with dashed
[p0053-l0012] [14.4pt] borders show…”
[p0053-l0013] [14.4pt] Symbols are another useful tool for distinguishing between components or
[p0053-l0014] [14.4pt] colors. You can use symbols on their own or with color to clarify your message;
[p0053-l0015] [14.4pt] for example, when using red, yellow, and green for status, you could add a +
[p0053-l0016] [14.4pt] (plus) to the green and a – (minus) to the red.
[p0053-l0017] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0053-l0018] [10.8pt] Consider what colors and symbols mean, especially in cultural terms, before using them in
[p0053-l0019] [10.8pt] your diagram. Ensure that they communicate your intended message and do not cause

=== PDF page 54 / printed label 54 ===
[p0054-l0001] [10.8pt] confusion or offense.
[p0054-l0002] [14.4pt] You can also create an accessible color palette. The easiest way to check whether
[p0054-l0003] [14.4pt] a color palette is accessible is to use a tool. Many free options exist; you can
[p0054-l0004] [14.4pt] install some as an extension in your internet browser, which is particularly useful
[p0054-l0005] [14.4pt] for checking website palettes.
[p0054-l0006] [14.4pt] DESIGN TOOLS FOR COLOR VISION DEFICIENCY
[p0054-l0007] [14.4pt] Here are some tools I recommended to help you create an accessible color
[p0054-l0008] [14.4pt] palette:
[p0054-l0009] [14.4pt] Color Oracle is a free application for Windows, Mac, and Linux that
[p0054-l0010] [14.4pt] can simulate four types of color vision deficiency (including
[p0054-l0011] [14.4pt] grayscale/monochromacy).
[p0054-l0012] [14.4pt] Coblis is a free online tool that allows you to upload an image and
[p0054-l0013] [14.4pt] simulate eight types of color vision deficiency.
[p0054-l0014] [14.4pt] Sim Daltonism is a better option than Color Oracle if you have a Mac
[p0054-l0015] [14.4pt] or iPhone because it simulates eight forms of color blindness and on
[p0054-l0016] [14.4pt] Mac has a draggable and resizable window.
[p0054-l0017] [14.4pt] Chromatic Vision Simulator is a free application for Android and iOS,
[p0054-l0018] [14.4pt] and also has a web version. It simulates up to three types of color
[p0054-l0019] [14.4pt] blindness live from a phone camera or by using a saved photo or image.
[p0054-l0020] [14.4pt] Viz Palette is a free tool for selecting color palettes for data and other
[p0054-l0021] [14.4pt] visualizations. It produces a color report to highlight colors which could
[p0054-l0022] [14.4pt] be confused and is optimized for copying and pasting in and out of
[p0054-l0023] [14.4pt] JavaScript.
[p0054-l0024] [14.4pt] Figure 3-2 shows some of the standard colors from draw.io, a free diagramming
[p0054-l0025] [14.4pt] and drawing application. For those reading this book in print, the elements in
[p0054-l0026] [14.4pt] Figure 3-2 look like the same shade in grayscale. For those reading a digital
[p0054-l0027] [14.4pt] version, the colors are all pastel tones, which are the same or similar luminosity.
[p0054-l0028] [14.4pt] This indicates that the color palette is not accessible. Draw.io offers several

=== PDF page 55 / printed label 55 ===
[p0055-l0001] [14.4pt] palettes, and you can select your own colors. Assuming that a default palette is
[p0055-l0002] [14.4pt] accessible could lead you to exclude some of your audience.
[p0055-l0003] [10.8pt] Figure 3-2. Flow diagram showing some of the standard colors from draw.io
[p0055-l0004] [10.8pt] (https://communicationpatternsbook.com)
[p0055-l0005] [14.4pt] Figure 3-3 is a simulation of Figure 3-2 for a person with deuteranopia, a type of
[p0055-l0006] [14.4pt] color blindness preventing the perception of green. Although the original
[p0055-l0007] [14.4pt] diagram has four colors, the simulation shown in Figure 3-3 appears to have only
[p0055-l0008] [14.4pt] three (at least without looking closely). There is hardly any difference between
[p0055-l0009] [14.4pt] the color of Customer UI and Customer API in Figure 3-3.
[p0055-l0010] [10.8pt] Figure 3-3. Deuteranopia (green-blind) simulation of Figure 3-2 (https://communicationpatternsbook.com)

=== PDF page 56 / printed label 56 ===
[p0056-l0001] [14.4pt] When considering a color palette, in addition to checking it with a simulator
[p0056-l0002] [14.4pt] tool, look to include colors with different levels of saturation. Contrast between
[p0056-l0003] [14.4pt] colors will improve differentiation for someone who requires high contrast or is
[p0056-l0004] [14.4pt] color blind.
[p0056-l0005] [14.4pt] Don’t trust that a diagramming application’s palette or a corporate color scheme
[p0056-l0006] [14.4pt] has been checked for compatibility with grayscale or color blindness. It would
[p0056-l0007] [14.4pt] be nice to think that designers and companies (especially larger ones with bigger
[p0056-l0008] [14.4pt] budgets) have considered accessibility when it comes to the colors they choose
[p0056-l0009] [14.4pt] for an official palette or the default colors in an app, but you cannot count on
[p0056-l0010] [14.4pt] this. Test your corporate color palette, or the default colors of the diagramming
[p0056-l0011] [14.4pt] application you use most often, using a color blindness simulator. Make sure you
[p0056-l0012] [14.4pt] give feedback on what you discover, whether good or bad!
[p0056-l0013] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0056-l0014] [10.8pt] Alternate text (alt text) offers a text description of an image or diagram. Especially for public
[p0056-l0015] [10.8pt] content (for example, documentation for users), you should consider carefully crafting alt text
[p0056-l0016] [10.8pt] for any diagrams or other visuals.
[p0056-l0017] [14.4pt] Ask for feedback on your diagrams, especially from those with color blindness
[p0056-l0018] [14.4pt] or a disability, and incorporate this feedback into the diagram and your other
[p0056-l0019] [14.4pt] future visuals.
[p0056-l0020] [20.16pt] Include a Legend
[p0056-l0021] [14.4pt] Including a legend, or a key, with your diagram is a valuable technique to aid
[p0056-l0022] [14.4pt] successful communication. Not including one assumes that the audience has
[p0056-l0023] [14.4pt] specific knowledge, be that a full understanding of the notation, knowledge of all
[p0056-l0024] [14.4pt] the terms and acronyms in a diagram, or knowledge of all the symbols and icons
[p0056-l0025] [14.4pt] contained in the diagram. Don’t rely on your audience to have this knowledge
[p0056-l0026] [14.4pt] unless you wish to risk how well your diagram communicates its message.
[p0056-l0027] [14.4pt] It’s a balancing act: we all know that you cannot please everyone all of the time.
[p0056-l0028] [14.4pt] But you can put guides in for those who need them without getting in the way of
[p0056-l0029] [14.4pt] those who don’t. Getting this balance right results in successful communication.

=== PDF page 57 / printed label 57 ===
[p0057-l0001] [14.4pt] Consider ramps and steps as a metaphor for a legend (see Figure 3-4). People
[p0057-l0002] [14.4pt] who need the ramp are enabled to get to where they need to go, while those who
[p0057-l0003] [14.4pt] don’t need it can choose to take the steps.
[p0057-l0004] [10.8pt] Figure 3-4. Ramps for those who need or want to use them, and steps for anyone who wants to take them
[p0057-l0005] [14.4pt] You don’t always need a legend and, sometimes, including one can mean you
[p0057-l0006] [14.4pt] fall foul of clutter antipatterns. The intention of the legend is to clarify, not
[p0057-l0007] [14.4pt] obscure, the message of the diagram. To fight clutter, where a legend is required,
[p0057-l0008] [14.4pt] you can link to it and therefore save all the space it would have taken up. For
[p0057-l0009] [14.4pt] example, on a web page, your legend could have a hide or show option (see
[p0057-l0010] [14.4pt] Figure 3-5). But make sure the link is explicit and obvious. You could also
[p0057-l0011] [14.4pt] include a legend for one or more diagrams at the top of a page.

=== PDF page 58 / printed label 58 ===
[p0058-l0001] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0058-l0002] [10.8pt] Look out for cases where it would be simpler to use labels instead of a legend. For many charts
[p0058-l0003] [10.8pt] and graphs, reading a label for a column or line is much easier than referencing a legend.
[p0058-l0004] [10.8pt] Figure 3-5. Including a link to a legend in a web page or document
[p0058-l0005] [14.4pt] A legend such as the one in Figure 3-6 can be particularly useful to an audience
[p0058-l0006] [14.4pt] faced with a UML diagram because many people do not know the UML notation
[p0058-l0007] [14.4pt] or cannot remember all its eccentricities. If a full legend like this takes up too
[p0058-l0008] [14.4pt] much space, you could include a partial legend, containing just the parts relevant
[p0058-l0009] [14.4pt] to the diagram it is explaining, or include a link to the legend, as previously
[p0058-l0010] [14.4pt] mentioned (See Figure 3-5).
[p0058-l0011] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0058-l0012] [10.8pt] Be explicit, not implicit. Most diagrams will benefit from a legend. If you aren’t including one,
[p0058-l0013] [10.8pt] have a good reason not to (for example, for a very simple diagram or chart, labels are a better
[p0058-l0014] [10.8pt] choice).

=== PDF page 59 / printed label 59 ===
[p0059-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 3-6. A UML legend showing the eccentricities of UML
[p0059-l0002] [20.16pt] Appropriate Labels
[p0059-l0003] [14.4pt] When creating a diagram, remember that what is written is as important as what
[p0059-l0004] [14.4pt] is not written. It is easy to include too much or too little information, or the
[p0059-l0005] [14.4pt] wrong information entirely. The appropriate labels pattern is about making sure
[p0059-l0006] [14.4pt] text and labels clearly communicate your message.
[p0059-l0007] [14.4pt] Besides the content of the text, you should consider its placement. Ensure that
[p0059-l0008] [14.4pt] your labels are close to the component or relationship you are labeling, and
[p0059-l0009] [14.4pt] balance the components and text with whitespace. The text should make the
[p0059-l0010] [14.4pt] message of the diagram explicit through clarity of content and placement.
[p0059-l0011] [14.4pt] Interestingly, coders often put emphasis on writing code with thoughtful naming
[p0059-l0012] [14.4pt] and structuring so that it does not require comments (explanatory text that is not
[p0059-l0013] [14.4pt] part of the program). With diagrams, the emphasis is more on making the
[p0059-l0014] [14.4pt] message explicit via composition (arrangement), the chosen components, and the
[p0059-l0015] [14.4pt] carefully crafted labels and other text. You often need to include explanation
[p0059-l0016] [14.4pt] (like comments in code) to fully convey a message in a diagram because its

=== PDF page 60 / printed label 60 ===
[p0060-l0001] [14.4pt] primary aim is to communicate.3
[p0060-l0002] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0060-l0003] [10.8pt] The art of creating a diagram involves balancing the competing factors of all the information
[p0060-l0004] [10.8pt] you need to communicate and the clarity you need to communicate successfully.
[p0060-l0005] [14.4pt] Figure 3-7 shows a C4 context diagram with not enough labels or descriptive
[p0060-l0006] [14.4pt] text. This gives you some idea of how your audience may feel when they see a
[p0060-l0007] [14.4pt] diagram that doesn’t give them enough information to understand the meaning.
[p0060-l0008] [14.4pt] This context diagram doesn’t give enough context!
[p0060-l0009] [10.8pt] Figure 3-7. A C4 context diagram with few labels or text, providing little context (antipattern)
[p0060-l0010] [14.4pt] Figure 3-8 adds descriptive text to the diagram’s components and descriptive

=== PDF page 61 / printed label 61 ===
[p0061-l0001] [14.4pt] labels to their relationships. The diagram now successfully conveys the context
[p0061-l0002] [14.4pt] of the illustrated system, without muddling the message with too much
[p0061-l0003] [14.4pt] information or badly placed labels.
[p0061-l0004] [10.8pt] Figure 3-8. A C4 context diagram with an appropriate level of information in labels and descriptions
[p0061-l0005] [14.4pt] With any diagram, it is important to clearly describe what each component
[p0061-l0006] [14.4pt] (system, person, and so forth) is or does, but also the relationships between
[p0061-l0007] [14.4pt] them. How you do this, and to what level of detail, will depend on your audience
[p0061-l0008] [14.4pt] and the overall goal of your diagram.
[p0061-l0009] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0061-l0010] [10.8pt] Take care to make your text legible to the widest audience possible when selecting a font in
[p0061-l0011] [10.8pt] your diagrams. Aim for no smaller than 12 pt. Atkinson Hyperlegible is a free font the Braille
[p0061-l0012] [10.8pt] Institute of America designed to be legible for low vision readers.

=== PDF page 62 / printed label 62 ===
[p0062-l0001] [14.4pt] A diagram with insufficient text, too much text, or misplaced text and labels will
[p0062-l0002] [14.4pt] leave your audience struggling to understand. Do not make your audience work
[p0062-l0003] [14.4pt] to understand your diagram.
[p0062-l0004] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0062-l0005] [14.4pt] I did say it wasn’t all about screen readers! You have a lot to consider when it
[p0062-l0006] [14.4pt] comes to accessibility and won’t always get it right, but every little thing you do
[p0062-l0007] [14.4pt] to make your diagrams more accessible makes a bigger difference than you
[p0062-l0008] [14.4pt] think. Coping with inaccessibility is incredibly draining, especially for those
[p0062-l0009] [14.4pt] who have to do so continually on a daily basis. Reducing this burden and making
[p0062-l0010] [14.4pt] people who have disabilities and other conditions feel seen offers a huge uplift.
[p0062-l0011] [14.4pt] The next chapter takes you on a journey and shows you how narrative can
[p0062-l0012] [14.4pt] improve accessible diagrams even further.
[p0062-l0013] [10.8pt] 1 A diff is a visual of the differences between an original and an updated file or list of files.
[p0062-l0014] [10.8pt] 2 Photophobia is light intolerance. Keratoconus is a disorder of the cornea. Glaucoma is damage
[p0062-l0015] [10.8pt] caused to the optical nerve, usually due to a buildup of fluid.
[p0062-l0016] [10.8pt] 3 And that is not the primary aim of code, although good code does communicate.

=== PDF page 63 / printed label 63 ===
[p0063-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 4. Narrative
[p0063-l0002] [14.4pt] After nourishment, shelter, and companionship, stories are the thing we need
[p0063-l0003] [14.4pt] most in the world.
[p0063-l0004] [14.4pt] —Philip Pullman
[p0063-l0005] [14.4pt] The term narrative may remind you of an English literature class, but stories
[p0063-l0006] [14.4pt] serve as a means for so much more, including social bonding, problem-solving,
[p0063-l0007] [14.4pt] and entertainment among others. As humans, we thrive on stories.
[p0063-l0008] [14.4pt] The other chapters in this part are more about what to show your audience to
[p0063-l0009] [14.4pt] communicate successfully; this one is more about the how. This chapter will
