[p0063-l0010] [14.4pt] help you tell your audience a story to get your message across.
[p0063-l0011] [20.16pt] The Big Picture Comes First
[p0063-l0012] [14.4pt] When you look at the cover of a box of LEGOs you don’t see a picture of each
[p0063-l0013] [14.4pt] individual brick that’s inside. Instead, you see the picture of an exciting, fully
[p0063-l0014] [14.4pt] assembled model…positioned in a life-like pirate’s bay with cliffs and sharks.
[p0063-l0015] [14.4pt] —Gregor Hohpe, The Software Architect Elevator
[p0063-l0016] [14.4pt] Diagrams do not exist in isolation; they are part of a narrative, and the big
[p0063-l0017] [14.4pt] picture comes first pattern helps order that narrative. Most diagrams are not the
[p0063-l0018] [14.4pt] beginning of the story, and many are very much down in the nitty-gritty details
[p0063-l0019] [14.4pt] of design.
[p0063-l0020] [14.4pt] Even if your audience is interested in the fine details, that is not what you should
[p0063-l0021] [14.4pt] show them first. They need the context and to be engaged and hooked into your
[p0063-l0022] [14.4pt] narrative. Fine details are boring and confusing when you don’t know the big
[p0063-l0023] [14.4pt] picture. The levels of abstraction discussed in “Mixing Levels of Abstraction”
[p0063-l0024] [14.4pt] need to be ordered to make sense. When you’ve been delving deep into the
[p0063-l0025] [14.4pt] details of a project, it’s easy to forget that you should first give your audience
[p0063-l0026] [14.4pt] context and explain the big why.
[p0063-l0027] [14.4pt] Imagine creating a presentation or document about a customer viewing media in
[p0063-l0028] [14.4pt] an online system. Would you start with Figure 4-1 or 4-2?

=== PDF page 64 / printed label 64 ===
[p0064-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 4-1. Data flow diagram—level 2
[p0064-l0002] [10.8pt] Figure 4-2. Data flow diagram—level 1
[p0064-l0003] [14.4pt] Figure 4-1 shows the detailed process of viewing the media. That is where you
[p0064-l0004] [14.4pt] want to get to eventually, but it is not where the document or presentation should
[p0064-l0005] [14.4pt] start. Before going to a level 2 data flow diagram, you need to give context and
[p0064-l0006] [14.4pt] show the level 1 data flow diagram, such as Figure 4-2.1
[p0064-l0007] [14.4pt] DATA FLOW DIAGRAMS
[p0064-l0008] [14.4pt] A level 1 data flow diagram is actually the second level of data flow
[p0064-l0009] [14.4pt] diagrams, after level 0, which is a very high level of an entire system,

=== PDF page 65 / printed label 65 ===
[p0065-l0001] [14.4pt] possibly showing flows to and from external systems (a similar view to a C4
[p0065-l0002] [14.4pt] context diagram). Level 1 then shows a more detailed view of the system,
[p0065-l0003] [14.4pt] breaking down significant processes. Level 2 provides an even more detailed
[p0065-l0004] [14.4pt] view, and level 3 is the most detailed level for data flow diagrams.
[p0065-l0005] [14.4pt] At levels 2 and 3, you should have one diagram per process (from the
[p0065-l0006] [14.4pt] preceding level); otherwise, your one diagram will end up too large and
[p0065-l0007] [14.4pt] complex to be useful. Depending on your system’s size and complexity, you
[p0065-l0008] [14.4pt] may not need to use all four levels, but for a large or complex system, you
[p0065-l0009] [14.4pt] should consider creating multiple diagrams per level, even at level 1.
[p0065-l0010] [14.4pt] Starting with level 1 is a move in the right direction but still doesn’t provide
[p0065-l0011] [14.4pt] context. A high-level architecture or context diagram, such as the one in
[p0065-l0012] [14.4pt] Figure 4-3, is where to start. You should also include other supporting materials
[p0065-l0013] [14.4pt] such as business context and benefits (or at least a summary and links to the full
[p0065-l0014] [14.4pt] versions in the document or presentation appendix).
[p0065-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0065-l0016] [10.8pt] You may not need to spend much time on the context and buildup to the main subject or
[p0065-l0017] [10.8pt] diagram you need to discuss. The amount of time and detail will depend on your audience and
[p0065-l0018] [10.8pt] what they already know.
[p0065-l0019] [14.4pt] You can then move on in the narrative to a diagram such as a C4 container
[p0065-l0020] [14.4pt] diagram, if needed, to explain the next conceptual link to the audience, before
[p0065-l0021] [14.4pt] moving on to the data flow diagrams. You are leading them through a story via
[p0065-l0022] [14.4pt] the diagrams, with each level adding more understanding.
[p0065-l0023] [14.4pt] When you don’t give any context, you lose your audience and are unlikely to get
[p0065-l0024] [14.4pt] your desired response. Your diagrams and supporting materials (such as
[p0065-l0025] [14.4pt] requirements, business context, and business benefits) need to be ordered in such
[p0065-l0026] [14.4pt] a way as to create a narrative, starting with the big picture (like Gregor’s LEGO
[p0065-l0027] [14.4pt] pirate bay with sharks).
[p0065-l0028] [14.4pt] TIP

=== PDF page 66 / printed label 66 ===
[p0066-l0001] [10.8pt] Put all your diagrams into a narrative order. Are there any holes in your narrative? Fill in those
[p0066-l0002] [10.8pt] holes before you fall into them in front of others.
[p0066-l0003] [10.8pt] Figure 4-3. C4 context diagram
[p0066-l0004] [20.16pt] Match Diagram Flow to Expectations
[p0066-l0005] [14.4pt] The match diagram flow to expectations pattern enables you to create a diagram
[p0066-l0006] [14.4pt] that is easy for your audience to read from start to finish. Many people create
[p0066-l0007] [14.4pt] diagrams without thinking about how the audience will read them. All diagrams
[p0066-l0008] [14.4pt] have a flow of information, whether they communicate structure or behavior,
[p0066-l0009] [14.4pt] that you want to match as closely as possible to the audience’s expectations.
[p0066-l0010] [14.4pt] While other patterns have covered what to include or exclude from a diagram,
[p0066-l0011] [14.4pt] this pattern is about using those components to order a story in a way that makes

=== PDF page 67 / printed label 67 ===
[p0067-l0001] [14.4pt] sense to your audience.
[p0067-l0002] [14.4pt] When you pick up a book, you expect the text to start in the top left and finish in
[p0067-l0003] [14.4pt] the bottom right (or top right to bottom left if you are reading a right to left
[p0067-l0004] [14.4pt] language). So why would this not be so with a diagram? Successful
[p0067-l0005] [14.4pt] communication breaks down as many barriers as possible between you and your
[p0067-l0006] [14.4pt] audience.
[p0067-l0007] [14.4pt] The flow isn’t quite as simple with a diagram as with a book, but having a focus
[p0067-l0008] [14.4pt] or the start of your diagram in or near the top left, or middle left, makes a lot
[p0067-l0009] [14.4pt] more sense than starting somewhere else on the canvas for those using languages
[p0067-l0010] [14.4pt] written from left to right. Follow this with a general flow top to bottom and left
[p0067-l0011] [14.4pt] to right to aid your audience in consuming the diagram’s message.
[p0067-l0012] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0067-l0013] [10.8pt] To avoid confusion, especially in a diagram that you cannot edit and rearrange, you can add a
[p0067-l0014] [10.8pt] label or symbol to show where the audience should start reading. Start here, or a symbol such
[p0067-l0015] [10.8pt] as an arrow, pointing finger, or Play button are all options. You can also use numbered labels to
[p0067-l0016] [10.8pt] draw the audience through the diagram in the correct order.
[p0067-l0017] [14.4pt] Figure 4-4 illustrates how a data flow diagram could end up if you don’t
[p0067-l0018] [14.4pt] consider how the audience will read it. Where should they start? The top left is
[p0067-l0019] [14.4pt] the natural place, but the start is actually closer to the bottom right at the
[p0067-l0020] [14.4pt] Customer box. The flow is then right to left and bottom to top, going against the
[p0067-l0021] [14.4pt] audience’s expectation.
[p0067-l0022] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0067-l0023] [10.8pt] Like a story, your diagram should have a beginning (where the audience should start to read), a
[p0067-l0024] [10.8pt] middle (the rest of the content in an appropriate order), and an end (the conclusions you want
[p0067-l0025] [10.8pt] the audience to draw).

=== PDF page 68 / printed label 68 ===
[p0068-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 4-4. Data flow diagram with no consideration of how the audience will read it (antipattern)
[p0068-l0002] [14.4pt] Figure 4-5 rearranges Figure 4-4 to better indicate the direction of flow. Read
[p0068-l0003] [14.4pt] through to see how much easier this is to follow. The start of the diagram is on

=== PDF page 69 / printed label 69 ===
[p0069-l0001] [14.4pt] the left (Customer) with requests flowing left to right and responses flowing
[p0069-l0002] [14.4pt] right to left. The numbered events acting on the data flow top to bottom and left
[p0069-l0003] [14.4pt] to right, along with the letter identifiers (A–C) of the databases.
[p0069-l0004] [10.8pt] Figure 4-5. Data flow diagram matching the left-to-right, top-to-bottom flow of English text
[p0069-l0005] [14.4pt] Another aspect of a visual’s flow to consider is the interactions it depicts (for
[p0069-l0006] [14.4pt] example, labeled relationship arrows). A request should follow the same
[p0069-l0007] [14.4pt] direction as text (left to right in English), and a response should flow in the
[p0069-l0008] [14.4pt] opposite direction.
[p0069-l0009] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0069-l0010] [10.8pt] Ensure that a response from a component always flows in the opposite direction of any
[p0069-l0011] [10.8pt] requests. Visually differentiating types of interactions helps your audience understand your
[p0069-l0012] [10.8pt] diagram more easily.
[p0069-l0013] [14.4pt] Getting the flow correct is easy in sequence diagrams. The flow starts in the top
[p0069-l0014] [14.4pt] left and requests flow left to right and responses right to left. Figure 4-6
[p0069-l0015] [14.4pt] illustrates a sequence diagram with a flow that meets audience expectations. The
[p0069-l0016] [14.4pt] order of the components along the top is critical to allowing the flow from left to
[p0069-l0017] [14.4pt] right and then right to left.

=== PDF page 70 / printed label 70 ===
[p0070-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 4-6. Sequence diagram showing requests flowing left-to-right and responses flowing right-to-left
[p0070-l0002] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0070-l0003] [10.8pt] Explain your diagram to someone (or even to a rubber duck) to check whether your
[p0070-l0004] [10.8pt] explanation flows across the diagram or jumps about.
[p0070-l0005] [14.4pt] Structural diagrams should have a similar flow of information, but require
[p0070-l0006] [14.4pt] additional considerations. When creating a diagram that includes infrastructure,
[p0070-l0007] [14.4pt] such as databases, the general expectation is that the infrastructure elements will
[p0070-l0008] [14.4pt] be placed at the bottom of the diagram, with elements such as systems and
[p0070-l0009] [14.4pt] containers above, and then elements such as actors or users at the top. This is not
[p0070-l0010] [14.4pt] a hard-and-fast rule, but it should be followed unless doing so means you fall
[p0070-l0011] [14.4pt] foul of antipatterns.
[p0070-l0012] [14.4pt] Logical diagrams showing, for example, a layered architecture should follow the
[p0070-l0013] [14.4pt] left-to-right and top-to-bottom flow but also take into account the layers. Similar
[p0070-l0014] [14.4pt] to other structural diagrams, layers should be arranged logically from top to
[p0070-l0015] [14.4pt] bottom, with the user-facing layer at the top (such as the user interface or API
[p0070-l0016] [14.4pt] layer). Within the layers, consider the most logical way to lay out elements from

=== PDF page 71 / printed label 71 ===
[p0071-l0001] [14.4pt] left to right.
[p0071-l0002] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0071-l0003] [10.8pt] Layout expectations differ a little for a hexagonal architecture diagrams. Think of the diagram
[p0071-l0004] [10.8pt] more like the face of a clock. The top left is still a good place to start because this is where
[p0071-l0005] [10.8pt] people’s eyes may naturally fall, but you could also draw their attention to the center. Use
[p0071-l0006] [10.8pt] color, bold/larger text, or thicker lines to draw their eyes to where you want them to start and
[p0071-l0007] [10.8pt] lay diagram elements out logically in a clockwise direction.
[p0071-l0008] [20.16pt] Clear Relationships
[p0071-l0009] [14.4pt] A diagram has two main elements: components (containers, processes, and so
[p0071-l0010] [14.4pt] on) and the relationships between them (arrows, groupings, and so on). Both are
[p0071-l0011] [14.4pt] critical to the message in a diagram, but relationships lead the audience through
[p0071-l0012] [14.4pt] a story, and that is why the clear relationships pattern is important.
[p0071-l0013] [14.4pt] Without clear relationships, your message gets lost or confused, which could
[p0071-l0014] [14.4pt] lead to consequences such as:
[p0071-l0015] [14.4pt] The design you give developers is not implemented the way you wanted it
[p0071-l0016] [14.4pt] to be.
[p0071-l0017] [14.4pt] You don’t get budget approval for the changes you want to make to the
[p0071-l0018] [14.4pt] system because key stakeholders did not understand how those changes
[p0071-l0019] [14.4pt] would add value.
[p0071-l0020] [14.4pt] It is best to have unidirectional (one-direction) relationships, and the label on
[p0071-l0021] [14.4pt] any arrow between two components should describe the relationship in the
[p0071-l0022] [14.4pt] direction shown. A line with arrowheads at both ends should be used only when
[p0071-l0023] [14.4pt] the same process truly does happen in both directions. This is fairly rare.
[p0071-l0024] [14.4pt] Relationships can also be made clearer using pattern and color—for example, a
[p0071-l0025] [14.4pt] dotted or dashed line for an arrow or a box.2 You’ll then need to include a
[p0071-l0026] [14.4pt] legend.3
[p0071-l0027] [14.4pt] Sequence diagrams, such as Figure 4-7, are a good example of unidirectional
[p0071-l0028] [14.4pt] relationships and flows. Don’t create a sequence diagram like Figure 4-8, which
[p0071-l0029] [14.4pt] combines each pair of unidirectional relationships into one bidirectional

=== PDF page 72 / printed label 72 ===
[p0072-l0001] [14.4pt] relationship. Compare the labels in Figures 4-7 and 4-8 to see how much
[p0072-l0002] [14.4pt] information is lost. The message of the diagram is not clear when bidirectional
[p0072-l0003] [14.4pt] relationships are used, and adding more information to the labels would reduce
[p0072-l0004] [14.4pt] whitespace and clarity.
[p0072-l0005] [10.8pt] Figure 4-7. Sequence diagram showing unidirectional relationships
[p0072-l0006] [10.8pt] Figure 4-8. Sequence diagram showing ambiguous bidirectional relationships (antipattern)
[p0072-l0007] [14.4pt] ArchiMate, an open modeling language for enterprise architecture, defines many
[p0072-l0008] [14.4pt] types of relationship via various combinations of patterns and shapes. This
[p0072-l0009] [14.4pt] notation is good for communicating a large amount of information in a small

=== PDF page 73 / printed label 73 ===
[p0073-l0001] [14.4pt] space, but it requires a legend because the audience might not be familiar with
[p0073-l0002] [14.4pt] the notation or remember every detail.
[p0073-l0003] [14.4pt] Figure 4-9 shows a key to the types of ArchiMate relationships. Including a key
[p0073-l0004] [14.4pt] (showing at least the types used in the displayed diagram) is needed whenever
[p0073-l0005] [14.4pt] you create an ArchiMate diagram, to ensure that your audience understands the
[p0073-l0006] [14.4pt] many types.
[p0073-l0007] [10.8pt] Figure 4-9. ArchiMate relationship key
[p0073-l0008] [14.4pt] ArchiMate is a perfectly valid notation to use, but consider your audience’s
[p0073-l0009] [14.4pt] needs. Another notation, such as C4, could communicate your message in a
[p0073-l0010] [14.4pt] much simpler way without a key (although including a key is nearly always a
[p0073-l0011] [14.4pt] good idea). I’ll discuss trade-offs with regard to notation further in Chapter 5.
[p0073-l0012] [14.4pt] Make the story that your relationships tell clear and easy for your audience to
[p0073-l0013] [14.4pt] understand.
[p0073-l0014] [14.4pt] TYPES OF RELATIONSHIPS
[p0073-l0015] [14.4pt] You can convey many types of relationships via visuals. Here are five
[p0073-l0016] [14.4pt] relationship types to consider:
[p0073-l0017] [14.4pt] Hierarchical
[p0073-l0018] [14.4pt] Illustrates the parent-child connection between elements. It often
[p0073-l0019] [14.4pt] represents organizational structures or classification systems. Examples
[p0073-l0020] [14.4pt] include organizational charts, family trees, and taxonomies.
[p0073-l0021] [14.4pt] Sequential

=== PDF page 74 / printed label 74 ===
[p0074-l0001] [14.4pt] Shows a linear progression or a series of steps. It indicates an order or a
[p0074-l0002] [14.4pt] process that must be followed. Examples include flowcharts, timelines,
[p0074-l0003] [14.4pt] and step-by-step guides.
[p0074-l0004] [14.4pt] Causal
[p0074-l0005] [14.4pt] Depicts cause and effect, as one element leads to or influences another.
[p0074-l0006] [14.4pt] This type of relationship is often seen in flowcharts, system diagrams,
[p0074-l0007] [14.4pt] and decision trees.
[p0074-l0008] [14.4pt] Proportional
[p0074-l0009] [14.4pt] Illustrates the relative size, quantity, or scale of elements in comparison
[p0074-l0010] [14.4pt] to one another. Examples include bar graphs, pie charts, and treemaps.
[p0074-l0011] [14.4pt] Spatial
[p0074-l0012] [14.4pt] Shows the physical arrangement or relative position of elements. This
[p0074-l0013] [14.4pt] type of relationship can be seen in maps, floor plans, and network
[p0074-l0014] [14.4pt] diagrams.
[p0074-l0015] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0074-l0016] [14.4pt] Throughout this chapter, you have learned techniques for creating flow and
[p0074-l0017] [14.4pt] narrative in your diagrams to improve audience understanding and keep their
[p0074-l0018] [14.4pt] attention. Along with the patterns and antipatterns from previous chapters, you
[p0074-l0019] [14.4pt] now have a large toolbox to draw from when creating diagrams, but there is still
[p0074-l0020] [14.4pt] more to add.
[p0074-l0021] [14.4pt] In this chapter, I introduced notation, which is the system of symbols used to
[p0074-l0022] [14.4pt] create your diagram and convey your message. The next chapter dives into some
[p0074-l0023] [14.4pt] antipatterns to help you spot when, and when not, to apply some of the common
[p0074-l0024] [14.4pt] notations in use today.
[p0074-l0025] [10.8pt] 1 See Figures 1-10, 1-11, and 1-12 in “Mixing Levels of Abstraction” for more on levels of abstraction
[p0074-l0026] [10.8pt] such as these.

=== PDF page 75 / printed label 75 ===
[p0075-l0001] [10.8pt] 2 Ensure you avoid the relying on color to communicate antipattern (see “Relying on Color to
[p0075-l0002] [10.8pt] Communicate”).
[p0075-l0003] [10.8pt] 3 See “Include a Legend” for more on this.

=== PDF page 76 / printed label 76 ===
[p0076-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 5. Notation
[p0076-l0002] [14.4pt] When you create diagrams, you might use a standard notation, such as Unified
[p0076-l0003] [14.4pt] Modeling Language (UML) or Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN),
[p0076-l0004] [14.4pt] or a nonstandard notation (for example, your own, or a corporate standard of
[p0076-l0005] [14.4pt] boxes and lines).1
[p0076-l0006] [14.4pt] You may not think much about the notation you use or, conversely, spend far too
[p0076-l0007] [14.4pt] long deciding on one. This chapter covers several antipatterns that will guide
[p0076-l0008] [14.4pt] you to see where a standard or nonstandard notation could reduce the likelihood
[p0076-l0009] [14.4pt] of successful communication with your audience.
[p0076-l0010] [20.16pt] Using Icons to Convey Meaning
[p0076-l0011] [14.4pt] Using icons to convey meaning is an antipattern that has arisen from cloud
[p0076-l0012] [14.4pt] provider icons becoming an almost formal notation. Before the cloud, people
[p0076-l0013] [14.4pt] rarely created diagrams using technology icons such as SQL Server, Java, or
[p0076-l0014] [14.4pt] Python and definitely did not do so without using labels. But now cloud provider
[p0076-l0015] [14.4pt] documentation is full of diagrams showing icons representing their versions of
[p0076-l0016] [14.4pt] data stores, serverless functions, and platform-as-a-service (PaaS) offerings,
[p0076-l0017] [14.4pt] among other things.
[p0076-l0018] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0076-l0019] [10.8pt] Cloud provider icon sets are frequently updated for various reasons, including for new
[p0076-l0020] [10.8pt] services. Different versions are available to use directly in diagramming applications, such as
[p0076-l0021] [10.8pt] draw.io. Use clear labels and be explicit about the version or type of the service to avoid
[p0076-l0022] [10.8pt] confusion if the icon changes in the future.
[p0076-l0023] [14.4pt] Using technology icons in a diagram isn’t bad in itself, but using them as the
[p0076-l0024] [14.4pt] only form of communication (or alongside very little textual information) can
[p0076-l0025] [14.4pt] confuse your audience. Diagrams following this antipattern could be seen as a
[p0076-l0026] [14.4pt] test of the audience’s knowledge of cloud provider icons. If they don’t know

=== PDF page 77 / printed label 77 ===
[p0077-l0001] [14.4pt] what the icon represents, they can’t understand the message the diagram is trying
[p0077-l0002] [14.4pt] to convey.
[p0077-l0003] [14.4pt] When considering icons for a diagram, ask yourself why you’d include them.
[p0077-l0004] [14.4pt] The answer should not be to convey information, because you cannot trust that
[p0077-l0005] [14.4pt] everyone in your audience has a perfect understanding of the icons you use. It is
[p0077-l0006] [14.4pt] like adding labels in another language and expecting everyone in your audience
[p0077-l0007] [14.4pt] to know this additional language, or using acronyms without defining them.2
[p0077-l0008] [14.4pt] Use icons only in addition to the information you want to convey. Use labels,
[p0077-l0009] [14.4pt] and make sure the text you use is clear too. You should be able to remove the
[p0077-l0010] [14.4pt] icons and have the message remain understandable. If you have a diagram
[p0077-l0011] [14.4pt] containing icons without labels and you cannot (or don’t have time to) edit it,
[p0077-l0012] [14.4pt] then add a legend with definitions of each icon. Consider whether your icons are
[p0077-l0013] [14.4pt] cluttering your diagram and making it harder to understand, rather than adding to
[p0077-l0014] [14.4pt] the message.
[p0077-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0077-l0016] [10.8pt] To test whether your icons or logos are being used to communicate information, try removing
[p0077-l0017] [10.8pt] them completely from the diagram and check that the message is still communicated
[p0077-l0018] [10.8pt] effectively by labels and other methods.
[p0077-l0019] [14.4pt] If you are using icons to symbolize information, including descriptive text as
[p0077-l0020] [14.4pt] well is good practice. For example, to show a score or rating, you might include
[p0077-l0021] [14.4pt] 3.5/5 next to three and a half stars, to make the information explicit and
[p0077-l0022] [14.4pt] accessible.3
[p0077-l0023] [14.4pt] Figure 5-1 shows the difference between adding labels to both icons and
[p0077-l0024] [14.4pt] relationships (at the top of the diagram) and not adding them (at the bottom of
[p0077-l0025] [14.4pt] the diagram). You can see why internet traffic would be routed to the Azure
[p0077-l0026] [14.4pt] CDN, but why would it be routed to the component below that, and what is that
[p0077-l0027] [14.4pt] component? It is Azure Front Door, but you cannot assume your audience knows
[p0077-l0028] [14.4pt] this or that they would know that all the requests for nonstatic content would be
[p0077-l0029] [14.4pt] routed this way.

=== PDF page 78 / printed label 78 ===
[p0078-l0001] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0078-l0002] [10.8pt] Cloud provider documentation is usually somewhere between the examples shown in Figure 5-
[p0078-l0003] [10.8pt] 1 (top and bottom): better than no labels at all, but still assuming the audience knows what the
[p0078-l0004] [10.8pt] icons mean. Avoid using diagrams lifted straight from cloud documentation for this reason.
[p0078-l0005] [10.8pt] Figure 5-1. Cloud provider diagram with labels (top) and without (bottom)
[p0078-l0006] [20.16pt] Using UML for UML’s Sake
[p0078-l0007] [14.4pt] UML is a useful notation when used appropriately, but many get into the mindset
[p0078-l0008] [14.4pt] that it should always be used, whatever the audience or reason for the diagram.
[p0078-l0009] [14.4pt] The key to deciding whether to use UML (or another standard) is to define the
[p0078-l0010] [14.4pt] goal or objective of the diagram and the audience. What are you trying to
[p0078-l0011] [14.4pt] communicate? Who are you communicating with? What knowledge do they
[p0078-l0012] [14.4pt] have?
[p0078-l0013] [14.4pt] UML has 14 diagram types, which are split evenly between structural and
[p0078-l0014] [14.4pt] behavioral categories. Only a few of these diagrams are used regularly, and
[p0078-l0015] [14.4pt] many professionals don’t use them at all, so many people have little to no

=== PDF page 79 / printed label 79 ===
[p0079-l0001] [14.4pt] knowledge of the UML notation and would not understand a UML diagram
[p0079-l0002] [14.4pt] without help. Even a very technical audience will not reliably have the
[p0079-l0003] [14.4pt] knowledge needed to understand a UML diagram.
[p0079-l0004] [14.4pt] Another downside to UML is that creating and updating UML diagrams can be a
[p0079-l0005] [14.4pt] lengthy process and the content of a diagram can go out-of-date very quickly
[p0079-l0006] [14.4pt] (especially in an Agile environment). Although UML has gone through many
[p0079-l0007] [14.4pt] formal releases since the 1990s, its roots are in the time of waterfall development
[p0079-l0008] [14.4pt] —a time when releases, and therefore documentation updates, happened in terms
[p0079-l0009] [14.4pt] of months or years, not hours or days (or even minutes), as they can now.
[p0079-l0010] [14.4pt] One big consideration for any formal notation is comprehension not only of the
[p0079-l0011] [14.4pt] audience but also of the author(s). The person who creates or updates the
[p0079-l0012] [14.4pt] diagram has to understand the notation very well. This limits who can create or
[p0079-l0013] [14.4pt] update a diagram if a formal notation is used or if a person cannot get up to
[p0079-l0014] [14.4pt] speed quickly, creating a bottleneck and increasing the likelihood of out-of-date
[p0079-l0015] [14.4pt] documentation.
[p0079-l0016] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0079-l0017] [10.8pt] Be consistent with symbols, colors, and fonts in your diagrams. This is important within a
[p0079-l0018] [10.8pt] diagram, but consistency between diagrams also reduces your audience’s cognitive load; they
[p0079-l0019] [10.8pt] won’t have to relearn how to read each new diagram.
[p0079-l0020] [14.4pt] Upon deciding that UML, or another notation such as BPMN, is not appropriate
[p0079-l0021] [14.4pt] for your audience, you need to decide what to use instead or how to adapt to the
[p0079-l0022] [14.4pt] audience. One alternative is to provide a legend, as I have mentioned before.
[p0079-l0023] [14.4pt] This is an especially useful technique if you cannot edit the diagram (maybe you
[p0079-l0024] [14.4pt] have only an exported image), but many people may need more than this to fully
[p0079-l0025] [14.4pt] understand what you are trying to communicate. You could also use another
[p0079-l0026] [14.4pt] notation, either a simple notation such as C4 or simply lines and boxes (a legend
[p0079-l0027] [14.4pt] is still a good idea with either of these options).
[p0079-l0028] [14.4pt] Figure 5-2 is an example of a UML component diagram.

=== PDF page 80 / printed label 80 ===
[p0080-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 5-2. UML component diagram
[p0080-l0002] [14.4pt] A UML component diagram is not an easy diagram to create. Most people would
[p0080-l0003] [14.4pt] require a legend to understand all the symbols. Who would need this type of
[p0080-l0004] [14.4pt] technical interface detail in a diagram? Even developers, who would be
[p0080-l0005] [14.4pt] implementing this design, would not find this more useful than the C4 diagram
[p0080-l0006] [14.4pt] in Figure 5-3.

=== PDF page 81 / printed label 81 ===
[p0081-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 5-3. C4 container diagram
[p0081-l0002] [14.4pt] The C4 container diagram in Figure 5-3 shows most of the same information as
[p0081-l0003] [14.4pt] Figure 5-2 but would be useful to both technical and business audiences, and
[p0081-l0004] [14.4pt] likely effective even without a legend. Throw in a legend and you have given
[p0081-l0005] [14.4pt] yourself a very high chance of successful communication. The information
[p0081-l0006] [14.4pt] conveyed by the symbols in Figure 5-2 is replaced by labels on the relationships
[p0081-l0007] [14.4pt] in Figure 5-3. The diagram is easier to understand and needs less technical

=== PDF page 82 / printed label 82 ===
[p0082-l0001] [14.4pt] knowledge and time to create and update.
[p0082-l0002] [14.4pt] Yet another option, which is often forgotten, is to simplify the UML notation.
[p0082-l0003] [14.4pt] You do not need to use UML exactly as specified if it does not meet your needs.
[p0082-l0004] [14.4pt] Adapt it to the audience, making sure they will understand what you create.
[p0082-l0005] [14.4pt] The UML sequence diagram in Figure 5-4 contains a lot of information and even
[p0082-l0006] [14.4pt] includes method names in the calls (relationships) between components. This
[p0082-l0007] [14.4pt] kind of detail goes out-of-date very quickly, and an audience is better served by a
[p0082-l0008] [14.4pt] simplified version such as Figure 5-5.
[p0082-l0009] [10.8pt] Figure 5-4. UML sequence diagram
[p0082-l0010] [14.4pt] In this simplified version (Figure 5-5), details such as method names have been
[p0082-l0011] [14.4pt] replaced by much more descriptive labels, meaning the information will remain
[p0082-l0012] [14.4pt] current for longer but also be easier to understand by those who are not familiar
[p0082-l0013] [14.4pt] with the codebase itself.

=== PDF page 83 / printed label 83 ===
[p0083-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 5-5. Simplified sequence diagram
[p0083-l0002] [14.4pt] Carefully consider your choice of formal or informal notation and adapt to your
[p0083-l0003] [14.4pt] audience and the goals of your diagram.
[p0083-l0004] [20.16pt] Mixing Behavior and Structure
[p0083-l0005] [14.4pt] UML diagrams fall into two categories, structural and behavioral, and there is
[p0083-l0006] [14.4pt] logic to this separation. Including behavior and structure in the same diagram
[p0083-l0007] [14.4pt] can be confusing, but many diagrams end up trying to communicate more than
[p0083-l0008] [14.4pt] they should.
[p0083-l0009] [14.4pt] THE SINGLE RESPONSIBILITY PRINCIPLE
[p0083-l0010] [14.4pt] The single responsibility principle is the first of the SOLID principles and
[p0083-l0011] [14.4pt] common practice in object-oriented coding.4 It states that a piece of code
[p0083-l0012] [14.4pt] (such as a method or module) should have only one reason to change. In
[p0083-l0013] [14.4pt] other words, do one thing and only one thing.
[p0083-l0014] [14.4pt] Some of the benefits of abiding by this principle are easier-to-understand and
[p0083-l0015] [14.4pt] more maintainable code. These benefits also apply to diagrams that follow

=== PDF page 84 / printed label 84 ===
[p0084-l0001] [14.4pt] the single-responsibility principle.
[p0084-l0002] [14.4pt] This rule of separating behavior and structure applies not just to UML, but to all
[p0084-l0003] [14.4pt] diagrams, whatever their notation. Apply the single responsibility principle to a
[p0084-l0004] [14.4pt] diagram to push the single message you aim to convey to the forefront to
[p0084-l0005] [14.4pt] increase the likelihood of successful communication with your audience.
[p0084-l0006] [14.4pt] Structural diagrams communicate the what and the where, such as showing
[p0084-l0007] [14.4pt] systems and their relationships, or the physical location of hardware or running
[p0084-l0008] [14.4pt] software. Behavioral diagrams communicate the how and to whom, such as the
[p0084-l0009] [14.4pt] flow of data or state change within a system. If a diagram tries to communicate a
[p0084-l0010] [14.4pt] mix of these, the message will most likely be lost.
[p0084-l0011] [14.4pt] Figure 5-6 shows the mixing structure and behavior antipattern. It is cluttered
[p0084-l0012] [14.4pt] and conveys no clear message.
[p0084-l0013] [10.8pt] Figure 5-6. Don’t mix structure and behavior, as in this diagram (antipattern)
[p0084-l0014] [14.4pt] The information in Figure 5-6 can be split into a structural diagram (Figure 5-7)
[p0084-l0015] [14.4pt] and a behavioral diagram (Figure 5-8) so that each has one clear message.

=== PDF page 85 / printed label 85 ===
[p0085-l0001] [14.4pt] Figure 5-7 communicates the conceptual structure of the system, and Figure 5-8
[p0085-l0002] [14.4pt] communicates the data flow (the behavior of the data) in the system.
[p0085-l0003] [10.8pt] Figure 5-7. Structural diagram at the conceptual level of abstraction
[p0085-l0004] [10.8pt] Figure 5-8. Behavioral data flow diagram

=== PDF page 86 / printed label 86 ===
[p0086-l0001] [14.4pt] I have mentioned before that it is usually better to have more than just one
[p0086-l0002] [14.4pt] diagram. Splitting structure and behavior is another example of how to best split
[p0086-l0003] [14.4pt] up your diagrams.
[p0086-l0004] [20.16pt] Going Against Expectations
[p0086-l0005] [14.4pt] When communicating about software architecture, going against expectations is
[p0086-l0006] [14.4pt] best avoided. It can be effective in other situations, but this is an antipattern for
[p0086-l0007] [14.4pt] our purposes. We’re not trying to be Steve Jobs (well, not every day).5 The
[p0086-l0008] [14.4pt] unexpected can be a useful tool to get people’s attention, but it detracts from
[p0086-l0009] [14.4pt] your message when used without thought.
[p0086-l0010] [14.4pt] Throughout their lives, your audience has developed mental models: internal
[p0086-l0011] [14.4pt] representations of the way something works in the real world. These mental
[p0086-l0012] [14.4pt] models affect their interactions with the physical world (for instance,
[p0086-l0013] [14.4pt] understanding the meaning of traffic lights) but also with the digital world (like
[p0086-l0014] [14.4pt] knowing how website menus work). For example, the burger menus used in
[p0086-l0015] [14.4pt] interfaces on smaller screens are a newer mental model. You must take care to
[p0086-l0016] [14.4pt] not break any mental models in your diagrams, presentations, and so on, or risk
[p0086-l0017] [14.4pt] causing misunderstanding.
[p0086-l0018] [14.4pt] Color is used in many diagrams but often without thought as to how the audience
[p0086-l0019] [14.4pt] will perceive it, as I’ve mentioned. Colors can have different meanings across
[p0086-l0020] [14.4pt] cultures, such as red being a sign of danger or meaning stop, but also a sign of
[p0086-l0021] [14.4pt] luck (such as in Asia). Avoid the going against expectations antipattern when it
[p0086-l0022] [14.4pt] comes to color. It will confuse your audience. Instead, use their expectations to
[p0086-l0023] [14.4pt] your advantage, using the colors in their mental model to communicate meaning.
[p0086-l0024] [14.4pt] But be wary of using color on its own when communicating (see “Relying on
[p0086-l0025] [14.4pt] Color to Communicate”).
[p0086-l0026] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0086-l0027] [10.8pt] Some colors that you know may not even be considered separate colors in some cultures. For
[p0086-l0028] [10.8pt] example, in Japan, the color green (midori) is often considered a shade of blue (ao), and the
[p0086-l0029] [10.8pt] name for green in Japanese has not existed very long compared to other names for colors.
[p0086-l0030] [10.8pt] Consider also that English words for shades of colors, such as lilac, may not have equivalents
[p0086-l0031] [10.8pt] in other languages or may refer to a different shade.

=== PDF page 87 / printed label 87 ===
[p0087-l0001] [14.4pt] People have expectations about the meaning of shapes and symbols too, which
[p0087-l0002] [14.4pt] can vary from culture to culture. Be especially aware of those that have a
[p0087-l0003] [14.4pt] religious significance (such as some star shapes); you do not want to offend or
[p0087-l0004] [14.4pt] suggest any link with the significance of a religious symbol. But even geometric
[p0087-l0005] [14.4pt] shapes can have meaning; for example, a triangle can represent action or
[p0087-l0006] [14.4pt] dynamic tension, and a square or rectangle can represent trust, order, or
[p0087-l0007] [14.4pt] formality. You may recognize the symbols in Figure 5-9 from remote controls or
[p0087-l0008] [14.4pt] music and video streaming websites, among others.
[p0087-l0009] [10.8pt] Figure 5-9. An example of conventional symbols
[p0087-l0010] [14.4pt] Triangles are seen as dynamic and represent action. They are the symbol used at
[p0087-l0011] [14.4pt] the end of a line to create an arrow. The triangles in Figure 5-9 point to the right,
[p0087-l0012] [14.4pt] symbolizing play and fast-forward. The square and rectangles in Figure 5-9
[p0087-l0013] [14.4pt] represent stop and pause. Although these symbols have not been around for long
[p0087-l0014] [14.4pt] historically, they have become ubiquitous.
[p0087-l0015] [14.4pt] Consider technology when avoiding this antipattern. There are conventions in
[p0087-l0016] [14.4pt] technology selection and how you implement them. Breaking these conventions
[p0087-l0017] [14.4pt] can be good and innovative, but you will need to have your justifications ready if
[p0087-l0018] [14.4pt] you are breaking convention for a reason. If you are breaking technological
[p0087-l0019] [14.4pt] conventions, make sure you have fully considered all the implications. Why does
[p0087-l0020] [14.4pt] everyone else follow these conventions?
[p0087-l0021] [14.4pt] Then there is conventional notation. You do not always have to use a formal
[p0087-l0022] [14.4pt] notation. As long as your audience understands it, you can create your own
[p0087-l0023] [14.4pt] notation, but that notation should meet the audience’s expectations rather than
[p0087-l0024] [14.4pt] clash with them.
[p0087-l0025] [14.4pt] Your audience may have expectations about the notation you will use in your
[p0087-l0026] [14.4pt] diagram(s). Maybe they expect UML, C4, or a nonstandard notation that is used
[p0087-l0027] [14.4pt] within a company. You may find that by applying the patterns and avoiding the
[p0087-l0028] [14.4pt] antipatterns in this book, you need to change or adapt the notation that your

=== PDF page 88 / printed label 88 ===
[p0088-l0001] [14.4pt] audience expects to see. Changing your notation is not a bad thing, but you may
[p0088-l0002] [14.4pt] need to do so carefully. Introduce your audience to the adapted or new notation
[p0088-l0003] [14.4pt] you are using, rather than slipping it in and hoping they don’t notice. Show them
[p0088-l0004] [14.4pt] the benefits. Point out problems that the notation you were using previously was
[p0088-l0005] [14.4pt] creating.
[p0088-l0006] [14.4pt] When using a standard notation, make sure to stick to the expectations about that
[p0088-l0007] [14.4pt] notation, and clearly communicate any variations from those expectations. Your
[p0088-l0008] [14.4pt] audience needs to know if any assumptions they make, because of the notation,
[p0088-l0009] [14.4pt] are incorrect. It may be better to use another formal notation or a custom one
[p0088-l0010] [14.4pt] instead to avoid confusion.
[p0088-l0011] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0088-l0012] [10.8pt] Your audience has expectations of direction at more than one level. Meet those expectations by
[p0088-l0013] [10.8pt] following “Match Diagram Flow to Expectations” within your diagrams and “The Big Picture
[p0088-l0014] [10.8pt] Comes First” for multiple diagrams, documentation, and presentations.
[p0088-l0015] [14.4pt] Don’t be afraid to be unconventional when you have a good reason, but make
[p0088-l0016] [14.4pt] sure you are consciously choosing to deviate from expectations. There is always
[p0088-l0017] [14.4pt] an exception to the rule.
[p0088-l0018] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0088-l0019] [14.4pt] You now have some understanding of what you need to consider before selecting
[p0088-l0020] [14.4pt] a notation for your diagrams. Applying what you have learned so far, you can
[p0088-l0021] [14.4pt] use these notations to their full effect.
[p0088-l0022] [14.4pt] The next and final chapter on visual communication will show you how to
[p0088-l0023] [14.4pt] arrange your carefully and thoughtfully constructed diagrams to take them to the
[p0088-l0024] [14.4pt] next level for your audience.
[p0088-l0025] [10.8pt] 1 UML is a general-purpose modeling language originally developed in 1994 in an attempt to
[p0088-l0026] [10.8pt] standardize the diverse set of notational systems and approaches to software design of the time.
[p0088-l0027] [10.8pt] BPMN is a graphical notation for specifying business processes.

=== PDF page 89 / printed label 89 ===
[p0089-l0001] [10.8pt] 2 See “Acronym Hell” for more on acronyms.
[p0089-l0002] [10.8pt] 3 See “Abstractions over Text” for more on using stars and other abstractions.
[p0089-l0003] [10.8pt] 4 SOLID stands for the single-responsibility principle, open-closed principle, Liskov substitution
[p0089-l0004] [10.8pt] principle, interface segregation principle, and dependency inversion principle.
[p0089-l0005] [10.8pt] 5 Steve Jobs was known for wowing audiences with the unexpected in his public presentations for
[p0089-l0006] [10.8pt] Apple.

=== PDF page 90 / printed label 90 ===
[p0090-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 6. Composition
[p0090-l0002] [14.4pt] Visual composition is an essential aspect of software architecture diagrams; it
[p0090-l0003] [14.4pt] helps you convey the structure, relationships, and dependencies of the system
[p0090-l0004] [14.4pt] being documented. Effective visual composition ensures that diagrams are
[p0090-l0005] [14.4pt] legible and improves the ease with which the audience can understand them.
[p0090-l0006] [14.4pt] In this chapter, you will explore how the composition of your diagrams can
[p0090-l0007] [14.4pt] increase audience comprehension, how to avoid misleading your audience, and
[p0090-l0008] [14.4pt] how to guide them through the narrative of your diagrams.
[p0090-l0009] [20.16pt] Illegible Diagrams
[p0090-l0010] [14.4pt] When you create a diagram, you need to consider how it will be consumed and
[p0090-l0011] [14.4pt] who will consume it. This section covers how to avoid the illegible diagrams
[p0090-l0012] [14.4pt] antipattern.
[p0090-l0013] [14.4pt] When creating a diagram in a tool such as draw.io or Visio, most people accept
[p0090-l0014] [14.4pt] the default canvas to work on. That default is typically something like A4 or
[p0090-l0015] [14.4pt] Letter paper size in portrait orientation. Is your audience going to view your
[p0090-l0016] [14.4pt] diagram on a printed piece of paper in portrait? These days, that is unlikely.
[p0090-l0017] [14.4pt] Maybe your audience will consume the diagram within a document in Microsoft
[p0090-l0018] [14.4pt] Word or Apache OpenOffice Writer (where the defaults, again, are A4 or Letter
[p0090-l0019] [14.4pt] in portrait), but if your diagram is created on a portrait canvas, the audience is
[p0090-l0020] [14.4pt] unlikely to be able to view it in its entirety on a computer screen (which is
[p0090-l0021] [14.4pt] landscape) without having to zoom out and risk not being able to read the text or
[p0090-l0022] [14.4pt] other details.
[p0090-l0023] [14.4pt] The majority of diagramming tools, including draw.io and Visio, allow you to
[p0090-l0024] [14.4pt] select the canvas size, and draw.io specifically has options for a 16:9 or 16:10
[p0090-l0025] [14.4pt] ratio (along with many others, which may vary according to the platform—see
[p0090-l0026] [14.4pt] Figure 6-1).1 Selecting the appropriate canvas/page size and ratio is the first
[p0090-l0027] [14.4pt] thing you should do when creating a diagram from scratch. Some tools, such as
[p0090-l0028] [14.4pt] draw.io, will then remember your preferences.

=== PDF page 91 / printed label 91 ===
[p0091-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-1. Example canvas/page options in draw.io
[p0091-l0002] [14.4pt] TIP

=== PDF page 92 / printed label 92 ===
[p0092-l0001] [10.8pt] Most diagrams are consumed on a computer or presentation screen, so unless you need to
[p0092-l0002] [10.8pt] adhere to a specific format (such as a printed book, poster, or document), you should create
[p0092-l0003] [10.8pt] your diagram for a viewing ratio of 16:9 or 16:10 (the typical ratio of most screens).2
[p0092-l0004] [14.4pt] Designing your diagram to fit the audience’s viewing format, as well as their
[p0092-l0005] [14.4pt] needs, will mean your audience can read and understand your diagram without
[p0092-l0006] [14.4pt] having to zoom, move around, or squint. The data flow diagram in Figure 6-2 is
[p0092-l0007] [14.4pt] in portrait orientation and therefore will not display optimally on monitors or
[p0092-l0008] [14.4pt] projectors because roughly two-thirds of the screen will be whitespace. This
[p0092-l0009] [14.4pt] wastes expensive screen real estate and makes it highly likely that text or other
[p0092-l0010] [14.4pt] details in the diagram are illegible to at least some of your audience.
[p0092-l0011] [10.8pt] Figure 6-2. A portrait version of a data flow diagram displayed on a 16:9 ratio screen
[p0092-l0012] [14.4pt] Figure 6-3 shows a landscape version of Figure 6-2 also displayed on a 16:9
[p0092-l0013] [14.4pt] ratio screen; you can see that the details are much easier to read.
[p0092-l0014] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0092-l0015] [10.8pt] Legibility also applies to the text in your diagram. Ensure that you have selected easy-to-read
[p0092-l0016] [10.8pt] fonts and that they are readable when your audience views them. This might be on a projector
[p0092-l0017] [10.8pt] or large screen during a presentation, printed, on a small laptop screen, and so on.

=== PDF page 93 / printed label 93 ===
[p0093-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-3. A landscape version of a data flow diagram displayed on a 16:9 ratio screen
[p0093-l0002] [14.4pt] Sometimes you will need to use an existing diagram that is oriented incorrectly,
[p0093-l0003] [14.4pt] and you won’t have time to edit or re-create it. There are techniques you can use
[p0093-l0004] [14.4pt] to help your audience in this scenario too.
[p0093-l0005] [14.4pt] When presenting a diagram, or any image, that isn’t oriented correctly (or is just
[p0093-l0006] [14.4pt] too ridiculously big to fit on a slide), you can show your audience an overview,
[p0093-l0007] [14.4pt] such as the one in Figure 6-4, and then zoom in on the elements you are talking
[p0093-l0008] [14.4pt] about by inserting cropped and enlarged versions of the diagram on subsequent
[p0093-l0009] [14.4pt] slides.
[p0093-l0010] [14.4pt] When cropping the diagram, make sure you include any required information
[p0093-l0011] [14.4pt] like a legend or repeat an element from a previous crop to give the audience
[p0093-l0012] [14.4pt] context. Figure 6-5 shows a cropped version of Figure 6-4. The audience has all
[p0093-l0013] [14.4pt] the information they need to read this part of the diagram.

=== PDF page 94 / printed label 94 ===
[p0094-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-4. Portrait version of a flow diagram that will not display well on a landscape screen

=== PDF page 95 / printed label 95 ===
[p0095-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-5. Example crop of Figure 6-4
[p0095-l0002] [14.4pt] When you duplicate notes, as in Figures 6-5 and 6-4, you can differentiate
[p0095-l0003] [14.4pt] between labels shown in the complete diagram and those that are cropped. One
[p0095-l0004] [14.4pt] way to do this is to change black text to gray; for example, you can place a white
[p0095-l0005] [14.4pt] box over the top and reduce its opacity (but make sure the text is still legible).
[p0095-l0006] [14.4pt] Figure 6-6 shows the second and final crop of Figure 6-4 (note you may need
[p0095-l0007] [14.4pt] more than two for some diagrams). The title is retained so that the audience has
[p0095-l0008] [14.4pt] all the information needed to understand this part of the diagram.
[p0095-l0009] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0095-l0010] [10.8pt] In general, create diagrams in landscape format unless you know that only portrait format is
[p0095-l0011] [10.8pt] needed. Landscape can always be rotated to fit in a printed portrait document if needed.

=== PDF page 96 / printed label 96 ===
[p0096-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-6. Second example crop of Figure 6-4 with caption and notes retained
[p0096-l0002] [14.4pt] During a presentation, the audience either looks or listens. Most people can’t do
[p0096-l0003] [14.4pt] both well at the same time, so you need to make the content as clear as possible
[p0096-l0004] [14.4pt] so that your audience can pay attention to you for longer and take in your
[p0096-l0005] [14.4pt] message.
[p0096-l0006] [20.16pt] Style Communicates
[p0096-l0007] [14.4pt] The style communicates pattern is also known as metastyle. You have likely
[p0096-l0008] [14.4pt] heard of metadata (data that provides information about other data, such as

=== PDF page 97 / printed label 97 ===
[p0097-l0001] [14.4pt] image file dimensions, categories, or author names), but what about metastyle?
[p0097-l0002] [14.4pt] You are bombarded with visual messages all the time through branding and
[p0097-l0003] [14.4pt] advertising. Think about your favorite drink. Its branding has been designed to
[p0097-l0004] [14.4pt] appeal to people—to make them feel a certain way and think of certain things.
[p0097-l0005] [14.4pt] The design of the packaging and the brand are communicating to you without
[p0097-l0006] [14.4pt] words.
[p0097-l0007] [14.4pt] Diagrams and visuals in software do exactly the same thing, whether you
[p0097-l0008] [14.4pt] consciously design them to or not, just as software architecture happens whether
[p0097-l0009] [14.4pt] someone with the title architect is working on the software or not. Consider the
[p0097-l0010] [14.4pt] top and bottom diagrams in Figure 6-7. What do the different styles say about
[p0097-l0011] [14.4pt] the stage of the project? Early, late? What do they make you think about the
[p0097-l0012] [14.4pt] design and thought processes? These visuals form part of your marketing
[p0097-l0013] [14.4pt] materials, which communicate to and influence your audience.
[p0097-l0014] [10.8pt] Figure 6-7. Sketch and solid-line style comparison
[p0097-l0015] [14.4pt] The style of a diagram is typically a result of personal preference and the
[p0097-l0016] [14.4pt] application used to draw it, rather than any conscious choice about how the
[p0097-l0017] [14.4pt] audience will perceive the diagram. Open your preferred diagramming
[p0097-l0018] [14.4pt] application (such as draw.io or Visio) and create something similar using the
[p0097-l0019] [14.4pt] default settings. Now ask yourself the questions I asked before Figure 6-7. In
[p0097-l0020] [14.4pt] addition, what do the default settings communicate to your audience? What do
[p0097-l0021] [14.4pt] you truly want to communicate to your audience?
[p0097-l0022] [14.4pt] When it comes to metastyle, neither one of these styles is always better than the
[p0097-l0023] [14.4pt] other; in the same way, you can’t say that any particular style of architecture is
[p0097-l0024] [14.4pt] always the best choice for a project, or indeed any type of diagram.

=== PDF page 98 / printed label 98 ===
[p0098-l0001] [14.4pt] Style choice can be influenced by the domain or company you work in. Consider
[p0098-l0002] [14.4pt] which of the two styles in Figure 6-7 you might choose if you were designing
[p0098-l0003] [14.4pt] systems for a theme park. Would you select the same style if you were designing
[p0098-l0004] [14.4pt] systems for a hospital or bank?
[p0098-l0005] [14.4pt] You should consider what you are trying to communicate to your audience and
[p0098-l0006] [14.4pt] then style your diagram to support it.
[p0098-l0007] [10.8pt] EXAMPLE
[p0098-l0008] [14.4pt] STYLE OVER SUBSTANCE
[p0098-l0009] [14.4pt] Nikki, an architect at Polyglot Media, needs approval for an architectural
[p0098-l0010] [14.4pt] change and sets up a meeting with the decision maker, Kaspar. It doesn’t go
[p0098-l0011] [14.4pt] well. Nikki presents the proposal, supported by diagrams, but Kaspar is
[p0098-l0012] [14.4pt] having none of it. He pushes back on almost every aspect of the proposed
[p0098-l0013] [14.4pt] architecture. It is a hard “no.”
[p0098-l0014] [14.4pt] After the meeting, Kaspar asks if he can run through some ideas with Nikki
[p0098-l0015] [14.4pt] and pulls out some diagrams in a distinctive visual style. The next day, Nikki
[p0098-l0016] [14.4pt] redraws the proposal diagrams in the same distinctive style and, a week later,
[p0098-l0017] [14.4pt] presents the same proposal to Kaspar, with the same diagrams but in
[p0098-l0018] [14.4pt] Kaspar’s visual style. The response is completely different—enthusiastic
[p0098-l0019] [14.4pt] approval, even though the content is the same.
[p0098-l0020] [14.4pt] Could Kaspar understand the proposal better? Could he imagine the
[p0098-l0021] [14.4pt] proposed diagrams fitting well with his own? He sees what he wants to see;
[p0098-l0022] [14.4pt] he sees style over substance.
[p0098-l0023] [20.16pt] Misleading Composition
[p0098-l0024] [14.4pt] In software architecture, you of course don’t want to mislead your audience, but
[p0098-l0025] [14.4pt] you can create misleading visuals by accident. Many examples of misleading
[p0098-l0026] [14.4pt] diagrams and charts exist, and understanding how they have been manipulated
[p0098-l0027] [14.4pt] (intentionally or unintentionally) can help you avoid misleading your audience in
[p0098-l0028] [14.4pt] your own content.
[p0098-l0029] [14.4pt] Making changes to the baseline is one way to manipulate a chart. Figure 6-8
[p0098-l0030] [14.4pt] shows the number of votes cast in an election. Setting the baseline at 450 has

=== PDF page 99 / printed label 99 ===
[p0099-l0001] [14.4pt] enabled the Cool Party to exaggerate the difference in the numbers and
[p0099-l0002] [14.4pt] encourage its supporters to vote for it again. The manipulation especially targets
[p0099-l0003] [14.4pt] supporters of the Bloop Party, who may feel they have to vote for one of the
[p0099-l0004] [14.4pt] other parties for their vote to make a difference.
[p0099-l0005] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0099-l0006] [10.8pt] The general rule is to always leave the baseline at 0. Exceptions exist, and a different type of
[p0099-l0007] [10.8pt] visual might be better in those cases (such as a table, rather than a chart or diagram).

=== PDF page 100 / printed label 100 ===
[p0100-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-8. Chart with baseline set at 450 (antipattern)
[p0100-l0002] [14.4pt] Now take a look at Figure 6-9. This shows the same numbers as Figure 6-8, but
[p0100-l0003] [14.4pt] the Cool Party lead doesn’t look as good now.

=== PDF page 101 / printed label 101 ===
[p0101-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-9. Chart with baseline set at 0
[p0101-l0002] [14.4pt] In fact, by manipulating the baseline, two opposing parties could use the same
[p0101-l0003] [14.4pt] statistics to show their own messages. Take a look at Figure 6-10, which again
[p0101-l0004] [14.4pt] uses the same numbers and same baseline as Figure 6-9 but has now been
[p0101-l0005] [14.4pt] changed to be used by an opposing political party. The Funky Party could use
[p0101-l0006] [14.4pt] this chart to persuade supporters of the Bloop Party and unlikely voters to vote
[p0101-l0007] [14.4pt] for the Funky Party so that the Cool Party doesn’t win again.

=== PDF page 102 / printed label 102 ===
[p0102-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-10. Chart with the same baseline and figures as Figure 6-9 but rearranged to promote another
[p0102-l0002] [10.8pt] party’s message
[p0102-l0003] [14.4pt] Figure 6-11 shows an example of using comparison to mislead (intentionally or
[p0102-l0004] [14.4pt] unintentionally). By putting the two charts side by side, the audience may be
[p0102-l0005] [14.4pt] misled into thinking that the bars in each chart represent the same scale, but they
[p0102-l0006] [14.4pt] do not. The bars in the ANZ chart on the right actually represent about half the
[p0102-l0007] [14.4pt] value of those on the left. The audience could end up with the mistaken belief
[p0102-l0008] [14.4pt] that sales in Australia and New Zealand are comparable to sales in the USA and

=== PDF page 103 / printed label 103 ===
[p0103-l0001] [14.4pt] Canada, but actually, sales in New Zealand are about half those of the USA.
[p0103-l0002] [10.8pt] Figure 6-11. Charts showing how comparison can mislead the audience (antipattern)
[p0103-l0003] [14.4pt] When charts and diagrams are in close proximity, use the same scale or make the
[p0103-l0004] [14.4pt] different scales explicit and obvious to the audience. When creating software
[p0103-l0005] [14.4pt] diagrams, it is important to think about both the accuracy of the information you
[p0103-l0006] [14.4pt] include as well as how it could be read by the audience.
[p0103-l0007] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0103-l0008] [10.8pt] Diagrams are an abstraction of reality because the reality is usually far too complex to
[p0103-l0009] [10.8pt] communicate. You need to balance abstraction with accuracy where it matters, accepting the
[p0103-l0010] [10.8pt] aphorism “all models are wrong, but some are useful” and aiming for the useful.3
[p0103-l0011] [14.4pt] Figure 6-12 shows a deployment diagram that doesn’t communicate the true
[p0103-l0012] [14.4pt] message to the audience. This C4 deployment diagram attempts to describe the
[p0103-l0013] [14.4pt] horizontal scaling of instances of each container or group of containers (see the
[p0103-l0014] [14.4pt] number in the top right of each of the three boxes inside the main Polyglot

=== PDF page 104 / printed label 104 ===
[p0104-l0001] [14.4pt] Media cloud infrastructure box, such as x3), but if you look at Figure 6-13, you
[p0104-l0002] [14.4pt] will see the true reality of the scaling.
[p0104-l0003] [10.8pt] Figure 6-12. Misleading deployment diagram
[p0104-l0004] [14.4pt] In Figure 6-12, scaling was communicated for each group, and the maximum
[p0104-l0005] [14.4pt] number of containers is stated as if there will always be that many instances.
[p0104-l0006] [14.4pt] However, the reality, shown in Figure 6-13, is that either there can be fewer

=== PDF page 105 / printed label 105 ===
[p0105-l0001] [14.4pt] instances or the elements of the group can have different scaling.
[p0105-l0002] [10.8pt] Figure 6-13. True deployment diagram
[p0105-l0003] [14.4pt] An audience seeing Figure 6-14 could easily think that the enterprise service bus
[p0105-l0004] [14.4pt] in the diagram has more resources or hardware or has better scalability or a
[p0105-l0005] [14.4pt] larger capacity than other elements shown.

=== PDF page 106 / printed label 106 ===
[p0106-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-14. Easy-to-misinterpret service bus diagram
[p0106-l0002] [14.4pt] Changing the scale of the enterprise service bus to match other elements, as in
[p0106-l0003] [14.4pt] Figure 6-15, is less likely to mislead your audience. Keep elements the same size
[p0106-l0004] [14.4pt] in logical diagrams unless there is a reason not to, like showing different
[p0106-l0005] [14.4pt] capacities. The consistent sizing in Figure 6-15 does have trade-offs, though; the
[p0106-l0006] [14.4pt] arrows and lines are potentially harder to read because of the smaller size of the
[p0106-l0007] [14.4pt] box. If this were a problem, you could consider splitting the diagram into
[p0106-l0008] [14.4pt] different types of services, for example, or addressing a particular concern.
[p0106-l0009] [10.8pt] Figure 6-15. Closer-to-reality service bus diagram

=== PDF page 107 / printed label 107 ===
[p0107-l0001] [20.16pt] Create a Visual Balance
[p0107-l0002] [14.4pt] The create a visual balance pattern is often overlooked and can set your diagram
[p0107-l0003] [14.4pt] apart from others. Balance is a key element in visual design, and it applies to
[p0107-l0004] [14.4pt] diagrams as well as photography and other visuals. Balance is an innate human
[p0107-l0005] [14.4pt] expectation and therefore an expectation of your audience. Giving your audience
[p0107-l0006] [14.4pt] what they want is fundamental to successful communication. (Of course, there is
[p0107-l0007] [14.4pt] an exception to every rule; what your audience thinks they want may not be what
[p0107-l0008] [14.4pt] they really need.)
[p0107-l0009] [14.4pt] Balancing a diagram does not make a bad diagram good, but it will enhance a
[p0107-l0010] [14.4pt] good diagram. One element of balance is symmetry. The diagram in Figure 6-16
[p0107-l0011] [14.4pt] is a perfectly valid C4 container diagram, but it doesn’t have the satisfying effect
[p0107-l0012] [14.4pt] of Figure 6-17. Compare the two and you will find yourself drawn more to
[p0107-l0013] [14.4pt] Figure 6-17.

=== PDF page 108 / printed label 108 ===
[p0108-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-16. Unbalanced C4 container diagram (antipattern)

=== PDF page 109 / printed label 109 ===
[p0109-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 6-17. Balanced C4 container diagram
[p0109-l0002] [14.4pt] Figures 6-16 and 6-17 contain all the same information and elements, but in
[p0109-l0003] [14.4pt] different places on the canvas. You do not need (and will not always be able) to
[p0109-l0004] [14.4pt] make something completely symmetrical (this is called bilateral symmetry), but
[p0109-l0005] [14.4pt] you should be able to apply another type of symmetry, such as approximate
[p0109-l0006] [14.4pt] symmetry (where there are small differences) as in Figure 6-17.
[p0109-l0007] [14.4pt] Symmetry can be applied to part of a diagram instead of the whole to improve
[p0109-l0008] [14.4pt] readability. For example, when creating a diagram in which elements fan out

=== PDF page 110 / printed label 110 ===
[p0110-l0001] [14.4pt] and/or reduce, you can use symmetry at those expansion and contraction points
[p0110-l0002] [14.4pt] when arranging elements.
[p0110-l0003] [14.4pt] If symmetry is not possible, you may be able to employ asymmetry to achieve a
[p0110-l0004] [14.4pt] similarly satisfying effect. In that case, you need to balance your elements by
[p0110-l0005] [14.4pt] using position, weight/size, and direction. Think of your canvas as a seesaw and
[p0110-l0006] [14.4pt] aim to get the balance correct for a given axis or fulcrum on that canvas.
[p0110-l0007] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0110-l0008] [10.8pt] Diagrams do not have to be pretty, but consistency within a diagram and a set of diagrams
[p0110-l0009] [10.8pt] makes them easier on the eye.
[p0110-l0010] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0110-l0011] [14.4pt] This chapter has given you tools to create legible, honest, and balanced diagrams
[p0110-l0012] [14.4pt] that communicate via their style as well as the information contained within
[p0110-l0013] [14.4pt] them.
[p0110-l0014] [14.4pt] Now that you’re at the end of Part I, you should have a huge toolbox for creating
[p0110-l0015] [14.4pt] and using diagrams and other visuals. These last techniques of composition add
[p0110-l0016] [14.4pt] the gleaming tiles to the roof, whereas the essentials in the first chapter provided
[p0110-l0017] [14.4pt] a strong foundation.
[p0110-l0018] [14.4pt] But you know that diagrams alone don’t communicate everything you need to
[p0110-l0019] [14.4pt] share about software architecture and design. Technical writing is a skill in itself,
[p0110-l0020] [14.4pt] some of it separate from visual communication and some of it overlapping.
[p0110-l0021] [14.4pt] Verbal and nonverbal communication further complement written and visual
[p0110-l0022] [14.4pt] communication, whether you are speaking in person or remotely.
[p0110-l0023] [14.4pt] Part II explores technical written, verbal, and nonverbal communication and the
[p0110-l0024] [14.4pt] techniques you can apply to all three, as well as techniques for getting your
[p0110-l0025] [14.4pt] audience to put their trust in you and what you say.
[p0110-l0026] [10.8pt] 1 Ensure you choose the landscape option so that 16 is the horizontal ratio.
[p0110-l0027] [10.8pt] 2 4:3 was the typical ratio of screens in years gone by.

=== PDF page 111 / printed label 111 ===
[p0111-l0001] [10.8pt] 3 An aphorism is a pithy observation which contains a general truth.

=== PDF page 112 / printed label 112 ===
[p0112-l0001] [28.8pt] Part II. Multimodal
[p0112-l0002] [28.8pt] Communication
[p0112-l0003] [14.4pt] Part II covers patterns, antipatterns, techniques, and frameworks for written,
[p0112-l0004] [14.4pt] verbal (spoken), and nonverbal communication. Whether you work remotely, in
[p0112-l0005] [14.4pt] an office, or a mixture of the two, you use all these types of communication
[p0112-l0006] [14.4pt] regularly. Putting some extra thought into how you write, speak, and use body
[p0112-l0007] [14.4pt] language and other nonverbal communication will make your message more
[p0112-l0008] [14.4pt] understandable and increase your chances of receiving your desired response.
[p0112-l0009] [14.4pt] The sections within the chapters in Part II are organized as collections of patterns
[p0112-l0010] [14.4pt] and antipatterns. You can apply the patterns for written communication to
[p0112-l0011] [14.4pt] whatever you write, including emails, documentation, and instant messages. The
[p0112-l0012] [14.4pt] verbal and nonverbal techniques can be applied whether you are talking to
[p0112-l0013] [14.4pt] someone (or a group) face-to-face or remotely.
[p0112-l0014] [14.4pt] Chapter 9, the final chapter in this part, contains patterns and techniques for
[p0112-l0015] [14.4pt] using rhetoric, which you can apply to all types of communication. Originally
[p0112-l0016] [14.4pt] developed by Aristotle over two millennia ago, you might not think rhetoric
[p0112-l0017] [14.4pt] would apply to communication in the modern technical world, but the techniques
[p0112-l0018] [14.4pt] of ethos, pathos, and logos have stood the test of time for a good reason.
[p0112-l0019] [14.4pt] Apply all these patterns and techniques to strengthen your message, whatever
[p0112-l0020] [14.4pt] format (or multiple formats) it takes.

=== PDF page 113 / printed label 113 ===
[p0113-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 7. Written
[p0113-l0002] [28.8pt] Communication
[p0113-l0003] [14.4pt] As a professional, you understand the importance of clear and effective written
[p0113-l0004] [14.4pt] communication. Whether you’re sending emails to colleagues, documenting
[p0113-l0005] [14.4pt] requirements, or creating reports, written communication is a critical aspect of
[p0113-l0006] [14.4pt] your job.
[p0113-l0007] [14.4pt] This chapter explores various tips and techniques to help you write clear,
[p0113-l0008] [14.4pt] concise, and impactful communications. You’ll look at patterns that will help
[p0113-l0009] [14.4pt] you improve your writing and antipatterns that will help you avoid common
[p0113-l0010] [14.4pt] pitfalls. Whether you’re a seasoned writer or just starting out, this chapter
[p0113-l0011] [14.4pt] provides practical guidance to help you take your writing skills to the next level.
[p0113-l0012] [20.16pt] Simple Language
[p0113-l0013] [14.4pt] To make your language clear, you need to take many factors into account. The
[p0113-l0014] [14.4pt] simple language pattern can help you. It is easy to fall into the trap of thinking
[p0113-l0015] [14.4pt] everyone understands all the words you say or write. Trying to sound clever by
[p0113-l0016] [14.4pt] using complex vocabulary usually leads to confusion. Rather than people
[p0113-l0017] [14.4pt] thinking more of you, they are likely to think less of you because they have not
[p0113-l0018] [14.4pt] understood.
[p0113-l0019] [14.4pt] For those who struggle with visual processing, such as people with dyslexia or
[p0113-l0020] [14.4pt] attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), complex words and sentences
[p0113-l0021] [14.4pt] may be an even larger hindrance than for a neurotypical person. Large amounts
[p0113-l0022] [14.4pt] of text, grouped together, can also cause problems, especially if little whitespace
[p0113-l0023] [14.4pt] remains. Those with autism often struggle to understand sarcasm and idioms,1 so
[p0113-l0024] [14.4pt] these are best kept out of your diagrams and presentations.
[p0113-l0025] [14.4pt] NEURODIVERSITY
[p0113-l0026] [14.4pt] Here are some quick definitions concerning neurodiversity:

=== PDF page 114 / printed label 114 ===
[p0114-l0001] [14.4pt] Neurodivergent
[p0114-l0002] [14.4pt] Describes someone whose brain processes, learns, or behaves differently
[p0114-l0003] [14.4pt] than what is considered typical.
[p0114-l0004] [14.4pt] Neurotypical
[p0114-l0005] [14.4pt] Describes someone whose cognitive functioning is considered typical of
[p0114-l0006] [14.4pt] the general population.
[p0114-l0007] [14.4pt] Nonnative speakers of a language have a smaller vocabulary than native
[p0114-l0008] [14.4pt] speakers, who typically have a vocabulary in that language of around 15,000–
[p0114-l0009] [14.4pt] 20,000 word families (groups of words with a common root). According to a
[p0114-l0010] [14.4pt] 2022 study, a vocabulary of around 4,000 word families is required to
[p0114-l0011] [14.4pt] understand around 95% of news stories written in English. Particularly when
[p0114-l0012] [14.4pt] communicating in a native language, you need to take into account that some of
[p0114-l0013] [14.4pt] your audience may not be native speakers, especially in today’s international and
[p0114-l0014] [14.4pt] online society.
[p0114-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0114-l0016] [10.8pt] Consider that some of your audience may use translation software to read your writing. Using
[p0114-l0017] [10.8pt] simple language without idioms will help them get a better translation.
[p0114-l0018] [14.4pt] Vocabularies vary from person to person, sometimes corresponding to education,
[p0114-l0019] [14.4pt] social standing, or age (older people having been exposed to more vocabulary
[p0114-l0020] [14.4pt] over the years, and different generations using different jargon). Vocabulary still
[p0114-l0021] [14.4pt] varies widely within these demographics because of factors like interests,
[p0114-l0022] [14.4pt] culture, and geography. Common vocabulary also differs from business, domain,
[p0114-l0023] [14.4pt] and technical vocabularies. Take into account that your audience’s vocabulary
[p0114-l0024] [14.4pt] will vary, even if their demographic doesn’t.
[p0114-l0025] [14.4pt] As with acronyms,2 it is a good idea to include a glossary for business, technical,
[p0114-l0026] [14.4pt] and domain vocabulary in your diagrams and documentation. If everyone knows
[p0114-l0027] [14.4pt] the exact definition of order details, for example, mismatches in meaning, where

=== PDF page 115 / printed label 115 ===
[p0115-l0001] [14.4pt] a name means something different in different parts of the codebase, become far
[p0115-l0002] [14.4pt] less likely. Naming is hard, and a glossary and ubiquitous language helps with
[p0115-l0003] [14.4pt] this.
[p0115-l0004] [14.4pt] Table 7-1 shows some options for simplifying your writing. Domain-driven
[p0115-l0005] [14.4pt] design (DDD), a software development approach, creates a ubiquitous
[p0115-l0006] [14.4pt] vocabulary that can be used within the domain you are modeling. As the
[p0115-l0007] [14.4pt] ubiquitous language originates from the business, and not your technical team,
[p0115-l0008] [14.4pt] you have only so much control over it. Create your own glossary to add to as
[p0115-l0009] [14.4pt] needed.
[p0115-l0010] [14.4pt] Table 7-1. Examples of simplified vocabulary and
[p0115-l0011] [14.4pt] phrases
[p0115-l0012] [14.4pt] Complex/less used
[p0115-l0013] [14.4pt] Simple/regularly used
[p0115-l0014] [14.4pt] acquire
[p0115-l0015] [14.4pt] buy
[p0115-l0016] [14.4pt] toward
[p0115-l0017] [14.4pt] to
[p0115-l0018] [14.4pt] adopt
[p0115-l0019] [14.4pt] use
[p0115-l0020] [14.4pt] dispatch
[p0115-l0021] [14.4pt] send
[p0115-l0022] [14.4pt] locate
[p0115-l0023] [14.4pt] find
[p0115-l0024] [14.4pt] patron
[p0115-l0025] [14.4pt] customer
[p0115-l0026] [14.4pt] a majority of
[p0115-l0027] [14.4pt] most
[p0115-l0028] [14.4pt] as a result of
[p0115-l0029] [14.4pt] because of/due to
[p0115-l0030] [14.4pt] is able to
[p0115-l0031] [14.4pt] can

=== PDF page 116 / printed label 116 ===
[p0116-l0001] [14.4pt] determine the location of
[p0116-l0002] [14.4pt] find
[p0116-l0003] [14.4pt] for the purpose of
[p0116-l0004] [14.4pt] for
[p0116-l0005] [14.4pt] have a tendency to
[p0116-l0006] [14.4pt] tend to
