[p0116-l0009] [14.4pt] make decisions about
[p0116-l0010] [14.4pt] decide on
[p0116-l0011] [14.4pt] is of the opinion
[p0116-l0012] [14.4pt] thinks/believes
[p0116-l0013] [14.4pt] in order to
[p0116-l0014] [14.4pt] to
[p0116-l0015] [14.4pt] When speaking or presenting to an audience who is not familiar with your
[p0116-l0016] [14.4pt] domain vocabulary, you should explain the terms that your audience needs to
[p0116-l0017] [14.4pt] understand. You can do this by specifically defining a term, or by providing a
[p0116-l0018] [14.4pt] synonym they may understand (for example, a nonnative speaker of English may
[p0116-l0019] [14.4pt] not understand locate but may understand find).
[p0116-l0020] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0116-l0021] [10.8pt] Keep your language simple and define any necessary complex and domain vocabulary to
[p0116-l0022] [10.8pt] smooth the learning curve for your audience.
[p0116-l0023] [20.16pt] Acronym Hell
[p0116-l0024] [14.4pt] You enter the state of acronym hell when a diagram, table, or any accompanying
[p0116-l0025] [14.4pt] text contains acronyms that are not defined for the audience. You cannot expect
[p0116-l0026] [14.4pt] your audience to understand an acronym as you intend. Acronyms can mean
[p0116-l0027] [14.4pt] different things to different people, even if your audience is aware of the context
[p0116-l0028] [14.4pt] you are describing.
[p0116-l0029] [14.4pt] It’s easy to assume that others share your understanding. This is often called the

=== PDF page 117 / printed label 117 ===
[p0117-l0001] [14.4pt] curse of knowledge. Your brain has a lot of information that you reference
[p0117-l0002] [14.4pt] without realizing it, and this information is not necessarily in the brains of your
[p0117-l0003] [14.4pt] audience.
[p0117-l0004] [14.4pt] THE CURSE OF KNOWLEDGE
[p0117-l0005] [14.4pt] The more of an expert you become on a subject, the harder it is to remember
[p0117-l0006] [14.4pt] what it is like to be inexperienced, making communication with nonexperts
[p0117-l0007] [14.4pt] more difficult.
[p0117-l0008] [14.4pt] Here is an example of the curse of knowledge: you mention a concept to
[p0117-l0009] [14.4pt] someone and they ask you to explain, but when you try to explain, you
[p0117-l0010] [14.4pt] realize that they don’t know other things you mention in your explanation,
[p0117-l0011] [14.4pt] and a tree of missing knowledge branches out. Explaining your original
[p0117-l0012] [14.4pt] concept is going to take more than a few minutes of conversation.
[p0117-l0013] [14.4pt] Using idioms without realizing that some of your audience may not
[p0117-l0014] [14.4pt] understand them is another example of the curse of knowledge.
[p0117-l0015] [14.4pt] Not defining your acronyms leaves them open to interpretation, which is not
[p0117-l0016] [14.4pt] going to help you communicate successfully. You are aiming for your audience
[p0117-l0017] [14.4pt] to have the same understanding as you, which they can’t if they don’t understand
[p0117-l0018] [14.4pt] an acronym at all or believe it have an alternate definition. Every industry or
[p0117-l0019] [14.4pt] domain has its own versions of acronyms, and the difference between business
[p0117-l0020] [14.4pt] and technical people’s understanding of the same acronym can vary widely even
[p0117-l0021] [14.4pt] in the same domain.
[p0117-l0022] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0117-l0023] [10.8pt] Some words have become so ubiquitous that people have forgotten they were originally
[p0117-l0024] [10.8pt] acronyms. Examples include RADAR (radio detection and ranging), LASER (light
[p0117-l0025] [10.8pt] amplification by stimulated emission of radiation), and self-contained underwater breathing
[p0117-l0026] [10.8pt] apparatus (SCUBA). The acronyms Joseph Cyril Bamford (JCB) and Bayerische Motoren
[p0117-l0027] [10.8pt] Werke (BMW) are ubiquitous in Europe.3
[p0117-l0028] [14.4pt] DDD provides a way of creating a ubiquitous language, which can be used to
[p0117-l0029] [14.4pt] enable successful communication. Even if you don’t employ DDD, you can still

=== PDF page 118 / printed label 118 ===
[p0118-l0001] [14.4pt] help create a ubiquitous language by defining all the acronyms you use.
[p0118-l0002] [14.4pt] The main way to avoid the acronym hell antipattern is to ensure that your
[p0118-l0003] [14.4pt] acronyms are defined, and you can do that in many ways. When speaking, say
[p0118-l0004] [14.4pt] the acronym and the full version (this helps people learn the acronym for when
[p0118-l0005] [14.4pt] others don’t include the definition). In text, you should typically include the
[p0118-l0006] [14.4pt] acronym in parentheses after the definition the first time you use it, or vice versa
[p0118-l0007] [14.4pt] (and, if needed, again after the first use in each section or chapter).
[p0118-l0008] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0118-l0009] [10.8pt] Wherever you include definitions of your acronyms, make sure that they are easily accessible
[p0118-l0010] [10.8pt] to your audience.
[p0118-l0011] [14.4pt] Acronyms can be spelled out in a legend or footnote if they are used in a
[p0118-l0012] [14.4pt] diagram or other visual. You can also include them in a glossary in your
[p0118-l0013] [14.4pt] document, slide deck, or documentation, along with definitions of other words
[p0118-l0014] [14.4pt] (your ubiquitous language).
[p0118-l0015] [14.4pt] MANY POSSIBLE MEANINGS
[p0118-l0016] [14.4pt] If you think an acronym has only one possible information technology
[p0118-l0017] [14.4pt] meaning, consider these:
[p0118-l0018] [14.4pt] DFD
[p0118-l0019] [14.4pt] Data flow diagram, deployment flow diagram, Document Freedom Day,
[p0118-l0020] [14.4pt] disk failure diagnostic, Development Finance Division, decision
[p0118-l0021] [14.4pt] feedback detection, detailed functional design, design for discard, design
[p0118-l0022] [14.4pt] for development…
[p0118-l0023] [14.4pt] SPA
[p0118-l0024] [14.4pt] Single-page application, single point of access, special protocol
[p0118-l0025] [14.4pt] assessment, serial port adapter, smart process application, sales and
[p0118-l0026] [14.4pt] purchase agreement, solution provider agreement, software process
[p0118-l0027] [14.4pt] assessment, systems and process assurance, scalable processing
[p0118-l0028] [14.4pt] architecture, service provider architecture, simple processing application,

=== PDF page 119 / printed label 119 ===
[p0119-l0001] [14.4pt] system performance analysis…
[p0119-l0002] [14.4pt] BLT
[p0119-l0003] [14.4pt] Business leadership team, basic language translator, bulk loading tool,
[p0119-l0004] [14.4pt] business liaison team, bottom line technology, build load test, binary
[p0119-l0005] [14.4pt] large object, bit-level tracing…and even bacon, lettuce, and tomato!
[p0119-l0006] [20.16pt] Structured Writing
[p0119-l0007] [14.4pt] Many forms of writing work best when they are planned and structured,
[p0119-l0008] [14.4pt] including technical writing. You often need to communicate complex concepts
[p0119-l0009] [14.4pt] or show how various concepts fit together, and this cannot be done effectively
[p0119-l0010] [14.4pt] without considering the structure of your writing.
[p0119-l0011] [14.4pt] It can help to think about structuring technical writing as you would think about
[p0119-l0012] [14.4pt] structuring a computer program. Program structures often take the form of a tree,
[p0119-l0013] [14.4pt] or pyramid.
[p0119-l0014] [14.4pt] Graph data is now much more commonplace, but navigating a graph with many
[p0119-l0015] [14.4pt] possible routes and navigating a pyramid structure are very different. In
[p0119-l0016] [14.4pt] technical writing, the pyramid structure, is much more efficient (graph structures
[p0119-l0017] [14.4pt] are more efficient for other scenarios, such as searching highly related data).
[p0119-l0018] [14.4pt] The Minto pyramid principle, developed by Barbara Minto in the 1960s as a
[p0119-l0019] [14.4pt] method for ordering and structuring information, is a useful tool for technical
[p0119-l0020] [14.4pt] writing. The principle became a standard at McKinsey & Company, where Minto
[p0119-l0021] [14.4pt] worked when she developed the principle.
[p0119-l0022] [14.4pt] The overall concept of the Minto pyramid principle is to start with what you
[p0119-l0023] [14.4pt] have identified as the key idea or message, break that into logical arguments, and
[p0119-l0024] [14.4pt] order those ideas logically, then break down each of those ideas and order them
[p0119-l0025] [14.4pt] logically until you have broken down all the elements of what you need to say.
[p0119-l0026] [14.4pt] In her own words:
[p0119-l0027] [14.4pt] The easiest order for a reader is to receive the major, more abstract ideas
[p0119-l0028] [14.4pt] before he is required to take in the minor supporting ones. And since the

=== PDF page 120 / printed label 120 ===
[p0120-l0001] [14.4pt] major ideas are always derived from the minor ones, the ideal structure of the
[p0120-l0002] [14.4pt] ideas will always be a pyramid of groups of ideas tied together by a single
[p0120-l0003] [14.4pt] overall thought.
[p0120-l0004] [14.4pt] In this way, each idea or element of your writing is linked vertically to an idea
[p0120-l0005] [14.4pt] before it, which will always be a summary of the ideas that follow it, and
[p0120-l0006] [14.4pt] horizontally linked to other ideas making up the logical argument.
[p0120-l0007] [14.4pt] As you move down the pyramid, you end up with a pattern of stating a major
[p0120-l0008] [14.4pt] idea and then answering the reader’s questions about that idea as you progress.
[p0120-l0009] [14.4pt] Figure 7-1 is an example of this structure: traversing the pyramid begins with the
[p0120-l0010] [14.4pt] key message, followed by the first supporting arguments, and then the
[p0120-l0011] [14.4pt] supporting data or arguments for the first argument. The next step in the traversal
[p0120-l0012] [14.4pt] is supporting argument 2.
[p0120-l0013] [10.8pt] Figure 7-1. Minto pyramid principle example structure
[p0120-l0014] [14.4pt] A pyramid structure is useful for your everyday communication as well as your
[p0120-l0015] [14.4pt] technical writing. In Example 7-1, an email is structured as the information came
[p0120-l0016] [14.4pt] into the author’s head. Example 7-2 shows the same information, but structured
[p0120-l0017] [14.4pt] as a pyramid.
[p0120-l0018] [14.4pt] Example 7-1. Email structured as the ideas came into the author’s head
[p0120-l0019] [10.8pt] Dear all,
[p0120-l0020] [10.8pt] Our 3rd-party supplier has informed me that they can no longer make the planned
[p0120-l0021] [10.8pt] kick-off meeting and also cannot make Friday or before 3 p.m. on Monday. I have
[p0120-l0022] [10.8pt] checked the diary, and it seems that some development team members are on leave
[p0120-l0023] [10.8pt] from Wednesday.
[p0120-l0024] [10.8pt] What with school pick-up and drop-off affecting several of us, Tuesday at 10 a.m.
[p0120-l0025] [10.8pt] looks like the best time at the moment. Does that work for you? Please let me know
[p0120-l0026] [10.8pt] ASAP.

=== PDF page 121 / printed label 121 ===
[p0121-l0001] [10.8pt] Kind regards,
[p0121-l0002] [14.4pt] Example 7-2. Email structured as a pyramid
[p0121-l0003] [10.8pt] Dear all,
[p0121-l0004] [10.8pt] We need to reschedule the project kick-off meeting. Can you let me know ASAP if
[p0121-l0005] [10.8pt] Tuesday at 10 a.m. works for you?
[p0121-l0006] [10.8pt] The team from our 3rd-party supplier can no longer make the original date and time,
[p0121-l0007] [10.8pt] and two of our developers are on leave from Wednesday until Friday. I also want to
[p0121-l0008] [10.8pt] avoid school drop-off and collection times as I know this affects several people
[p0121-l0009] [10.8pt] involved.
[p0121-l0010] [10.8pt] Kind regards,
[p0121-l0011] [14.4pt] This comparison shows the power of the pyramid structure. In Example 7-2, the
[p0121-l0012] [14.4pt] intention of the email is clear from the start, as well as the desired response of
[p0121-l0013] [14.4pt] the reader, and further explanation follows. Some readers might ignore the
[p0121-l0014] [14.4pt] information after the first paragraph if they didn’t need it, and simply follow the
[p0121-l0015] [14.4pt] request.
[p0121-l0016] [14.4pt] Example 7-2 shows how you can apply this pyramid structure to a simple email.
[p0121-l0017] [14.4pt] Applying it to your technical writing (and the structure of slide decks) will take
[p0121-l0018] [14.4pt] practice but will pay dividends in the improvement of your communication.
[p0121-l0019] [14.4pt] Consider Example 7-3, which introduces a project. Compare it to Example 7-4 to
[p0121-l0020] [14.4pt] see how applying the pyramid structure enhances the readability and
[p0121-l0021] [14.4pt] understandability of the introduction. Would you structure this any differently to
[p0121-l0022] [14.4pt] further improve its readability?
[p0121-l0023] [14.4pt] Example 7-3. Unstructured program introduction
[p0121-l0024] [10.8pt] To identify any problem areas or bottlenecks in the systems and processes within
[p0121-l0025] [10.8pt] Polyglot Media, collaborative modeling, such as EventStorming, will be used to map
[p0121-l0026] [10.8pt] current systems and processes.
[p0121-l0027] [10.8pt] An initial cross-functional working group will create this map, with further
[p0121-l0028] [10.8pt] cross-functional groups assigned to break down problems identified and design
[p0121-l0029] [10.8pt] optimizations in an ongoing and iterative process.
[p0121-l0030] [10.8pt] This will all lead to better-optimized systems and processes to better meet the
[p0121-l0031] [10.8pt] needs of a rapidly growing customer base (including internal customers), with
[p0121-l0032] [10.8pt] Polyglot Media having seen better-than-expected growth in the last financial year.
[p0121-l0033] [14.4pt] Example 7-4. Pyramid structured program introduction
[p0121-l0034] [10.8pt] Polyglot Media has seen better-than-expected growth in the last financial year,

=== PDF page 122 / printed label 122 ===
[p0122-l0001] [10.8pt] leading to the need to optimize systems and processes to better meet the needs of a
[p0122-l0002] [10.8pt] rapidly growing customer base.
[p0122-l0003] [10.8pt] To meet these needs, there is a requirement for a map of the current systems and
[p0122-l0004] [10.8pt] processes as they are, with any problem areas or bottlenecks identified. This will
[p0122-l0005] [10.8pt] be the output of an initial cross-functional working group, which will assess the
[p0122-l0006] [10.8pt] current situation using techniques such as EventStorming and other types of
[p0122-l0007] [10.8pt] collaborative modeling.
[p0122-l0008] [10.8pt] The optimization process is expected to be ongoing and iterative, finding solutions
[p0122-l0009] [10.8pt] to the current problem areas identified and continually optimizing for both
[p0122-l0010] [10.8pt] internal and external customers.
[p0122-l0011] [14.4pt] This section has given a very high-level overview of the pyramid structure,
[p0122-l0012] [14.4pt] which you can begin to apply to your writing, documentation, and presentations.
[p0122-l0013] [14.4pt] For more information, see The Minto Pyramid Principle by Barbara Minto
[p0122-l0014] [14.4pt] (Prentice Hall, 2010) and Barbara Minto’s official website.
[p0122-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0122-l0016] [10.8pt] The pyramid principle can be compared to headlines and the structure of articles in a
[p0122-l0017] [10.8pt] newspaper. The headline gives you an understanding of the whole story. Each sentence and
[p0122-l0018] [10.8pt] paragraph you read gives you more detail, but you can stop at any time and still know the key
[p0122-l0019] [10.8pt] points of the story.
[p0122-l0020] [20.16pt] Syntax of Technical Writing
[p0122-l0021] [14.4pt] Technical writing differs from creative types of writing. Technical writing is
[p0122-l0022] [14.4pt] intended for a specific audience (such as developers or customers of the
[p0122-l0023] [14.4pt] product), whereas creative writing usually has no particular audience in mind,
[p0122-l0024] [14.4pt] except possibly an age range or genre preference. The purpose of technical
[p0122-l0025] [14.4pt] writing is to inform or instruct, whereas creative writing exists to entertain.
[p0122-l0026] [14.4pt] Because of these two main differences, technical and creative writing differ
[p0122-l0027] [14.4pt] significantly in format, style, and structure. The syntax of technical writing is
[p0122-l0028] [14.4pt] important to ensure that your writing is clear and informative. Clarity is the most
[p0122-l0029] [14.4pt] fundamental rule of technical writing.
[p0122-l0030] [16.56pt] Strong Verbs

=== PDF page 123 / printed label 123 ===
[p0123-l0001] [14.4pt] Choose strong, precise, active verbs (words that describe actions, states, or
[p0123-l0002] [14.4pt] occurrences). Verbs such as be, was, and happen are examples of weak, or
[p0123-l0003] [14.4pt] inactive, verbs. Limiting these in your technical writing and using carefully
[p0123-l0004] [14.4pt] chosen strong verbs means your content is more specific and clear.
[p0123-l0005] [14.4pt] Balance the use of strong verbs with your audience’s familiarity with the verbs
[p0123-l0006] [14.4pt] you are choosing. Weaker verbs are appropriate when your audience may not
[p0123-l0007] [14.4pt] understand a stronger verb, and you do not need to eliminate all weak verbs
[p0123-l0008] [14.4pt] from your writing.
[p0123-l0009] [14.4pt] Here are some examples of changing a verb to create a more precise and concise
[p0123-l0010] [14.4pt] sentence:
[p0123-l0011] [14.4pt] The error notification happens when…
[p0123-l0012] [14.4pt] The service generates the notification when…
[p0123-l0013] [14.4pt] There are three things that have made us decide to…
[p0123-l0014] [14.4pt] Three issues convinced us to decide to…
[p0123-l0015] [14.4pt] I am very careful to ensure…
[p0123-l0016] [14.4pt] I carefully ensure…
[p0123-l0017] [14.4pt] Once the vector is entered, it is changed…
[p0123-l0018] [14.4pt] Once the service receives the vector, it transforms…
[p0123-l0019] [14.4pt] Examples of strong verbs include govern, amend, extract, realize, notify,
[p0123-l0020] [14.4pt] convince, inspect, guide, scan, serve, transform, raise, generate, and ensure.
[p0123-l0021] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0123-l0022] [10.8pt] Avoid the phrases there is or there are. These can often be deleted with minor additional
[p0123-l0023] [10.8pt] changes to the sentence. Consider how You should know three critical things about cloud
[p0123-l0024] [10.8pt] computing is a lot clearer and more persuasive than There are three critical things you should
[p0123-l0025] [10.8pt] know about cloud computing.
[p0123-l0026] [16.56pt] Short Sentences

=== PDF page 124 / printed label 124 ===
[p0124-l0001] [14.4pt] We read short sentences faster. Removing superfluous words reduces the length
[p0124-l0002] [14.4pt] of sentences and the document as a whole. Short documents are also faster to
[p0124-l0003] [14.4pt] read.
[p0124-l0004] [14.4pt] Just as with code, it is easier to maintain and avoid bugs (mistakes) in shorter
[p0124-l0005] [14.4pt] documentation.
[p0124-l0006] [14.4pt] Short sentences are also easier to read and deliver a more powerful message.
[p0124-l0007] [14.4pt] This is another application of the single responsibility principle. Your sentences
[p0124-l0008] [14.4pt] should have only one reason to exist.
[p0124-l0009] [16.56pt] Precise Paragraphs
[p0124-l0010] [14.4pt] The opening sentence of your paragraph is the most important. Your audience
[p0124-l0011] [14.4pt] may scan your content and choose which parts to read based on the first
[p0124-l0012] [14.4pt] sentence. The first sentence, therefore, needs to grab the reader’s attention either
[p0124-l0013] [14.4pt] by covering the central point of the paragraph (as discussed in “Structured
[p0124-l0014] [14.4pt] Writing”) or by using a tool such as a rhetorical question.
[p0124-l0015] [14.4pt] Paragraphs are another opportunity to apply the single responsibility principle.
[p0124-l0016] [14.4pt] Their content typically includes more than a single sentence, but each paragraph
[p0124-l0017] [14.4pt] should exist for one overall reason. You can compare a sentence to a method,
[p0124-l0018] [14.4pt] and a paragraph to a class, in code.
[p0124-l0019] [14.4pt] Your paragraphs should cover one topic and include what you are telling the
[p0124-l0020] [14.4pt] reader, why what you are saying is important, and how the reader can use that
[p0124-l0021] [14.4pt] knowledge or know it to be true. Sometimes these details may be covered across
[p0124-l0022] [14.4pt] more than one paragraph.
[p0124-l0023] [14.4pt] Whereas sentences should generally be short, paragraphs should not be too short
[p0124-l0024] [14.4pt] or too long. The sweet spot is at around three to five sentences. Go over seven
[p0124-l0025] [14.4pt] sentences, and your paragraph becomes a wall of text that your audience is likely
[p0124-l0026] [14.4pt] to avoid. Rearrange and split up longer paragraphs to avoid reader fatigue. If you
[p0124-l0027] [14.4pt] find that you have many short paragraphs in your writing, you should consider
[p0124-l0028] [14.4pt] reorganizing the content into coherent three-to-five-sentence paragraphs or
[p0124-l0029] [14.4pt] changing your paragraphs into a list.
[p0124-l0030] [16.56pt] Consistent Vocabulary

=== PDF page 125 / printed label 125 ===
[p0125-l0001] [14.4pt] Use vocabulary consistently throughout the piece that you are writing. Switching
[p0125-l0002] [14.4pt] between different words when you have the same intended meaning is the same
[p0125-l0003] [14.4pt] as changing the name of a variable in the middle of a method. Your code will not
[p0125-l0004] [14.4pt] compile and, in writing, nor will your audience’s understanding.
[p0125-l0005] [14.4pt] Many words are used interchangeably in technical writing (even if they don’t
[p0125-l0006] [14.4pt] really share the exact same definition: application, program, or software;
[p0125-l0007] [14.4pt] engineer or developer; user, client, or customer). Pick one and stick to it when
[p0125-l0008] [14.4pt] you mean the same thing, and explicitly call out the difference if you use a
[p0125-l0009] [14.4pt] similar word to refer to something different.
[p0125-l0010] [14.4pt] When you first mention a name for something quite long, you can also introduce
[p0125-l0011] [14.4pt] a shortened version or acronym and then continue to use the shorter version.
[p0125-l0012] [14.4pt] Once you have begun using the shorter version, do not cycle back and forth
[p0125-l0013] [14.4pt] between the long and short versions. Consistently use one or the other.
[p0125-l0014] [14.4pt] If you will use the longer version only a few times, you may not want to bother
[p0125-l0015] [14.4pt] defining and then using the shorter version. You should consider whether your
[p0125-l0016] [14.4pt] audience would appreciate you using the opportunity to teach them the
[p0125-l0017] [14.4pt] connection between the long name and short or acronym version.
[p0125-l0018] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0125-l0019] [10.8pt] Make sure to always define any acronyms you use (see “Acronym Hell”).
[p0125-l0020] [16.56pt] Audience Empathy
[p0125-l0021] [14.4pt] Defining your audience is one of the fundamentals of communication (as
[p0125-l0022] [14.4pt] discussed in “Know Your Audience”). Your audience affects how you write and
[p0125-l0023] [14.4pt] how you structure your writing. Asking yourself these questions about your
[p0125-l0024] [14.4pt] audience’s knowledge will help you set a baseline that you will build on in your
[p0125-l0025] [14.4pt] writing:
[p0125-l0026] [14.4pt] How much does your audience know about what you are writing about?
[p0125-l0027] [14.4pt] Your audience may have some knowledge that you can build on. Stating
[p0125-l0028] [14.4pt] basic facts that your audience knows may put them off reading.

=== PDF page 126 / printed label 126 ===
[p0126-l0001] [14.4pt] Does your audience know of something similar?
[p0126-l0002] [14.4pt] If your audience already knows something similar, you can make
[p0126-l0003] [14.4pt] comparisons to help them understand your topic.
[p0126-l0004] [14.4pt] Does your audience have knowledge they haven’t used in a long time?
[p0126-l0005] [14.4pt] If your audience hasn’t used knowledge in a long time (maybe they learned it
[p0126-l0006] [14.4pt] at college over 10 years ago), you should provide an overview with more
[p0126-l0007] [14.4pt] detailed information that they can choose to consume as needed.
[p0126-l0008] [14.4pt] Does your audience have out-of-date knowledge?
[p0126-l0009] [14.4pt] Technology moves incredibly fast. If information has changed, provide
[p0126-l0010] [14.4pt] comparisons of new and old and explain the advantages and disadvantages of
[p0126-l0011] [14.4pt] these changes.
[p0126-l0012] [14.4pt] In addition, asking yourself questions about what your audience needs will help
[p0126-l0013] [14.4pt] you plan and structure your article or documentation:
[p0126-l0014] [14.4pt] What is your audience trying to accomplish?
[p0126-l0015] [14.4pt] This is what you will enable them to do through your writing. Are they
[p0126-l0016] [14.4pt] developers needing to know how to implement your architecture? Customers
[p0126-l0017] [14.4pt] wanting to learn to use a new feature in your software? You can structure
[p0126-l0018] [14.4pt] your answer to this question by completing this sentence: After reading, the
[p0126-l0019] [14.4pt] audience will be able to…
[p0126-l0020] [14.4pt] What does your audience need to learn to accomplish their goal?
[p0126-l0021] [14.4pt] This is the difference between their current knowledge and what they need to
[p0126-l0022] [14.4pt] know to accomplish their goal (the answer to the previous question). The
[p0126-l0023] [14.4pt] answer to this question is what you must teach in your writing. You can
[p0126-l0024] [14.4pt] structure your answer by completing this sentence: After reading, the
[p0126-l0025] [14.4pt] audience will have learned…
[p0126-l0026] [14.4pt] Does your audience need to do this in a certain order?
[p0126-l0027] [14.4pt] Your answer will determine how you structure your writing. If your audience
[p0126-l0028] [14.4pt] needs to follow steps in a certain order, you will need to use a numbered list

=== PDF page 127 / printed label 127 ===
[p0127-l0001] [14.4pt] and put instructions in that order. If your audience needs to know one step
[p0127-l0002] [14.4pt] before they can be taught another, you should sequence your writing with
[p0127-l0003] [14.4pt] that in mind.
[p0127-l0004] [14.4pt] With these answers, you can decide what information to include in your writing
[p0127-l0005] [14.4pt] and how to structure it to meet the needs of your audience.
[p0127-l0006] [14.4pt] TIPS FOR TECHNICAL DOCUMENTS
[p0127-l0007] [14.4pt] Besides all the tips and techniques in this chapter that you can apply to the
[p0127-l0008] [14.4pt] writing of your documents, here are some tips about the content to include in
[p0127-l0009] [14.4pt] a technical document:
[p0127-l0010] [14.4pt] Begin with the key points
[p0127-l0011] [14.4pt] Put your key points or takeaways up front. Readers may read only the
[p0127-l0012] [14.4pt] first page or first few paragraphs but should still leave knowing the most
[p0127-l0013] [14.4pt] important information.
[p0127-l0014] [14.4pt] State the scope
[p0127-l0015] [14.4pt] By stating the scope of the technical document, you set the reader’s
[p0127-l0016] [14.4pt] expectations of what will be included if they read on. This can save the
[p0127-l0017] [14.4pt] reader time by helping them to find the document with the information
[p0127-l0018] [14.4pt] they want and to avoid reading a document that they are not interested in.
[p0127-l0019] [14.4pt] State the nonscope
[p0127-l0020] [14.4pt] If the reader might expect a topic to be included, but it isn’t, telling them
[p0127-l0021] [14.4pt] so right from the start will save them time or disappointment later. If the
[p0127-l0022] [14.4pt] topic not covered is available somewhere else, you can link to it for
[p0127-l0023] [14.4pt] reference.
[p0127-l0024] [14.4pt] State the intended audience
[p0127-l0025] [14.4pt] This information helps the reader decide whether they should be reading
[p0127-l0026] [14.4pt] the document. Without this, some intended readers may skip the
[p0127-l0027] [14.4pt] document, and other readers may waste time reading something that
[p0127-l0028] [14.4pt] doesn’t meet their needs.

=== PDF page 128 / printed label 128 ===
[p0128-l0001] [14.4pt] State required prerequisite knowledge or reading
[p0128-l0002] [14.4pt] Tell the reader anything that is out of this document’s scope that they will
[p0128-l0003] [14.4pt] need to know or understand before reading the document. Link to
[p0128-l0004] [14.4pt] resources and required reading if possible.
[p0128-l0005] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0128-l0006] [14.4pt] The techniques you have learned in this chapter can be applied to all your
[p0128-l0007] [14.4pt] writing, including emails and other communications, documentation, and the
[p0128-l0008] [14.4pt] labels and other text in your diagrams. Improving your writing affects a lot more
[p0128-l0009] [14.4pt] of your communication than you might think.
[p0128-l0010] [14.4pt] The other main ways that you communicate are verbally and nonverbally, which
[p0128-l0011] [14.4pt] complement each other and your written skills. In the next chapter, you will
[p0128-l0012] [14.4pt] discover techniques to improve verbal and nonverbal communication, both in
[p0128-l0013] [14.4pt] your understanding of others and others’ understanding of you.
[p0128-l0014] [10.8pt] 1 An idiom is a group of words in a fixed order that has a particular meaning that is different from the
[p0128-l0015] [10.8pt] meanings of each word on its own.
[p0128-l0016] [10.8pt] 2 See “Acronym Hell”.
[p0128-l0017] [10.8pt] 3 Notice how I define acronyms I use.

=== PDF page 129 / printed label 129 ===
[p0129-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 8. Verbal and Nonverbal
[p0129-l0002] [28.8pt] Communication
[p0129-l0003] [14.4pt] Verbal and nonverbal communication are important aspects of any technical
[p0129-l0004] [14.4pt] professional’s job, whether you are working onsite or remotely. Nonverbal
[p0129-l0005] [14.4pt] communication covers not just body language, gestures, and facial expressions,
[p0129-l0006] [14.4pt] but also eye contact, tone of voice, personal space, touch, appearance, and the
[p0129-l0007] [14.4pt] use of tools and props.
[p0129-l0008] [14.4pt] Communication is essentially encoding a message to send and decoding a
[p0129-l0009] [14.4pt] message received. Just like in a software system, the decoded message needs to
[p0129-l0010] [14.4pt] match the encoded one for communication to be considered successful. The
[p0129-l0011] [14.4pt] patterns in this chapter will improve your encoding and decoding skills, along
[p0129-l0012] [14.4pt] with your skills of persuasion and influence, which are often the goals of your
[p0129-l0013] [14.4pt] communication.
[p0129-l0014] [20.16pt] Encoding Messages
[p0129-l0015] [14.4pt] Communication can be one-way, when a response is not required, or two-way,
[p0129-l0016] [14.4pt] when the recipient responds to the initial message. In both cases, that message
[p0129-l0017] [14.4pt] must always be encoded, or packaged, so that the recipient can then decode it.
[p0129-l0018] [14.4pt] The trick is getting the encoding right so that your message is understood as you
[p0129-l0019] [14.4pt] want it to be.
[p0129-l0020] [16.56pt] Using the Acceptance Prophecy
[p0129-l0021] [14.4pt] Start thinking about how your message will be packaged up for your recipient to
[p0129-l0022] [14.4pt] understand before you begin communicating. The acceptance prophecy is one of
[p0129-l0023] [14.4pt] the first patterns you can put into practice. It states that when you think others
[p0129-l0024] [14.4pt] will like you, you behave more warmly toward them and, therefore, they like
[p0129-l0025] [14.4pt] you more. The opposite is also true: if you think someone won’t like you, you
[p0129-l0026] [14.4pt] will behave more coldly toward them, making them less inclined to like you.

=== PDF page 130 / printed label 130 ===
[p0130-l0001] [14.4pt] It’s a self-fulfilling prophecy, and it makes a lot of sense if you think about how
[p0130-l0002] [14.4pt] friends or enemies act toward each other. Some people care more about others’
[p0130-l0003] [14.4pt] acceptance of them, and some are naturally more accepting, but research has
[p0130-l0004] [14.4pt] shown both that people respond better to others who are genuinely warm toward
[p0130-l0005] [14.4pt] them and that people anticipating others’ acceptance act more warmly toward
[p0130-l0006] [14.4pt] them.1,2
[p0130-l0007] [14.4pt] Becoming a social optimist, as opposed to a social pessimist, is a technique that
[p0130-l0008] [14.4pt] can boost your credibility and the likelihood of you succeeding in your
[p0130-l0009] [14.4pt] communication goals. When presenting your architecture designs to
[p0130-l0010] [14.4pt] stakeholders, you should start by convincing yourself that the people in your
[p0130-l0011] [14.4pt] audience are your friends and will approve of your design (of course, it is also
[p0130-l0012] [14.4pt] important to plan for questions or misgivings they may have). If you are talking
[p0130-l0013] [14.4pt] to a customer in presales, you can follow the same pattern, convincing yourself
[p0130-l0014] [14.4pt] that they will like the product and want to buy it.
[p0130-l0015] [14.4pt] This won’t come naturally to many and won’t happen overnight, but you can
[p0130-l0016] [14.4pt] keep practicing until this self-fulfilling prophecy regularly comes true.
[p0130-l0017] [16.56pt] Giving Your Full Attention
[p0130-l0018] [14.4pt] Another way to connect with the person or people you are talking to is to ensure
[p0130-l0019] [14.4pt] you are giving your full attention. This has many benefits, including making
[p0130-l0020] [14.4pt] them feel more respected and appreciated, which boosts the likelihood that
[p0130-l0021] [14.4pt] they’ll agree with you, or do what you want them to. Your audience is also more
[p0130-l0022] [14.4pt] likely to reciprocate and give their full attention back to you and what you are
[p0130-l0023] [14.4pt] saying, making them more likely to understand your message. You boost your
[p0130-l0024] [14.4pt] credibility and build an emotional connection with them. You can also benefit by
[p0130-l0025] [14.4pt] picking up more of their body language and other signals, and what they are
[p0130-l0026] [14.4pt] saying themselves.
[p0130-l0027] [14.4pt] When you have a 1:1 meeting with your manager or direct report, this technique
[p0130-l0028] [14.4pt] is particularly useful. The goals of a 1:1 meeting may be to give or receive
[p0130-l0029] [14.4pt] support, or to convince your manager of the promotion you think you are due. To
[p0130-l0030] [14.4pt] show that you are giving someone your full attention, try the following:
[p0130-l0031] [14.4pt] Give eye contact (unless it’s not appreciated, in which case, focus on
[p0130-l0032] [14.4pt] something like notes instead), and don’t let your gaze wander into the

=== PDF page 131 / printed label 131 ===
[p0131-l0001] [14.4pt] distance.
[p0131-l0002] [14.4pt] Don’t use your phone or laptop while they are talking.
[p0131-l0003] [14.4pt] Take notes using a pen and paper.
[p0131-l0004] [14.4pt] If you have to take notes on a device such as a tablet or laptop, tell them
[p0131-l0005] [14.4pt] that this is what you are doing so they don’t think your attention is
[p0131-l0006] [14.4pt] elsewhere
[p0131-l0007] [14.4pt] Turn off notifications on your devices.
[p0131-l0008] [14.4pt] Don’t interrupt when they are speaking.
[p0131-l0009] [14.4pt] Ask clarifying questions when needed.
[p0131-l0010] [14.4pt] Repeat your understanding back to them to ensure you have understood.
[p0131-l0011] [14.4pt] Other situations when these techniques are particularly useful include talking to
[p0131-l0012] [14.4pt] stakeholders or customers about their requirements, to potential investors, or to
[p0131-l0013] [14.4pt] customers about potential sales.
[p0131-l0014] [16.56pt] Using Body Language and Gestures
[p0131-l0015] [14.4pt] Body language plays a large part in communication when you are visible to your
[p0131-l0016] [14.4pt] audience. This includes facial expressions, posture, and gestures. Keeping this in
[p0131-l0017] [14.4pt] mind, you can use deliberate body language to add to the message you are
[p0131-l0018] [14.4pt] communicating.
[p0131-l0019] [14.4pt] Gestures, which are mostly made with your hands, are one of the easiest aspects
[p0131-l0020] [14.4pt] of body language to control and use to your advantage. Hand gestures come
[p0131-l0021] [14.4pt] naturally and are used even by blind people talking to other blind people.
[p0131-l0022] [14.4pt] Gestures can help you remember what you say and encourage others to listen to
[p0131-l0023] [14.4pt] you.
[p0131-l0024] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0131-l0025] [10.8pt] Besides using body language to express yourself, you should be monitoring others’ body
[p0131-l0026] [10.8pt] language and adapting to it. For example, if you see a nervous reaction, you can provide
[p0131-l0027] [10.8pt] reassurances and explain further.

=== PDF page 132 / printed label 132 ===
[p0132-l0001] [14.4pt] Explanatory gestures (such as holding your hands out wide when
[p0132-l0002] [14.4pt] communicating that something is large) can help people better understand what
[p0132-l0003] [14.4pt] you say, whereas power gestures (such as stabbing a finger forward for
[p0132-l0004] [14.4pt] emphasis) express dominance and authority. It is important to use all gestures
[p0132-l0005] [14.4pt] carefully so that they work toward your goals and not against them. Here are
[p0132-l0006] [14.4pt] some tips for doing so:
[p0132-l0007] [14.4pt] Keep gestures within the rectangular space from the top of your chest to
[p0132-l0008] [14.4pt] your hips and about half your body width to either side of you (see
[p0132-l0009] [14.4pt] Figure 8-1). Gestures outside of this can be seen as over-the-top,
[p0132-l0010] [14.4pt] aggressive, or out of control.
[p0132-l0011] [14.4pt] Match your gestures to what you are saying and make them purposeful.
[p0132-l0012] [14.4pt] Be wary of different cultural meanings of hand gestures.
[p0132-l0013] [14.4pt] Find a balance between being stiff and being too fast or gesturing too often.
[p0132-l0014] [14.4pt] When using gestures remotely, consider the framing of your camera. Make
[p0132-l0015] [14.4pt] your gestures even smaller than you would in person; they can look more
[p0132-l0016] [14.4pt] exaggerated on a screen.
[p0132-l0017] [10.8pt] Figure 8-1. Keep gestures within the rectangular space from the top of your chest to your hips and about
[p0132-l0018] [10.8pt] half your body width to either side of you

=== PDF page 133 / printed label 133 ===
[p0133-l0001] [14.4pt] Here are some gestures you can use and examples of when you might want to
[p0133-l0002] [14.4pt] use them. The first three are illustrated in Figure 8-2:
[p0133-l0003] [14.4pt] A clenched fist shows intensity. You may want to use this when
[p0133-l0004] [14.4pt] communicating the success of a project or pleasure at something going
[p0133-l0005] [14.4pt] well. Be careful to not use it when sounding irritated; that would signify
[p0133-l0006] [14.4pt] anger or aggression.
[p0133-l0007] [14.4pt] Using your index finger and thumb to indicate a small gap is a great way to
[p0133-l0008] [14.4pt] emphasize that something is small, has little effect, or was “so close.” Use
[p0133-l0009] [14.4pt] this to emphasize small problems or trade-offs, or when a goal was nearly
[p0133-l0010] [14.4pt] met or a disaster was close but averted.
[p0133-l0011] [14.4pt] As you are listing things, you can use your fingers to count them off. This is
[p0133-l0012] [14.4pt] particularly appropriate when you have said something like “We found
[p0133-l0013] [14.4pt] three important benefits…”
[p0133-l0014] [14.4pt] A sweeping motion with both hands, from one side of your body to the
[p0133-l0015] [14.4pt] other, can be used to indicate wiping the slate clean to start again. You
[p0133-l0016] [14.4pt] might want to use this when communicating the need to rearchitect a
[p0133-l0017] [14.4pt] product when moving to the cloud, for example.
[p0133-l0018] [14.4pt] Putting both your hands up can indicate concession or apology. This is
[p0133-l0019] [14.4pt] useful when you want to emphasize the sincerity of your verbal concession
[p0133-l0020] [14.4pt] or apology. When you are saying something sarcastically, you can pair this
[p0133-l0021] [14.4pt] gesture with wide eyes.
[p0133-l0022] [10.8pt] Figure 8-2. Examples of hand gestures
[p0133-l0023] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0133-l0024] [10.8pt] Use gestures even when others cannot see you, such as in a phone call or audio-only meeting.
[p0133-l0025] [10.8pt] Your body movement impacts the sound of your voice, so using gestures will make you sound

=== PDF page 134 / printed label 134 ===
[p0134-l0001] [10.8pt] more natural and better convey your meaning.
[p0134-l0002] [14.4pt] Aim to implement these encoding techniques one or two at a time, and they will
[p0134-l0003] [14.4pt] become natural as you practice them more and more.
[p0134-l0004] [20.16pt] Decoding Messages
[p0134-l0005] [14.4pt] Receiving a message is not enough. To understand it, you need to decode it. And
[p0134-l0006] [14.4pt] to do that, you will use your senses and your brain.
[p0134-l0007] [14.4pt] In Thinking, Fast and Slow (Farrar, Straus and Giroux, 2011), Daniel Kahneman
[p0134-l0008] [14.4pt] explains that the brain has two systems, which he calls system 1 and system 2.3
[p0134-l0009] [14.4pt] Your system 1 does most of your thinking subconsciously, driven by your
[p0134-l0010] [14.4pt] experiences and instinct, and will act on and decode the messages you receive
[p0134-l0011] [14.4pt] and pass the results to your system 2. System 2 is the rational and logical part of
[p0134-l0012] [14.4pt] your brain, but it is influenced by the decisions made in system 1.
[p0134-l0013] [14.4pt] You can end up acting on the decisions of system 1 without engaging system 2
[p0134-l0014] [14.4pt] much, which happens more often when you are communicating in real time and
[p0134-l0015] [14.4pt] need to decode messages faster and respond. When you are not communicating
[p0134-l0016] [14.4pt] in real time, you have more time to engage system 2 and make a more reasoned
[p0134-l0017] [14.4pt] response.
[p0134-l0018] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0134-l0019] [10.8pt] Overreliance on system 1 can lead to biases and errors in judgment, while overuse of system 2
[p0134-l0020] [10.8pt] can lead to analysis paralysis (overanalyzing or overthinking). We need both systems to
[p0134-l0021] [10.8pt] function well.
[p0134-l0022] [16.56pt] Battling Bias
[p0134-l0023] [14.4pt] Cognitive bias can affect your decision making and reasoning even if you do
[p0134-l0024] [14.4pt] engage your system 2, but it can have a profound effect if you use only your
[p0134-l0025] [14.4pt] system 1. Cognitive biases are unconscious errors in thinking, essentially
[p0134-l0026] [14.4pt] simplification errors, in the same way that you may find early optimization of
[p0134-l0027] [14.4pt] your code causes errors later. These biases can affect your interactions with

=== PDF page 135 / printed label 135 ===
[p0135-l0001] [14.4pt] others, your judgments, and even your own safety.
[p0135-l0002] [14.4pt] It is unlikely you can ever be free of cognitive bias, but being aware of your
[p0135-l0003] [14.4pt] biases is the first step toward lessening their effects on your decision making.
[p0135-l0004] [14.4pt] You can train yourself to use new patterns of thinking by doing the following:
[p0135-l0005] [14.4pt] Recognize and learn about your biases. Research the types of cognitive bias
[p0135-l0006] [14.4pt] and how they affect people.4
[p0135-l0007] [14.4pt] Slow down your decision making. For example, document important
[p0135-l0008] [14.4pt] decisions by using architecture decision records (ADRs) so that the
[p0135-l0009] [14.4pt] template forces you to think carefully and not make a rushed decision.5
[p0135-l0010] [14.4pt] Consider when biases might affect you and create prompts to remind
[p0135-l0011] [14.4pt] yourself to pause and consider how you are being affected (for example,
[p0135-l0012] [14.4pt] you could add prompts to an ADR template or interview feedback form).
[p0135-l0013] [14.4pt] Minimize distractions when you are making decisions or decoding
[p0135-l0014] [14.4pt] messages; for example, don’t read an email while listening to a podcast.
[p0135-l0015] [14.4pt] Ask for feedback and advice from others so that you can take other
[p0135-l0016] [14.4pt] perspectives into account.
[p0135-l0017] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0135-l0018] [10.8pt] When considering feedback or advice from others, remember that they also have cognitive
[p0135-l0019] [10.8pt] biases. If they are the same as yours, you can both fall prey to confirmation bias!
[p0135-l0020] [14.4pt] I encourage you to learn more about all the types of cognitive biases, but let’s
[p0135-l0021] [14.4pt] briefly look at three of the most common ones.
[p0135-l0022] [14.4pt] Confirmation bias
[p0135-l0023] [14.4pt] In confirmation bias, you interpret new information as confirmation of your
[p0135-l0024] [14.4pt] existing beliefs and opinions. Your brain is more likely to process information
[p0135-l0025] [14.4pt] that supports your beliefs and label it as true. You should be very aware of this
[p0135-l0026] [14.4pt] when you’re online because many search and social media algorithms have
[p0135-l0027] [14.4pt] developed to show you exactly what you want to see, creating a search bubble or

=== PDF page 136 / printed label 136 ===
[p0136-l0001] [14.4pt] echo chamber in which you rarely, if ever, see anything that challenges what you
[p0136-l0002] [14.4pt] already believe. It is easy to see how people can be fooled by misinformation
[p0136-l0003] [14.4pt] when it is echoed back at them from all sides.
[p0136-l0004] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0136-l0005] [10.8pt] Be aware of confirmation bias when researching new technology, creating an ADR, or
[p0136-l0006] [10.8pt] attending a conference. Just being aware that a conference could become an echo chamber can
[p0136-l0007] [10.8pt] help you to be more discerning about the information you take in. Look for perspectives that
[p0136-l0008] [10.8pt] you don’t agree with and make a note of them. They may turn into an interesting new avenue
[p0136-l0009] [10.8pt] for you.
[p0136-l0010] [14.4pt] Hindsight bias
[p0136-l0011] [14.4pt] Hindsight bias is the tendency to perceive past events as more predictable than
[p0136-l0012] [14.4pt] they were. This bias can affect you at the following different intensities:
[p0136-l0013] [14.4pt] Predictability is a low-intensity hindsight bias. If you’re thinking “I knew
[p0136-l0014] [14.4pt] that upgrade would make things worse,” you might be exhibiting this bias.
[p0136-l0015] [14.4pt] Inevitability is a mid-intensity hindsight bias. It could manifest in thoughts
[p0136-l0016] [14.4pt] like “That upgrade just had to make things worse.”
[p0136-l0017] [14.4pt] Memory distortion is a high-intensity hindsight bias. For example, “I said
[p0136-l0018] [14.4pt] applying that upgrade would make things worse” when no one said
[p0136-l0019] [14.4pt] anything of the sort indicates a distorted memory.
[p0136-l0020] [14.4pt] It can be easy to fall prey to this bias when things go horribly wrong or
[p0136-l0021] [14.4pt] incredibly well, or when a client changes their mind about something.
[p0136-l0022] [14.4pt] Groupthink
[p0136-l0023] [14.4pt] Groupthink occurs among a group of people in which the desire for harmony or
[p0136-l0024] [14.4pt] conformity results in a flawed or irrational decision making. Members of the
[p0136-l0025] [14.4pt] group may not express dissenting viewpoints, fail to critically analyze
[p0136-l0026] [14.4pt] alternatives, or ignore outside perspectives to reach a consensus. Groupthink can
[p0136-l0027] [14.4pt] hinder creativity and innovation and is often associated with poor decision
[p0136-l0028] [14.4pt] making.

=== PDF page 137 / printed label 137 ===
[p0137-l0001] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0137-l0002] [10.8pt] The training of large language models can be easily affected by bias. Consider that these
[p0137-l0003] [10.8pt] models know only what they have been trained on, and so any bias in the training data will lead
[p0137-l0004] [10.8pt] to bias in outputs from the model.
[p0137-l0005] [10.8pt] EXAMPLE
[p0137-l0006] [14.4pt] GINO EMBRACES DISAGREEMENT
[p0137-l0007] [14.4pt] When creating ADRs at Polyglot Media, Gino always adds the heading
[p0137-l0008] [14.4pt] “Consultation” and asks for input from a range of people on the rest of the
[p0137-l0009] [14.4pt] content of the ADR.
[p0137-l0010] [14.4pt] He selects a diverse group of people in hopes of recognizing more biases,
[p0137-l0011] [14.4pt] but who he selects varies depending on the type of decision. The diversity
[p0137-l0012] [14.4pt] covers role (such as architects, product owners, or developers) but also
[p0137-l0013] [14.4pt] demographic differences such as age, gender, experience, and tenure in the
[p0137-l0014] [14.4pt] organization.
[p0137-l0015] [14.4pt] Gino has seen how these factors, and others, can lead to new perspectives on
[p0137-l0016] [14.4pt] a technology or a decision that will affect a downstream team. He says the
[p0137-l0017] [14.4pt] worst thing that can happen is everyone agreeing with him.
[p0137-l0018] [16.56pt] Being Present
[p0137-l0019] [14.4pt] To give yourself the best chance of decoding a message, stay present in the
[p0137-l0020] [14.4pt] moment and put your own views and biases aside (such as dislike of an
[p0137-l0021] [14.4pt] architectural style or cognitive biases). This is a skill you will need to practice
[p0137-l0022] [14.4pt] but is incredibly useful when you need to understand someone (such as a
[p0137-l0023] [14.4pt] customer, internal user, your report, or a colleague).
[p0137-l0024] [14.4pt] To be present in a situation, follow these tips:
[p0137-l0025] [14.4pt] Don’t assume you know what someone is going to say. This can mean you
[p0137-l0026] [14.4pt] miss what they actually say and figuratively “put words in their mouth.”
[p0137-l0027] [14.4pt] Put aside any ideas about their intentions or judgments about them. This
[p0137-l0028] [14.4pt] can help avoid confirmation bias.

=== PDF page 138 / printed label 138 ===
[p0138-l0001] [14.4pt] Use listening noises such as mmm and body language such as nodding,
[p0138-l0002] [14.4pt] smiling, or putting your head to one side to emphasize you are listening
[p0138-l0003] [14.4pt] closely.
[p0138-l0004] [14.4pt] Let go of your own viewpoint and ego to better focus on them and their
[p0138-l0005] [14.4pt] message.
[p0138-l0006] [14.4pt] Don’t interrupt or seek to challenge what they say, and instead use
[p0138-l0007] [14.4pt] clarification questions once they have finished to clear up anything you
[p0138-l0008] [14.4pt] don’t understand or think is missing. Asking questions and fully exploring a
[p0138-l0009] [14.4pt] person’s ideas can be a way of taking them through the thought processes
[p0138-l0010] [14.4pt] that support your own argument.
[p0138-l0011] [14.4pt] Pay attention to their body language to understand their message more
[p0138-l0012] [14.4pt] clearly. Look for mismatches between body language and their words to
[p0138-l0013] [14.4pt] identify anything that may not be true or the whole truth. This could warn
[p0138-l0014] [14.4pt] you when someone is being manipulative or when they are scared of telling
[p0138-l0015] [14.4pt] you something.
[p0138-l0016] [14.4pt] When they have finished, and you have asked any clarifying questions,
[p0138-l0017] [14.4pt] summarize what they have said to you to make sure you have understood
[p0138-l0018] [14.4pt] correctly.
[p0138-l0019] [14.4pt] Finally, decide the next steps to take, depending on the message you have
[p0138-l0020] [14.4pt] been given.
[p0138-l0021] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0138-l0022] [10.8pt] Many of these techniques fall under the heading of active listening, where you go beyond just
[p0138-l0023] [10.8pt] hearing what is said. You become an active participant and seek to understand what is said.
[p0138-l0024] [10.8pt] Active listening can improve the comprehension of all participants, not only your own.
[p0138-l0025] [16.56pt] Awareness of Cultural Differences
[p0138-l0026] [14.4pt] Cultural differences can disrupt the accurate decoding of verbal and nonverbal
[p0138-l0027] [14.4pt] communication. It can be hard to remember that not everyone is like you and
[p0138-l0028] [14.4pt] that even people from the same country can have very different cultural
[p0138-l0029] [14.4pt] backgrounds. Age, gender, gender identity, sexuality, and race are among the

=== PDF page 139 / printed label 139 ===
[p0139-l0001] [14.4pt] factors that make us different from one another. Our backgrounds influence how
[p0139-l0002] [14.4pt] we communicate—how we encode and decode messages. Differences in the
[p0139-l0003] [14.4pt] interpretation of a word or gesture can make a huge difference in whether
[p0139-l0004] [14.4pt] communication is successful.
[p0139-l0005] [14.4pt] When you are communicating with people whose background differs from
[p0139-l0006] [14.4pt] yours, which is more and more likely as workforces become more distributed
[p0139-l0007] [14.4pt] and diverse, find out about important differences and things that may
[p0139-l0008] [14.4pt] inadvertently offend. You can do this by researching, but asking questions of the
[p0139-l0009] [14.4pt] people you are communicating with will often get you there faster.
[p0139-l0010] [14.4pt] In some cultures, confrontation or expressing disagreement in public is seen as
[p0139-l0011] [14.4pt] rude or aggressive, whereas in others it is perfectly normal. Another cultural
[p0139-l0012] [14.4pt] business difference revolves around the boss; some people support their manager
[p0139-l0013] [14.4pt] at every step and never contradict them, whereas other cultures support a much
[p0139-l0014] [14.4pt] flatter hierarchy that encourages speaking your view.
[p0139-l0015] [14.4pt] From this, you may think that diversity negatively affects a team or its output,
[p0139-l0016] [14.4pt] but research and analysis has shown that “contextual diversity can positively
[p0139-l0017] [14.4pt] affect task performance.”6 Taras et al. found that teams with members who had
[p0139-l0018] [14.4pt] been exposed to a diversity of institutions, politics, and economics (termed
[p0139-l0019] [14.4pt] contextual diversity) had advantages in problem solving, decision making, and
[p0139-l0020] [14.4pt] creativity.
[p0139-l0021] [14.4pt] In contrast, the same research showed that “personal diversity”—differing
[p0139-l0022] [14.4pt] characteristics like age, culture, and language—“can negatively affect team
[p0139-l0023] [14.4pt] climate.” This shows that culture and other personal diversity must be taken into
[p0139-l0024] [14.4pt] account for communication to be successful, but diversity of experience is
[p0139-l0025] [14.4pt] overall positive.
[p0139-l0026] [14.4pt] Aim to create relationships with offshore teams, colleagues in other countries,
[p0139-l0027] [14.4pt] and colleagues who differ from you to foster an environment of understanding.
[p0139-l0028] [14.4pt] Keep in mind that differences in culture can affect a team’s success. Learning
[p0139-l0029] [14.4pt] about each other and each other’s cultures will help to mitigate this.
[p0139-l0030] [14.4pt] Diversity has been shown to improve creativity and even a company’s bottom
[p0139-l0031] [14.4pt] line, so avoiding miscommunication with others who have a different personal
[p0139-l0032] [14.4pt] background from you will help your company in the long run by making it a
[p0139-l0033] [14.4pt] place where diversity can thrive.

=== PDF page 140 / printed label 140 ===
[p0140-l0001] [20.16pt] Influence and Persuasion
[p0140-l0002] [14.4pt] The art of influence and persuasion is useful not only to sales and marketing.
[p0140-l0003] [14.4pt] When you communicate with stakeholders, customers, and colleagues, you are
[p0140-l0004] [14.4pt] often trying to influence them or persuade them of something. Maybe you think
[p0140-l0005] [14.4pt] that something needs to be changed, that your designs meet their requirements,
[p0140-l0006] [14.4pt] or that their requirements need to change. You need to persuade and influence
[p0140-l0007] [14.4pt] others to gain support, implement your ideas, and achieve your goals. You will
[p0140-l0008] [14.4pt] also have others aiming to persuade and influence you.
[p0140-l0009] [14.4pt] Although many techniques of persuasion exist, it is important to realize that they
[p0140-l0010] [14.4pt] must be used with a foundation of demonstrating value, listening to people’s
[p0140-l0011] [14.4pt] needs and feedback, and coming up with useful ideas and compromises. Your
[p0140-l0012] [14.4pt] ideas and solutions need to be aligned with your audience’s goals to be
[p0140-l0013] [14.4pt] persuasive. Aligning the goals of the project, program, and so on to others’ goals
[p0140-l0014] [14.4pt] will incline people to agree without the need for many (if any) persuasive tactics.
[p0140-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0140-l0016] [10.8pt] Ideally, you should do as much discovery work in advance as possible so that you can develop
[p0140-l0017] [10.8pt] your ideas before being in a live situation with your audience.
[p0140-l0018] [14.4pt] People will generally resist your proposals and suggestions if they think you
[p0140-l0019] [14.4pt] haven’t understood their problems and needs. You must understand their needs
[p0140-l0020] [14.4pt] and concerns as well as communicate that you have this understanding. Ask
[p0140-l0021] [14.4pt] open-ended questions to get this information and be explicit about how you will
[p0140-l0022] [14.4pt] solve their problems. You need to show them what they will gain (for example, a
[p0140-l0023] [14.4pt] reduction in technical debt).
[p0140-l0024] [14.4pt] Remember that persuasion is a process rather than an event. Listening,
[p0140-l0025] [14.4pt] generating ideas, and demonstrating alignment of goals and value to your
[p0140-l0026] [14.4pt] audience all happen across conversations, meetings, presentations, and other
[p0140-l0027] [14.4pt] communications. Apply influence and persuasion techniques throughout your
[p0140-l0028] [14.4pt] communications with your audience to improve the likelihood of gaining their
[p0140-l0029] [14.4pt] approval or agreement.
[p0140-l0030] [14.4pt] Stating the most important message first in the form of a headline statement is a

=== PDF page 141 / printed label 141 ===
[p0141-l0001] [14.4pt] good technique. You can think of it like a newspaper headline, and it should be a
[p0141-l0002] [14.4pt] similar length. Use this statement to begin your conversation or meeting, or
[p0141-l0003] [14.4pt] directly after the title slide of a talk or presentation. You can also do this in
[p0141-l0004] [14.4pt] writing—for example, as the subject of an email or at the top of an agenda.
[p0141-l0005] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0141-l0006] [10.8pt] A startling statistic or fact can be used to create impact, such as “Every year our software saves
[p0141-l0007] [10.8pt] thousands of lives” or “This release increased transaction throughput by 150%.”
[p0141-l0008] [14.4pt] Another technique is to use bold words and statements. You need to make an
[p0141-l0009] [14.4pt] explicit commitment and demonstrate self-belief to get your audience to also
[p0141-l0010] [14.4pt] invest in that belief. To strengthen your language:
[p0141-l0011] [14.4pt] Begin statements with phrases like The plan is… and We will… that contain
[p0141-l0012] [14.4pt] strong verbs like influence and optimize. (See “Strong Verbs”.)
[p0141-l0013] [14.4pt] Avoid words such as try, maybe, and hopefully.
[p0141-l0014] [14.4pt] Use concise sentences and deliberate pauses while avoiding thinking noises
[p0141-l0015] [14.4pt] such as um and er.
[p0141-l0016] [14.4pt] To put these techniques into practice, say, “The plan is to see results by Q2 next
[p0141-l0017] [14.4pt] year” rather than “We will hopefully see results by Q2 next year” when
[p0141-l0018] [14.4pt] explaining your designs to stakeholders.
[p0141-l0019] [14.4pt] Boost your credibility to gain your audience’s trust. One tool to do this is a
[p0141-l0020] [14.4pt] credibility statement or position, which is generally used to introduce yourself
[p0141-l0021] [14.4pt] and establish authority from the start. You can see written examples in people’s
[p0141-l0022] [14.4pt] profiles on sites such as LinkedIn or Mastodon. You can also use a verbal
[p0141-l0023] [14.4pt] credibility statement when networking or in an external meeting or
[p0141-l0024] [14.4pt] presentation.7
[p0141-l0025] [14.4pt] Include your name and role and then highlight your expertise. Use strong words
[p0141-l0026] [14.4pt] like specialist, experienced, or internationally recognized (see Example 8-1).
[p0141-l0027] [14.4pt] Aim for around 10 seconds in length when you are speaking at a networking
[p0141-l0028] [14.4pt] event or in a meeting and around 60 seconds for an event or conference
[p0141-l0029] [14.4pt] introduction.

=== PDF page 142 / printed label 142 ===
[p0142-l0001] [14.4pt] Example 8-1. Start of a credibility statement
[p0142-l0002] [10.8pt] My name is Jacqui Read, and I am an internationally recognized consultant, software
[p0142-l0003] [10.8pt] architect, and O'Reilly author with over 15 years of experience in software
[p0142-l0004] [10.8pt] architecture and development across a range of domains.
[p0142-l0005] [14.4pt] When you are communicating to persuade, it is good practice to identify some
[p0142-l0006] [14.4pt] likely audience questions or pushback and to plan responses. You will inevitably
[p0142-l0007] [14.4pt] get questions that you haven’t anticipated, so it is important to plan for when that
[p0142-l0008] [14.4pt] happens. Here are some things you can do to give yourself time to think:
[p0142-l0009] [14.4pt] Pause and take a breath to give everyone time to process the question.
[p0142-l0010] [14.4pt] Show appreciation that the question was asked. “Thank you, that is an
[p0142-l0011] [14.4pt] important topic…” (but don’t overdo this if there are lots of questions—
[p0142-l0012] [14.4pt] rehearse a few ways of saying this; for example, “I’m glad you brought that
[p0142-l0013] [14.4pt] up…”).
[p0142-l0014] [14.4pt] Repeat the question to make sure that you and the rest of the audience heard
[p0142-l0015] [14.4pt] it.
[p0142-l0016] [14.4pt] Ask a clarifying question if you need to, such as “What recovery time
[p0142-l0017] [14.4pt] objective were you looking to achieve?” You can use the answer to improve
[p0142-l0018] [14.4pt] your response.
[p0142-l0019] [14.4pt] You can then give a concise answer, making sure not to show annoyance or
[p0142-l0020] [14.4pt] defensiveness. If you don’t have the answer, say that you will find out and get
[p0142-l0021] [14.4pt] back to them at a later time. Don’t forget to follow through on this promise.
[p0142-l0022] [14.4pt] Here are some further techniques for influencing and persuading peers,
[p0142-l0023] [14.4pt] stakeholders, and customers:
[p0142-l0024] [14.4pt] Reciprocity
[p0142-l0025] [14.4pt] People often feel an internal need to reciprocate generous gestures as well as
[p0142-l0026] [14.4pt] an external need to be seen repaying a debt. You can take advantage of the
[p0142-l0027] [14.4pt] expectation of reciprocity by being generous; for example, if you offer a free
[p0142-l0028] [14.4pt] trial of your software product, your customers might feel like they should
[p0142-l0029] [14.4pt] give something in return (subscribe or pay). If you offer to help your peer or
[p0142-l0030] [14.4pt] manager review their presentation, you give them reason to do you a favor in
[p0142-l0031] [14.4pt] the future.

=== PDF page 143 / printed label 143 ===
[p0143-l0001] [14.4pt] Thoughtful pauses
[p0143-l0002] [14.4pt] Many people feel uncomfortable with silence and will try to fill it. Pause and
[p0143-l0003] [14.4pt] you may find that silence filled either with useful information or agreement
[p0143-l0004] [14.4pt] with what you have said. Silence also gives your audience time to digest
[p0143-l0005] [14.4pt] what you have said. You can also use it to emphasize a point.
[p0143-l0006] [14.4pt] Give options
[p0143-l0007] [14.4pt] Instead of a yes or no (all or nothing) question, give options that will all
[p0143-l0008] [14.4pt] benefit you so that you do not mind which option is chosen. Many software
[p0143-l0009] [14.4pt] licensing models offer several tiers, giving options to customers. When
[p0143-l0010] [14.4pt] getting buy-in from stakeholders on your designs, you could offer two
[p0143-l0011] [14.4pt] options instead of one. This gives a sense of control to your audience or
[p0143-l0012] [14.4pt] recipient. Give only a few options; the more options someone has, the less
[p0143-l0013] [14.4pt] satisfied they are by their choice.
[p0143-l0014] [14.4pt] Repetition
[p0143-l0015] [14.4pt] The more someone hears or thinks something, the more they believe it to be
[p0143-l0016] [14.4pt] true. This is related to confirmation bias. Keep repeating what you want
[p0143-l0017] [14.4pt] others to believe—for example, about your abilities (to boost your
[p0143-l0018] [14.4pt] credibility) or the benefits of your software (to persuade customers). You can
[p0143-l0019] [14.4pt] build your position iteratively by introducing your position gently and
[p0143-l0020] [14.4pt] coming back to it again and again.
[p0143-l0021] [14.4pt] Cognitive reframing
[p0143-l0022] [14.4pt] Cognitive reframing means altering your mindset to look at something from
[p0143-l0023] [14.4pt] a different perspective in order to shift thinking and behavior.8 Use cognitive
[p0143-l0024] [14.4pt] reframing to take an event or situation (that might be unpleasant or
[p0143-l0025] [14.4pt] unsatisfactory) and guide others to think about it differently to move forward
[p0143-l0026] [14.4pt] or generate new ideas. For example, “We’ve found that serverless doesn’t
[p0143-l0027] [14.4pt] meet our needs, and it’s taken a long time to come to this conclusion, but
[p0143-l0028] [14.4pt] what have we learned that will help us to identify what does meet our
[p0143-l0029] [14.4pt] requirements?” Summarize the situation and encourage your audience to
[p0143-l0030] [14.4pt] think about potential next steps.

=== PDF page 144 / printed label 144 ===
[p0144-l0001] [14.4pt] Redefining
[p0144-l0002] [14.4pt] This is similar to reframing but leads your audience from what they are
[p0144-l0003] [14.4pt] concerned with to what you want them to focus on. For example, a
[p0144-l0004] [14.4pt] stakeholder may say, “That option will take a huge chunk of our budget,”
[p0144-l0005] [14.4pt] and you can counter with, “Yes, this option is costly, but that’s not the
[p0144-l0006] [14.4pt] priority in this situation. Our security and compliance requirements are not
[p0144-l0007] [14.4pt] fully met by the other options and would leave us open to attack and possibly
[p0144-l0008] [14.4pt] legal prosecution.”
[p0144-l0009] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0144-l0010] [10.8pt] Sometimes your efforts to influence and persuade won’t work because of underlying reasons
[p0144-l0011] [10.8pt] that you cannot control. A marking scheme when bidding for a contract may mean you don’t
[p0144-l0012] [10.8pt] get the highest score, despite your efforts. The decision maker may have set end-of-year
[p0144-l0013] [10.8pt] objectives that conflict with your position. You may be suggesting the right thing but at the
[p0144-l0014] [10.8pt] wrong time.
[p0144-l0015] [14.4pt] It is possible to combine many of the techniques in this section to become a
[p0144-l0016] [14.4pt] more convincing and highly regarded communicator, enhancing your scope of
[p0144-l0017] [14.4pt] influence in your organization and beyond. The next chapter discusses more
[p0144-l0018] [14.4pt] patterns for increasing your influence in the context of Aristotle’s rhetoric
[p0144-l0019] [14.4pt] triangle.
[p0144-l0020] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0144-l0021] [14.4pt] Both verbal and nonverbal communication are important even in individual
[p0144-l0022] [14.4pt] contributor roles (those without management duties). Enhancing these skills
[p0144-l0023] [14.4pt] with the techniques in this chapter will help set you apart from others when it
[p0144-l0024] [14.4pt] comes to recruitment, promotion, and avoiding redundancy.
[p0144-l0025] [14.4pt] You should have a formidable tool set for written, verbal, and nonverbal
[p0144-l0026] [14.4pt] communication from Chapters 7 and 8. Chapter 9 adds further techniques that
[p0144-l0027] [14.4pt] apply to all of your communication.

=== PDF page 145 / printed label 145 ===
[p0145-l0001] [10.8pt] 1 Ambady et al. “Toward a Histology of Social Behavior: Judgmental Accuracy from Thin Slices of
[p0145-l0002] [10.8pt] the Behavioral Stream,” Advances in Experimental Social Psychology 32 (2000): 201–71,
[p0145-l0003] [10.8pt] https://doi.org/10.1016/S0065-2601(00)80006-4.
[p0145-l0004] [10.8pt] 2 Danu Stinson et al., “Deconstructing the Reign of Error: Interpersonal Warmth Explains the Serl-

[p0145-l0006] [10.8pt] (July 2009), https://doi.org/10.1177/0146167209338629.

[p0145-l0008] [10.8pt] 4 I recommend reading these books on cognitive bias: Thinking, Fast and Slow by Daniel Kahneman,
[p0145-l0009] [10.8pt] The Art of Thinking Clearly by Rolf Dobelli (Harper Collins, 2013), and Nudge: The Final Edition by

[p0145-l0016] [10.8pt] may be useful when in meetings with senior people who don’t know you (such as C-suite executives).

=== PDF page 146 / printed label 146 ===
[p0146-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 9. The Rhetoric Triangle
[p0146-l0002] [14.4pt] You may have heard of Aristotle’s rhetoric triangle or its constituent terms
[p0146-l0003] [14.4pt] ethos, pathos, and logos. The rhetoric triangle was central to Aristotle’s
[p0146-l0004] [14.4pt] teachings on the art of persuasion and was a fundamental tool for effective
[p0146-l0005] [14.4pt] communication in ancient Greece. But how is it relevant to an architect or
[p0146-l0006] [14.4pt] developer today?
[p0146-l0007] [14.4pt] At its core, the rhetoric triangle is a framework for understanding the three key
[p0146-l0008] [14.4pt] elements of persuasive communication: ethos, logos, and pathos (see Figure 9-
[p0146-l0009] [14.4pt] 1). Ethos refers to the credibility and trustworthiness of the speaker, pathos to
[p0146-l0010] [14.4pt] the emotional appeal used to engage the audience, and logos to the logical and
[p0146-l0011] [14.4pt] rational argument presented. By mastering the balance and interplay between
[p0146-l0012] [14.4pt] these three elements, you effectively engage your audience and achieve your
[p0146-l0013] [14.4pt] desired outcome. Studying the rhetoric triangle was considered essential for
[p0146-l0014] [14.4pt] ancient Greek students and remains a valuable tool nearly 2,500 years later for
[p0146-l0015] [14.4pt] anyone looking to improve their ability to communicate persuasively.

=== PDF page 147 / printed label 147 ===
[p0147-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 9-1. Aristotle’s rhetoric triangle
[p0147-l0002] [20.16pt] Ethos
[p0147-l0003] [14.4pt] Ethos refers to the credibility, reliability, and trustworthiness of the speaker or
[p0147-l0004] [14.4pt] author. In verbal and written communication, ethos can play a significant role in
[p0147-l0005] [14.4pt] how the audience perceives the speaker or author and the message they are
[p0147-l0006] [14.4pt] communicating.
[p0147-l0007] [16.56pt] Establish Your Credentials
[p0147-l0008] [14.4pt] One way to use ethos in your speaking and writing is to communicate your
[p0147-l0009] [14.4pt] credibility to your audience. This can be done in various ways but will involve
[p0147-l0010] [14.4pt] highlighting your relevant qualifications, experience, awards, publications, and

=== PDF page 148 / printed label 148 ===
[p0148-l0001] [14.4pt] so forth.
[p0148-l0002] [14.4pt] Within your company, you typically do not need to communicate your credibility
[p0148-l0003] [14.4pt] directly—for example, with a credibility statement (as discussed in “Influence
[p0148-l0004] [14.4pt] and Persuasion”). Your job title communicates much of what your coworkers
[p0148-l0005] [14.4pt] need to know. Building credibility internally is more about the actions you take
[p0148-l0006] [14.4pt] and the more subtle things that you say, such as the outcomes of projects you
[p0148-l0007] [14.4pt] have worked on or the technologies or techniques you have read about or seen at
[p0148-l0008] [14.4pt] a conference.
[p0148-l0009] [14.4pt] It is important to avoid coming across as bragging or name-dropping. Talking
[p0148-l0010] [14.4pt] about approaches and outcomes is much more subtle than direct statements
[p0148-l0011] [14.4pt] about work on a product or at a company. The result of subtle statements should
[p0148-l0012] [14.4pt] be that colleagues form good opinions of you.
[p0148-l0013] [10.8pt] EXAMPLE
[p0148-l0014] [14.4pt] ESTABLISHING CREDENTIALS AT POLYGLOT MEDIA
[p0148-l0015] [14.4pt] When Nikki starts working at Polyglot Media as a software architect, she
[p0148-l0016] [14.4pt] doesn’t know any of her coworkers. Only her line manager knows anything
[p0148-l0017] [14.4pt] about her experience from her interview.
[p0148-l0018] [14.4pt] As she absorbs information from meetings and conversations, Nikki makes
[p0148-l0019] [14.4pt] relevant comments about her previous experience. For example, in a meeting
[p0148-l0020] [14.4pt] where a colleague mentions issues with scaling data stores, Nikki shares a
[p0148-l0021] [14.4pt] particular method she used to resolve something similar in a previous role
[p0148-l0022] [14.4pt] and offers to chat more. She also posts reading recommendations in a virtual
[p0148-l0023] [14.4pt] chat channel on other relevant topics.
[p0148-l0024] [14.4pt] As Nikki shares information, her new colleagues build a good impression of
[p0148-l0025] [14.4pt] her experience and knowledge. Nikki never has to express her credibility
[p0148-l0026] [14.4pt] formally.
[p0148-l0027] [14.4pt] When giving a presentation, you can establish your credentials with a biography
[p0148-l0028] [14.4pt] that you can deliver yourself or have someone use to introduce you. If presenting
[p0148-l0029] [14.4pt] at a conference or formal talk, the event’s website and literature would likely
[p0148-l0030] [14.4pt] also include this biography. You may want to repeat some of this information
[p0148-l0031] [14.4pt] when introducing yourself during the talk, if someone else introduced you.

=== PDF page 149 / printed label 149 ===
[p0149-l0001] [14.4pt] Whether you present publicly or not, it is a good idea to have a short biography
[p0149-l0002] [14.4pt] to use in your social media.1 This can be a headline about yourself and a
[p0149-l0003] [14.4pt] paragraph of information, a list of the things you do (separated by pipes), and so
[p0149-l0004] [14.4pt] on.
[p0149-l0005] [14.4pt] Credentials aren’t limited to academic qualifications. As a life-long learner in the
[p0149-l0006] [14.4pt] technology arena, you might also include the following credentials in a
[p0149-l0007] [14.4pt] biography:
[p0149-l0008] [14.4pt] Professional qualifications
[p0149-l0009] [14.4pt] Blog posts you have authored
[p0149-l0010] [14.4pt] User groups, meet-ups, or conferences you have spoken at
[p0149-l0011] [14.4pt] Mentorships you have participated in (as mentor or mentee)
[p0149-l0012] [14.4pt] Groups or events you run or organize
[p0149-l0013] [14.4pt] Open source projects you contribute to
[p0149-l0014] [14.4pt] Volunteer work you do
[p0149-l0015] [14.4pt] Recommendations and testimonials from others
[p0149-l0016] [14.4pt] Honors, awards, and competition wins
[p0149-l0017] [14.4pt] Publications you have contributed to
[p0149-l0018] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0149-l0019] [10.8pt] If you are networking, either 1:1 or in a small group, you can establish your credentials with a
[p0149-l0020] [10.8pt] short introduction you prepare and rehearse in advance. What impression do you want to
[p0149-l0021] [10.8pt] make? The answer will help you design an effective introduction.
[p0149-l0022] [14.4pt] You can also communicate credibility in some forms of writing. A blog post or
[p0149-l0023] [14.4pt] article typically has a short author biography. A book usually has an author
[p0149-l0024] [14.4pt] biography inside and on the back cover, establishing ethos as it is perused in a
[p0149-l0025] [14.4pt] bookshop. When browsing books online, the buyer is often greeted by a short
[p0149-l0026] [14.4pt] biography or introduction along with a description of the book.

=== PDF page 150 / printed label 150 ===
[p0150-l0001] [16.56pt] Use Trustworthy Sources
[p0150-l0002] [14.4pt] Another way to establish credibility is to use trustworthy sources for your
[p0150-l0003] [14.4pt] information and to cite those sources. This has the added benefit of enabling
[p0150-l0004] [14.4pt] your audience to find out more. Giving them more value can also improve their
[p0150-l0005] [14.4pt] impression of you.
[p0150-l0006] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0150-l0007] [10.8pt] When citing sources, it is easy to come across as saying, “I have read and interpreted these
[p0150-l0008] [10.8pt] sources for you so you don’t need to bother,” which can sound patronizing. To avoid this, you
[p0150-l0009] [10.8pt] can tie in personal experiences or anecdotes with a sprinkling of quotes, statistics, or
[p0150-l0010] [10.8pt] references. You can also title your citations “Further Reading” rather than “References.” Show
[p0150-l0011] [10.8pt] you are open to challenge and collaboration.
[p0150-l0012] [14.4pt] Trustworthy sources include academic journals, reputable experts in the field,
[p0150-l0013] [14.4pt] government agencies, and more; for example:
[p0150-l0014] [14.4pt] Books (physical or ebooks) written by respected authors and published by
[p0150-l0015] [14.4pt] reputable publishers
[p0150-l0016] [14.4pt] News articles from well-established newspapers or news websites
[p0150-l0017] [14.4pt] Professional organizations, such as trade associations or professional
[p0150-l0018] [14.4pt] societies
[p0150-l0019] [14.4pt] Online databases of academic and scholarly articles, as well as historical
[p0150-l0020] [14.4pt] documents and data, such as ProQuest or JSTOR
[p0150-l0021] [14.4pt] Government agencies that publish reports and whitepapers, such as the
[p0150-l0022] [14.4pt] Office of National Statistics in the UK
[p0150-l0023] [14.4pt] Papers or slide presentations presented at conferences or academic events
[p0150-l0024] [14.4pt] Blogs or articles written by reputable people
[p0150-l0025] [14.4pt] Your own experiences and those of credible people you know
[p0150-l0026] [14.4pt] TIP

=== PDF page 151 / printed label 151 ===
[p0151-l0001] [10.8pt] Using a variety of sources to back up your message is another way to add credibility. If more
[p0151-l0002] [10.8pt] than one trustworthy source supports your message or argument, your audience will have more
[p0151-l0003] [10.8pt] confidence in what you say or write.
[p0151-l0004] [14.4pt] To cite your sources in presentations, you can add footnotes to the appropriate
[p0151-l0005] [14.4pt] slides and name your sources as you speak. This is preferable to an appendix in a
[p0151-l0006] [14.4pt] slide deck because you want your audience to sense your credibility as you
