[p0151-l0007] [14.4pt] speak, not afterward (and they might not read slide handouts).
[p0151-l0008] [14.4pt] In writing, you can also make citations in footnotes or, as with speaking, in the
[p0151-l0009] [14.4pt] text itself (whether in the main text or parentheses like these). If your writing
[p0151-l0010] [14.4pt] appears online, it is good practice to link directly to any online sources.
[p0151-l0011] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0151-l0012] [10.8pt] When citing online sources, you are referencing content that can change, whereas a published
[p0151-l0013] [10.8pt] book or scientific paper will remain the same. To avoid outdated citations, you can use tools
[p0151-l0014] [10.8pt] such as the Wayback Machine. To cite a preserved version of your source (as long as it allows
[p0151-l0015] [10.8pt] crawlers), visit the Wayback Machine and enter the address under Save Page Now. Update
[p0151-l0016] [10.8pt] your citation with the address to your preserved web page in the archive. Archive.is is a similar
[p0151-l0017] [10.8pt] alternative service.
[p0151-l0018] [14.4pt] Anecdotes and real-life stories are effective ways to demonstrate your ideas,
[p0151-l0019] [14.4pt] designs, and warnings. People accept narratives much more easily than facts and
[p0151-l0020] [14.4pt] bullet points, so use stories as examples when you can to create a bigger impact
[p0151-l0021] [14.4pt] than you would with references to studies, books, and so on.2
[p0151-l0022] [16.56pt] Be Transparent
[p0151-l0023] [14.4pt] Being transparent about your motivations, biases, and conflicts of interest can
[p0151-l0024] [14.4pt] help build trust with the audience. You may think that you would need to declare
[p0151-l0025] [14.4pt] an interest only if you stand to gain from it, but transparency is also about
[p0151-l0026] [14.4pt] explaining your perspective to your audience so that they understand where you
[p0151-l0027] [14.4pt] are coming from. Transparency can enhance credibility by, for example,
[p0151-l0028] [14.4pt] revealing how you or someone close to you has been affected by the subject you
[p0151-l0029] [14.4pt] are talking about.

=== PDF page 152 / printed label 152 ===
[p0152-l0001] [14.4pt] Transparency is more applicable when communicating with those outside your
[p0152-l0002] [14.4pt] company but can still be important within your company. For example, you
[p0152-l0003] [14.4pt] should make it clear if you worked for a company that you are now
[p0152-l0004] [14.4pt] recommending or marking in a procurement process.
[p0152-l0005] [14.4pt] When speaking or writing, you should communicate your motivations to your
[p0152-l0006] [14.4pt] audience. Once they know why you are trying to communicate something to
[p0152-l0007] [14.4pt] them, they can use this information to determine the amount of trust to put in
[p0152-l0008] [14.4pt] you and the credibility of your message.
[p0152-l0009] [14.4pt] Citations can also improve transparency. In addition to citing sources that back
[p0152-l0010] [14.4pt] up your views, cite sources that do not. Doing so communicates that you think
[p0152-l0011] [14.4pt] your audience is intelligent enough to form their own opinion and reinforces
[p0152-l0012] [14.4pt] your confidence in your position; you think the audience will still agree with you
[p0152-l0013] [14.4pt] even if they consume an opposing opinion.
[p0152-l0014] [14.4pt] Whether spoken or written, stating your motivations may be a good introduction
[p0152-l0015] [14.4pt] or attention-getter. They might also impress or further engage your audience
[p0152-l0016] [14.4pt] later as a dramatic reveal. Imagine revealing that the protagonist or villain of
[p0152-l0017] [14.4pt] your narrative is yourself or someone close to you. That would dramatically
[p0152-l0018] [14.4pt] reveal your motivations.
[p0152-l0019] [14.4pt] It is highly important to communicate any biases or conflicts of interest to your
[p0152-l0020] [14.4pt] audience. If they know or find out later that you have an incentive, financial or
[p0152-l0021] [14.4pt] otherwise, to argue a certain way, then they will lose trust in you.
[p0152-l0022] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0152-l0023] [10.8pt] Declaring a conflict of interest is often a legal requirement in business, finance, and politics, so
[p0152-l0024] [10.8pt] be very careful to abide by the law.
[p0152-l0025] [14.4pt] One way to declare a bias or conflict of interest is up front in your introduction
[p0152-l0026] [14.4pt] or early on. This is a good way to make sure that your audience doesn’t get any
[p0152-l0027] [14.4pt] incorrect ideas about how you fit in with your message or argument before
[p0152-l0028] [14.4pt] revealing your connection. Otherwise, they may feel they have already been
[p0152-l0029] [14.4pt] misled, and you will lose their trust.

=== PDF page 153 / printed label 153 ===
[p0153-l0001] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0153-l0002] [10.8pt] Avoiding a conflict of interest may be better than having to declare it. The audience may
[p0153-l0003] [10.8pt] decide that you are too biased for them to accept what you say. Would someone else be better
[p0153-l0004] [10.8pt] placed to write this blog post or give this presentation?
[p0153-l0005] [14.4pt] If you have several topics or subsections in your writing or speaking, but your
[p0153-l0006] [14.4pt] bias or conflict of interest applies to only one or a group, you may want to reveal
[p0153-l0007] [14.4pt] this just before you speak or write about the relevant topic. Your audience will
[p0153-l0008] [14.4pt] then know exactly what your bias or conflict of interest applies to. It is
[p0153-l0009] [14.4pt] especially important in a written piece, as your audience might skip to the topic
[p0153-l0010] [14.4pt] and miss your declaration if it were at the beginning.
[p0153-l0011] [14.4pt] You could also communicate bias via a disclaimer or in your biography. A
[p0153-l0012] [14.4pt] disclaimer could appear in your content (such as a statement about earning
[p0153-l0013] [14.4pt] commission from clicks on product links in your blog posts) or in your
[p0153-l0014] [14.4pt] biography (for example, declaring who you or a close family member works
[p0153-l0015] [14.4pt] for).
[p0153-l0016] [14.4pt] BIAS AND CONFLICT OF INTEREST
[p0153-l0017] [14.4pt] You may need to declare a bias or conflict of interest about a wide range of
[p0153-l0018] [14.4pt] things, including the following:
[p0153-l0019] [14.4pt] Employment or employment relationship
[p0153-l0020] [14.4pt] If you have a current or former employment relationship with an
[p0153-l0021] [14.4pt] organization, company, or individual that is relevant to the topic you are
[p0153-l0022] [14.4pt] discussing, you need to declare it. For example, if you are promoting a
[p0153-l0023] [14.4pt] product and work for the company that makes it, you need to declare
[p0153-l0024] [14.4pt] your employment relationship.
[p0153-l0025] [14.4pt] Financial interests
[p0153-l0026] [14.4pt] If you have financial interests that could be impacted by the topic you are
[p0153-l0027] [14.4pt] discussing, you need to declare them. For example, if you own stocks in
[p0153-l0028] [14.4pt] a company that is involved in a bid you are working on, you need to
[p0153-l0029] [14.4pt] declare it.

=== PDF page 154 / printed label 154 ===
[p0154-l0001] [14.4pt] Personal relationships
[p0154-l0002] [14.4pt] If you have a personal relationship with someone or an organization that
[p0154-l0003] [14.4pt] is relevant to the topic you are discussing, you need to declare it. For
[p0154-l0004] [14.4pt] example, if you are presenting a topic that is relevant to a friend’s
[p0154-l0005] [14.4pt] business, you need to declare your relationship with them.
[p0154-l0006] [14.4pt] Ideological or political bias
[p0154-l0007] [14.4pt] If you have a strong ideological or political bias that could impact the
[p0154-l0008] [14.4pt] impartiality of your presentation or writing, you need to declare it. For
[p0154-l0009] [14.4pt] example, if you are discussing a political topic and have a strong partisan
[p0154-l0010] [14.4pt] bias, you need to declare it.
[p0154-l0011] [14.4pt] Professional association
[p0154-l0012] [14.4pt] If you belong to a professional association that could be relevant to the
[p0154-l0013] [14.4pt] topic you are discussing, you need to declare it. For example, if you are a
[p0154-l0014] [14.4pt] member of an association such as Sherwood Applied Business Security
[p0154-l0015] [14.4pt] Architecture (SABSA) and you are discussing security frameworks, you
[p0154-l0016] [14.4pt] need to declare it.
[p0154-l0017] [16.56pt] Demonstrate Your Knowledge
[p0154-l0018] [14.4pt] Demonstrating a deep understanding of the topic can help establish credibility
[p0154-l0019] [14.4pt] and show that you are an expert in the field. One way to demonstrate knowledge
[p0154-l0020] [14.4pt] is to use specific examples or case studies to support your message or argument,
[p0154-l0021] [14.4pt] showing how you have applied your knowledge in practice. It is one thing to
[p0154-l0022] [14.4pt] quote your theories to your audience but quite another to show your theories
[p0154-l0023] [14.4pt] working in the real world.
[p0154-l0024] [14.4pt] When using technical terms, make sure to use them correctly. A technical review
[p0154-l0025] [14.4pt] of your writing or slide deck could help make sure you haven’t mixed up your
[p0154-l0026] [14.4pt] frameworks and your libraries or written the wrong abbreviation or acronym.
[p0154-l0027] [14.4pt] Explaining complex concepts on a level that your audience can understand
[p0154-l0028] [14.4pt] demonstrates your ability to translate technical information and shows that you

=== PDF page 155 / printed label 155 ===
[p0155-l0001] [14.4pt] understand the topic deeply enough to break it into easy-to-understand terms.
[p0155-l0002] [14.4pt] Trying to make yourself look clever by bamboozling your audience with
[p0155-l0003] [14.4pt] technical terminology and concepts will only lead to miscommunication, which
[p0155-l0004] [14.4pt] could in turn lead to making the wrong decisions.
[p0155-l0005] [14.4pt] As a technical professional, you know the importance of keeping your skills and
[p0155-l0006] [14.4pt] knowledge up-to-date to do your job well. Demonstrate that knowledge to your
[p0155-l0007] [14.4pt] audience by discussing developments in the field and the impacts on your work.
[p0155-l0008] [14.4pt] Showing a commitment to ongoing learning and professional development
[p0155-l0009] [14.4pt] reveals the type of person you are. Dropping references to, and examples of, up-
[p0155-l0010] [14.4pt] to-date information into your conversations, talks, slide decks, and writing will
[p0155-l0011] [14.4pt] show that you are keeping abreast of current topics.
[p0155-l0012] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0155-l0013] [10.8pt] If you have only a superficial knowledge of a topic, it will likely come across. When you don’t
[p0155-l0014] [10.8pt] have much knowledge of a subject, asking questions and making comparisons may be better
[p0155-l0015] [10.8pt] than trying to look like you know more.
[p0155-l0016] [14.4pt] Conferences and user groups aren’t just ways to keep your knowledge up-to-
[p0155-l0017] [14.4pt] date; they also improve your ethos and demonstrate knowledge. By participating
[p0155-l0018] [14.4pt] in relevant events and networking with others, you will absorb more knowledge
[p0155-l0019] [14.4pt] of new developments and trends and build your personal brand. Other people
[p0155-l0020] [14.4pt] who attend will see that you are improving yourself. You can also refer to and
[p0155-l0021] [14.4pt] cite talks you’ve attended or conversations you’ve had. Events, both in person
[p0155-l0022] [14.4pt] and online, can be a good source of anecdotes for your speaking and writing.
[p0155-l0023] [14.4pt] Online conferences and meetups are examples (and can be great places to start
[p0155-l0024] [14.4pt] presenting), as are sites such as LinkedIn and Dribbble.
[p0155-l0025] [20.16pt] Pathos
[p0155-l0026] [14.4pt] Pathos refers to an emotional appeal, which can be used to connect with the
[p0155-l0027] [14.4pt] audience. In verbal and written communication, pathos can play a significant
[p0155-l0028] [14.4pt] role in making a message more memorable, relatable, and persuasive.

=== PDF page 156 / printed label 156 ===
[p0156-l0001] [16.56pt] Tell a Story
[p0156-l0002] [14.4pt] Telling a story can be an effective way to make a message more relatable and
[p0156-l0003] [14.4pt] memorable, while also connecting with the audience emotionally. You may think
[p0156-l0004] [14.4pt] that stories don’t fit in with your technical writing or speaking, but they can
[p0156-l0005] [14.4pt] capture your audience’s attention, keep that attention, communicate concepts,
[p0156-l0006] [14.4pt] and more (see Chapter 4 for more on narrative). User stories often include
[p0156-l0007] [14.4pt] personas, which provide a background story.
[p0156-l0008] [14.4pt] You can use stories to provide real-world examples that support your message.
[p0156-l0009] [14.4pt] Tell how another company has used the technology you are proposing to create a
[p0156-l0010] [14.4pt] successful product or improve its deployment pipelines. Use the story to
[p0156-l0011] [14.4pt] illustrate the benefits and show how shortcomings have been overcome.
[p0156-l0012] [14.4pt] Stories can also help explain complex concepts. Like a longer version of an
[p0156-l0013] [14.4pt] analogy, a story can clarify and simplify an idea. A well-crafted story can help
[p0156-l0014] [14.4pt] the audience better understand the technical concepts by putting them in a
[p0156-l0015] [14.4pt] relatable and memorable context.
[p0156-l0016] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0156-l0017] [10.8pt] For good examples of using storytelling in presentations, watch some TED Talks. They cover a
[p0156-l0018] [10.8pt] huge variety of topics, including technical and software topics. Many are short and easy to fit
[p0156-l0019] [10.8pt] into a small gap in your busy day.
[p0156-l0020] [14.4pt] You can also use stories to give context and background to your message. By
[p0156-l0021] [14.4pt] setting the stage for the technical details with a narrative, the audience is better
[p0156-l0022] [14.4pt] able to understand the significance and relevance of those details. Why are you
[p0156-l0023] [14.4pt] communicating with your audience? Are you solving a problem? How did that
[p0156-l0024] [14.4pt] problem present itself? How does it affect your audience or others? Once you
[p0156-l0025] [14.4pt] have given your audience context, move on to the technical details and how you
[p0156-l0026] [14.4pt] will solve the problem.
[p0156-l0027] [14.4pt] Storytelling builds empathy. Share stories that highlight the challenges and
[p0156-l0028] [14.4pt] struggles of users, developers, and other stakeholders. In this way, you humanize
[p0156-l0029] [14.4pt] your technical information and make it more relatable to the audience.
[p0156-l0030] [14.4pt] Your stories should engage and inspire. Illustrate your message’s affect on the

=== PDF page 157 / printed label 157 ===
[p0157-l0001] [14.4pt] world and the business to motivate your audience to take the action that you
[p0157-l0002] [14.4pt] want them to take (such as signing off on a decision, approving a request for
[p0157-l0003] [14.4pt] funding, or buying your product). Stories are a valuable tool in getting what you
[p0157-l0004] [14.4pt] need from your audience.
[p0157-l0005] [14.4pt] Here are a few types of stories you can use:
[p0157-l0006] [14.4pt] Success stories can demonstrate how a particular solution or technology
[p0157-l0007] [14.4pt] was successfully implemented by another team or company. These stories
[p0157-l0008] [14.4pt] can build credibility by showing that your recommendations are based on
[p0157-l0009] [14.4pt] experience and a track record.
[p0157-l0010] [14.4pt] Failure stories might discuss how a project or solution failed and offer
[p0157-l0011] [14.4pt] lessons learned from the experience. You can use this type of story to steer
[p0157-l0012] [14.4pt] your audience away from a solution, or as the basis for a lessons-learned
[p0157-l0013] [14.4pt] session. Communicate why the failure happened so that your audience is
[p0157-l0014] [14.4pt] clear on this. See “Lessons Learned at Polyglot Media” for an example of a
[p0157-l0015] [14.4pt] failure story.
[p0157-l0016] [14.4pt] Use case scenarios describe a typical situation in which the technology or
[p0157-l0017] [14.4pt] solution would be used. You can use a use case scenario to help your
[p0157-l0018] [14.4pt] audience visualize how the technology would be applied in a real-world
[p0157-l0019] [14.4pt] situation (preferably the situation you want to apply it to, such as within
[p0157-l0020] [14.4pt] your business).
[p0157-l0021] [14.4pt] Clarity stories explain why a decision was made. You can structure these in
[p0157-l0022] [14.4pt] four parts:
[p0157-l0023] [14.4pt] a. What happened in the past
[p0157-l0024] [14.4pt] b. The turning point where change was needed
[p0157-l0025] [14.4pt] c. What will be done now
[p0157-l0026] [14.4pt] d. What will happen in the future
[p0157-l0027] [10.8pt] EXAMPLE
[p0157-l0028] [14.4pt] LESSONS LEARNED AT POLYGLOT MEDIA
[p0157-l0029] [14.4pt] The main software system at Polyglot Media was once a big ball of mud:

=== PDF page 158 / printed label 158 ===
[p0158-l0001] [14.4pt] one monolithic system that had grown organically and sat on top of one huge
[p0158-l0002] [14.4pt] relational database. The Polyglot Media system was prone to bottlenecks,
[p0158-l0003] [14.4pt] and responsiveness was becoming an issue for customers. Development
[p0158-l0004] [14.4pt] teams struggled to squash all the bugs and get new features into production
[p0158-l0005] [14.4pt] within a reasonable time window.
[p0158-l0006] [14.4pt] The architects at the time, Vlad and Libby, had learned from reading and
[p0158-l0007] [14.4pt] conferences that jumping to a microservices architecture was not a silver
[p0158-l0008] [14.4pt] bullet. After a lot of research and prototyping, they decided serverless would
[p0158-l0009] [14.4pt] meet their needs, along with polyglot persistence (multiple types of
[p0158-l0010] [14.4pt] databases). It was a huge undertaking, but the monolith was broken into
[p0158-l0011] [14.4pt] many small functions, and the relational database became eight smaller data
[p0158-l0012] [14.4pt] stores of varying types.
[p0158-l0013] [14.4pt] To avoid having to pause the development of new functionality, they used
[p0158-l0014] [14.4pt] the strangler fig methodology to migrate to the new system. At first, the
[p0158-l0015] [14.4pt] development process seemed a lot easier; developers could quickly identify
[p0158-l0016] [14.4pt] where they needed to make changes and deploy only code that had been
[p0158-l0017] [14.4pt] changed. But teams started having problems coordinating changes with one
[p0158-l0018] [14.4pt] another, causing merge problems and breaking changes between functions.
[p0158-l0019] [14.4pt] Although serverless yielded cost savings on resources, it turned out that
[p0158-l0020] [14.4pt] functions were talking to many other functions, creating a spiderweb of
[p0158-l0021] [14.4pt] dependencies (an antipattern known as serverless pinball). The system was
[p0158-l0022] [14.4pt] distributed, but still tightly coupled, and the problem with responsiveness
[p0158-l0023] [14.4pt] had not been solved either. Polyglot Media now had a distributed ball of
[p0158-l0024] [14.4pt] mud.
[p0158-l0025] [14.4pt] Libby and Vlad were already working on solving the new and preexisting
[p0158-l0026] [14.4pt] problems when Nikki joined Polyglot Media to bolster the architecture team.
[p0158-l0027] [14.4pt] She brought new ideas about team topologies and agreed that reducing
[p0158-l0028] [14.4pt] coupling as much as possible was a good idea.3
[p0158-l0029] [14.4pt] The transition is still an ongoing process, but the architecture team realized
[p0158-l0030] [14.4pt] that, like microservices, serverless was not a silver bullet. Functions were
[p0158-l0031] [14.4pt] too small and needed to talk to too many other functions for it to work for
[p0158-l0032] [14.4pt] the whole Polyglot Media system. The development teams have become a
[p0158-l0033] [14.4pt] prototype for applying team topologies in Polyglot Media, and the inverse

=== PDF page 159 / printed label 159 ===
[p0159-l0001] [14.4pt] Conway maneuver has been applied to support the composing of functions
[p0159-l0002] [14.4pt] into larger services that are maintained by one cross-functional team per
[p0159-l0003] [14.4pt] service.4 Services and data stores are being composed based on
[p0159-l0004] [14.4pt] dependencies, the need for scaling, and other integrators and disintegrators.
[p0159-l0005] [14.4pt] An event-based architecture using queues is being rolled out, to reduce the
[p0159-l0006] [14.4pt] level of coupling between services and increase responsiveness.5 The first
[p0159-l0007] [14.4pt] responsiveness bottleneck tackled using this approach was media activity
[p0159-l0008] [14.4pt] logging, which was hogging required resources in the media service.
[p0159-l0009] [16.56pt] Speak from the Heart
[p0159-l0010] [14.4pt] In verbal or written communication speaking from the heart means being
[p0159-l0011] [14.4pt] authentic and sincere. It involves connecting with your audience on an emotional
[p0159-l0012] [14.4pt] level and conveying your message with passion and conviction.
[p0159-l0013] [14.4pt] An effective method of connecting with your audience emotionally is one we’ve
[p0159-l0014] [14.4pt] already discussed: telling personal stories. Stories are important to humans, and
[p0159-l0015] [14.4pt] sharing one that is personal to you but also relevant to your message will
[p0159-l0016] [14.4pt] demonstrate your authenticity while connecting emotionally. The story doesn’t
[p0159-l0017] [14.4pt] need to have involved you directly but should have a connection to you. Do not
[p0159-l0018] [14.4pt] make something up and pass it off as true. That is a sure way to lose credibility
[p0159-l0019] [14.4pt] with your audience.
[p0159-l0020] [14.4pt] You can use your voice, body language, and writing to show emotion and
[p0159-l0021] [14.4pt] communicate vulnerability. By showing your audience how you feel about your
[p0159-l0022] [14.4pt] message or argument, or showing them how you have been affected, they will
[p0159-l0023] [14.4pt] feel a stronger connection to both you and your views. The strongest messages
[p0159-l0024] [14.4pt] are those that show a personal vulnerability or failure, as opposed to those that
[p0159-l0025] [14.4pt] make you feel or look good.
[p0159-l0026] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0159-l0027] [10.8pt] Some people put on a pretense and try to be someone they are not in an attempt to convey their
[p0159-l0028] [10.8pt] message. Audiences are likely to see through this and question your authenticity. Be yourself,
[p0159-l0029] [10.8pt] speak in your own voice, and avoid using scripted or rehearsed lines that are not natural to you
[p0159-l0030] [10.8pt] to make that connection with your audience.

=== PDF page 160 / printed label 160 ===
[p0160-l0001] [14.4pt] Another method is to use concrete examples or anecdotes such as case studies or
[p0160-l0002] [14.4pt] interesting use cases. These bring your message to life and make it easier for
[p0160-l0003] [14.4pt] your audience to relate to what you are saying or writing. Use this technique
[p0160-l0004] [14.4pt] along with a display of emotion to really pack a punch.
[p0160-l0005] [14.4pt] You can practice active listening to help connect with your audience emotionally
[p0160-l0006] [14.4pt] during a conversation, meeting, or talk in front of an audience. You must be fully
[p0160-l0007] [14.4pt] present, not daydreaming, glancing at your phone, or checking email on your
[p0160-l0008] [14.4pt] laptop. When you show that you are actively listening, you convey the sincerity
[p0160-l0009] [14.4pt] of your message and show that you care about what they are saying.
[p0160-l0010] [14.4pt] To show you are actively listening, try the following:
[p0160-l0011] [14.4pt] 1. Listen in full, without interrupting.
[p0160-l0012] [14.4pt] 2. Ask clarifying questions once they have finished.
[p0160-l0013] [14.4pt] 3. Summarize their view to them, and let them correct anything you have
[p0160-l0014] [14.4pt] misunderstood.
[p0160-l0015] [14.4pt] 4. Decide what you need to do and how to respond.
[p0160-l0016] [14.4pt] Consider implementing these additional active listening tips:
[p0160-l0017] [14.4pt] Make listening noises (uh huh, mmm, and so forth) and use listening body
[p0160-l0018] [14.4pt] language (head nods, eye contact, and so on).
[p0160-l0019] [14.4pt] Don’t fill their silence; wait for them to continue.
[p0160-l0020] [14.4pt] Read their body language.
[p0160-l0021] [14.4pt] Mirror (copy) their body language.
[p0160-l0022] [14.4pt] Spot inconsistencies, such as saying “yes” while frowning.
[p0160-l0023] [16.56pt] Use Vivid Language and Strong Imagery
[p0160-l0024] [14.4pt] Using vivid language and strong imagery can help paint a picture in the
[p0160-l0025] [14.4pt] audience’s mind and evoke emotions, adding to the emotional appeal of your
[p0160-l0026] [14.4pt] message or argument and also making it more memorable.
[p0160-l0027] [14.4pt] One technique is to use sensory language—language that appeals to the five

=== PDF page 161 / printed label 161 ===
[p0161-l0001] [14.4pt] senses (sight, sound, smell, touch, and taste). As your audience experiences your
[p0161-l0002] [14.4pt] message through each of their senses, it becomes more real and far more
[p0161-l0003] [14.4pt] memorable. When presenting, you can use sound and visuals along with sensory
[p0161-l0004] [14.4pt] language.
[p0161-l0005] [14.4pt] Metaphors, similes, and analogies, which compare one thing to something else,
[p0161-l0006] [14.4pt] are also useful tools. When communicating with stakeholders, you may wish to
[p0161-l0007] [14.4pt] convey a strong idea, feeling, or complex concept, and using a metaphor, simile,
[p0161-l0008] [14.4pt] or analogy is an effective approach. Consider the context and audience to make
[p0161-l0009] [14.4pt] sure that your comparison makes sense and communicates the idea that you
[p0161-l0010] [14.4pt] intend.
[p0161-l0011] [14.4pt] METAPHORS, SIMILES, AND ANALOGIES
[p0161-l0012] [14.4pt] Metaphors compare something the audience doesn’t know with something
[p0161-l0013] [14.4pt] they do know by saying that something is something else. For example, the
[p0161-l0014] [14.4pt] development team is a well-oiled machine.
[p0161-l0015] [14.4pt] Similes compare two dissimilar things by saying that something is like
[p0161-l0016] [14.4pt] something else. For example, the connections between microservices are like
[p0161-l0017] [14.4pt] a spiderweb or are as tangled as a spiderweb.
[p0161-l0018] [14.4pt] Analogies compare two things to make a point, but also explain the
[p0161-l0019] [14.4pt] comparison. For example, maintaining software is like taking care of a car.
[p0161-l0020] [14.4pt] You need to keep up with regular maintenance tasks to keep the software
[p0161-l0021] [14.4pt] running smoothly and fix any problems that arise before they become bigger
[p0161-l0022] [14.4pt] issues.
[p0161-l0023] [14.4pt] Visual aids are another method you can employ. When speaking, you can use
[p0161-l0024] [14.4pt] visuals on slides, including videos and animations, but also physical props to
[p0161-l0025] [14.4pt] demonstrate a concept. Humans remember visuals better than spoken or verbal
[p0161-l0026] [14.4pt] ones, so using photos, videos, GIFs, or other visuals is more effective than just
[p0161-l0027] [14.4pt] words.
[p0161-l0028] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0161-l0029] [10.8pt] Physical props can be even more effective than animations or videos when presenting to an
[p0161-l0030] [10.8pt] audience. One of the most memorable talks I can think of, by Jules May, used physical balls

=== PDF page 162 / printed label 162 ===
[p0162-l0001] [10.8pt] and sticks to explain how a quantum computer works.
[p0162-l0002] [14.4pt] Other useful tools for creating vivid language and strong imagery include the
[p0162-l0003] [14.4pt] following:
[p0162-l0004] [14.4pt] Personification
[p0162-l0005] [14.4pt] Giving human characteristics to nonhuman things can increase empathy with
[p0162-l0006] [14.4pt] your audience: the server was so overloaded that it was begging for a break.
[p0162-l0007] [14.4pt] Hyperbole
[p0162-l0008] [14.4pt] Exaggerated language can evoke strong emotions in your audience: this code
[p0162-l0009] [14.4pt] is older than the internet!
[p0162-l0010] [14.4pt] Strong action words
[p0162-l0011] [14.4pt] Strong active verbs engage your audience in many ways, keeping attention
[p0162-l0012] [14.4pt] but also communicating what you really mean (for example, implement,
[p0162-l0013] [14.4pt] build, test, debug, optimize, integrate…) instead of weak action verbs (for
[p0162-l0014] [14.4pt] example, work, make, show, try…). See “Strong Verbs”.
[p0162-l0015] [14.4pt] Emotional words
[p0162-l0016] [14.4pt] Words that have strong emotional connotations can elicit emotions in the
[p0162-l0017] [14.4pt] audience and make them more likely to rise to your call to action (such as
[p0162-l0018] [14.4pt] life-changing, transformative, innovative, cutting-edge, phenomenal,
[p0162-l0019] [14.4pt] unmatched, and unrivaled).
[p0162-l0020] [20.16pt] Logos
[p0162-l0021] [14.4pt] Logos refers to an appeal to reason and logic that makes a message more
[p0162-l0022] [14.4pt] convincing. In verbal and written communication, logos can play a significant
[p0162-l0023] [14.4pt] role in building a strong argument and making a message more persuasive.
[p0162-l0024] [16.56pt] Use Data and Facts
[p0162-l0025] [14.4pt] When presenting your message or argument, you should use data or facts to

=== PDF page 163 / printed label 163 ===
[p0163-l0001] [14.4pt] support your views. You could include concrete data from reliable research
[p0163-l0002] [14.4pt] studies, statistics published by trustworthy sources, or historical facts and
[p0163-l0003] [14.4pt] figures.
[p0163-l0004] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0163-l0005] [10.8pt] Make sure that any data or facts that you base your views on are from credible and reliable
[p0163-l0006] [10.8pt] sources, or that you state any biases, limitations, or problems with your sources (see “Establish
[p0163-l0007] [10.8pt] Your Credentials”).
[p0163-l0008] [14.4pt] When stating facts or data, cite your sources. When speaking, you may want to
[p0163-l0009] [14.4pt] just state the facts or data, but showing the statement or an illustration of the
[p0163-l0010] [14.4pt] statement at the same time as stating it can add a huge impact when using visuals
[p0163-l0011] [14.4pt] such as slides.
[p0163-l0012] [14.4pt] In writing, you can state facts and data within text or slides, add data or citations
[p0163-l0013] [14.4pt] in footnotes (see Example 9-1), or refer to them in examples, illustrations, or
[p0163-l0014] [14.4pt] appendixes. If the amount of data is large, including an appendix is a good way
[p0163-l0015] [14.4pt] to avoid breaking the flow of the main text.
[p0163-l0016] [14.4pt] Example 9-1. Using footnotes to cite the source of data in the main text
[p0163-l0017] [10.8pt] A journal article published in 2020 proposes the creation of a "Phish Scale" to
[p0163-l0018] [10.8pt] rate the difficulty of phishing training exercises.¹ As a company, we can...
[p0163-l0019] [10.8pt] ---
[p0163-l0020] [10.8pt] [1] Michelle Steves et. al, "Categorizing Human Phishing Difficulty: A Phish
[p0163-l0021] [10.8pt] Scale," Journal of Cybersecurity, 6, no. 1. 2020,
[p0163-l0022] [10.8pt] https://doi.org/10.1093/cybsec/tyaa009
[p0163-l0023] [14.4pt] Presenting data and facts can help support your argument and increase the
[p0163-l0024] [14.4pt] validity of your message.
[p0163-l0025] [16.56pt] Make Logical Connections
[p0163-l0026] [14.4pt] Making logical connections between points can help build a strong argument and
[p0163-l0027] [14.4pt] show that your message is reasoned. Besides using varied sources of reliable
[p0163-l0028] [14.4pt] information as a basis for your message or argument, you should make
[p0163-l0029] [14.4pt] connections between them, and between the topics or points you’re describing.
[p0163-l0030] [14.4pt] This is a similar concept to connecting data points on a graph to show a pattern.

=== PDF page 164 / printed label 164 ===
[p0164-l0001] [14.4pt] By making these connections, you show your thought processes and that the
[p0164-l0002] [14.4pt] basis for your message or argument is not just disparate data points, but a
[p0164-l0003] [14.4pt] network of supporting information. It’s like completing a dot-to-dot puzzle for
[p0164-l0004] [14.4pt] the audience so that they don’t have to.
[p0164-l0005] [14.4pt] The connections you offer must make logical sense to your audience; otherwise,
[p0164-l0006] [14.4pt] they will lose faith in your message or even in you. You can do this in several
[p0164-l0007] [14.4pt] ways:
[p0164-l0008] [14.4pt] Organize your content in a structured and logical manner to help your
[p0164-l0009] [14.4pt] audience understand the connection between ideas and how they relate to
[p0164-l0010] [14.4pt] each other.
[p0164-l0011] [14.4pt] Use transition words and phrases to connect ideas and show relationships.
[p0164-l0012] [14.4pt] For example, therefore, consequently, and in conclusion can be used to
[p0164-l0013] [14.4pt] show the logical connections between ideas.
[p0164-l0014] [14.4pt] Give examples to illustrate the logical connections between ideas and make
[p0164-l0015] [14.4pt] content more relatable. Narrative and storytelling can be employed here.
[p0164-l0016] [14.4pt] Use visuals such as diagrams and flowcharts to illustrate logical
[p0164-l0017] [14.4pt] connections between ideas and make content easier to understand. In a slide
[p0164-l0018] [14.4pt] deck, you can add animations and transitions to show connections,
[p0164-l0019] [14.4pt] alongside other visuals.
[p0164-l0020] [16.56pt] Use Reasoning and Argumentation
[p0164-l0021] [14.4pt] Using clear reasoning and argumentation can show that your message is rational.
[p0164-l0022] [14.4pt] It is one thing to present your audience with the data to support your views, but
[p0164-l0023] [14.4pt] that data also requires explanation. Your audience needs to understand your
[p0164-l0024] [14.4pt] reasoning.
[p0164-l0025] [14.4pt] When justifying important choices such as architectural decisions, your
[p0164-l0026] [14.4pt] reasoning is important. A couple of techniques are particularly useful here:
[p0164-l0027] [14.4pt] Trade-off analysis
[p0164-l0028] [14.4pt] When comparing options for a solution, each always has pros and con. By
[p0164-l0029] [14.4pt] communicating these to your audience, you can show the benefits of the
[p0164-l0030] [14.4pt] choice made and the trade-offs that have been accepted as a consequence.

=== PDF page 165 / printed label 165 ===
[p0165-l0001] [14.4pt] You also show how the other options considered were not the best option.
[p0165-l0002] [14.4pt] ADRs
[p0165-l0003] [14.4pt] ADRs can be a good place to document your trade-off analysis but also
[p0165-l0004] [14.4pt] communicate more of your reasoning and argumentation. ADRs allow you to
[p0165-l0005] [14.4pt] document a decision with its options and consequences and can be a method
[p0165-l0006] [14.4pt] for getting input before a decision is made. In writing, you can include a link
[p0165-l0007] [14.4pt] to the full ADR, and in speaking, you can talk about the ADR or show it in
[p0165-l0008] [14.4pt] parts in a slide deck. Example 9-2 shows how to structure an ADR, created
[p0165-l0009] [14.4pt] from personal experience and the experiences of others passed on to me (for
[p0165-l0010] [14.4pt] a full example of an ADR, see “ADRs”).
[p0165-l0011] [14.4pt] Example 9-2. ADR structure
[p0165-l0012] [10.8pt] # Identifier & Title - a statement of the decision made
[p0165-l0013] [10.8pt] ## Status
[p0165-l0014] [10.8pt] Draft/Decided/Superseded by ADR-XXX
[p0165-l0015] [10.8pt] ## Context
[p0165-l0016] [10.8pt] Why you need to make the decision. Assumptions, constraints, and decision drivers.
[p0165-l0017] [10.8pt] ## Evaluation Criteria
[p0165-l0018] [10.8pt] What is important to you in making this decision?
[p0165-l0019] [10.8pt] Which of your architectural characteristics apply to making this decision?
[p0165-l0020] [10.8pt] Should any constraints or decision drivers become a criterion?
[p0165-l0021] [10.8pt] ## Options
[p0165-l0022] [10.8pt] Outlines of the options considered against the evaluation criteria (usually using a
[p0165-l0023] [10.8pt] score or rating), and trade-offs outside of the evaluation criteria.
[p0165-l0024] [10.8pt] ## Decision
[p0165-l0025] [10.8pt] The choice that was made and why.
[p0165-l0026] [10.8pt] ## Implications
[p0165-l0027] [10.8pt] The positive and negative consequences of the decision made.
[p0165-l0028] [10.8pt] ## Consultation
[p0165-l0029] [10.8pt] If taking input from others, they should document it here. Details of those invited
[p0165-l0030] [10.8pt] to give input can be recorded, whether they provide input or not. Although
[p0165-l0031] [10.8pt] consultation takes place before a decision is made, it is documented at the end
[p0165-l0032] [10.8pt] because it can become long and obscure the decision itself.
[p0165-l0033] [14.4pt] These techniques are also useful for addressing counterarguments and

=== PDF page 166 / printed label 166 ===
[p0166-l0001] [14.4pt] responding to objections. By explaining your reasoning, you demonstrate a
[p0166-l0002] [14.4pt] thorough understanding of the topic and increase the credibility of your message.
[p0166-l0003] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0166-l0004] [10.8pt] Consider objections and counterarguments that your audience may have and be prepared to
[p0166-l0005] [10.8pt] respond using reasoning and argumentation. You will not be able to predict all the issues that
[p0166-l0006] [10.8pt] are brought up, but being prepared for most of them will give you the thinking space for any
[p0166-l0007] [10.8pt] surprises.
[p0166-l0008] [14.4pt] ADRs and trade-off analyses highlight counterarguments or issues to your
[p0166-l0009] [14.4pt] audience before someone else can bring them up. Preempting counterarguments
[p0166-l0010] [14.4pt] strengthens your arguments by showing why alternative solutions were
[p0166-l0011] [14.4pt] discounted. ADRs have value long after the decision in question is made. They
[p0166-l0012] [14.4pt] are the story of the decision, which can be retold however many times and to
[p0166-l0013] [14.4pt] whoever needs to understand it.
[p0166-l0014] [14.4pt] In writing, you could respond to counterarguments by using the previously
[p0166-l0015] [14.4pt] mentioned trade-off analyses and ADRs. Another option is to include a
[p0166-l0016] [14.4pt] frequently asked questions (FAQ) list with your own counterarguments to
[p0166-l0017] [14.4pt] questions and objections that you predict. This has the added advantage of not
[p0166-l0018] [14.4pt] having readers ask the same question of you over and over again.
[p0166-l0019] [14.4pt] When speaking, you should script and rehearse answers to the objections that
[p0166-l0020] [14.4pt] you predict. How much preparation you put into this will depend on how
[p0166-l0021] [14.4pt] important the outcome is to you.
[p0166-l0022] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0166-l0023] [10.8pt] Objections can be subjective. An objection such as “it is too slow to implement” may prompt
[p0166-l0024] [10.8pt] you to revisit your decision criteria, but it could also represent a difference of opinion.
[p0166-l0025] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0166-l0026] [14.4pt] You now have techniques to increase your trustworthiness and the value of what
[p0166-l0027] [14.4pt] you communicate. Did you think, before you read this chapter, that knowledge

=== PDF page 167 / printed label 167 ===
[p0167-l0001] [14.4pt] from over two thousand years ago could be so helpful to you today? Recording
[p0167-l0002] [14.4pt] and sharing knowledge has been key to developing human civilization, and yet it
[p0167-l0003] [14.4pt] is not given priority when it comes to architecting and writing software.
[p0167-l0004] [14.4pt] The word documentation often causes people to involuntarily shudder or show
[p0167-l0005] [14.4pt] some similar form of body language (which you may pick up on better now that
[p0167-l0006] [14.4pt] you’ve read Part II). Part III will help you feel differently, reduce this reaction in
[p0167-l0007] [14.4pt] others, and curb anxiety over how to document, what to document, and how to
[p0167-l0008] [14.4pt] create a reserve of knowledge that is useful and accessible.
[p0167-l0009] [10.8pt] 1 You may want to disassociate any nonprofessional social media accounts from professional ones.
[p0167-l0010] [10.8pt] For example, add a professional biography to your LinkedIn account, but give your Mastodon
[p0167-l0011] [10.8pt] account (where you post about personal topics) a completely different biography.
[p0167-l0012] [10.8pt] 2 See Chapter 4 for more on narrative.
[p0167-l0013] [10.8pt] 3 See Team Topologies for further information.
[p0167-l0014] [10.8pt] 4 Conway’s law states that “any organization that designs a system (defined broadly) will produce a
[p0167-l0015] [10.8pt] design whose structure is a copy of the organization’s communication structure.” The idea of the
[p0167-l0016] [10.8pt] inverse Conway maneuver is to create your team and communication structure in the way you want to
[p0167-l0017] [10.8pt] design your system so that the system the teams produce will be as designed.
[p0167-l0018] [10.8pt] 5 See Figure 12-3 for the ADR for this decision.

=== PDF page 168 / printed label 168 ===
[p0168-l0001] [28.8pt] Part III. Communicating
[p0168-l0002] [28.8pt] Knowledge
[p0168-l0003] [14.4pt] Most people think that knowledge management in organizations and technical
[p0168-l0004] [14.4pt] teams means documentation, but that is only part of what you need to
[p0168-l0005] [14.4pt] communicate knowledge. Your team and organization’s collective knowledge,
[p0168-l0006] [14.4pt] including knowledge about your product or project, must be managed and
[p0168-l0007] [14.4pt] guided to remain up-to-date, available, and accessible to the right people.
[p0168-l0008] [14.4pt] In Part III, you will learn the overarching principles of good knowledge
[p0168-l0009] [14.4pt] development and management. Properly capturing and maintaining knowledge,
[p0168-l0010] [14.4pt] and leveraging people, will enable you to get the best return on your investment
[p0168-l0011] [14.4pt] in knowledge and documentation.
[p0168-l0012] [14.4pt] Chapter 10 will take you through some high-level patterns and principles to
[p0168-l0013] [14.4pt] enhance your knowledge management and documentation. Chapter 11 gives you
[p0168-l0014] [14.4pt] patterns and techniques to leverage people to improve knowledge development
[p0168-l0015] [14.4pt] and management. Chapter 12 has patterns and methods for making the
[p0168-l0016] [14.4pt] architecture practices that you use for knowledge management more effective so
[p0168-l0017] [14.4pt] that you aren’t just going through the motions without reaping the benefits.
[p0168-l0018] [14.4pt] Apply the patterns, principles, and practices in Part III to your team, department,
[p0168-l0019] [14.4pt] and organization to optimize your collective knowledge and improve
[p0168-l0020] [14.4pt] productivity and innovation.

=== PDF page 169 / printed label 169 ===
[p0169-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 10. Knowledge
[p0169-l0002] [28.8pt] Management Principles
[p0169-l0003] [14.4pt] When creating software architecture, you adhere to principles both explicitly and
[p0169-l0004] [14.4pt] implicitly. Your company may have explicit principles that you need to comply
[p0169-l0005] [14.4pt] with, such as security and architecture principles or coding patterns and
[p0169-l0006] [14.4pt] principles, or you may be following a well-architected framework such as those
[p0169-l0007] [14.4pt] from Azure, Amazon Web Services (AWS), or Google Cloud Platform (GCP).1
[p0169-l0008] [14.4pt] Other principles are implicit and may not be adhered to uniformly or even well-
[p0169-l0009] [14.4pt] known. These tend to be individuals’ lessons learned.
[p0169-l0010] [14.4pt] This chapter discusses principles for improving your knowledge management
[p0169-l0011] [14.4pt] and documentation, which I hope you will make explicit in your work.
[p0169-l0012] [20.16pt] Products over Projects
[p0169-l0013] [14.4pt] You have probably noticed that many companies organize their people and work
[p0169-l0014] [14.4pt] around projects. Budgets are allocated to projects, and knowledge management
[p0169-l0015] [14.4pt] is based on projects as well. But projects are transitory—a lot more transitory
[p0169-l0016] [14.4pt] than products, which often have contributions from more than one project.
[p0169-l0017] [14.4pt] What happens to all the knowledge associated with your project when it ends? If
[p0169-l0018] [14.4pt] documentation is organized by project, that knowledge is likely difficult to find
[p0169-l0019] [14.4pt] and easily forgotten or even lost. But if your documentation is organized by
[p0169-l0020] [14.4pt] product, you should be able to find the knowledge easily and reference or reuse
[p0169-l0021] [14.4pt] it in other projects. This is a major difference between a project mindset and a
[p0169-l0022] [14.4pt] product mindset.
[p0169-l0023] [16.56pt] Project Mindset
[p0169-l0024] [14.4pt] During the initial development of a product or capability, you will collect many
[p0169-l0025] [14.4pt] artifacts that contain key information, including high-level requirements, domain
[p0169-l0026] [14.4pt] analysis, ADRs, and other important knowledge.

=== PDF page 170 / printed label 170 ===
[p0170-l0001] [14.4pt] Now imagine a new project is created for a new iteration of this product. Then
[p0170-l0002] [14.4pt] after that, another project. If your knowledge is stored by project, you will have
[p0170-l0003] [14.4pt] several problems:
[p0170-l0004] [14.4pt] Finding the documentation
[p0170-l0005] [14.4pt] At the very least, you will need to find the documentation for the previous
[p0170-l0006] [14.4pt] project(s). Depending on how documentation is stored at your company, this
[p0170-l0007] [14.4pt] could take anywhere from a few minutes to several weeks.
[p0170-l0008] [14.4pt] Knowing whether prior documentation exists
[p0170-l0009] [14.4pt] The team working on the new project may not know if documentation exists
[p0170-l0010] [14.4pt] from the past project(s) or even if a previous project existed. This is
[p0170-l0011] [14.4pt] particularly common beyond the second iteration of the product. How do you
[p0170-l0012] [14.4pt] know there is knowledge to be found?
[p0170-l0013] [14.4pt] Imagine your new project is changing your product, but the team has no
[p0170-l0014] [14.4pt] information as to why certain decisions were made, what the original
[p0170-l0015] [14.4pt] requirements were, how the CI/CD pipeline works, and so on. Because of this
[p0170-l0016] [14.4pt] lack of information, the new project will likely break something without the
[p0170-l0017] [14.4pt] team realizing, be that code- or requirement-based.
[p0170-l0018] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0170-l0019] [10.8pt] Some knowledge and documentation are pertinent to only a particular project. This type of
[p0170-l0020] [10.8pt] information should still be stored in line with the product; you never know when it may be
[p0170-l0021] [10.8pt] relevant. You don’t want to be hunting around for it when needed.
[p0170-l0022] [16.56pt] Product Mindset
[p0170-l0023] [14.4pt] Switching to a product mindset and organizing knowledge by product improves
[p0170-l0024] [14.4pt] the discoverability of documentation. Teams working on new projects can find
[p0170-l0025] [14.4pt] all prior documentation about the product they are working on.
[p0170-l0026] [14.4pt] Besides improved discoverability, organizing knowledge by product has other
[p0170-l0027] [14.4pt] advantages:
[p0170-l0028] [14.4pt] Long-term focus

=== PDF page 171 / printed label 171 ===
[p0171-l0001] [14.4pt] A product-centric approach encourages thinking beyond the immediate
[p0171-l0002] [14.4pt] project and focusing on a long-term view. This can mean the software is
[p0171-l0003] [14.4pt] designed to better meet changing needs. In a project mindset, the focus is
[p0171-l0004] [14.4pt] more on meeting deadlines. You may end up working on the next project, so
[p0171-l0005] [14.4pt] you can thank yourself later for planning for the long term.
[p0171-l0006] [14.4pt] Collaboration and reusability
[p0171-l0007] [14.4pt] Collaboration and reusability improve with a product mindset, especially
[p0171-l0008] [14.4pt] when there is more than one project or team working concurrently on a
[p0171-l0009] [14.4pt] product, but also across sequential projects and product portfolios. People
[p0171-l0010] [14.4pt] working on other products or in other parts of the business can more easily
[p0171-l0011] [14.4pt] reference knowledge that is organized by product rather than multiple
[p0171-l0012] [14.4pt] projects.
[p0171-l0013] [14.4pt] Consistency across products and projects
[p0171-l0014] [14.4pt] Templates and standards can emerge from best practices when everyone has
[p0171-l0015] [14.4pt] visibility into products, including those they are not directly working on.
[p0171-l0016] [14.4pt] This leads to consistency across products and projects, reducing both the
[p0171-l0017] [14.4pt] learning curve when people move between them and the costs through
[p0171-l0018] [14.4pt] solution and tool reuse.
[p0171-l0019] [14.4pt] Visibility
[p0171-l0020] [14.4pt] Organizing knowledge by product gives a holistic view of a product, rather
[p0171-l0021] [14.4pt] than blinkered snapshots (or duplication of artifacts), which is what you get
[p0171-l0022] [14.4pt] from a project-centered approach. With a holistic view, you can better see the
[p0171-l0023] [14.4pt] impact of changes and identify areas for improvement.
[p0171-l0024] [14.4pt] Customer focus
[p0171-l0025] [14.4pt] Focusing on the product also lends focus to the customer who uses the
[p0171-l0026] [14.4pt] product. Knowledge of customer needs does not get lost when a project ends;
[p0171-l0027] [14.4pt] it gets picked up by all projects or teams working on the product.
[p0171-l0028] [14.4pt] Continuous improvement
[p0171-l0029] [14.4pt] A product-centric approach encourages continuous improvement and
[p0171-l0030] [14.4pt] evolution of software over time because of the holistic nature of the

=== PDF page 172 / printed label 172 ===
[p0172-l0001] [14.4pt] information. It is easier to identify areas for improvement and make
[p0172-l0002] [14.4pt] incremental changes over time.
[p0172-l0003] [14.4pt] The way you go about organizing knowledge by product will depend on the tools
[p0172-l0004] [14.4pt] available to you, the size of your company, and other factors, but the key is to
[p0172-l0005] [14.4pt] have a consistent and easily accessible method so that all relevant information is
[p0172-l0006] [14.4pt] available, up-to-date, and easy to maintain.
[p0172-l0007] [14.4pt] Organizing knowledge artifacts by product doesn’t mean you lose the link to
[p0172-l0008] [14.4pt] projects. Take a look at Figure 10-1 to see some artifacts organized by product.
[p0172-l0009] [14.4pt] They are also referenced by projects and can be referenced by more than one
[p0172-l0010] [14.4pt] project where required to create a constellation of artifacts. “Abstractions over
[p0172-l0011] [14.4pt] Text” will go further into referencing and reuse.
[p0172-l0012] [10.8pt] Figure 10-1. Knowledge artifacts organized by product and referenced by projects
[p0172-l0013] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0172-l0014] [10.8pt] When considering the structure and classification of documentation, you should integrate
[p0172-l0015] [10.8pt] relevant concepts and standards used across your organization, such as internal product IDs or
[p0172-l0016] [10.8pt] terminology from an ISO accreditation.

=== PDF page 173 / printed label 173 ===
[p0173-l0001] [14.4pt] Here are some suggestions for methods of organizing knowledge by product:
[p0173-l0002] [14.4pt] Centralize documentation
[p0173-l0003] [14.4pt] Create a centralized documentation portal to ensure that all knowledge is
[p0173-l0004] [14.4pt] stored together, and organize this by product. If any knowledge cannot be
[p0173-l0005] [14.4pt] stored there,2 ensure that the documentation portal links to it, and link to the
[p0173-l0006] [14.4pt] portal from the external documentation too, if possible.
[p0173-l0007] [14.4pt] Tag
[p0173-l0008] [14.4pt] Folders are an outdated system because they force an artifact to belong in
[p0173-l0009] [14.4pt] only one place. An artifact can have multiple categories and therefore can
[p0173-l0010] [14.4pt] have multiple tags. You can add tags to group artifacts by product. You can
[p0173-l0011] [14.4pt] then add other tags, such as the project name or the artifact type, to group
[p0173-l0012] [14.4pt] artifacts in other ways. Tags can be used in many knowledge management
[p0173-l0013] [14.4pt] systems available at the moment. Avoid systems that allow only folders and
[p0173-l0014] [14.4pt] hierarchies.
[p0173-l0015] [14.4pt] Use folders and hierarchies strategically
[p0173-l0016] [14.4pt] If you are using folders, or don’t have the option to use tags, organize by
[p0173-l0017] [14.4pt] product at the highest level possible to gain the most holistic view of your
[p0173-l0018] [14.4pt] products. If you have to organize your products under another level of
[p0173-l0019] [14.4pt] folders, such as by department, you lose that overview.
[p0173-l0020] [14.4pt] Use metadata
[p0173-l0021] [14.4pt] Metadata is information that describes other data, such as the content of a
[p0173-l0022] [14.4pt] web page, a document, or image. Tags are a common type of metadata, but
[p0173-l0023] [14.4pt] should be used along with other types where possible. If you use tags to
[p0173-l0024] [14.4pt] describe every aspect related to your artifact (products, projects, type of
[p0173-l0025] [14.4pt] artifact, author, and so forth), it can become a big mess. If you have metadata
[p0173-l0026] [14.4pt] that specifically states these pieces of information as keys and values, the
[p0173-l0027] [14.4pt] information is much clearer. Many knowledge management applications,
[p0173-l0028] [14.4pt] wikis, or file systems, such as Confluence, Microsoft SharePoint, and
[p0173-l0029] [14.4pt] Obsidian, either use metadata behind the scenes or allow you to add and edit

=== PDF page 174 / printed label 174 ===
[p0174-l0001] [14.4pt] it. (See Example 10-1.)
[p0174-l0002] [14.4pt] Use a perspective-driven approach
[p0174-l0003] [14.4pt] Organize documentation into perspectives that address a stakeholder concern
[p0174-l0004] [14.4pt] (see “Perspective-Driven Documentation” for more on perspectives).
[p0174-l0005] [14.4pt] Example 10-1. YAML metadata
[p0174-l0006] [10.8pt] ---
[p0174-l0007] [10.8pt] product: "My Cool Product"
[p0174-l0008] [10.8pt] author: "Kate"
[p0174-l0009] [10.8pt] project: "Project Trilby"
[p0174-l0010] [10.8pt] type: "requirements"
[p0174-l0011] [10.8pt] tags:
[p0174-l0012] [10.8pt] - tag1
[p0174-l0013] [10.8pt] - tag2
[p0174-l0014] [10.8pt] ---
[p0174-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0174-l0016] [10.8pt] If you are working on a project that spans products, you can still apply product over project by
[p0174-l0017] [10.8pt] using tags and/or a perspective-driven approach. At a basic level, you can create a project
[p0174-l0018] [10.8pt] dashboard that links to knowledge in each product involved. This could be a page in
[p0174-l0019] [10.8pt] Confluence, links in a Markdown document, a SharePoint page, or links to files in a folder.
[p0174-l0020] [20.16pt] Abstractions over Text
[p0174-l0021] [14.4pt] When communicating about software architecture, presenting information
[p0174-l0022] [14.4pt] visually while abstracting some of the detail is usually more effective than
[p0174-l0023] [14.4pt] presenting paragraphs of text. Even if all your information could be text, it isn’t
[p0174-l0024] [14.4pt] the most effective way to communicate. Visuals cannot, and should not, replace
[p0174-l0025] [14.4pt] all textual information, but humans process visuals and text very differently.
[p0174-l0026] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0174-l0027] [10.8pt] Accommodating all of your audience’s needs in documentation can be hard. Even when your
[p0174-l0028] [10.8pt] audience is quite narrow, individuals can have different objectives (such as learning from
[p0174-l0029] [10.8pt] scratch or refreshing their knowledge). Making detailed content optional, along with
[p0174-l0030] [10.8pt] abstractions, can help. See “Tips for Technical Documents” for more tips on documentation

=== PDF page 175 / printed label 175 ===
[p0175-l0001] [10.8pt] content.
[p0175-l0002] [16.56pt] Lists
[p0175-l0003] [14.4pt] Some information is most effectively presented in bullet points or an ordered
[p0175-l0004] [14.4pt] (numbered) list. List items are useful for summarizing information. They can be
[p0175-l0005] [14.4pt] short and to the point, pushing important ideas to the forefront.
[p0175-l0006] [14.4pt] Readers will often read lists even if they scan the rest of the content. Lists can
[p0175-l0007] [14.4pt] also persuade the reader to read the paragraphs around them. The space around
[p0175-l0008] [14.4pt] list items also provides readers with a visual break, encouraging them to read on,
[p0175-l0009] [14.4pt] which can be useful if you need to communicate a lot of information (such as in
[p0175-l0010] [14.4pt] a whitepaper) and keep your reader interested.
[p0175-l0011] [14.4pt] Here are some tips for using lists:
[p0175-l0012] [14.4pt] Start with your most important point. If your reader doesn’t finish reading
[p0175-l0013] [14.4pt] your list or is scanning it, they should still consume your most important
[p0175-l0014] [14.4pt] statement.
[p0175-l0015] [14.4pt] Begin each list item with the same part of speech for smooth reading (such
[p0175-l0016] [14.4pt] as all nouns, verbs, or adjectives). It doesn’t really matter which part of
[p0175-l0017] [14.4pt] speech as long as it is the same.
[p0175-l0018] [14.4pt] Use numbers only when order is important—for example, to indicate steps
[p0175-l0019] [14.4pt] in a process or recipe, or to count. The reader will ascribe meaning to
[p0175-l0020] [14.4pt] numbers even if there is none.
[p0175-l0021] [14.4pt] Aim for each list item to be around the same length as the others in the list.
[p0175-l0022] [14.4pt] Variation stands out as a distraction.
[p0175-l0023] [14.4pt] Emphasize the first statement or sentence when a list item is a few
[p0175-l0024] [14.4pt] sentences long or when the first statement is being expanded upon (for
[p0175-l0025] [14.4pt] example, by italicizing it) so it stands out for a reader who is scanning.
[p0175-l0026] [14.4pt] Limit each list item to a few sentences. Any more than that and you should
[p0175-l0027] [14.4pt] consider either summarizing to make it shorter or using paragraphs instead.
[p0175-l0028] [14.4pt] Add extra space around list items if you have more than two. Negative
[p0175-l0029] [14.4pt] space is a visual break, which readers need more when a list is long.

=== PDF page 176 / printed label 176 ===
[p0176-l0001] [14.4pt] Use consistent capitalization, punctuation, and grammar for list items.
[p0176-l0002] [14.4pt] Failure to do so will make it harder for your reader to consume the
[p0176-l0003] [14.4pt] information.
[p0176-l0004] [14.4pt] Use sublists carefully because they can become visually confusing. Sublists
[p0176-l0005] [14.4pt] should do the following:
[p0176-l0006] [14.4pt] Abide by the same rules as the main list
[p0176-l0007] [14.4pt] Use a different symbol or numbering system than the main list, and/or
[p0176-l0008] [14.4pt] be obviously indented
[p0176-l0009] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0176-l0010] [10.8pt] You may be thinking that it would be nice to see some examples of these tips on lists. Look
[p0176-l0011] [10.8pt] more carefully at the preceding list; it follows all the advice given, except for emphasizing the
[p0176-l0012] [10.8pt] first statement (which is done using italics with indentation elsewhere in this book when the
[p0176-l0013] [10.8pt] list items are more than a couple of sentences long).
[p0176-l0014] [16.56pt] Tables
[p0176-l0015] [14.4pt] Tables are a useful way to present information that would take a lot of paragraph
[p0176-l0016] [14.4pt] text to explain, isn’t appropriate to make into a diagram, or is relational. They
[p0176-l0017] [14.4pt] are also an alternative to including a chart or graph. You may not be restricted to
[p0176-l0018] [14.4pt] using a simple table with text and could highlight data by using color and/or
[p0176-l0019] [14.4pt] pattern to add to or emphasize your message.
[p0176-l0020] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0176-l0021] [10.8pt] Tables are inappropriate when the information could be quickly summarized or when the table
[p0176-l0022] [10.8pt] would require too much explanation in accompanying text to be efficient. Tables are good for
[p0176-l0023] [10.8pt] displaying repeated information; use paragraphs for irregular (nonrepetitive) content.
[p0176-l0024] [14.4pt] Tables are efficient in the following situations:
[p0176-l0025] [14.4pt] Presenting data with details that would be lost in a chart or graph
[p0176-l0026] [14.4pt] Showing exact values, which would not be seen on a chart or graph

=== PDF page 177 / printed label 177 ===
[p0177-l0001] [14.4pt] Comparing data points, because your reader can do a side-by-side analysis
[p0177-l0002] [14.4pt] Considering accessibility, when graphs and charts may not be readable by
[p0177-l0003] [14.4pt] some of your audience
[p0177-l0004] [14.4pt] Here are some examples of appropriate table use in software architecture:
[p0177-l0005] [14.4pt] Requirements traceability matrix
[p0177-l0006] [14.4pt] Connecting requirements to the component that satisfies them
[p0177-l0007] [14.4pt] Component interface specification
[p0177-l0008] [14.4pt] Documenting the interfaces between design components
[p0177-l0009] [14.4pt] Performance metrics report
[p0177-l0010] [14.4pt] Showing data such as response time, resource utilization, and so on
[p0177-l0011] [14.4pt] Testing matrix
[p0177-l0012] [14.4pt] Documenting testing scenarios and results for each component or subsystem
[p0177-l0013] [14.4pt] Stakeholder analysis matrix
[p0177-l0014] [14.4pt] Documenting stakeholders along with their interests, concerns, and so on
[p0177-l0015] [14.4pt] Communicating metrics
[p0177-l0016] [14.4pt] Displaying metrics in groups or multiple units
[p0177-l0017] [14.4pt] Here are some tips for creating tables:
[p0177-l0018] [14.4pt] Introduce the table with a sentence that gives the audience context, ending
[p0177-l0019] [14.4pt] with a colon.
[p0177-l0020] [14.4pt] Give columns meaningful and concise headers that visually contrast with
[p0177-l0021] [14.4pt] the table content (such as using bold or a larger font). Apply this to row
[p0177-l0022] [14.4pt] headers where needed.
[p0177-l0023] [14.4pt] Restrict each column to one type of data (such as price or country).
[p0177-l0024] [14.4pt] Limit table cells to two sentences (consider using a list instead if you need

=== PDF page 178 / printed label 178 ===
[p0178-l0001] [14.4pt] to include more information).
[p0178-l0002] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0178-l0003] [10.8pt] Remember that your audience may access your documentation in different ways. A table that is
[p0178-l0004] [10.8pt] easy to consume on a typical computer monitor may not work well on a tablet, phone, or even
[p0178-l0005] [10.8pt] laptop.
[p0178-l0006] [16.56pt] Visual Abstractions
[p0178-l0007] [14.4pt] One reason for using visuals is to abstract away information to make your
[p0178-l0008] [14.4pt] message easier to consume. You might use a star rating alongside a numerical
[p0178-l0009] [14.4pt] score, for example.
[p0178-l0010] [14.4pt] Stars are usually used to show an integer score of 0 to 5 (as in Figure 10-2) but
[p0178-l0011] [14.4pt] can be adapted to show a floating-point score (for example, 3.6/5) by showing
[p0178-l0012] [14.4pt] partial stars, or show a score out of 10 by representing each number as half a
[p0178-l0013] [14.4pt] star. Showing more than five stars isn’t effective as it is hard to see the numerical
[p0178-l0014] [14.4pt] meaning at a glance.
[p0178-l0015] [10.8pt] Figure 10-2. Star rating of four out of five
[p0178-l0016] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0178-l0017] [10.8pt] The stars in Figure 10-2 are designed to meet WCAG guidelines for nontext contrast. Notice
[p0178-l0018] [10.8pt] that the border on the colored stars is thicker than on the empty (white) star. This is to ensure
[p0178-l0019] [10.8pt] that anyone who cannot see the difference between yellow (or gray) and white can still see that
[p0178-l0020] [10.8pt] four stars are highlighted. For more information, see the WCAG Non-text Contrast guidelines.
[p0178-l0021] [14.4pt] Harvey balls (shown in Figure 10-3) are another method of visually abstracting a
[p0178-l0022] [14.4pt] score or five-point scale. They are commonly used to show the degree to which
[p0178-l0023] [14.4pt] something meets a criterion, so they can be a useful tool when evaluating options
[p0178-l0024] [14.4pt] (for example, in an ADR). An important benefit to Harvey balls is that you use

=== PDF page 179 / printed label 179 ===
[p0179-l0001] [14.4pt] only one to communicate a value. This makes them much easier to fit into a table
[p0179-l0002] [14.4pt] or restricted space than five stars. Bear in mind that determining which color is
[p0179-l0003] [14.4pt] representing which data can be confusing. To make the meaning less ambiguous,
[p0179-l0004] [14.4pt] fill the ball in a clockwise direction (as shown in Figure 10-3) and make sure
[p0179-l0005] [14.4pt] that Harvey balls are used consistently across your organization.
[p0179-l0006] [10.8pt] Figure 10-3. Harvey balls
[p0179-l0007] [14.4pt] Traffic lights are a common way of representing negative, neutral, and positive
[p0179-l0008] [14.4pt] information. When using red, amber, and green, ensure that you either place
[p0179-l0009] [14.4pt] them in the same position as an actual traffic light (in the UK and US, this is red
[p0179-l0010] [14.4pt] at the top and green at the bottom) or use another symbol along with the color
[p0179-l0011] [14.4pt] (such as a + on the green and a – on the red) so that the meaning is still
[p0179-l0012] [14.4pt] communicated if the reader is color vision deficient or the colors are represented
[p0179-l0013] [14.4pt] in grayscale.
[p0179-l0014] [16.56pt] Word Clouds
[p0179-l0015] [14.4pt] Word clouds are another method for presenting text and can be more efficient at
[p0179-l0016] [14.4pt] communicating your message than paragraphs of text or tables of data. A word
[p0179-l0017] [14.4pt] cloud is a collection of words drawn from a particular source and depicted in
[p0179-l0018] [14.4pt] different sizes (and usually colors). The larger (and possibly bolder) the word,
[p0179-l0019] [14.4pt] the more often it appears in the source text. The source text is typically free text,
[p0179-l0020] [14.4pt] such as open questions from market research, but could also be answers to a
[p0179-l0021] [14.4pt] multiple-choice question (in which case a word’s prominence signifies the
[p0179-l0022] [14.4pt] number of times an answer was chosen).
[p0179-l0023] [14.4pt] In software architecture, the most common way to use word clouds is in a
[p0179-l0024] [14.4pt] presentation, but they can be embedded in other documents too. One example
[p0179-l0025] [14.4pt] might be to illustrate your chosen architectural characteristics and emphasize the
[p0179-l0026] [14.4pt] most important ones (see “Architecture Characteristics” for more on these).
[p0179-l0027] [14.4pt] Some interactive presentation software even allows for creating word clouds on
[p0179-l0028] [14.4pt] the fly, with your audience typing in answers and seeing them almost instantly

=== PDF page 180 / printed label 180 ===
[p0180-l0001] [14.4pt] on your presentation screen. This can be highly engaging, whether creating them
[p0180-l0002] [14.4pt] spontaneously or beforehand. It is easy to see trends and important words, and
[p0180-l0003] [14.4pt] they are useful to a reader who is scanning a document too.
[p0180-l0004] [14.4pt] Figure 10-4 was created using text from the Wikipedia entry on tag (word)
[p0180-l0005] [14.4pt] clouds. It is general practice to remove common words (such as the, a, as, and
[p0180-l0006] [14.4pt] if). Many generators offer display options like color, shape, and spacing.3
[p0180-l0007] [10.8pt] Figure 10-4. A word cloud, generated at https://simplewordcloud.com
[p0180-l0008] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0180-l0009] [10.8pt] An interactive word cloud is a good way to make requirements solicitation more engaging or
[p0180-l0010] [10.8pt] to present open-ended data collected from users or stakeholders. If you were aiming to get
[p0180-l0011] [10.8pt] stakeholder buy-in for a change to a product, a word cloud that illustrates rationale for the
[p0180-l0012] [10.8pt] change (such as a summary of customer complaints) would be a good persuasive tool.
[p0180-l0013] [16.56pt] Charts, Graphs, and Diagrams
[p0180-l0014] [14.4pt] You have likely heard the phrase a picture is worth a thousand words. Charts,
[p0180-l0015] [14.4pt] graphs, and diagrams are great ways to summarize information and can often
[p0180-l0016] [14.4pt] communicate a message without accompanying text at all (except labels and
[p0180-l0017] [14.4pt] titles). For example, a graph with an obvious trend and a headline stating what

=== PDF page 181 / printed label 181 ===
[p0181-l0001] [14.4pt] you want your audience to take from the graph can speak for itself. Although
[p0181-l0002] [14.4pt] tables of data are useful for showing precise figures, a chart or graph can give
[p0181-l0003] [14.4pt] clarity quickly to patterns and trends.
[p0181-l0004] [14.4pt] Charts and graphs are also good choices for comparing data, particularly if you
[p0181-l0005] [14.4pt] are including all the comparative data in one chart, such as a line graph or a bar
[p0181-l0006] [14.4pt] chart. They are also more engaging and generally quicker to read and understand
[p0181-l0007] [14.4pt] than a table of data.
[p0181-l0008] [16.56pt] Other Abstractions
[p0181-l0009] [14.4pt] Other ways to create engaging content that communicates efficiently include the
[p0181-l0010] [14.4pt] following:
[p0181-l0011] [14.4pt] Infographics
[p0181-l0012] [14.4pt] These are often used in reports, marketing, and digital documentation for
[p0181-l0013] [14.4pt] summarizing complex information in an easy-to-consume format.
[p0181-l0014] [14.4pt] Infographics can be particularly useful for making user documentation more
[p0181-l0015] [14.4pt] interesting and consumable, but they should be accompanied by an
[p0181-l0016] [14.4pt] accessible text version.
[p0181-l0017] [14.4pt] Images, illustrations, and animations
[p0181-l0018] [14.4pt] Memes, comic strips, and cartoons are often used to illustrate or emphasize a
[p0181-l0019] [14.4pt] point or just create a visual break.
[p0181-l0020] [14.4pt] Video and audio
[p0181-l0021] [14.4pt] These can be used in digital content to present information in a different way
[p0181-l0022] [14.4pt] from text. Some in your audience may absorb information better from a
[p0181-l0023] [14.4pt] video than from reading, and these formats can be good ways to
[p0181-l0024] [14.4pt] communicate emotion when needed.
[p0181-l0025] [14.4pt] Using a variety of methods to communicate generally improves communication
[p0181-l0026] [14.4pt] with your audience. It makes your message more memorable and engaging and
[p0181-l0027] [14.4pt] easier to understand.

=== PDF page 182 / printed label 182 ===
[p0182-l0001] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0182-l0002] [10.8pt] When creating graphical, video, and audio content, beware of accessibility. You may need to
[p0182-l0003] [10.8pt] create an alternative format or alternative text so that the information is accessible to those
[p0182-l0004] [10.8pt] with vision impairments, those who may need to translate the text, those who may not be able
[p0182-l0005] [10.8pt] to access content because of audio or firewall restrictions, and so on. Ensure that the content is
[p0182-l0006] [10.8pt] legible and understandable to all.
[p0182-l0007] [20.16pt] Perspective-Driven Documentation
[p0182-l0008] [14.4pt] Perspective-driven documentation is a pattern that focuses on who you are
[p0182-l0009] [14.4pt] communicating with and why. The word perspective, in the general sense, can be
[p0182-l0010] [14.4pt] defined as a particular way of looking at something. You put things in
[p0182-l0011] [14.4pt] perspective. In perspective-driven documentation, a perspective is a collection of
[p0182-l0012] [14.4pt] one or more artifacts that address one or more (typically related) concerns of a
[p0182-l0013] [14.4pt] particular stakeholder. Those perspectives could be web pages, diagrams, or
[p0182-l0014] [14.4pt] tables (more on this in “Implementing Perspectives”).
[p0182-l0015] [14.4pt] HOW TO DEFINE A PERSPECTIVE
[p0182-l0016] [14.4pt] The process of defining a perspective is a collaboration between the
[p0182-l0017] [14.4pt] stakeholder and the perspective author (such as an architect or developer).
[p0182-l0018] [14.4pt] The perspective must address the stakeholder’s concerns, but the stakeholder
[p0182-l0019] [14.4pt] likely doesn’t know which artifacts or information will address those
[p0182-l0020] [14.4pt] concerns.
[p0182-l0021] [14.4pt] The perspective author should work with the stakeholder to identify the
[p0182-l0022] [14.4pt] artifacts (text, diagrams, tables, and so on) that must be created and curated
[p0182-l0023] [14.4pt] into a perspective to address a concern.
[p0182-l0024] [14.4pt] The definition process can be refined into templates, checklists, or forms,
[p0182-l0025] [14.4pt] which become an anticorruption layer between the stakeholder and the
[p0182-l0026] [14.4pt] documentation or artifact author, and the basis for creating perspectives in
[p0182-l0027] [14.4pt] documentation across the company.
[p0182-l0028] [14.4pt] Documentation exists for stakeholders (developers, architects, product owners,
[p0182-l0029] [14.4pt] project managers, a security team, DevOps, customers, and so forth) to find the

=== PDF page 183 / printed label 183 ===
[p0183-l0001] [14.4pt] information they need when they need it. Stakeholders’ needs and concerns vary
[p0183-l0002] [14.4pt] widely. A developer is going to need completely different information (and
[p0183-l0003] [14.4pt] likely need it at a different time) than a product owner, who will also need
[p0183-l0004] [14.4pt] completely different information than a customer will.
[p0183-l0005] [14.4pt] Traditional documentation, which is stored in long word processing documents
[p0183-l0006] [14.4pt] and spreadsheets (or something similar), does not allow stakeholders to easily
[p0183-l0007] [14.4pt] access or maintain the information they need. It is either hard to find the
[p0183-l0008] [14.4pt] information or hard to maintain duplicated artifacts. A wiki or other knowledge
[p0183-l0009] [14.4pt] management application is an improvement on traditional documentation but can
[p0183-l0010] [14.4pt] be enhanced immensely using perspectives.
[p0183-l0011] [16.56pt] DRY Perspectives
[p0183-l0012] [14.4pt] One of the key principles of perspective-driven documentation is don’t repeat
[p0183-l0013] [14.4pt] yourself (DRY). Although a particular artifact may be useful to more than one
[p0183-l0014] [14.4pt] stakeholder, and therefore used in multiple perspectives, you do not want to
[p0183-l0015] [14.4pt] duplicate artifacts because that impacts the maintainability of the documentation.
[p0183-l0016] [14.4pt] This principle affects how you implement perspective-driven documentation and
[p0183-l0017] [14.4pt] which tools can be used. The tools and applications must allow you to embed an
[p0183-l0018] [14.4pt] artifact in more than one perspective and for that artifact to automatically update
[p0183-l0019] [14.4pt] everywhere when the original is updated.
[p0183-l0020] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0183-l0021] [10.8pt] Andy Hunt and Dave Thomas define the DRY principle in terms of knowledge even though its
[p0183-l0022] [10.8pt] application is for code: “every piece of knowledge must have a single, unambiguous,
[p0183-l0023] [10.8pt] authoritative representation within a system.”4 This principle discourages duplication of
[p0183-l0024] [10.8pt] information, for example, defining a commonly used string as a variable and then referencing
[p0183-l0025] [10.8pt] that variable when needed, instead of duplicating the string.
[p0183-l0026] [14.4pt] In documentation and knowledge management, you can avoid duplicating any
[p0183-l0027] [14.4pt] artifact (diagram, table of data, paragraphs of text, and so on) by embedding it
[p0183-l0028] [14.4pt] into a page or document (or referencing it, for example, using a hyperlink, but
[p0183-l0029] [14.4pt] this is less accessible).

=== PDF page 184 / printed label 184 ===
[p0184-l0001] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0184-l0002] [10.8pt] When linking to an artifact, be aware that some systems and applications can create dynamic
[p0184-l0003] [10.8pt] links but many create brittle links. A brittle link will break if the artifact you have linked to is
[p0184-l0004] [10.8pt] moved or the name is changed. A dynamic link will still work if the artifact is updated within
[p0184-l0005] [10.8pt] the system that created the link. Check whether your links are brittle or dynamic and take this
[p0184-l0006] [10.8pt] into account, especially when choosing a new knowledge management system.
[p0184-l0007] [14.4pt] The trade-off to the DRY principle is that you do not always want all the
[p0184-l0008] [14.4pt] instances of your artifact to update automatically. You may want your artifact to
[p0184-l0009] [14.4pt] represent a frozen point in time, or you may need to make small changes for a
[p0184-l0010] [14.4pt] particular instance. Be aware of whether you prefer a static or altered version of
[p0184-l0011] [14.4pt] an artifact; then create an explicit copy, named in a way that the reasons for its
[p0184-l0012] [14.4pt] creation are obvious. Some examples include an artifact related to a specific
[p0184-l0013] [14.4pt] software release or a particular version of a standard or license.
[p0184-l0014] [16.56pt] Fractal Perspectives
[p0184-l0015] [14.4pt] Another key principle of perspective-driven documentation is that perspectives
[p0184-l0016] [14.4pt] are fractal. This means that one perspective can be embedded into another
[p0184-l0017] [14.4pt] perspective, in the same way that an artifact (such as a diagram) can be. Because
[p0184-l0018] [14.4pt] of this, you can create a reusable grouping of artifacts as a perspective, embed
[p0184-l0019] [14.4pt] that perspective in another perspective, and then embed that perspective in yet
[p0184-l0020] [14.4pt] another perspective (see Figure 10-5). Further, an artifact or perspective can be
[p0184-l0021] [14.4pt] embedded in multiple other perspectives. The DRY principle is adhered to
[p0184-l0022] [14.4pt] because all the perspectives and artifacts are not copies, but the same instance
[p0184-l0023] [14.4pt] embedded in multiple perspectives.

=== PDF page 185 / printed label 185 ===
