[p0185-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 10-5. Fractal perspectives illustrated: multiple artifacts and two other perspectives embedded in a
[p0185-l0002] [10.8pt] perspective
[p0185-l0003] [14.4pt] Creating templates or checklists for perspectives is a good idea once you have
[p0185-l0004] [14.4pt] identified patterns and arrangements that work for you. Once you know which
[p0185-l0005] [14.4pt] artifacts are generally needed to address a particular concern (for example, that a
[p0185-l0006] [14.4pt] system be responsive) or that generally apply to a particular stakeholder, you can
[p0185-l0007] [14.4pt] document that knowledge in templates or checklists. Those tools will speed up
[p0185-l0008] [14.4pt] the creation of perspectives the next time you need them.
[p0185-l0009] [14.4pt] Templates may be applicable across projects, products, programs, or even across
[p0185-l0010] [14.4pt] departments or organizational units within the larger company. They can also be
[p0185-l0011] [14.4pt] useful in determining which artifacts to create once you know the stakeholders
[p0185-l0012] [14.4pt] and concerns.
[p0185-l0013] [14.4pt] LAYERING DIAGRAMS
[p0185-l0014] [14.4pt] When creating diagrams, it is good practice to split the information into
[p0185-l0015] [14.4pt] layers. Many applications that are used to create diagrams, such as draw.io or
[p0185-l0016] [14.4pt] Visio, support layers.
[p0185-l0017] [14.4pt] Layers are useful for following the DRY and single responsibility principles.

=== PDF page 186 / printed label 186 ===
[p0186-l0001] [14.4pt] You can create several artifacts from one layered master diagram, with
[p0186-l0002] [14.4pt] different combinations of layers visible in each. If you need documentation
[p0186-l0003] [14.4pt] to be available in more than one language, you can create a layer for labels
[p0186-l0004] [14.4pt] and other text in each language. This is good practice and creates less-
[p0186-l0005] [14.4pt] cluttered diagrams that follow the single responsibility principle.
[p0186-l0006] [14.4pt] The single responsibility principle introduced in Chapter 5 states that a class,
[p0186-l0007] [14.4pt] in code, should have one (and only one) reason to change. When your code
[p0186-l0008] [14.4pt] needs modification, only the parts of the code that need to be changed will
[p0186-l0009] [14.4pt] be changed, and unrelated code will stay the same. Following this principle
[p0186-l0010] [14.4pt] reduces the risk of inadvertently introducing a bug or regression into an
[p0186-l0011] [14.4pt] unrelated part of the code, and testing can focus on the code that has
[p0186-l0012] [14.4pt] changed.
[p0186-l0013] [14.4pt] This principle can also be applied to the artifacts you create for
[p0186-l0014] [14.4pt] documentation. If the security of your architecture changes, you need to find
[p0186-l0015] [14.4pt] and update only security-related artifacts. That will save you a lot of time,
[p0186-l0016] [14.4pt] both for finding and updating those artifacts.
[p0186-l0017] [16.56pt] Implementing Perspectives
[p0186-l0018] [14.4pt] Now let’s discuss the practice of implementing these principles and ideas.
[p0186-l0019] [14.4pt] Support varies widely among knowledge management applications (such as
[p0186-l0020] [14.4pt] wikis, Notion, SharePoint, Confluence, Obsidian, Logseq, and so on), but you
[p0186-l0021] [14.4pt] can look out for a few key features in these and other supporting applications (or
[p0186-l0022] [14.4pt] a custom knowledge management app):
[p0186-l0023] [14.4pt] Tags and metadata
[p0186-l0024] [14.4pt] These are key features for organizing your artifacts, especially when using
[p0186-l0025] [14.4pt] one artifact in many perspectives. Tags are also useful for maintaining
[p0186-l0026] [14.4pt] artifacts, allowing you to easily find a particular type of artifact, or artifacts
[p0186-l0027] [14.4pt] belonging to a particular product or project. Add metadata and/or tags to
[p0186-l0028] [14.4pt] artifacts to define them, the product they belong to, and so on. Some
[p0186-l0029] [14.4pt] applications may allow you to add metadata to perspectives themselves (such
[p0186-l0030] [14.4pt] as pages) to define which artifacts should be shown and in what order, rather
[p0186-l0031] [14.4pt] than manually adding artifacts and perspectives to the page.5

=== PDF page 187 / printed label 187 ===
[p0187-l0001] [14.4pt] Embedding and referencing
[p0187-l0002] [14.4pt] To keep knowledge DRY and create perspectives, you need to be able to
[p0187-l0003] [14.4pt] embed artifacts. You could embed them into a wiki or web page, or use a
[p0187-l0004] [14.4pt] proprietary tool like Confluence or SharePoint to create a page and then
[p0187-l0005] [14.4pt] embed the artifact. Ideally, you want the type of embedding that won’t
[p0187-l0006] [14.4pt] require your audience to leave the current page or context to view the
[p0187-l0007] [14.4pt] embedded artifact, rather than a link to click. If embedding is not possible,
[p0187-l0008] [14.4pt] linking to the artifact is a good backup. Ideally, the link should be durable,
[p0187-l0009] [14.4pt] but at a bare minimum, the reference should allow the reader to find and
[p0187-l0010] [14.4pt] view that artifact.
[p0187-l0011] [14.4pt] Flat structure
[p0187-l0012] [14.4pt] When organizing artifacts, most people create some sort of folder hierarchy,
[p0187-l0013] [14.4pt] but in truth, many artifacts need to sit in more than one folder. By using tags,
[p0187-l0014] [14.4pt] you can use a flat structure for your files. Everything is in one place, so you
[p0187-l0015] [14.4pt] always know where to find things. Tags should enable you to find what you
[p0187-l0016] [14.4pt] need, and when they don’t, you know where to look.
[p0187-l0017] [14.4pt] Templates and checklists
[p0187-l0018] [14.4pt] At the very minimum, most tools should allow you to create a page or
[p0187-l0019] [14.4pt] something similar that contains definitions and checklists for creating types
[p0187-l0020] [14.4pt] of perspectives; the items in a checklist or template would be artifacts (or
[p0187-l0021] [14.4pt] perspectives) included to address a concern of a particular stakeholder. Some
[p0187-l0022] [14.4pt] tools may allow you to create a template for a layout of a perspective.
[p0187-l0023] [14.4pt] Layers
[p0187-l0024] [14.4pt] The tool you use to create diagrams or other visuals should support layers so
[p0187-l0025] [14.4pt] that you can separate different concerns into different layers—for example,
[p0187-l0026] [14.4pt] security protocols on one layer and communication patterns, such as sync
[p0187-l0027] [14.4pt] and async, on another. This arrangement enables you to create several assets
[p0187-l0028] [14.4pt] from one diagram file and helps keep them in sync.
[p0187-l0029] [14.4pt] NOTE

=== PDF page 188 / printed label 188 ===
[p0188-l0001] [10.8pt] Knowledge graphs improve the discoverability of information by linking it together. Whereas
[p0188-l0002] [10.8pt] storing information in folders creates a hierarchy, a graph allows a piece of information to be
[p0188-l0003] [10.8pt] linked to one or many other pieces of information. Backlinks make it easy to traverse links in
[p0188-l0004] [10.8pt] either direction. Obsidian is a fantastic tool for experimenting with knowledge graphs.
[p0188-l0005] [14.4pt] PERSPECTIVES AND VIEWS IN ARCHITECTURE
[p0188-l0006] [14.4pt] You may have heard the term perspective or view in other areas of
[p0188-l0007] [14.4pt] architecture. Here are some places you may have heard these terms:
[p0188-l0008] [14.4pt] The Open Group Architecture Framework (TOGAF), an enterprise
[p0188-l0009] [14.4pt] architecture framework, contains the concept of viewpoints and views,
[p0188-l0010] [14.4pt] which are similar but more limited concepts compared to perspective-
[p0188-l0011] [14.4pt] driven documentation.
[p0188-l0012] [14.4pt] Both the SABSA and Zachman frameworks also contain the concept of
[p0188-l0013] [14.4pt] levels or perspectives, creating a matrix of models or assets within each
[p0188-l0014] [14.4pt] perspective across classifications of what, why, how, who, where, and
[p0188-l0015] [14.4pt] when. This is a much more rigid idea compared to perspective-driven
[p0188-l0016] [14.4pt] documentation.
[p0188-l0017] [14.4pt] The Diátaxis framework divides documentation into four quadrants
[p0188-l0018] [14.4pt] (tutorials, how-to guides, explanation, and reference) based on the
[p0188-l0019] [14.4pt] needs of the audience. These quadrants can be considered higher-level
[p0188-l0020] [14.4pt] perspectives.
[p0188-l0021] [14.4pt] The 4+1 Model also contains the concept of views—in this case, the
[p0188-l0022] [14.4pt] logical, process, development, physical, and scenarios views. These
[p0188-l0023] [14.4pt] views combine the concepts of addressing the needs of a particular
[p0188-l0024] [14.4pt] audience and levels of abstraction. The 4+1 views could be considered
[p0188-l0025] [14.4pt] high-level perspectives.
[p0188-l0026] [14.4pt] The perspectives in Software Systems Architecture, 2nd edition, by Nick
[p0188-l0027] [14.4pt] Rozanski and Eoin Woods (Addison-Wesley, 2011) are more like
[p0188-l0028] [14.4pt] architecture characteristics, such as security, performance and
[p0188-l0029] [14.4pt] scalability, and availability and resilience.
[p0188-l0030] [14.4pt] I chose the term perspective rather than view to name perspective-driven

=== PDF page 189 / printed label 189 ===
[p0189-l0001] [14.4pt] documentation because the term view is overloaded with multiple meanings
[p0189-l0002] [14.4pt] in software development and architecture. Perspective also fits better with its
[p0189-l0003] [14.4pt] intention; to address a stakeholder’s wants and needs. A perspective is
[p0189-l0004] [14.4pt] personal.
[p0189-l0005] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0189-l0006] [14.4pt] You now have a set of higher-level principles for recording, communicating, and
[p0189-l0007] [14.4pt] storing knowledge that you can apply to your company’s knowledge and your
[p0189-l0008] [14.4pt] personal knowledge. But applying these principles to your knowledge processes
[p0189-l0009] [14.4pt] and documentation is just one aspect of knowledge management.
[p0189-l0010] [14.4pt] What underlies everything in software is people. You will discover in the next
[p0189-l0011] [14.4pt] chapter that people are a huge part of knowledge management and can be an
[p0189-l0012] [14.4pt] incredible aid to improving the development, communication, and recording of
[p0189-l0013] [14.4pt] your knowledge.
[p0189-l0014] [10.8pt] 1 The frameworks from Azure, AWS, and GCP allow you to consistently evaluate your architectures
[p0189-l0015] [10.8pt] against cloud best practices and are maintained by cloud providers.
[p0189-l0016] [10.8pt] 2 This might include a knowledge base, documentation of code that is stored in a repository, or user
[p0189-l0017] [10.8pt] documentation published online.
[p0189-l0018] [10.8pt] 3 Search for word cloud generator online to find a selection.
[p0189-l0019] [10.8pt] 4 Andrew Hunt and David Thomas, The Pragmatic Programmer, 1st edition (Reading, MA: Addison-
[p0189-l0020] [10.8pt] Wesley, 1999).
[p0189-l0021] [10.8pt] 5 Using Obsidian with the DataView plug-in would allow you to add metadata to pages to automate
[p0189-l0022] [10.8pt] building of perspectives.

=== PDF page 190 / printed label 190 ===
[p0190-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 11. Knowledge and
[p0190-l0002] [28.8pt] People
[p0190-l0003] [14.4pt] Software and architecture ultimately come down to people. People use software,
[p0190-l0004] [14.4pt] software helps people, and people architect, design, and code software. You
[p0190-l0005] [14.4pt] should therefore not be surprised that some of these knowledge patterns revolve
[p0190-l0006] [14.4pt] around people.
[p0190-l0007] [14.4pt] Your peers, team members, and other colleagues are assets not just to the
[p0190-l0008] [14.4pt] company but also to you. Utilize them wisely, and they can help improve your
[p0190-l0009] [14.4pt] knowledge management, documentation, and software architecture overall.
[p0190-l0010] [20.16pt] Get Feedback Early and Often
[p0190-l0011] [14.4pt] One mistake that many people make is putting a lot of time and effort into their
[p0190-l0012] [14.4pt] work before they get any feedback on it. This can waste effort and money as
[p0190-l0013] [14.4pt] well as impact the architectural design of a system. This applies both to
[p0190-l0014] [14.4pt] individuals and teams.
[p0190-l0015] [14.4pt] If you have experience with Agile and the reasons behind it, you probably know
[p0190-l0016] [14.4pt] that Agile is all about getting feedback early with the fastest feedback loop you
[p0190-l0017] [14.4pt] can get. Iterative and incremental changes. Fail fast or fail hard. You should
[p0190-l0018] [14.4pt] follow these same principles when creating artifacts and documentation.
[p0190-l0019] [14.4pt] If you are not getting feedback on your ideas and designs, you are missing out on
[p0190-l0020] [14.4pt] changing requirements and that second pair of eyes to help you with a sanity
[p0190-l0021] [14.4pt] check. Consider the butterfly effect: one incorrect assumption early on could
[p0190-l0022] [14.4pt] steer the architecture in a completely wrong direction.
[p0190-l0023] [14.4pt] Not getting feedback can mean you play into the hands of your own personal
[p0190-l0024] [14.4pt] sunk cost fallacy: the longer you work on something, the less you want to make
[p0190-l0025] [14.4pt] changes to it. It’s your baby. If you haven’t gotten feedback early on, you are
[p0190-l0026] [14.4pt] less likely to seek it and may miss out on key input to your design, diagram, and
[p0190-l0027] [14.4pt] so on.

=== PDF page 191 / printed label 191 ===
[p0191-l0001] [14.4pt] THE SUNK COST FALLACY
[p0191-l0002] [14.4pt] The sunk cost fallacy is a cognitive bias in which individuals continue to
[p0191-l0003] [14.4pt] invest resources (such as time, money, and effort) in a project or decision,
[p0191-l0004] [14.4pt] despite evidence that the investment is no longer justified or that the project
[p0191-l0005] [14.4pt] is unlikely to succeed. This bias is rooted in the belief that, because
[p0191-l0006] [14.4pt] resources have already been invested, continued investment is necessary to
[p0191-l0007] [14.4pt] ensure that the initial investment was not for nothing. You have invested too
[p0191-l0008] [14.4pt] much to quit.
[p0191-l0009] [14.4pt] In technical projects, the sunk cost fallacy can manifest in several ways. For
[p0191-l0010] [14.4pt] example, a company may continue to push forward with a project that has
[p0191-l0011] [14.4pt] already exceeded its original budget or timeline, even if evidence indicates
[p0191-l0012] [14.4pt] that the project is no longer feasible or that it will not deliver the intended
[p0191-l0013] [14.4pt] value. Alternatively, a team may continue (or be forced) to use a technology
[p0191-l0014] [14.4pt] or tool that is no longer effective or efficient, simply because resources have
[p0191-l0015] [14.4pt] already been invested in it.
[p0191-l0016] [14.4pt] Get feedback on small parts of your architecture design and the overall design.
[p0191-l0017] [14.4pt] Sometimes creating a diagram or set of diagrams for part of an architecture can
[p0191-l0018] [14.4pt] take a long time, maybe days. Imagine putting three days of your effort into
[p0191-l0019] [14.4pt] diagrams only to find out that an assumption you made was wrong or an
[p0191-l0020] [14.4pt] understanding you had was false. In the worst case, you may have to start again
[p0191-l0021] [14.4pt] from scratch, or even tell a development team to stop work that is based on your
[p0191-l0022] [14.4pt] diagrams. Even the best case means you have to put more time and effort into
[p0191-l0023] [14.4pt] changing your diagrams.
[p0191-l0024] [14.4pt] Consider these additional benefits to individuals and teams that get feedback
[p0191-l0025] [14.4pt] early and often:
[p0191-l0026] [14.4pt] Identify issues and errors early
[p0191-l0027] [14.4pt] The more time between a decision or action and the time it is challenged, the
[p0191-l0028] [14.4pt] higher the cost to make the change. You want to identify issues before they
[p0191-l0029] [14.4pt] become more costly to fix.1 Figure 11-1 illustrates a situation that you can
[p0191-l0030] [14.4pt] avoid by getting feedback.
[p0191-l0031] [14.4pt] Identify potential improvements and optimizations

=== PDF page 192 / printed label 192 ===
[p0192-l0001] [14.4pt] Involving more people with different backgrounds and experiences means
[p0192-l0002] [14.4pt] you get a more holistic and diverse analysis of your designs, making it more
[p0192-l0003] [14.4pt] likely that you spot room for improvement with feedback than without.
[p0192-l0004] [14.4pt] Ensure alignment with business needs
[p0192-l0005] [14.4pt] Business stakeholders or others closer to the business can provide you with
[p0192-l0006] [14.4pt] feedback on the business needs to be met by your design.
[p0192-l0007] [14.4pt] Establish a dialogue
[p0192-l0008] [14.4pt] Stakeholders will appreciate being involved and alerted to to trade-offs and
[p0192-l0009] [14.4pt] negatives that result from their requirements. They may even change their
[p0192-l0010] [14.4pt] requirements based on your feedback, which you would want to happen
[p0192-l0011] [14.4pt] earlier rather than later.
[p0192-l0012] [14.4pt] Identify risks and challenges and take appropriate action
[p0192-l0013] [14.4pt] The earlier risks are identified, the better. These risks or challenges can be
[p0192-l0014] [14.4pt] proactively mitigated, and further feedback can be sought to accept any
[p0192-l0015] [14.4pt] residual risk (that couldn’t be mitigated).
[p0192-l0016] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0192-l0017] [10.8pt] Getting feedback from peers who are not involved in your project is a good idea. They can
[p0192-l0018] [10.8pt] give you an outside perspective to help you avoid the curse of knowledge and break down the
[p0192-l0019] [10.8pt] walls of any echo chamber you may be stuck in.
[p0192-l0020] [10.8pt] Figure 11-1. Not getting feedback on decision 1 leads to costly changes later

=== PDF page 193 / printed label 193 ===
[p0193-l0001] [14.4pt] So when should you get feedback? The overall answer is early and often, but
[p0193-l0002] [14.4pt] when exactly will depend on the situation. One key time to get feedback is while
[p0193-l0003] [14.4pt] documenting assumptions (to make sure you do document all assumptions).
[p0193-l0004] [14.4pt] You need to get sign-off (confirmation) on your assumptions as soon as possible,
[p0193-l0005] [14.4pt] before those assumptions affect the design of your system or product. This is not
[p0193-l0006] [14.4pt] always possible, so if you cannot get sign-off on an assumption, state that clearly
[p0193-l0007] [14.4pt] in your documentation. Give your assumptions IDs in your documentation to
[p0193-l0008] [14.4pt] reference effectively so you will know where changes may be needed if an
[p0193-l0009] [14.4pt] assumption is found to be false.
[p0193-l0010] [14.4pt] If you are new to a product or role, I advise you to get feedback from technical
[p0193-l0011] [14.4pt] and business colleagues as often as you can. This will save you time and
[p0193-l0012] [14.4pt] improve your reputation in the long run. Consider any frustration at your
[p0193-l0013] [14.4pt] requests for input to be short-term, whereas the cost to your reputation of
[p0193-l0014] [14.4pt] producing something that is ultimately “wrong” or costly will be more lasting.
[p0193-l0015] [14.4pt] But don’t think just because you are senior, or even in charge, that you should
[p0193-l0016] [14.4pt] not be seeking feedback from your peers, subordinates, and business colleagues.
[p0193-l0017] [14.4pt] Even the most junior member of your team is likely to know something you do
[p0193-l0018] [14.4pt] not or have a different perspective than you. Never let rank get in the way of
[p0193-l0019] [14.4pt] receiving wisdom.
[p0193-l0020] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0193-l0021] [10.8pt] Playing back assumptions and your understanding of stakeholders’ needs builds confidence in
[p0193-l0022] [10.8pt] your designs and solutions by showing that you have been listening. Rarely will a design
[p0193-l0023] [10.8pt] become reality if there is a lack of confidence in it.
[p0193-l0024] [14.4pt] Now that you know why and when to get feedback, you also want to know how.
[p0193-l0025] [14.4pt] Keep in mind that you don’t explicitly have to ask for feedback. You can just
[p0193-l0026] [14.4pt] show people what you have been working on to allow them to give feedback if
[p0193-l0027] [14.4pt] they want to. Be aware that more junior members of the team, or those junior to
[p0193-l0028] [14.4pt] yourself, may need encouragement to give their opinion, so it is best to explicitly
[p0193-l0029] [14.4pt] ask for their input.
[p0193-l0030] [14.4pt] Lots of methods are available for getting feedback. Here are a few examples:

=== PDF page 194 / printed label 194 ===
[p0194-l0001] [14.4pt] The easiest approach is to simply ask or to show your work without
[p0194-l0002] [14.4pt] applying any pressure to provide feedback.
[p0194-l0003] [14.4pt] Feedback can be incorporated into formal processes, such as pull requests
[p0194-l0004] [14.4pt] or at checkpoints in your personal or team workflow.
[p0194-l0005] [14.4pt] ADRs are a great tool for getting feedback on proposed draft decisions.2
[p0194-l0006] [14.4pt] Including a feedback or consultation section where others can share advice
[p0194-l0007] [14.4pt] and setting a deadline for input will enable others to view the decision and
[p0194-l0008] [14.4pt] reasoning and give their feedback.
[p0194-l0009] [14.4pt] Your existing meetings, stand-ups, and reviews are good opportunities to
[p0194-l0010] [14.4pt] bring up what you have been working on to get informal or formal
[p0194-l0011] [14.4pt] feedback.
[p0194-l0012] [14.4pt] If you have a request for change (RFC) process at your company, this may
[p0194-l0013] [14.4pt] be the expected method for getting feedback on your ideas for changes to a
[p0194-l0014] [14.4pt] product or system.

=== PDF page 195 / printed label 195 ===
[p0195-l0001] [10.8pt] EXAMPLE
[p0195-l0002] [14.4pt] FEEDBACK IS PART OF THE PROCESS
[p0195-l0003] [14.4pt] In the development, architecture, and technical writing teams at Polyglot
[p0195-l0004] [14.4pt] Media, feedback is built into the workflows. Design and documentation
[p0195-l0005] [14.4pt] tasks are split into small and logical chunks, in the same way that coding
[p0195-l0006] [14.4pt] tasks or user stories are. As a result, the publishing and feedback cycle for
[p0195-l0007] [14.4pt] artifacts and documentation is quick and agile.
[p0195-l0008] [14.4pt] Each team member can ask for a review via a dedicated review channel in
[p0195-l0009] [14.4pt] their messaging system, and feedback is expected within a short time frame.
[p0195-l0010] [14.4pt] All are encouraged to seek feedback in this way before the task is deemed
[p0195-l0011] [14.4pt] finished.
[p0195-l0012] [14.4pt] Formal feedback is sought at the following checkpoints:
[p0195-l0013] [14.4pt] When a pull request is made for documentation in a repository, or when
[p0195-l0014] [14.4pt] an artifact is deemed to be ready, feedback is sought from peers, and
[p0195-l0015] [14.4pt] technical feedback is sought from developers and architects if needed.
[p0195-l0016] [14.4pt] When documentation is in preview (for example, the documentation
[p0195-l0017] [14.4pt] website is published in a nonproduction environment), feedback is
[p0195-l0018] [14.4pt] sought from stakeholders, such as a product owner.
[p0195-l0019] [14.4pt] Once documentation and artifacts have been published, feedback comes
[p0195-l0020] [14.4pt] from customers, the support team, and other colleagues.
[p0195-l0021] [14.4pt] At Polyglot Media, feedback is considered an ongoing and agile process.
[p0195-l0022] [20.16pt] Share the Load
[p0195-l0023] [14.4pt] The creation and maintenance of documentation (and the general communication
[p0195-l0024] [14.4pt] of software architecture itself) should never fall to one person alone (unless you
[p0195-l0025] [14.4pt] truly are a one-person band). No role is solely responsible; each role is usually
[p0195-l0026] [14.4pt] responsible for different types or elements of documentation. Both the creation
[p0195-l0027] [14.4pt] and maintenance of documentation must be shared for it to be effective and up-
[p0195-l0028] [14.4pt] to-date. Let’s look at how you can share the load.

=== PDF page 196 / printed label 196 ===
[p0196-l0001] [16.56pt] Nonproprietary Formats
[p0196-l0002] [14.4pt] Using nonproprietary applications and file formats is a great way to expand the
[p0196-l0003] [14.4pt] number of people who can create and update (and even view) your
[p0196-l0004] [14.4pt] documentation. Using nonproprietary formats and applications results in the
[p0196-l0005] [14.4pt] following:
[p0196-l0006] [14.4pt] Fewer license woes
[p0196-l0007] [14.4pt] You don’t need to worry about licenses (as much)—for example, who on the
[p0196-l0008] [14.4pt] team needs a full Visio license or who needs an Atlassian account and what
[p0196-l0009] [14.4pt] permissions they need (both of these licenses are very costly and come with
[p0196-l0010] [14.4pt] further costs for managing the licenses). But do make sure to check the
[p0196-l0011] [14.4pt] license of any product that is being used.
[p0196-l0012] [14.4pt] Higher editor accessability
[p0196-l0013] [14.4pt] It is easier to gain permission to use one of the many possible editors, for
[p0196-l0014] [14.4pt] anyone who needs to create or edit the documentation. Many nonproprietary
[p0196-l0015] [14.4pt] applications are free to use, even commercially. There are often several
[p0196-l0016] [14.4pt] options for apps that can read and write a nonproprietary file format. Some
[p0196-l0017] [14.4pt] apps even work in the browser, like draw.io, so they don’t need to be
[p0196-l0018] [14.4pt] installed by the user.
[p0196-l0019] [14.4pt] Higher interoperability
[p0196-l0020] [14.4pt] As the file format is not owned by a company or person, and is open and
[p0196-l0021] [14.4pt] publicly documented, anyone can develop an app that can read and write the
[p0196-l0022] [14.4pt] format, meaning you can use your artifacts in more than just one app.
[p0196-l0023] [14.4pt] Reduced vendor lock-in
[p0196-l0024] [14.4pt] Having all your documentation in Markdown (or another plain-text format)3
[p0196-l0025] [14.4pt] means that anyone with a text editor can create, read, and update it. Anyone
[p0196-l0026] [14.4pt] who needs to has many application options to read and write the
[p0196-l0027] [14.4pt] nonproprietary format. This is not true of documentation in Word, Notion, or
[p0196-l0028] [14.4pt] another proprietary format.
[p0196-l0029] [14.4pt] You do not need to avoid all proprietary software, and probably can’t, but it

=== PDF page 197 / printed label 197 ===
[p0197-l0001] [14.4pt] should be a consideration for anything durable (for example, documentation you
[p0197-l0002] [14.4pt] will need in the future). Nonproprietary software is likely more future-proof than
[p0197-l0003] [14.4pt] proprietary would be. As with anything, trade-offs exist, such as a lack of
[p0197-l0004] [14.4pt] support for many nonproprietary and open source applications. You need to
[p0197-l0005] [14.4pt] weigh all the trade-offs when selecting your tools.
[p0197-l0006] [14.4pt] NONPROPRIETARY FORMATS
[p0197-l0007] [14.4pt] Nonproprietary software and file formats are not owned or controlled by a
[p0197-l0008] [14.4pt] specific company or individual. Nonproprietary software is typically
[p0197-l0009] [14.4pt] developed and distributed under an open source license.
[p0197-l0010] [14.4pt] Nonproprietary file formats are open and publicly documented, allowing
[p0197-l0011] [14.4pt] anyone to create software that can read and write the format. Some examples
[p0197-l0012] [14.4pt] of nonproprietary software and file formats include the following:
[p0197-l0013] [14.4pt] Markdown
[p0197-l0014] [14.4pt] A plain-text format supported by many editors and build tools
[p0197-l0015] [14.4pt] AsciiDoc
[p0197-l0016] [14.4pt] A plain-text format that can be built by tools such as Asciidoctor and
[p0197-l0017] [14.4pt] Antora into formatted documents
[p0197-l0018] [14.4pt] Git
[p0197-l0019] [14.4pt] A free and open source distributed version control system
[p0197-l0020] [14.4pt] ODF (Open Document Format)
[p0197-l0021] [14.4pt] An open format for word processing documents, spreadsheets,
[p0197-l0022] [14.4pt] presentations, and graphics
[p0197-l0023] [14.4pt] draw.io
[p0197-l0024] [14.4pt] An open source application and file format for graphics
[p0197-l0025] [14.4pt] PNG (Portable Network Graphics)
[p0197-l0026] [14.4pt] An open raster graphics alternative to GIF (Graphics Interchange
[p0197-l0027] [14.4pt] Format)

=== PDF page 198 / printed label 198 ===
[p0198-l0001] [14.4pt] PDF (Portable Document Format)
[p0198-l0002] [14.4pt] A file format for presenting text and graphics independent of software
[p0198-l0003] [14.4pt] and hardware
[p0198-l0004] [14.4pt] YAML (YAML Ain’t Markup Language)
[p0198-l0005] [14.4pt] An open human-readable data serialization language often used for
[p0198-l0006] [14.4pt] metadata and configuration files
[p0198-l0007] [14.4pt] HTML (Hypertext Markup Language)
[p0198-l0008] [14.4pt] The standard markup language for documents designed to be viewed in a
[p0198-l0009] [14.4pt] web browser
[p0198-l0010] [16.56pt] Accessibility
[p0198-l0011] [14.4pt] When choosing a format or notation for your documentation, you need to take
[p0198-l0012] [14.4pt] into account whether it can be used by all the people who need to read, create,
[p0198-l0013] [14.4pt] and update your artifacts. As well as selecting a nonproprietary format, you
[p0198-l0014] [14.4pt] should consider both the audience and the author’s understanding.
[p0198-l0015] [14.4pt] A standard such as UML or ArchiMate may end up reducing access. The number
[p0198-l0016] [14.4pt] of people with a full understanding or qualification in these standards is small.4
[p0198-l0017] [14.4pt] You would need to invest time and money into training to increase the pool of
[p0198-l0018] [14.4pt] people who can maintain your documentation and repeat this every time
[p0198-l0019] [14.4pt] someone new joins the team (see Figure 11-2).

=== PDF page 199 / printed label 199 ===
[p0199-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 11-2. Choosing a notation or format affects who can understand or maintain your documentation
[p0199-l0002] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0199-l0003] [10.8pt] Paying for proprietary tools, such as Microsoft Word and Google Docs, can easily melt into the
[p0199-l0004] [10.8pt] background in the same way as filling your vehicle with fuel. It’s the price of utility, but it all
[p0199-l0005] [10.8pt] adds up. Moving to a nonproprietary format that is widely supported, like Markdown, means
[p0199-l0006] [10.8pt] that you can stop paying and start using the myriad of free tools available. You save money
[p0199-l0007] [10.8pt] and gain many more benefits, such as better compatibility.5
[p0199-l0008] [14.4pt] A simple custom notation that you develop in-house would be simpler for
[p0199-l0009] [14.4pt] anyone to pick up. Some standards, such as the C4 Model, also are far easier to
[p0199-l0010] [14.4pt] understand without prior knowledge and are easy to learn to create and update
[p0199-l0011] [14.4pt] documentation. Using standards that are simple, such as flow diagrams or
[p0199-l0012] [14.4pt] simplified sequence diagrams, will increase your pool of maintainers.
[p0199-l0013] [14.4pt] This selection of formats and standards applies to all your documentation.
[p0199-l0014] [14.4pt] Imagine if everyone who creates and maintains documentation had to be able to
[p0199-l0015] [14.4pt] write HTML and Cascading Style Sheets (CSS). That would be a tall order,
[p0199-l0016] [14.4pt] involving a lot of syntax and logic. Now consider writing your documentation in

=== PDF page 200 / printed label 200 ===
[p0200-l0001] [14.4pt] Markdown or Asciidoc. Both formats allow a lot of control over the appearance
[p0200-l0002] [14.4pt] of text on-screen but are far simpler to understand and learn how to use.
[p0200-l0003] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0200-l0004] [10.8pt] Email is not a knowledge repository. It is accessible to only its sender and recipient. It is hard
[p0200-l0005] [10.8pt] to search. Email is usually deleted when someone leaves an organization, and many
[p0200-l0006] [10.8pt] organizations purge old email regularly. Move knowledge from email to a location it will be
[p0200-l0007] [10.8pt] safe, searchable, secure, and accessible to all who need it.
[p0200-l0008] [16.56pt] Collaboration
[p0200-l0009] [14.4pt] Another way to enhance the maintenance of documentation is to use
[p0200-l0010] [14.4pt] collaboration tools. Tools such as Google Docs, Microsoft Teams, Slack, and
[p0200-l0011] [14.4pt] online whiteboards allow you to easily share the creation and maintenance of
[p0200-l0012] [14.4pt] artifacts and documentation. Collaboration with tools like these might be done
[p0200-l0013] [14.4pt] synchronously (at the same time) or asynchronously (at varying times). Both
[p0200-l0014] [14.4pt] methods are useful in different circumstances.
[p0200-l0015] [14.4pt] By working together on creating or maintaining an artifact, you can lighten the
[p0200-l0016] [14.4pt] load by getting input from more than one person at a time and even change who
[p0200-l0017] [14.4pt] is driving (in control of the keyboard, mouse, or other input device) in the same
[p0200-l0018] [14.4pt] way developers do when pair or ensemble programming. If no one wants to do a
[p0200-l0019] [14.4pt] particular task, do it together.
[p0200-l0020] [14.4pt] Working together also means that you can coach or teach one another, which can
[p0200-l0021] [14.4pt] be especially useful for newer team members. If you are looking to delegate a
[p0200-l0022] [14.4pt] task that only you currently understand, using a collaborative tool is an excellent
[p0200-l0023] [14.4pt] option—especially if any participants are working remotely.
[p0200-l0024] [14.4pt] Collaboration tools such as Slack and Microsoft Teams are also excellent ways
[p0200-l0025] [14.4pt] to notify or prompt people. It may be useful to have an automatic notification for
[p0200-l0026] [14.4pt] a channel or team when documentation has been updated or created.6 If nothing
[p0200-l0027] [14.4pt] else, this helps create expectations around maintaining documentation. You may
[p0200-l0028] [14.4pt] also want an automatic notification of changes (whether for code, such as a new
[p0200-l0029] [14.4pt] release, or a part of the documentation, such as the requirements) that prompts
[p0200-l0030] [14.4pt] the team to check for downstream effects (for example, does a business analyst’s
[p0200-l0031] [14.4pt] change to requirements require changes to the architecture by an architect or

=== PDF page 201 / printed label 201 ===
[p0201-l0001] [14.4pt] developer?).
[p0201-l0002] [16.56pt] Roles and Responsibilities
[p0201-l0003] [14.4pt] Assigning specific documentation and communication roles to each individual
[p0201-l0004] [14.4pt] on your team can be useful, but don’t fall into the trap of creating bottlenecks
[p0201-l0005] [14.4pt] (for example, a few people being responsible for most of the work), or single
[p0201-l0006] [14.4pt] points of failure (for example, a team member is ill or leaves). Ensure that you at
[p0201-l0007] [14.4pt] least have an understudy for each role so that someone could step in to take over
[p0201-l0008] [14.4pt] or help when needed. Consider the workload of any particular role and assign the
[p0201-l0009] [14.4pt] appropriate number of people to it.
[p0201-l0010] [14.4pt] One way to consider creating roles around documentation is by the type of
[p0201-l0011] [14.4pt] documentation, such as recording requirements or the output from an
[p0201-l0012] [14.4pt] EventStorming session. Another way is to assign the role of creating a type of
[p0201-l0013] [14.4pt] documentation to one person and the role of reviewing that type of
[p0201-l0014] [14.4pt] documentation to someone else. Some types of documentation will naturally
[p0201-l0015] [14.4pt] align to a role on your team, but some may be more aligned to who has the skills
[p0201-l0016] [14.4pt] or time.
[p0201-l0017] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0201-l0018] [10.8pt] Never assume you will have a handover period with any of the members of your team. Ensure
[p0201-l0019] [10.8pt] you do not have a single point of failure (one person responsible for something) when it comes
[p0201-l0020] [10.8pt] to documentation and communication. You never know when someone might go on long-term
[p0201-l0021] [10.8pt] sick leave, be made redundant, or be put on gardening leave.7
[p0201-l0022] [16.56pt] Further Techniques
[p0201-l0023] [14.4pt] Knowledge sharing, or lunch-and-learn, sessions are a great way to spread
[p0201-l0024] [14.4pt] knowledge throughout the team or organization and should be based around (or
[p0201-l0025] [14.4pt] the preparation of such sessions should involve the creation of) documentation
[p0201-l0026] [14.4pt] or other resources from which the same information can be learned. These
[p0201-l0027] [14.4pt] sessions can be live or recorded, but if live, they should create artifacts for future
[p0201-l0028] [14.4pt] team members or employees to learn from. The amount of preparation that goes
[p0201-l0029] [14.4pt] into one of these sessions should not be wasted by making it a one-off learning
[p0201-l0030] [14.4pt] opportunity. Sessions such as these don’t just share knowledge but also

=== PDF page 202 / printed label 202 ===
[p0202-l0001] [14.4pt] contribute to a culture of sharing and learning.
[p0202-l0002] [14.4pt] It makes sense to use a template for a particular artifact that is created multiple
[p0202-l0003] [14.4pt] times for a product or project, or across multiple projects and products.
[p0202-l0004] [14.4pt] Templates are similar to patterns: a template is a reusable solution that has been
[p0202-l0005] [14.4pt] shown to be effective.
[p0202-l0006] [14.4pt] Using templates has many advantages. One is to make sharing the load easier.
[p0202-l0007] [14.4pt] Even someone new to a project has somewhere to start when a template is
[p0202-l0008] [14.4pt] available, and, if templates are shared across products or projects, anyone
[p0202-l0009] [14.4pt] switching products or projects will be off to a flying start because of their prior
[p0202-l0010] [14.4pt] experience with the template.
[p0202-l0011] [14.4pt] Whether you work in an Agile system or not, a documentation sprint or simply a
[p0202-l0012] [14.4pt] dedicated time block is another way to share the work of documentation and
[p0202-l0013] [14.4pt] communication. This dedicated time means that people who may normally be
[p0202-l0014] [14.4pt] too busy have no excuse to not be creating or maintaining documentation. You
[p0202-l0015] [14.4pt] can combine techniques such as knowledge-sharing or collaboration sessions
[p0202-l0016] [14.4pt] with dedicated time for extra impact, especially if these sessions don’t typically
[p0202-l0017] [14.4pt] happen outside of this dedicated space.
[p0202-l0018] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0202-l0019] [10.8pt] If you ever hope to allow members of your team to share and delegate communicating and
[p0202-l0020] [10.8pt] documenting your software architecture and product, you need to be proactive in your
[p0202-l0021] [10.8pt] approach to documentation. Share the load and make documentation and communication a
[p0202-l0022] [10.8pt] collaborative effort.
[p0202-l0023] [20.16pt] Just-in-Time Architecture
[p0202-l0024] [14.4pt] You aren’t gonna need it (YAGNI) is a principle in coding that encourages
[p0202-l0025] [14.4pt] developing only functionality that is needed now and does not try to predict
[p0202-l0026] [14.4pt] future needs.8 This is another coding principle that should be applied to
[p0202-l0027] [14.4pt] knowledge management and documentation.
[p0202-l0028] [14.4pt] NOTE

=== PDF page 203 / printed label 203 ===
[p0203-l0001] [10.8pt] YAGNI is based on the idea that predictions may not come true and you are therefore wasting
[p0203-l0002] [10.8pt] effort now and possibly creating problems in the future when changes need to be made.
[p0203-l0003] [14.4pt] Another principle (of software architecture this time) that also fits in nicely with
[p0203-l0004] [14.4pt] YAGNI is that of deferring architectural decisions for as long as possible.
[p0203-l0005] [14.4pt] Combining this with YAGNI, you get just-in-time architecture and
[p0203-l0006] [14.4pt] documentation: don’t decide and document what you think you will need in the
[p0203-l0007] [14.4pt] future but only what you know you need now.
[p0203-l0008] [14.4pt] Following the just-in-time pattern has the following benefits, whether you are
[p0203-l0009] [14.4pt] working in a waterfall, Agile, or somewhere-in-between environment:
[p0203-l0010] [14.4pt] Reduction of waste
[p0203-l0011] [14.4pt] When you produce architecture or documentation just in time, you are
[p0203-l0012] [14.4pt] producing it for a reason and with all the knowledge available at the time. If
[p0203-l0013] [14.4pt] you produce an artifact ahead of time, something will likely change that
[p0203-l0014] [14.4pt] requires you to revise it. If another decision, artifact, or actual
[p0203-l0015] [14.4pt] coding/designing is based on that revised artifact, you’ll have a chain of
[p0203-l0016] [14.4pt] artifacts, decisions, and code that needs to change as well. That is a lot of
[p0203-l0017] [14.4pt] wasted effort that becomes costly.
[p0203-l0018] [14.4pt] Greater agility and flexibility
[p0203-l0019] [14.4pt] Requirements change, and by using the just-in-time architecture pattern, you
[p0203-l0020] [14.4pt] can be highly responsive to these changes. When doing the work, you have
[p0203-l0021] [14.4pt] the latest information on requirements and decisions. Also, having a just-in-
[p0203-l0022] [14.4pt] time system in place puts you in a good position to respond to changes that
[p0203-l0023] [14.4pt] may require updates to previous artifacts because your planning is based on
[p0203-l0024] [14.4pt] need rather than prediction. You reduce the number of artifacts that will need
[p0203-l0025] [14.4pt] to be changed and are therefore more likely to have the resources and ability
[p0203-l0026] [14.4pt] to adapt.
[p0203-l0027] [14.4pt] Efficient use of resources
[p0203-l0028] [14.4pt] By focusing on what is needed now, not tomorrow, you prioritize your team’s
[p0203-l0029] [14.4pt] activities. All team members, technical and business, are focused on the
[p0203-l0030] [14.4pt] current most important aspect of the product or project. Less time and effort

=== PDF page 204 / printed label 204 ===
[p0204-l0001] [14.4pt] are wasted by all when artifacts are as up-to-date as they can be. And
[p0204-l0002] [14.4pt] because everyone’s time is used more efficiently, more flexibility exists for
[p0204-l0003] [14.4pt] research, proof-of-concept work, and unexpected work.
[p0204-l0004] [14.4pt] Up-to-date information
[p0204-l0005] [14.4pt] Not only will you have the most up-to-date information and feedback to
[p0204-l0006] [14.4pt] make a decision or create an artifact, but your artifact will also be used as
[p0204-l0007] [14.4pt] soon as you produce it and therefore be up-to-date when it is used. By
[p0204-l0008] [14.4pt] deferring the creation of an artifact or the making of a decision, you know
[p0204-l0009] [14.4pt] more than you did before. In general, you will be in a good position to
[p0204-l0010] [14.4pt] maintain any artifacts that do require change with your just-in-time processes
[p0204-l0011] [14.4pt] in place.
[p0204-l0012] [14.4pt] Improved time-to-market
[p0204-l0013] [14.4pt] Just-in-time means that all effort is being put into what is most important
[p0204-l0014] [14.4pt] right now, rather than what might be important in the future. Therefore, the
[p0204-l0015] [14.4pt] changes or features that are important now can be tested and released more
[p0204-l0016] [14.4pt] quickly than if focus were diluted across other changes that don’t matter
[p0204-l0017] [14.4pt] now.
[p0204-l0018] [14.4pt] Improved fit with Agile practices
[p0204-l0019] [14.4pt] Although just-in-time architecture can be used in a waterfall environment, it
[p0204-l0020] [14.4pt] molds your architecture processes into something a lot more Agile. It allows
[p0204-l0021] [14.4pt] those involved in the architecture and documentation to fit into the Agile
[p0204-l0022] [14.4pt] processes of the development team and improves the overall Agile feedback
[p0204-l0023] [14.4pt] cycle because the architecture is more responsive to change. Just-in-time
[p0204-l0024] [14.4pt] architecture even lends itself to adding architecture tasks to a Kanban or
[p0204-l0025] [14.4pt] Scrum board.
[p0204-l0026] [14.4pt] Improved clarity
[p0204-l0027] [14.4pt] The phrase can’t see the forest for the trees could be applied to many sets of
[p0204-l0028] [14.4pt] documentation. When only what is important now (or before now) is
[p0204-l0029] [14.4pt] included in the documentation, you have less to search through to find what
[p0204-l0030] [14.4pt] you need.

=== PDF page 205 / printed label 205 ===
[p0205-l0001] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0205-l0002] [10.8pt] When documentation is incorrect, or its maintenance costs much more than it delivers, the net
[p0205-l0003] [10.8pt] overall value of the documentation will be negative.
[p0205-l0004] [14.4pt] If you are working in a waterfall environment, or an environment that is not
[p0205-l0005] [14.4pt] suited to regular incremental changes, it is possible and beneficial to apply just-
[p0205-l0006] [14.4pt] in-time architecture. You may be in the position of needing to architect the
[p0205-l0007] [14.4pt] whole system before any code is written, but you can still prioritize and order the
[p0205-l0008] [14.4pt] decisions that need to be made and the artifacts that need to be created. These
[p0205-l0009] [14.4pt] can be interspersed with research or proof-of-concept activities to get more up-
[p0205-l0010] [14.4pt] to-date information. Defer as much as you can and you may find everyone’s
[p0205-l0011] [14.4pt] processes becoming a little more agile.
[p0205-l0012] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0205-l0013] [10.8pt] You can combine just-in-time architecture with just-long-enough architecture: retire
[p0205-l0014] [10.8pt] documentation and artifacts that have served their purpose and make it obvious that they’re no
[p0205-l0015] [10.8pt] longer up-to-date. Just-long-enough architecture saves time you would have spent maintaining
[p0205-l0016] [10.8pt] content that is no longer useful and prevents readers from consuming out-of-date information.
[p0205-l0017] [14.4pt] Of course, trade-offs always exist. Here are some to take into account when
[p0205-l0018] [14.4pt] producing just-in-time architecture:
[p0205-l0019] [14.4pt] People who want the information now
[p0205-l0020] [14.4pt] There will always be someone who wants all the information as soon as
[p0205-l0021] [14.4pt] possible. These are the people you need to convince by doing. Tell them as
[p0205-l0022] [14.4pt] well, but show them the benefits by practicing just-in-time architecture as
[p0205-l0023] [14.4pt] much as possible. If they insist, you can give them an artifact that is very
[p0205-l0024] [14.4pt] obviously marked as a draft or pending and let them decide whether to base
[p0205-l0025] [14.4pt] any of their own work on draft or pending information.
[p0205-l0026] [14.4pt] People who want you to predict the future
[p0205-l0027] [14.4pt] You are probably asked to make predictions all the time. How long will this
[p0205-l0028] [14.4pt] take? Will we have the information we need by this date? Rally, an Agile

=== PDF page 206 / printed label 206 ===
[p0206-l0001] [14.4pt] software provider, has shown that the teams who estimate the least produce
[p0206-l0002] [14.4pt] the most.9 Your project manager needs to create their budget and report on
[p0206-l0003] [14.4pt] their milestones, but it is all just guesswork. Just-in-time architecture can
[p0206-l0004] [14.4pt] help with things like resource management in the long run because, in
[p0206-l0005] [14.4pt] general, everyone is more flexible to be able to take on unexpected work.
[p0206-l0006] [14.4pt] Aim to break down milestones into decisions and tasks to create artifacts to
[p0206-l0007] [14.4pt] help you determine if things are on track.
[p0206-l0008] [14.4pt] Losing important information because it isn’t relevant now
[p0206-l0009] [14.4pt] If you are focusing on making decisions and creating artifacts that are needed
[p0206-l0010] [14.4pt] now, you could easily lose important ideas and information that could be
[p0206-l0011] [14.4pt] relevant later. Create a place to record information that could be relevant in
[p0206-l0012] [14.4pt] the future (such as a page in a wiki). This should be checked when working
[p0206-l0013] [14.4pt] on subsequent artifacts and decisions.
[p0206-l0014] [14.4pt] WHY DEFER DECISIONS?
[p0206-l0015] [14.4pt] Deferring architecture decisions for as long as possible is good practice for a
[p0206-l0016] [14.4pt] few reasons:
[p0206-l0017] [14.4pt] Increased flexibility
[p0206-l0018] [14.4pt] By putting off a decision, you can more easily adapt to changing
[p0206-l0019] [14.4pt] requirements and circumstances.
[p0206-l0020] [14.4pt] Improved learning
[p0206-l0021] [14.4pt] Delaying a decision means more time for learning and experimentation.
[p0206-l0022] [14.4pt] Reduced risk
[p0206-l0023] [14.4pt] You reduce the risk of making incorrect or suboptimal decisions based
[p0206-l0024] [14.4pt] on incomplete or inaccurate information.
[p0206-l0025] [14.4pt] Improved collaboration
[p0206-l0026] [14.4pt] Having more time to collect information and advice means you are more
[p0206-l0027] [14.4pt] likely to gain input from a diverse set of people, and from people critical

=== PDF page 207 / printed label 207 ===
[p0207-l0001] [14.4pt] to the decision.
[p0207-l0002] [14.4pt] Reduced complexity
[p0207-l0003] [14.4pt] Deferring decisions generally reduces complexity because you focus on
[p0207-l0004] [14.4pt] only what is currently needed and don’t have to rewrite things that need
[p0207-l0005] [14.4pt] to be changed after decisions have been made. Refactoring to add
[p0207-l0006] [14.4pt] something is easier than rewriting to remove or make a change.
[p0207-l0007] [14.4pt] Increased efficiency
[p0207-l0008] [14.4pt] Deferring decisions leads to a more streamlined process and less wasted
[p0207-l0009] [14.4pt] effort. When a decision is made earlier than needed, it is more likely that
[p0207-l0010] [14.4pt] changes will affect that decision, meaning updates to code or designs
[p0207-l0011] [14.4pt] based on that decision. You may avoid needing to make some decisions
[p0207-l0012] [14.4pt] all together, when requirements or information change.
[p0207-l0013] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0207-l0014] [14.4pt] Your colleagues and peers are essential resources when it comes to managing
[p0207-l0015] [14.4pt] knowledge and documentation, and the techniques and patterns you have learned
[p0207-l0016] [14.4pt] in this chapter will enable you to utilize one another.
[p0207-l0017] [14.4pt] Now that you have higher-level principles in your toolbox and can leverage
[p0207-l0018] [14.4pt] people to support you, it is time to look at some common practices for
[p0207-l0019] [14.4pt] knowledge management and documentation and how you can make them
[p0207-l0020] [14.4pt] effective practices in your work.
[p0207-l0021] [10.8pt] 1 ADRs are a good technique to help identify issues before a big decision is made.
[p0207-l0022] [10.8pt] 2 See “ADRs” for more information on ADRs.
[p0207-l0023] [10.8pt] 3 For more on Markdown, see “All Documentation as Code”.
[p0207-l0024] [10.8pt] 4 You will find the same problem when adopting a proprietary tool or standard for most situations, not
[p0207-l0025] [10.8pt] just for diagramming.

=== PDF page 208 / printed label 208 ===
[p0208-l0001] [10.8pt] 5 If you have ever been in a situation where one person is using Microsoft Word and another is using
[p0208-l0002] [10.8pt] an application such as LibreOffice Writer to edit the same document, you will understand that the
[p0208-l0003] [10.8pt] Word format docx does not have high compatibility.
[p0208-l0004] [10.8pt] 6 The notification could be manual, but you want to automate everything you can.
[p0208-l0005] [10.8pt] 7 Gardening leave is a widely used term in the UK meaning a time period where an employee is
[p0208-l0006] [10.8pt] suspended from work on full pay until the end of their notice period. A company typically does this
[p0208-l0007] [10.8pt] when it thinks the employee may negatively influence the organization or aid a competitor.
[p0208-l0008] [10.8pt] 8 YAGNI is not an excuse not to follow best practices, though.
[p0208-l0009] [10.8pt] 9 Jeff Sutherland, inventor and cocreator of Scrum, says “estimating tasks will slow you down. Don’t
[p0208-l0010] [10.8pt] do it. We gave it up over 10 years ago. Today we have good data from Rally on 60,000 teams. The
[p0208-l0011] [10.8pt] slowest estimate tasks in hours. No estimation at all will improve team performance over hour
[p0208-l0012] [10.8pt] estimation.”

=== PDF page 209 / printed label 209 ===
[p0209-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 12. Effective Practices
[p0209-l0002] [14.4pt] Many methods, techniques, and practices for architecting and designing software
[p0209-l0003] [14.4pt] exist, but it is not enough to just follow them, create the outputs, and move on.
[p0209-l0004] [14.4pt] The artifacts you create must be constructed and used effectively to be of any
[p0209-l0005] [14.4pt] use in the overall architecture. The patterns in this chapter will enable you to put
[p0209-l0006] [14.4pt] the techniques discussed into effective practice.
[p0209-l0007] [20.16pt] ADRs
[p0209-l0008] [14.4pt] An architecture decision record (ADR) is a record of an architectural decision
[p0209-l0009] [14.4pt] and the reasoning behind it that can be used in the decision-making process
[p0209-l0010] [14.4pt] itself. ADRs are key to communicating architectural decisions made and the
[p0209-l0011] [14.4pt] outcome of the decision, not only to architects but to all stakeholders. Many
[p0209-l0012] [14.4pt] decisions are made throughout the life of a product or project, but without
[p0209-l0013] [14.4pt] documenting these decisions, they (and the reasoning that supports them) are
[p0209-l0014] [14.4pt] easily lost and forgotten.
[p0209-l0015] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0209-l0016] [10.8pt] ADRs were originally envisioned by Michael Nygard in 2011, and his introductory blog post is
[p0209-l0017] [10.8pt] structured as an ADR. His original ADR template is now often extended to record the
[p0209-l0018] [10.8pt] decision-making process as well as the decision made.
[p0209-l0019] [14.4pt] Here are some situations that ADRs help you avoid:
[p0209-l0020] [14.4pt] A decision is made early in the life of a product. Later, an individual or
[p0209-l0021] [14.4pt] team decides to change that decision. Without knowing how and why the
[p0209-l0022] [14.4pt] initial decision was made, the change may cause huge problems. Maybe a
[p0209-l0023] [14.4pt] particular technology was chosen to meet one or more requirements, and
[p0209-l0024] [14.4pt] overriding that choice means those requirements are no longer met. You
[p0209-l0025] [14.4pt] may not realize this until a lot of time and money have been wasted.

=== PDF page 210 / printed label 210 ===
[p0210-l0001] [14.4pt] What Birgitta Böckeler of Thoughtworks calls “whack-a-mole” decisions: a
[p0210-l0002] [14.4pt] decision is regularly brought up again and again, wasting time
[p0210-l0003] [14.4pt] reinvestigating only to discover that the original decision was made for
[p0210-l0004] [14.4pt] good reasons.
[p0210-l0005] [14.4pt] When onboarding, a new team member should have lots of questions about
[p0210-l0006] [14.4pt] how your team has done things and why. Current team members likely do
[p0210-l0007] [14.4pt] not have all the answers in their heads or want to spend their time
[p0210-l0008] [14.4pt] explaining things over and over again.
[p0210-l0009] [14.4pt] Someone leaves the company and takes all the architectural decision
[p0210-l0010] [14.4pt] knowledge and reasoning with them because it’s all in their head.
[p0210-l0011] [14.4pt] WHICH DECISIONS NEED AN ADR?
[p0210-l0012] [14.4pt] Write an ADR for the decision you are making in the following situations:
[p0210-l0013] [14.4pt] The decision will impact the way developers write the software.
[p0210-l0014] [14.4pt] The decision will be hard or expensive to change; therefore, it is
[p0210-l0015] [14.4pt] important to get the best option (and for everyone to understand why it
[p0210-l0016] [14.4pt] was made before it is possibly overturned in the future ).
[p0210-l0017] [14.4pt] The decision keeps being revisited, wasting time working out that there
[p0210-l0018] [14.4pt] were good reasons for the original decision.
[p0210-l0019] [14.4pt] The decision keeps coming up in questions from new team members.
[p0210-l0020] [14.4pt] You are adopting a decision made by another team or company (yes,
[p0210-l0021] [14.4pt] you should create your own ADR and reference the original ADR).
[p0210-l0022] [14.4pt] The decision will have a long-lasting impact and/or affect multiple
[p0210-l0023] [14.4pt] components or systems.
[p0210-l0024] [14.4pt] The decision will have an effect outside of your team (in this case, it is
[p0210-l0025] [14.4pt] wise to use the Consultation section to gather advice—see ADR
[p0210-l0026] [14.4pt] Stucture).
[p0210-l0027] [14.4pt] The decision is complex or hard to understand.
[p0210-l0028] [14.4pt] You are proposing a change informally or using a request for change

=== PDF page 211 / printed label 211 ===
[p0211-l0001] [14.4pt] (RFC). An ADR can be used to collect feedback (in the Consultation
[p0211-l0002] [14.4pt] section) and either replace a formal RFC or document the decision
[p0211-l0003] [14.4pt] made in an RFC (if the RFC is rejected, you can use rejected as your
[p0211-l0004] [14.4pt] status).
[p0211-l0005] [16.56pt] ADR Structure
[p0211-l0006] [14.4pt] A quick internet search will show you many opinions on how an ADR should be
[p0211-l0007] [14.4pt] structured. They are usually structured as individual files for each decision and
[p0211-l0008] [14.4pt] written in Markdown or Asciidoc, although you can use other methods (such as a
[p0211-l0009] [14.4pt] list in SharePoint or pages in a wiki). Adjust the style of your ADR template to
[p0211-l0010] [14.4pt] the storage method you are using.
[p0211-l0011] [14.4pt] My suggested structure for an ADR is shown in Example 12-1 (and was also
[p0211-l0012] [14.4pt] shown in Example 9-2 from “Use Reasoning and Argumentation”). The
[p0211-l0013] [14.4pt] headings in this structure are expanded upon in the following pages. You can see
[p0211-l0014] [14.4pt] examples of ADRs from Polyglot Media in “ADR Content”.
[p0211-l0015] [14.4pt] Example 12-1. ADR structure
[p0211-l0016] [10.8pt] # Identifier and Title (a statement of the decision made)
[p0211-l0017] [10.8pt] ## Status
[p0211-l0018] [10.8pt] Draft/Decided/Superseded by ADR-XXX
[p0211-l0019] [10.8pt] ## Context
[p0211-l0020] [10.8pt] Why you need to make the decision. Assumptions, constraints, and decision drivers.
[p0211-l0021] [10.8pt] ## Evaluation Criteria
[p0211-l0022] [10.8pt] What is important to you in making this decision?
[p0211-l0023] [10.8pt] Which of your architectural characteristics apply to making this decision?
[p0211-l0024] [10.8pt] Should any constraints or decision drivers become a criterion?
[p0211-l0025] [10.8pt] ## Options
[p0211-l0026] [10.8pt] Outlines of the options considered against the evaluation criteria (usually using a
[p0211-l0027] [10.8pt] score or rating), and trade-offs outside of the evaluation criteria.
[p0211-l0028] [10.8pt] ## Decision
[p0211-l0029] [10.8pt] The choice that was made and why.
[p0211-l0030] [10.8pt] ## Implications
[p0211-l0031] [10.8pt] The positive and negative consequences of the decision made.

=== PDF page 212 / printed label 212 ===
[p0212-l0001] [10.8pt] ## Consultation
[p0212-l0002] [10.8pt] If taking input from others, they should document it here. Details of those invited
[p0212-l0003] [10.8pt] to give input can be recorded, whether they provide input or not. Although
[p0212-l0004] [10.8pt] consultation takes place before a decision is made, it is documented at the end
[p0212-l0005] [10.8pt] because it can become long and obscure the decision itself.
[p0212-l0006] [14.4pt] Title and filename
[p0212-l0007] [14.4pt] The title and filename should start with an identifier and then the decision made
[p0212-l0008] [14.4pt] (for example, 001 Use event-driven architecture). Naming the file and top header
[p0212-l0009] [14.4pt] with the actual decision means that someone can read through the list of ADRs
[p0212-l0010] [14.4pt] and know what decisions were made, then read the file if they need more
[p0212-l0011] [14.4pt] information.
[p0212-l0012] [14.4pt] Status
[p0212-l0013] [14.4pt] This section tells the reader the current condition of the decision. You can define
[p0212-l0014] [14.4pt] a set of statuses to fit your workflow, but I recommend keeping the workflow
[p0212-l0015] [14.4pt] simple (as in Figure 12-1). Figure 12-2 is not recommended; it is unnecessarily
[p0212-l0016] [14.4pt] complex.
[p0212-l0017] [10.8pt] Figure 12-1. A simple (recommended) set of statuses for ADRs
[p0212-l0018] [10.8pt] Figure 12-2. A complex (not recommended) set of statuses for ADRs
[p0212-l0019] [14.4pt] Use the Draft status when the decision has not been finalized. Decided is the
[p0212-l0020] [14.4pt] next status, once the decision has been finalized.
[p0212-l0021] [14.4pt] A decision can also be changed. The original ADR should be immutable (not

=== PDF page 213 / printed label 213 ===
[p0213-l0001] [14.4pt] changed) except for the status because maintaining a history of the decision-
[p0213-l0002] [14.4pt] making process is important. If, for example, ADR 021 is given a status like
[p0213-l0003] [14.4pt] Decided, Supersedes ADR 001, then the status of ADR 001 should be changed to
[p0213-l0004] [14.4pt] something like Superseded by ADR 021. You may also want to include a date the
[p0213-l0005] [14.4pt] status was last changed.
[p0213-l0006] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0213-l0007] [10.8pt] I recommend using Decided instead of Accepted and Rejected statuses for ADRs. The decision
[p0213-l0008] [10.8pt] is always made and, most of the time, it is about selecting one of multiple options rather than a
[p0213-l0009] [10.8pt] yes/no decision. Decided simplifies the set of statuses and can be used for all types of
[p0213-l0010] [10.8pt] decisions. The actual decision will be reflected in the name of the ADR and under the Decision
[p0213-l0011] [10.8pt] heading.
[p0213-l0012] [14.4pt] Context
[p0213-l0013] [14.4pt] This section is where many other templates expect you to add things like the
[p0213-l0014] [14.4pt] options considered and any criteria. Example 12-1 breaks those out into their
[p0213-l0015] [14.4pt] own sections explicitly to make sure they are not left out. I have seen many
[p0213-l0016] [14.4pt] ADRs missing this key information. So the Context section should answer: why
[p0213-l0017] [14.4pt] does this decision need to be made, and in what environment is it being made?
[p0213-l0018] [14.4pt] You should include any assumptions, constraints, or decision drivers here as
[p0213-l0019] [14.4pt] further context.
[p0213-l0020] [14.4pt] Evaluation Criteria
[p0213-l0021] [14.4pt] This section is often left out of ADRs, but this is important so the reader
[p0213-l0022] [14.4pt] understands how the options were analyzed and compared. What is important to
[p0213-l0023] [14.4pt] consider in this decision?
[p0213-l0024] [14.4pt] If you have selected architecture characteristics (and if you haven’t, see
[p0213-l0025] [14.4pt] “Architecture Characteristics”), the ones applicable to this decision can be used
[p0213-l0026] [14.4pt] here to help you and your stakeholders make the decision. Your architecture
[p0213-l0027] [14.4pt] characteristics are your priorities, so they are a sensible place to start.
[p0213-l0028] [14.4pt] Other criteria you might consider are as follows:
[p0213-l0029] [14.4pt] Enterprise (or business) considerations (such as technological alignment)

=== PDF page 214 / printed label 214 ===
[p0214-l0001] [14.4pt] Constraints (such as applicable laws, General Data Protection Regulation
[p0214-l0002] [14.4pt] [GDPR], licenses, cost, and so on)
[p0214-l0003] [14.4pt] Particular security considerations or requirements (for example, data
[p0214-l0004] [14.4pt] residency and encryption at rest)
[p0214-l0005] [14.4pt] Other applicable functional requirements
[p0214-l0006] [14.4pt] Options
[p0214-l0007] [14.4pt] This section is fairly self-explanatory: which options are you considering when
[p0214-l0008] [14.4pt] making this decision? If this were a decision about the overall architecture style,
[p0214-l0009] [14.4pt] you might include modular monolith, event-driven, and pure microservices as
[p0214-l0010] [14.4pt] options. State these options and evaluate them using the evaluation criteria (see
[p0214-l0011] [14.4pt] “ADR Content” for an example of how to do this with a table). Visual scoring,
[p0214-l0012] [14.4pt] such as a star rating or Harvey balls, is a useful abstraction of your decision-
[p0214-l0013] [14.4pt] making process for quick reference later on (see “Visual Abstractions”).
[p0214-l0014] [14.4pt] You should also state the trade-offs of each option and their pros and cons. All
[p0214-l0015] [14.4pt] trade-offs should be stated and considered, whether they apply to your
[p0214-l0016] [14.4pt] evaluation criteria or not. You may find something important that influences the
[p0214-l0017] [14.4pt] evaluation criteria during the decision-making process or affects a revisit to this
[p0214-l0018] [14.4pt] decision in the future.
[p0214-l0019] [14.4pt] Decision
[p0214-l0020] [14.4pt] A simple statement of the decision made and why should be included in the
[p0214-l0021] [14.4pt] Decision section. This doesn’t need to be long and complicated because most
[p0214-l0022] [14.4pt] details about the decision are included under the other headings.
[p0214-l0023] [14.4pt] Implications
[p0214-l0024] [14.4pt] This section is important; you will state here both the positive and negative
[p0214-l0025] [14.4pt] consequences of your decision. There are no silver bullets in software
[p0214-l0026] [14.4pt] architecture, so there will always be a downside to your decision. The negatives
[p0214-l0027] [14.4pt] are warnings to the reader about what to look out for, and they show that you
[p0214-l0028] [14.4pt] took negative consequences into account. The positives will tell the reader why
[p0214-l0029] [14.4pt] you are accepting the negative consequences. Trade-offs will always be
[p0214-l0030] [14.4pt] necessary.

=== PDF page 215 / printed label 215 ===
[p0215-l0001] [14.4pt] Consultation
[p0215-l0002] [14.4pt] This section is optional, but architecture decisions are rarely made in isolation.
[p0215-l0003] [14.4pt] This is the place to note advice (not opinions). Either you or your contributors
[p0215-l0004] [14.4pt] can add it here. You can also add research and references here, such as a blog
[p0215-l0005] [14.4pt] post or article on the subject and the conclusion you drew from it.
[p0215-l0006] [14.4pt] Having this record of consultation keeps you accountable for making an
[p0215-l0007] [14.4pt] informed decision. You do not have to follow all the advice you receive (of
[p0215-l0008] [14.4pt] course, some of it may be conflicting), but you should have reasons (and state
[p0215-l0009] [14.4pt] them in the Decision section).
[p0215-l0010] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0215-l0011] [10.8pt] The Consultation section is used during the decision process despite it being last in the
[p0215-l0012] [10.8pt] template. It comes last because it can become quite long and would obscure other information
[p0215-l0013] [10.8pt] if placed earlier in the template.
[p0215-l0014] [16.56pt] ADR Content
[p0215-l0015] [14.4pt] When creating your ADR, it is important to remember what your teachers
[p0215-l0016] [14.4pt] always told you about showing your work. Don’t just state costs that you have
[p0215-l0017] [14.4pt] calculated, but include the figures you entered into the calculation and how you
[p0215-l0018] [14.4pt] calculated them (for example, the calculations themselves, or a link to an online
[p0215-l0019] [14.4pt] calculator and the date you used it—prices change).
[p0215-l0020] [14.4pt] The reader should not have to simply accept your calculated answer, and you do
[p0215-l0021] [14.4pt] not want to have to field questions about how you came to your answers if you
[p0215-l0022] [14.4pt] aren’t prepared. Remember that things can change over time, so it is important
[p0215-l0023] [14.4pt] that calculations can be repeated with new inputs or changes in prices.
[p0215-l0024] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0215-l0025] [10.8pt] The trade-off to showing your work is that your ADR can become long and noisy. Mitigate this
[p0215-l0026] [10.8pt] by linking to external data or calculations, or by using methods in the ADR file format you
[p0215-l0027] [10.8pt] have chosen to hide extra information (such as <details> and <summary> tags in HTML or
[p0215-l0028] [10.8pt] Markdown).

=== PDF page 216 / printed label 216 ===
[p0216-l0001] [14.4pt] The amount of detail included in your ADR should match the ramifications of
[p0216-l0002] [14.4pt] the decision. More important decisions should require more effort, but make sure
[p0216-l0003] [14.4pt] that all ADRs contain the necessary information to understand why an option
[p0216-l0004] [14.4pt] was chosen. The amount of detail in your ADR also depends on the purpose of
[p0216-l0005] [14.4pt] the record. Is it being used simply as a record or as a living document for
[p0216-l0006] [14.4pt] collaboration, persuasion, and decision making?
[p0216-l0007] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0216-l0008] [10.8pt] You should reference ADRs from your code and other documentation, including diagrams.
[p0216-l0009] [10.8pt] Make it easy for someone reading your documentation or diagram to find the ADR you
[p0216-l0010] [10.8pt] reference. Use links or references to ADRs to explain the reasoning behind the design or code
[p0216-l0011] [10.8pt] choices you have made.
[p0216-l0012] [14.4pt] Figures 12-3 and 12-4 show an example of an ADR used to influence
[p0216-l0013] [14.4pt] stakeholders and make a decision. The ADR is detailed, showing the reasoning
[p0216-l0014] [14.4pt] behind the scoring, and contains input from people involved in the consultation.
[p0216-l0015] [14.4pt] Figure 12-5 shows how the same decision might be recorded if the purpose were
[p0216-l0016] [14.4pt] to record the decision for future reference. You will need to decide what to
[p0216-l0017] [14.4pt] include based on the purpose of your ADR.1
[p0216-l0018] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0216-l0019] [10.8pt] Not all decisions are made openly, although they should be in most cases. If you have a
[p0216-l0020] [10.8pt] problem with individuals or groups making decisions without consulting others, ADRs can
[p0216-l0021] [10.8pt] help. You will need to ensure ADRs are part of a process, such as not signing off on a budget
[p0216-l0022] [10.8pt] until an ADR with suitable consultation has been decided. But beware of adding too much red
[p0216-l0023] [10.8pt] tape to the process or it will be circumnavigated.

=== PDF page 217 / printed label 217 ===
[p0217-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 12-3. ADR-044 Use an Event-Driven Distributed Architecture, part 1

=== PDF page 218 / printed label 218 ===
[p0218-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 12-4. ADR-044 Use an Event-Driven Distributed Architecture, part 2

=== PDF page 219 / printed label 219 ===
[p0219-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 12-5. ADR-044 Change to Event-Driven Architecture
[p0219-l0002] [16.56pt] ADR Storage

=== PDF page 220 / printed label 220 ===
[p0220-l0001] [14.4pt] The storage location of your ADRs is important because all stakeholders need to
[p0220-l0002] [14.4pt] have access, whether they are contributing to ADRs or reading them. This access
[p0220-l0003] [14.4pt] isn’t just for developers and architects.
[p0220-l0004] [14.4pt] Access needs to be permitted and easy. You may find it useful to have a central
[p0220-l0005] [14.4pt] place for all ADRs in the company. This approach allows everyone access to
[p0220-l0006] [14.4pt] learn from others’ decisions.
[p0220-l0007] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0220-l0008] [10.8pt] Your ADRs are related to, but should be kept separate from, any RAID log used for a project
[p0220-l0009] [10.8pt] or product.2 If D in your RAID log stands for decisions, you should link to where ADRs are
[p0220-l0010] [10.8pt] recorded from this part of your log. If D in your RAID log stands for dependencies, I
[p0220-l0011] [10.8pt] recommend adding a further D for decisions (turning it into a RAIDD log) and linking to your
[p0220-l0012] [10.8pt] ADRs. Keeping ADRs separate means they are easier to find and access.
[p0220-l0013] [14.4pt] The storage location and mechanism (for example, written in Markdown in a
[p0220-l0014] [14.4pt] wiki) must fit within the workflow of those who need to write, edit, and read
[p0220-l0015] [14.4pt] ADRs. For those who normally access a code repository, consider linking to the
[p0220-l0016] [14.4pt] ADR storage space in the Readme file and referencing specific ADRs from
[p0220-l0017] [14.4pt] documentation stored with the code when appropriate. Some people will never
[p0220-l0018] [14.4pt] touch a code repository or documentation stored there, and it should also be easy
[p0220-l0019] [14.4pt] for them find and edit ADRs.
[p0220-l0020] [14.4pt] The other consideration for storing ADRs is maintaining the data. If you are
[p0220-l0021] [14.4pt] hyperlinking between ADRs or to ADRs from other documentation and assets,
[p0220-l0022] [14.4pt] these links should never be broken. Ensure that you have a process in place to
[p0220-l0023] [14.4pt] find and fix broken links if ADRs are moved or renamed. Better still, use a tool
[p0220-l0024] [14.4pt] that does this for you.
[p0220-l0025] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0220-l0026] [10.8pt] Don’t lose your ADRs when a project ends. ADRs are specific to the product or system they
[p0220-l0027] [10.8pt] are made for, not a project that works on a product. Store ADRs against the product, not a
[p0220-l0028] [10.8pt] project (see “Products over Projects”).

=== PDF page 221 / printed label 221 ===
[p0221-l0001] [16.56pt] ADR Culture
[p0221-l0002] [14.4pt] The final part of ADR creation and maintenance is to create a culture and
[p0221-l0003] [14.4pt] expectation of creating and reading ADRs. How do you get your team or
[p0221-l0004] [14.4pt] colleagues to start creating ADRs? As with everything in software, it always
[p0221-l0005] [14.4pt] comes down to people. The answer to this question has two parts: educate them
[p0221-l0006] [14.4pt] and make it easy.
[p0221-l0007] [14.4pt] The first part is education: show them the benefits by creating ADRs for past and
[p0221-l0008] [14.4pt] new decisions. Telling people is one thing; showing them is quite another. It is
[p0221-l0009] [14.4pt] important to show them that ADRs apply to them, even if they are not architects
[p0221-l0010] [14.4pt] themselves:
[p0221-l0011] [14.4pt] When others ask a question, you can direct them to an ADR you have
[p0221-l0012] [14.4pt] written to find the answer.
[p0221-l0013] [14.4pt] If a discussion starts about making a change, you can mention an ADR of
[p0221-l0014] [14.4pt] the original decision to support the discussion.
[p0221-l0015] [14.4pt] The second part is making it easy: create ADRs using the systems and
[p0221-l0016] [14.4pt] applications your colleagues are already using. If your colleagues are using
[p0221-l0017] [14.4pt] Visual Studio Code or another IDE, use that to create the ADRs. If everyone
[p0221-l0018] [14.4pt] uses Notion, use that. You need to make it as effortless as possible for people to
[p0221-l0019] [14.4pt] add writing, contributing, and referring to ADRs to their usual processes. The
[p0221-l0020] [14.4pt] ADR template should be easy to fill in and understand, and everyone should
[p0221-l0021] [14.4pt] understand which parts are mandatory and which are not.
[p0221-l0022] [14.4pt] To help ADRs become part of your team and company culture, try these
[p0221-l0023] [14.4pt] approaches:
[p0221-l0024] [14.4pt] Add discussion of decisions to your weekly review meeting so that an ADR
[p0221-l0025] [14.4pt] can be created for a decision if required.
[p0221-l0026] [14.4pt] Make reviewing ADRs related to the product part of the onboarding process
[p0221-l0027] [14.4pt] to bring new team members up to speed quickly.
[p0221-l0028] [14.4pt] When someone asks a question that isn’t answered by an existing ADR,
[p0221-l0029] [14.4pt] consider whether a new ADR is needed. (See “Which Decisions Need an
[p0221-l0030] [14.4pt] ADR?”.)

=== PDF page 222 / printed label 222 ===
[p0222-l0001] [14.4pt] Bear in mind that behavior change takes time. Don’t give up early when
[p0222-l0002] [14.4pt] introducing ADRs to your team and company.
[p0222-l0003] [14.4pt] DECISION-MAKING MYTHS
[p0222-l0004] [14.4pt] Quashing some myths around decision making can lead to a clearer and
[p0222-l0005] [14.4pt] faster decision-making process.
[p0222-l0006] [14.4pt] Myth 1: Decision making is linear
[p0222-l0007] [14.4pt] Decision making should be an agile process, iterating over the ADR or
[p0222-l0008] [14.4pt] proposal until it is decided and revisiting past decisions as needed.
[p0222-l0009] [14.4pt] The longer you put off a decision, the more information you have to base
[p0222-l0010] [14.4pt] that decision on, but decisions cannot be put off forever. New
[p0222-l0011] [14.4pt] information arises and circumstances change, which may affect decisions
[p0222-l0012] [14.4pt] in progress or that have already been decided. This is a very good reason
[p0222-l0013] [14.4pt] to record decisions using ADRs. You want to be able to revisit ADRs and
[p0222-l0014] [14.4pt] reuse the information and analysis in them, saving effort on new
[p0222-l0015] [14.4pt] decisions.
[p0222-l0016] [14.4pt] Myth 2: Giving more choices is better for making decisions
[p0222-l0017] [14.4pt] Although you might think that presenting or gathering more options
[p0222-l0018] [14.4pt] improves your chances of finding the best one, research shows that
[p0222-l0019] [14.4pt] people are less likely to make a decision when given lots of options and
[p0222-l0020] [14.4pt] less likely to be happy with the result.3 All options have pros and cons,
[p0222-l0021] [14.4pt] and evaluating many options, each with its own pros and cons, is a much
[p0222-l0022] [14.4pt] higher mental load than fewer options.
[p0222-l0023] [14.4pt] Where possible, create an ADR or proposal with no more than three
[p0222-l0024] [14.4pt] options and use abstractions such as star ratings and tables to help
[p0222-l0025] [14.4pt] readers compare them.
[p0222-l0026] [14.4pt] Myth 3: The decision should be made by the most senior person
[p0222-l0027] [14.4pt] The most senior person, or person with the most authority, is not
[p0222-l0028] [14.4pt] necessarily the best person to have the final say. The person with the
[p0222-l0029] [14.4pt] most expertise, or who will be affected the most by the outcome, should
