[p0222-l0030] [14.4pt] be the decision owner and own the decision and the process of making it.

=== PDF page 223 / printed label 223 ===
[p0223-l0001] [14.4pt] The decision owner should understand the problem space and possible
[p0223-l0002] [14.4pt] solutions and use research and contributions from others to bolster their
[p0223-l0003] [14.4pt] knowledge. They will take the decision process through to a conclusion.
[p0223-l0004] [14.4pt] Myth 4: All stakeholders should be involved in the feedback process
[p0223-l0005] [14.4pt] Each stakeholder will be affected differently by the decision. The
[p0223-l0006] [14.4pt] decision owner should ensure that stakeholders who are important to the
[p0223-l0007] [14.4pt] success of implementing the decision have the chance to give input and
[p0223-l0008] [14.4pt] commit to whatever the outcome is.
[p0223-l0009] [14.4pt] Some stakeholders are useful to consult as you draft the ADR, helping to
[p0223-l0010] [14.4pt] identify concerns or holes in the proposed options. You will find that
[p0223-l0011] [14.4pt] some stakeholders do not need to be involved in the decision-making
[p0223-l0012] [14.4pt] process but will need to be informed of the outcome.
[p0223-l0013] [14.4pt] Myth 5: You should ask for all kinds of feedback
[p0223-l0014] [14.4pt] Be specific and tailor your requests for feedback to each stakeholder.
[p0223-l0015] [14.4pt] Generic feedback is often not useful for making a decision and can mean
[p0223-l0016] [14.4pt] relevant feedback is harder to find and use. Ask involved stakeholders to
[p0223-l0017] [14.4pt] do the following:
[p0223-l0018] [14.4pt] Let you know if anything in your ADR or proposal is unclear.
[p0223-l0019] [14.4pt] Ask clarifying questions if they need to.
[p0223-l0020] [14.4pt] Provide feedback only when they are sure they understand fully.
[p0223-l0021] [14.4pt] You can update the ADR (before it is decided) to answer questions from
[p0223-l0022] [14.4pt] stakeholders or to improve clarity. Once the decision is made,
[p0223-l0023] [14.4pt] communicate it to all stakeholders.
[p0223-l0024] [14.4pt] Myth 6: All stakeholders must agree on the outcome
[p0223-l0025] [14.4pt] Stakeholders need to commit to the outcome to make it successful but do
[p0223-l0026] [14.4pt] not all need to agree that the outcome is the best solution.
[p0223-l0027] [14.4pt] Once all relevant stakeholders have been consulted, it is the decision

=== PDF page 224 / printed label 224 ===
[p0224-l0001] [14.4pt] owner who will make the decision. The decision owner must then ask all
[p0224-l0002] [14.4pt] relevant stakeholders to either commit or not. Any stakeholder who
[p0224-l0003] [14.4pt] thinks the outcome is unsafe needs to explain why, and the decision
[p0224-l0004] [14.4pt] owner must work with them to address their concerns. Stakeholders can
[p0224-l0005] [14.4pt] also indicate if they agree or disagree with the decision, but commitment
[p0224-l0006] [14.4pt] is key to success.
[p0224-l0007] [14.4pt] When all relevant stakeholders have committed to the outcome, the
[p0224-l0008] [14.4pt] decision can be finalized. Then the decision will not be revisited unless
[p0224-l0009] [14.4pt] new information emerges, at which point a new decision process will
[p0224-l0010] [14.4pt] start, the outcome of which will override the original decision. In this
[p0224-l0011] [14.4pt] case, your original ADR’s status would change from Decided to
[p0224-l0012] [14.4pt] Superseded.
[p0224-l0013] [14.4pt] Myth 7: The decision owner can make a rational decision
[p0224-l0014] [14.4pt] In “Battling Bias”, I covered some of the biases that everyone is subject
[p0224-l0015] [14.4pt] to. The decision owner gets input from relevant stakeholders in an
[p0224-l0016] [14.4pt] attempt to mitigate at least some of these biases. You must be aware of
[p0224-l0017] [14.4pt] the biases you and others involved hold. Keeping these in mind will help,
[p0224-l0018] [14.4pt] but you can never totally eliminate bias.
[p0224-l0019] [14.4pt] As mentioned, requiring that the stakeholders involved commit rather
[p0224-l0020] [14.4pt] than agree is a way to mitigate groupthink. When members of the group
[p0224-l0021] [14.4pt] are not expecting full group consensus, they are more likely to express
[p0224-l0022] [14.4pt] dissenting viewpoints and critically analyze the options in front of them.
[p0224-l0023] [20.16pt] Architecture Characteristics
[p0224-l0024] [14.4pt] Architecture characteristics, also known as system quality attributes or quality
[p0224-l0025] [14.4pt] characteristics, are the priorities for your system or product. Taking the time to
[p0224-l0026] [14.4pt] pull these characteristics out of your analysis can enable you to design your
[p0224-l0027] [14.4pt] architecture to meet your stakeholders’ priorities. Recording them so they can be
[p0224-l0028] [14.4pt] used effectively will put you on the road to actually meeting these priorities.

=== PDF page 225 / printed label 225 ===
[p0225-l0001] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0225-l0002] [10.8pt] This section looks at how to create and record effective architecture characteristics. For a
[p0225-l0003] [10.8pt] deeper dive into architecture characteristics themselves, I recommend Chapter 4 of
[p0225-l0004] [10.8pt] Fundamentals of Software Architecture by Mark Richards and Neal Ford (O’Reilly, 2020).
[p0225-l0005] [14.4pt] Architecture characteristics can be created and applied whether you are
[p0225-l0006] [14.4pt] practicing an approach such as DDD or another formal or informal method for
[p0225-l0007] [14.4pt] analyzing the problem space and creating an architecture that solves that
[p0225-l0008] [14.4pt] problem. The documented requirements are a good place to start drawing out the
[p0225-l0009] [14.4pt] architectural characteristics, and this includes functional and nonfunctional
[p0225-l0010] [14.4pt] requirements. Architecture characteristics are often associated with
[p0225-l0011] [14.4pt] nonfunctional requirements (requirements for the operation of a system rather
[p0225-l0012] [14.4pt] than the behavior) and are like high-level forms of nonfunctional requirements.
[p0225-l0013] [14.4pt] Nonfunctional requirements can cause confusion and headaches for even the
[p0225-l0014] [14.4pt] most seasoned architect, business analyst, or project manager. They can seem
[p0225-l0015] [14.4pt] intangible, and it is hard to know where to start. Effective architecture
[p0225-l0016] [14.4pt] characteristics will become a compass when making project, architecture, and
[p0225-l0017] [14.4pt] design decisions. They communicate the priorities of the system or product to all
[p0225-l0018] [14.4pt] stakeholders and become the cornerstone of decision making.
[p0225-l0019] [14.4pt] Defining one ultimate list of architecture characteristics is impossible because
[p0225-l0020] [14.4pt] every situation is different; as with so many things in software architecture, it
[p0225-l0021] [14.4pt] depends. But although a definitive list doesn’t exist, you can create a good
[p0225-l0022] [14.4pt] enough list that fits your company and/or product. Here are some examples that
[p0225-l0023] [14.4pt] you can take and develop into a custom list of characteristics for your situation:
[p0225-l0024] [14.4pt] Accessibility
[p0225-l0025] [14.4pt] The ability of users of all abilities to successfully use the system
[p0225-l0026] [14.4pt] Availability
[p0225-l0027] [14.4pt] The ability of the system to be usable by users when they need it
[p0225-l0028] [14.4pt] Configurability
[p0225-l0029] [14.4pt] The ability of the end user to make easy changes to elements of the software

=== PDF page 226 / printed label 226 ===
[p0226-l0001] [14.4pt] Continuity
[p0226-l0002] [14.4pt] The ability to recover from disaster
[p0226-l0003] [14.4pt] Extensibility
[p0226-l0004] [14.4pt] The ease of adding new functionality
[p0226-l0005] [14.4pt] Portability
[p0226-l0006] [14.4pt] The ability of the system to run on multiple platforms
[p0226-l0007] [14.4pt] Privacy
[p0226-l0008] [14.4pt] The ability of the system to hide data from internal and external users
[p0226-l0009] [14.4pt] Scalability
[p0226-l0010] [14.4pt] The ability of the system to operate consistently as the number of users
[p0226-l0011] [14.4pt] increases
[p0226-l0012] [14.4pt] EXAMPLES OF ARCHITECTURE CHARACTERISTIC LISTS
[p0226-l0013] [14.4pt] For more ideas on what to consider for your list of architecture
[p0226-l0014] [14.4pt] characteristics, the following define their own lists:
[p0226-l0015] [14.4pt] Wikipedia defines a list of system quality attributes.
[p0226-l0016] [14.4pt] ISO/IEC 25010 defines a list of quality characteristics.
[p0226-l0017] [14.4pt] Mark Richards defines a list of architecture characteristics in his
[p0226-l0018] [14.4pt] Architecture Characteristics Worksheet online.
[p0226-l0019] [14.4pt] Mark Richards and Neal Ford define a (partial) list of architecture
[p0226-l0020] [14.4pt] characteristics in Chapter 4 of Fundamentals of Software Architecture.
[p0226-l0021] [14.4pt] Mark Richards and Neal Ford recommend choosing no more than seven
[p0226-l0022] [14.4pt] architecture characteristics for your product or system. Your product owner
[p0226-l0023] [14.4pt] would likely choose everything from a list presented to them, but your design
[p0226-l0024] [14.4pt] cannot meet every single architecture characteristic, so you need to prioritize.

=== PDF page 227 / printed label 227 ===
[p0227-l0001] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0227-l0002] [10.8pt] When you list architecture characteristics for your company, make sure to define each one so
[p0227-l0003] [10.8pt] that everyone has a clear understanding of what each means.
[p0227-l0004] [14.4pt] The good news is that some architecture characteristics can be considered
[p0227-l0005] [14.4pt] implicit because they are always important. These do not need to be counted
[p0227-l0006] [14.4pt] toward your seven, but you can include them in your top seven if you think they
[p0227-l0007] [14.4pt] are critical enough to prioritize for your product or system.
[p0227-l0008] [14.4pt] Here are some architecture characteristics that you can consider implicit:
[p0227-l0009] [14.4pt] Feasibility
[p0227-l0010] [14.4pt] Whether the solution is possible given the constraints present (money, time,
[p0227-l0011] [14.4pt] resources, and so on)
[p0227-l0012] [14.4pt] Maintainability
[p0227-l0013] [14.4pt] The ability to efficiently maintain the system
[p0227-l0014] [14.4pt] Security
[p0227-l0015] [14.4pt] The ability of the system to remain inaccessible to those without
[p0227-l0016] [14.4pt] authorization
[p0227-l0017] [14.4pt] Simplicity
[p0227-l0018] [14.4pt] Whether the system is simple and uncomplicated
[p0227-l0019] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0227-l0020] [10.8pt] Cost can also be considered separately from feasibility as an implicit architecture
[p0227-l0021] [10.8pt] characteristic. Most businesses aim to save money in one way or another, so cost is always
[p0227-l0022] [10.8pt] going to be an important consideration, especially if shareholders are in the equation.
[p0227-l0023] [14.4pt] Consider defining architecture characteristics to be an agile process. It is
[p0227-l0024] [14.4pt] important to note that they can change with time. Scalability may not make the
[p0227-l0025] [14.4pt] top seven priorities at the beginning of a product’s life, but it could become more

=== PDF page 228 / printed label 228 ===
[p0228-l0001] [14.4pt] important as the user base grows. Security is not likely to become less important,
[p0228-l0002] [14.4pt] but it may become more important if the product starts to store personally
[p0228-l0003] [14.4pt] identifiable information or new legislation comes into effect. In that case,
[p0228-l0004] [14.4pt] security may need to be bumped from the implicit characteristics into the top
[p0228-l0005] [14.4pt] seven for design changes to be prioritized and effective.
[p0228-l0006] [14.4pt] As the product or system moves through the phases of initial product, growth,
[p0228-l0007] [14.4pt] and optimization, you should revisit the architectural characteristics and assess
[p0228-l0008] [14.4pt] whether their importance has changed (see Figure 12-6). Defined business
[p0228-l0009] [14.4pt] milestones could trigger this review, or a review could be set for every x months,
[p0228-l0010] [14.4pt] depending on the timescales you are working within.
[p0228-l0011] [10.8pt] Figure 12-6. Using architecture characteristics as the product lifecycle progresses
[p0228-l0012] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0228-l0013] [10.8pt] Once you have defined architecture characteristics for the product or system, you should use
[p0228-l0014] [10.8pt] them to develop and back up the rest of your design for the system’s architecture. One example
[p0228-l0015] [10.8pt] of this is using architecture characteristics as criteria in decision making in ADRs.
[p0228-l0016] [14.4pt] How you record your architecture characteristics, and the analysis that got you
[p0228-l0017] [14.4pt] there, will impact their effectiveness. Writing a list of your top seven

=== PDF page 229 / printed label 229 ===
[p0229-l0001] [14.4pt] characteristics and any implicit characteristics is the bare minimum. Table 12-1
[p0229-l0002] [14.4pt] shows an example of how to record effective architecture characteristics in a
[p0229-l0003] [14.4pt] table.
[p0229-l0004] [14.4pt] Table 12-1. Effective architecture characteristics sample from Polyglot Media
[p0229-l0005] [14.4pt] ID
[p0229-l0006] [14.4pt] Characteristic
[p0229-l0007] [14.4pt] Applicable to
[p0229-l0008] [14.4pt] Source
[p0229-l0009] [14.4pt] AC01
[p0229-l0010] [14.4pt] Auditability
[p0229-l0011] [14.4pt] Media Service
[p0229-l0012] [14.4pt] REQ 014 The
[p0229-l0013] [14.4pt] system will record
[p0229-l0014] [14.4pt] access and use of all
[p0229-l0015] [14.4pt] media for analysis
[p0229-l0016] [14.4pt] REQ 025 and REQ
[p0229-l0017] [14.4pt] 026
[p0229-l0018] [14.4pt] AC02
[p0229-l0019] [14.4pt] Fault-tolerance
[p0229-l0020] [14.4pt] Payment Interface,
[p0229-l0021] [14.4pt] External Media

[p0229-l0023] [14.4pt] Customer API,
[p0229-l0024] [14.4pt] Customer UI

[p0229-l0031] [14.4pt] new external media
[p0229-l0032] [14.4pt] source to the system
[p0229-l0033] [14.4pt] To record effective architecture characteristics, try the following:
[p0229-l0034] [14.4pt] Indicate which area(s) of the system the characteristics apply to
[p0229-l0035] [14.4pt] This might be all areas but is more likely a subset. This can be a general
[p0229-l0036] [14.4pt] statement initially and can be updated to specific services, data stores, or
[p0229-l0037] [14.4pt] components when these are identified.
[p0229-l0038] [14.4pt] Record the source(s) of the architecture characteristic
[p0229-l0039] [14.4pt] This may be a requirement (it is good practice for requirements to have an
[p0229-l0040] [14.4pt] identity and for you to reference this), or it might be a record of your analysis
[p0229-l0041] [14.4pt] or reference to the output of an EventStorming or domain storytelling

=== PDF page 230 / printed label 230 ===
[p0230-l0001] [14.4pt] session. You can also document any reasoning for including this
[p0230-l0002] [14.4pt] characteristic that is not included in the sources you have indicated.
[p0230-l0003] [14.4pt] Give each characteristic an identity
[p0230-l0004] [14.4pt] The identity can be used to refer to the characteristic in other parts of your
[p0230-l0005] [14.4pt] documentation. This includes ADRs, where characteristics can be used as
[p0230-l0006] [14.4pt] decision criteria, and diagrams.
[p0230-l0007] [14.4pt] State all characteristics that were considered
[p0230-l0008] [14.4pt] List all the characteristics that were considered and/or link to the master list
[p0230-l0009] [14.4pt] of architecture characteristics. It is good practice to document any
[p0230-l0010] [14.4pt] characteristics that nearly entered the top seven so that they can be
[p0230-l0011] [14.4pt] considered easily when reviewing the characteristics at a later date.
[p0230-l0012] [14.4pt] Record a history using dates
[p0230-l0013] [14.4pt] Include the original date, last updated date, and next review date so that
[p0230-l0014] [14.4pt] anyone referencing the characteristics knows whether they are up-to-date and
[p0230-l0015] [14.4pt] still valid.
[p0230-l0016] [14.4pt] Identify the top three priorities
[p0230-l0017] [14.4pt] It can help to identify a top three from your selected architecture
[p0230-l0018] [14.4pt] characteristics (not necessarily in order) to help prioritize decisions made
[p0230-l0019] [14.4pt] based on these characteristics. If you do this, work with stakeholders to
[p0230-l0020] [14.4pt] discuss and choose the top three and document the chosen priorities and
[p0230-l0021] [14.4pt] reasoning.
[p0230-l0022] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0230-l0023] [10.8pt] An ADR is an effective structure for recording your decisions about architecture
[p0230-l0024] [10.8pt] characteristics.4 If you are using ADRs to record decisions, you could use one to record your
[p0230-l0025] [10.8pt] architecture characteristic selection. When reviewing your architectural characteristics, you
[p0230-l0026] [10.8pt] can supersede the original ADR with a new one if changes are made.
[p0230-l0027] [20.16pt] All Documentation as Code

=== PDF page 231 / printed label 231 ===
[p0231-l0001] [14.4pt] Documentation as code as a set of principles can be applied to all
[p0231-l0002] [14.4pt] documentation, not just technical documentation. The standard definition of
[p0231-l0003] [14.4pt] documentation as code is to create your documentation in the same environment
[p0231-l0004] [14.4pt] or IDE that you use to create your code, using a markup language such as
[p0231-l0005] [14.4pt] Markdown, and store it in version control, automating most processes.
[p0231-l0006] [14.4pt] Documentation as code can also mean that your documentation is automatically
[p0231-l0007] [14.4pt] generated.
[p0231-l0008] [16.56pt] Technical Documentation
[p0231-l0009] [14.4pt] For documentation that naturally sits near the code, such as documentation of the
[p0231-l0010] [14.4pt] code that is written by the same people who write the code, using
[p0231-l0011] [14.4pt] documentation-as-code processes has many benefits:
[p0231-l0012] [14.4pt] A documentation culture
[p0231-l0013] [14.4pt] When documentation is easy and part of the standard workflow, it is much
[p0231-l0014] [14.4pt] more likely to be created and kept up-to-date.
[p0231-l0015] [14.4pt] Improved accuracy
[p0231-l0016] [14.4pt] If you subject documentation to the same quality control processes as code,
[p0231-l0017] [14.4pt] such as pull requests, code reviews, and tests, you reduce the risk of errors.
[p0231-l0018] [14.4pt] More efficient workflows
[p0231-l0019] [14.4pt] Integrating documentation as code into your workflows streamlines your
[p0231-l0020] [14.4pt] technical documentation process, compared to writing it separately.
[p0231-l0021] [14.4pt] Easier maintenance
[p0231-l0022] [14.4pt] As long as you don’t add too much red tape to your documentation process,
[p0231-l0023] [14.4pt] such as over-the-top approvals, it is easier to maintain technical
[p0231-l0024] [14.4pt] documentation as code because of simpler formats, like Markdown, and not
[p0231-l0025] [14.4pt] having to change tools.
[p0231-l0026] [14.4pt] Increased flexibility
[p0231-l0027] [14.4pt] Formats such as Markdown and Asciidoc can be easily and automatically
[p0231-l0028] [14.4pt] converted (either using a specialist tool or a pipeline) into a range of formats

=== PDF page 232 / printed label 232 ===
[p0232-l0001] [14.4pt] (such as HTML or PDF) if you need to publish the documentation.
[p0232-l0002] [14.4pt] Better documentation discoverability
[p0232-l0003] [14.4pt] When documentation is stored in the same place as the code, or in a known
[p0232-l0004] [14.4pt] repository, it is easy to find what you need.
[p0232-l0005] [14.4pt] Better collaboration
[p0232-l0006] [14.4pt] Storing documentation in version control makes it simple for multiple people
[p0232-l0007] [14.4pt] to make changes to a single file and merge all the changes. An approval
[p0232-l0008] [14.4pt] process adds more automated collaboration.
[p0232-l0009] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0232-l0010] [10.8pt] Why should you produce technical documentation? In addition to making upskilling any
[p0232-l0011] [10.8pt] developer on a part of your codebase much easier and more efficient, external users or
[p0232-l0012] [10.8pt] customers often need it. How well a software product or API is documented can be a huge
[p0232-l0013] [10.8pt] factor for those choosing vendors, even outweighing cost and performance factors.
[p0232-l0014] [14.4pt] The documentation process generally follows the steps shown in Figure 12-7:
[p0232-l0015] [14.4pt] authoring, conversion, and publishing. Authoring is done using your IDE and the
[p0232-l0016] [14.4pt] review process in your version control system. Verification can be a part of the
[p0232-l0017] [14.4pt] review process or be performed separately in your pipeline. Conversion (for
[p0232-l0018] [14.4pt] example, from Markdown to HTML) is performed either automatically by your
[p0232-l0019] [14.4pt] pipeline or manually, usually using a third-party tool. Publishing is then handled,
[p0232-l0020] [14.4pt] either automatically or manually, by your pipeline.

=== PDF page 233 / printed label 233 ===
[p0233-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 12-7. Documentation-as-code overview
[p0233-l0002] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0233-l0003] [10.8pt] Consider whether you need to draft a version of the documentation for review before
[p0233-l0004] [10.8pt] deployment. The choice will depend on how rigorous your review and verification process is,
[p0233-l0005] [10.8pt] how well your conversion process works (for instance, does something look bad or break if not
[p0233-l0006] [10.8pt] formatted in a certain way?), and whether your documentation is public. A static site generator,
[p0233-l0007] [10.8pt] such as Hugo, can be useful for conversion and deployment in this situation because you can
[p0233-l0008] [10.8pt] take advantage of its draft status for pages and the ability for a test build to include draft pages.
[p0233-l0009] [14.4pt] You can put documentation as code into practice in a way that suits your
[p0233-l0010] [14.4pt] processes and environment based on the following principles:
[p0233-l0011] [14.4pt] Documentation source files (like Markdown) are stored in a version control
[p0233-l0012] [14.4pt] system (like Git).
[p0233-l0013] [14.4pt] Documentation is reviewed by a trusted set of reviewers (such as
[p0233-l0014] [14.4pt] developers and technical writers).

=== PDF page 234 / printed label 234 ===
[p0234-l0001] [14.4pt] Documentation is verified both by reviewers (for accuracy and sense) and
[p0234-l0002] [14.4pt] automatically (for syntax and similar), for example, using tests.
[p0234-l0003] [14.4pt] Documentation artifacts are built automatically (for example, an HTML
[p0234-l0004] [14.4pt] version of the documentation).
[p0234-l0005] [14.4pt] Artifacts are published without much human intervention.
[p0234-l0006] [14.4pt] Automate a documentation-as-code process with the same system(s) that
[p0234-l0007] [14.4pt] automate the testing, building, and deployment of code. This means that you are
[p0234-l0008] [14.4pt] not duplicating anything and are therefore saving time and effort. Reviews
[p0234-l0009] [14.4pt] should be handled in the same way as a code review for the same reason.
[p0234-l0010] [14.4pt] The simplest form of documentation as code is to include documentation as
[p0234-l0011] [14.4pt] Markdown files in the same repository as the code. The documentation is then
[p0234-l0012] [14.4pt] available to anyone who clones the repository and can be viewed in a text editor,
[p0234-l0013] [14.4pt] browser, or any application that supports Markdown. If you use GitHub to
[p0234-l0014] [14.4pt] manage your Git repository, you can take advantage of GitHub Pages, which
[p0234-l0015] [14.4pt] displays your documentation in your chosen theme and makes it easily
[p0234-l0016] [14.4pt] accessible.
[p0234-l0017] [14.4pt] Treating your documentation as code means that you can write tests that will run
[p0234-l0018] [14.4pt] automatically in the same way as for your code (and you can include these tests
[p0234-l0019] [14.4pt] in the same place as your code tests for extra efficiency and easier determination
[p0234-l0020] [14.4pt] of build success). Two tests useful in most documentation are checking for
[p0234-l0021] [14.4pt] broken links and readability issues.
[p0234-l0022] [14.4pt] When adding diagrams in documentation as code, use a text-based diagram tool,
[p0234-l0023] [14.4pt] such as Mermaid or PlantUML. Creating diagrams using a text-based tool makes
[p0234-l0024] [14.4pt] it easier to use version control because the diagrams can be compared in the
[p0234-l0025] [14.4pt] same way as plain text.5 When deciding which tool to use, bear in mind the
[p0234-l0026] [14.4pt] types of output that can be produced, whether your conversion and publishing
[p0234-l0027] [14.4pt] tools support it, and how easy it is to write the language required by the diagram
[p0234-l0028] [14.4pt] tool.6
[p0234-l0029] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0234-l0030] [10.8pt] When generating and deploying documentation along with code, consider the automation you
[p0234-l0031] [10.8pt] need to make it practical. If you support five versions of your product, do you need to maintain

=== PDF page 235 / printed label 235 ===
[p0235-l0001] [10.8pt] five versions of documentation? Should a pull request involving only documentation be
[p0235-l0002] [10.8pt] automatically approved if it blocks the deployment pipeline? Consider your unique situation to
[p0235-l0003] [10.8pt] find the balance.
[p0235-l0004] [16.56pt] Automatically Generated Documentation
[p0235-l0005] [14.4pt] For code that changes regularly (and any documentation that refers to the details
[p0235-l0006] [14.4pt] of the code, like variable or method names), automatic generation is the best way
[p0235-l0007] [14.4pt] to handle documentation. Depending on your code language and environment,
[p0235-l0008] [14.4pt] you have many options for automatically generating code documentation.
[p0235-l0009] [14.4pt] When deploying an API with automatically generated documentation, the whole
[p0235-l0010] [14.4pt] deployment process is much faster because the documentation doesn’t have to be
[p0235-l0011] [14.4pt] manually updated for end users, whether those are customers or internal users.
[p0235-l0012] [14.4pt] Autogeneration can also help developers understand a part of the codebase that
[p0235-l0013] [14.4pt] they are not familiar with (and maybe no one currently on the team is familiar
[p0235-l0014] [14.4pt] with).
[p0235-l0015] [14.4pt] Documentation generators do have trade-offs and limitations. They can tell you
[p0235-l0016] [14.4pt] only about the code. A documentation generator cannot tell you about business
[p0235-l0017] [14.4pt] requirements or why something was or wasn’t implemented in a certain way. It
[p0235-l0018] [14.4pt] cannot tell you why. Important code will be given the same precedence as
[p0235-l0019] [14.4pt] unimportant or boilerplate code. Depending on the generator, some of these
[p0235-l0020] [14.4pt] limitations can be minimized by using appropriate comments or settings, but in
[p0235-l0021] [14.4pt] general, you will need more than automatically generated code for your technical
[p0235-l0022] [14.4pt] documentation.
[p0235-l0023] [14.4pt] Some generators use static analysis of the code (examining the source code
[p0235-l0024] [14.4pt] without executing the program), and some require comments or inline
[p0235-l0025] [14.4pt] documentation in the code to generate the documentation. This is something to
[p0235-l0026] [14.4pt] bear in mind when selecting a tool. If you can find a tool that meets your
[p0235-l0027] [14.4pt] documentation needs and doesn’t require you to add anything to your code, that
[p0235-l0028] [14.4pt] is likely preferable to one that requires you to annotate all your current and
[p0235-l0029] [14.4pt] future code. If an annotation is missing, that code will be missing from the
[p0235-l0030] [14.4pt] documentation. Add checks to code reviews or pull requests to ensure the
[p0235-l0031] [14.4pt] annotations are present or write automatic fitness functions to catch this.
[p0235-l0032] [14.4pt] When choosing a tool, keep your audience in mind. What do they need? Why are

=== PDF page 236 / printed label 236 ===
[p0236-l0001] [14.4pt] you creating this documentation? Your audience for automatically generated
[p0236-l0002] [14.4pt] code is going to be technical. If it isn’t, consider another way to create
[p0236-l0003] [14.4pt] documentation because automatically generated documentation is all about the
[p0236-l0004] [14.4pt] code. It will be optimized for developers who need to work with the code or API
[p0236-l0005] [14.4pt] that is being documented.
[p0236-l0006] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0236-l0007] [10.8pt] As Kevlin Henney supposedly put it, “Six hours of debugging can save you five minutes of
[p0236-l0008] [10.8pt] reading documentation.”7 Let’s optimize that documentation and make it accessible so that
[p0236-l0009] [10.8pt] those hours are not wasted.
[p0236-l0010] [14.4pt] AI is one of the newest ways of generating documentation. If you use tools such
[p0236-l0011] [14.4pt] as Visual Studio Code, you can find extensions that use AI to add documentation
[p0236-l0012] [14.4pt] to code. GitHub Copilot is another tool that can generate comment
[p0236-l0013] [14.4pt] documentation in code.
[p0236-l0014] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0236-l0015] [10.8pt] When using AI to generate documentation or anything else, you should check the output
[p0236-l0016] [10.8pt] carefully. AI tools are still in their infancy, even compared to standard automatic
[p0236-l0017] [10.8pt] documentation generation tools, and confidently produce convincing outputs that are
[p0236-l0018] [10.8pt] completely wrong.
[p0236-l0019] [16.56pt] Other Documentation
[p0236-l0020] [14.4pt] Much documentation is business related, or technical but not related directly to
[p0236-l0021] [14.4pt] the code, including requirements, architecture characteristics, and ADRs. These
[p0236-l0022] [14.4pt] types of documentation have many trade-offs when it comes to documentation as
[p0236-l0023] [14.4pt] code, but you can still get the benefits if you apply the principles and bear these
[p0236-l0024] [14.4pt] trade-offs in mind.
[p0236-l0025] [14.4pt] The people who write business-related documentation and artifacts don’t
[p0236-l0026] [14.4pt] necessarily know how to use the IDE the developers use and are unlikely to
[p0236-l0027] [14.4pt] know the ins and outs of version control. You want to make documentation an
[p0236-l0028] [14.4pt] easy process so it becomes part of the culture of your team and company, and

=== PDF page 237 / printed label 237 ===
[p0237-l0001] [14.4pt] forcing people to upskill in areas not particularly connected to their job does not
[p0237-l0002] [14.4pt] accomplish that goal. You need to apply the principles without forcing
[p0237-l0003] [14.4pt] nondevelopers into the developer world.
[p0237-l0004] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0237-l0005] [10.8pt] Don’t treat all documentation the same. It has different audiences, different authors, and
[p0237-l0006] [10.8pt] different reasons for existing. You can still apply the documentation-as-code principles, but
[p0237-l0007] [10.8pt] don’t make the documentation process harder for anyone.
[p0237-l0008] [14.4pt] All documentation can be written using plain text, and Markdown is an excellent
[p0237-l0009] [14.4pt] option for nontechnical writers to pick up easily. Many what you see is what you
[p0237-l0010] [14.4pt] get (WYSIWYG) editor options are available for Markdown, giving a more
[p0237-l0011] [14.4pt] Microsoft Word–like experience.
[p0237-l0012] [14.4pt] Nontechnical writers can be introduced to concepts such as writing in small
[p0237-l0013] [14.4pt] chunks, getting feedback, and then integrating the new work into their current
[p0237-l0014] [14.4pt] work. These processes can be applied even without version control to content
[p0237-l0015] [14.4pt] that is not plain text. If using Google Docs or Microsoft Word, you can use
[p0237-l0016] [14.4pt] comments to get feedback and accept or reject changes to a document.
[p0237-l0017] [14.4pt] You can apply version control to all documentation if you use the appropriate
[p0237-l0018] [14.4pt] tools. Nontechnical authors won’t want to use the command line, but it would
[p0237-l0019] [14.4pt] likely not take much training to get them to use a tool such as GitHub Desktop or
[p0237-l0020] [14.4pt] a tool with Git or another form of versioning built in, such as GitBook.
[p0237-l0021] [14.4pt] Developers or technical writers could easily create meta documentation on how
[p0237-l0022] [14.4pt] to use a version control tool and your documentation-as-code process.
[p0237-l0023] [14.4pt] Behind the scenes, without intervention from nontechnical authors, you can
[p0237-l0024] [14.4pt] apply the principle of building and publishing documentation. If version control
[p0237-l0025] [14.4pt] is used, it can be a pipeline similar to the one used for technical documentation.
[p0237-l0026] [14.4pt] If version control is not used, you can, for example, design a batch process that
[p0237-l0027] [14.4pt] runs on the files in a folder.
[p0237-l0028] [14.4pt] Diagrams in technical documentation-as-code would normally be created using
[p0237-l0029] [14.4pt] Mermaid or a similar text-to-diagram tool. Although text-based diagrams lend
[p0237-l0030] [14.4pt] themselves better to being stored in version control, they are not the only way to
[p0237-l0031] [14.4pt] version diagrams. For nontechnical writers, learning to write diagrams as text is

=== PDF page 238 / printed label 238 ===
[p0238-l0001] [14.4pt] likely a step too far. Instead, you can include the original diagram files with any
[p0238-l0002] [14.4pt] generated files, such as PNGs, and the documentation text files. Anyone with
[p0238-l0003] [14.4pt] access to the files (be they in a repository or just a folder) will be able to
[p0238-l0004] [14.4pt] maintain those diagrams. Some formats, such as draw.io, are stored as text so can
[p0238-l0005] [14.4pt] even be compared in a similar way to plain text.
[p0238-l0006] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0238-l0007] [10.8pt] You should be able to integrate technical writers into the same system that your developers are
[p0238-l0008] [10.8pt] using if they are authoring documentation. Learning version control, Asciidoc or Markdown,
[p0238-l0009] [10.8pt] and Mermaid or a similar tool, has a smaller learning curve and is much more relevant to a
[p0238-l0010] [10.8pt] technical writer’s role than to a business analyst or project manager, for example.
[p0238-l0011] [14.4pt] OPEN SOURCE TOOLS FOR DOCS AS CODE
[p0238-l0012] [14.4pt] I recommend the following open source tools for generating documentation:
[p0238-l0013] [14.4pt] Docusaurus
[p0238-l0014] [14.4pt] Creates documentation sites from Markdown files.
[p0238-l0015] [14.4pt] MkDocs
[p0238-l0016] [14.4pt] Builds project documentation.
[p0238-l0017] [14.4pt] Docsify
[p0238-l0018] [14.4pt] Generates your documentation website on the fly from Markdown files.
[p0238-l0019] [14.4pt] Backstage
[p0238-l0020] [14.4pt] Builds developer portals and was originally developed at Spotify.
[p0238-l0021] [14.4pt] docToolchain
[p0238-l0022] [14.4pt] A collection of scripts that makes it easy to create and maintain powerful
[p0238-l0023] [14.4pt] technical documentation.
[p0238-l0024] [14.4pt] The following are automatic documentation generators:

=== PDF page 239 / printed label 239 ===
[p0239-l0001] [14.4pt] Doxygen
[p0239-l0002] [14.4pt] Generates documentation from various popular coding languages.
[p0239-l0003] [14.4pt] Swagger
[p0239-l0004] [14.4pt] An open source (and professional) tool set for developing and
[p0239-l0005] [14.4pt] documenting APIs.
[p0239-l0006] [14.4pt] docfx
[p0239-l0007] [14.4pt] Converts .Net assembly, XML code comments, REST API Swagger files,
[p0239-l0008] [14.4pt] and Markdown into HTML, JSON, or PDF files.
[p0239-l0009] [14.4pt] phpDocumentor
[p0239-l0010] [14.4pt] Generates documentation automatically for PHP projects, including
[p0239-l0011] [14.4pt] UML class diagrams.
[p0239-l0012] [14.4pt] Slate
[p0239-l0013] [14.4pt] Generates responsive API documentation.
[p0239-l0014] [14.4pt] Magidoc
[p0239-l0015] [14.4pt] A static documentation website generator for GraphQL.
[p0239-l0016] [14.4pt] And these tools help you write diagrams as code:
[p0239-l0017] [14.4pt] Mermaid
[p0239-l0018] [14.4pt] A JavaScript-based diagramming and charting tool.
[p0239-l0019] [14.4pt] PlantUML
[p0239-l0020] [14.4pt] Creates UML diagrams with support for many non-UML diagrams too.
[p0239-l0021] [14.4pt] GraphViz
[p0239-l0022] [14.4pt] Represents structural information as diagrams of abstract graphs and
[p0239-l0023] [14.4pt] networks.
[p0239-l0024] [14.4pt] Kroki

=== PDF page 240 / printed label 240 ===
[p0240-l0001] [14.4pt] Creates diagrams from text descriptions.
[p0240-l0002] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0240-l0003] [14.4pt] You may have already used the practices in this chapter, but now you know how
[p0240-l0004] [14.4pt] to use them effectively and get the best return on investment for your time and
[p0240-l0005] [14.4pt] energy. Others may be reticent to try what you have learned, especially in this
[p0240-l0006] [14.4pt] area of knowledge management and documentation, which many people seem to
[p0240-l0007] [14.4pt] dislike. Show (rather than tell) how things can be better, and they should come
[p0240-l0008] [14.4pt] around to this better way of working.
[p0240-l0009] [14.4pt] What you have learned about knowledge management in Part III applies to in-
[p0240-l0010] [14.4pt] person, hybrid, and fully remote methods of working. In Part IV, you will find
[p0240-l0011] [14.4pt] many patterns, antipatterns, and techniques that will enhance your hybrid, fully
[p0240-l0012] [14.4pt] remote, and in-person communication.
[p0240-l0013] [10.8pt] 1 Templates for ADRs are available in the Appendix A and on the book’s website, where text versions
[p0240-l0014] [10.8pt] of Figures 12-3, 12-4, and 12-5 are also available.
[p0240-l0015] [10.8pt] 2 RAID, sometimes called DAIR, can stand for different things depending on who is using the log. R
[p0240-l0016] [10.8pt] is usually risks, A can be actions or assumptions, I is usually issues, and D can be decisions or
[p0240-l0017] [10.8pt] dependencies. To include all of these, some people use a RAAIDD log.
[p0240-l0018] [10.8pt] 3 This is often referred to as the paradox of choice, which is also the name of a book by Barry
[p0240-l0019] [10.8pt] Schwartz on the subject (Harper Perennial, 2004).
[p0240-l0020] [10.8pt] 4 See “ADRs”.
[p0240-l0021] [10.8pt] 5 Some diagramming applications also save data in a text-based format (for example, draw.io).
[p0240-l0022] [10.8pt] 6 For example, PlantUML has its own domain-specific language whereas Mermaid uses “Markdown-
[p0240-l0023] [10.8pt] inspired text definitions.”
[p0240-l0024] [10.8pt] 7 This quote is often attributed to Kevlin Henney, but the origin is unclear, and it may have been said
[p0240-l0025] [10.8pt] or written by others as well.

=== PDF page 241 / printed label 241 ===
[p0241-l0001] [28.8pt] Part IV. Communicating Remotely
[p0241-l0002] [14.4pt] Many modern teams are distributed in nature, either fully remote or hybrid, and
[p0241-l0003] [14.4pt] can be spread across the globe. Creating software products in this environment
[p0241-l0004] [14.4pt] requires different communication patterns than for a group of people sharing the
[p0241-l0005] [14.4pt] same room and whiteboard. Whether you are communicating with colleagues,
[p0241-l0006] [14.4pt] customers, or other businesses, you need to consider many factors, including
[p0241-l0007] [14.4pt] time, work patterns, culture, inclusivity, and the channels you use to
[p0241-l0008] [14.4pt] communicate.
[p0241-l0009] [14.4pt] Distributed communication has advantages and disadvantages compared to in-
[p0241-l0010] [14.4pt] person. You can exploit or mitigate these to get the best outcomes for your teams
[p0241-l0011] [14.4pt] and organization by applying the patterns and techniques presented in Part IV.
[p0241-l0012] [14.4pt] Before you dive in, I want to define synchronous and asynchronous
[p0241-l0013] [14.4pt] communication, which are covered in depth throughout the following chapters:
[p0241-l0014] [14.4pt] Asynchronous communication does not assume or expect the recipient to
[p0241-l0015] [14.4pt] pick up or respond to the message as soon as it is received. This type of
[p0241-l0016] [14.4pt] communication usually completes within hours or days, as opposed to
[p0241-l0017] [14.4pt] seconds or minutes.1
[p0241-l0018] [14.4pt] Synchronous communication relies on all parties being available at the same
[p0241-l0019] [14.4pt] time and responding in real time. This type of communication completes
[p0241-l0020] [14.4pt] within seconds or minutes.
[p0241-l0021] [10.8pt] 1 By completes, I mean that the message is received and understood, and any needed response is sent,
[p0241-l0022] [10.8pt] received, and understood.

=== PDF page 242 / printed label 242 ===
[p0242-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 13. Remote Time
[p0242-l0002] [14.4pt] Taking both time and energy into consideration is necessary when
[p0242-l0003] [14.4pt] communicating with colleagues, other businesses, and customers. Whether you
[p0242-l0004] [14.4pt] are working remotely or in an office, you likely need to communicate with
[p0242-l0005] [14.4pt] someone in another time zone or who works different hours.
[p0242-l0006] [14.4pt] Working across time zones and with multiple working patterns has benefits and
[p0242-l0007] [14.4pt] downsides. For example, you could hand over work to a team in a time zone
[p0242-l0008] [14.4pt] behind yours at the end of your working day so that it does not sit idle until you
[p0242-l0009] [14.4pt] start work again. You will also find that you end up with a more diverse set of
[p0242-l0010] [14.4pt] ideas and practices from a more diverse group of colleagues, escaping from local
[p0242-l0011] [14.4pt] echo chambers to see things differently. On the other hand, you may get
[p0242-l0012] [14.4pt] frustrated when someone you need a response from has already finished work
[p0242-l0013] [14.4pt] for the day, or expend a lot of effort finding a time that all required meeting
[p0242-l0014] [14.4pt] attendees are available for a meeting, due to lack of overlapping working hours.
[p0242-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0242-l0016] [10.8pt] Whether you are communicating asynchronously or synchronously, you should aim to
[p0242-l0017] [10.8pt] communicate regularly to keep everyone updated.
[p0242-l0018] [14.4pt] This chapter explores patterns that will help you overcome hurdles with time,
[p0242-l0019] [14.4pt] working patterns, and your and others’ energy and productivity so that your
[p0242-l0020] [14.4pt] remote and hybrid communication becomes more efficient.
[p0242-l0021] [20.16pt] Synchronize Time
[p0242-l0022] [14.4pt] For those who live in a country that falls completely within one time zone, it is
[p0242-l0023] [14.4pt] easy to forget that some countries have many time zones, and it is therefore more
[p0242-l0024] [14.4pt] common to need to consider time zones than you might think. Companies are
[p0242-l0025] [14.4pt] increasingly taking advantage of a global talent pool, which results in ever-
[p0242-l0026] [14.4pt] increasing differences in start and end times for people working at the same

=== PDF page 243 / printed label 243 ===
[p0243-l0001] [14.4pt] company or on the same team.
[p0243-l0002] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0243-l0003] [10.8pt] Besides time-zone confusion, consider date confusion. In the US, it is most common to specify
[p0243-l0004] [10.8pt] dates as month-day-year, whereas the most common format globally is day-month-year.1
[p0243-l0005] [10.8pt] Depending on your location, the date 10-11-2023 could be read as November 10, 2023 or
[p0243-l0006] [10.8pt] October 11, 2023. To avoid confusion, use a word or abbreviation for the month (10-Nov
[p0243-l0007] [10.8pt] 2023) or use the ISO 8601 year-month-day (2023-11-10).2
[p0243-l0008] [16.56pt] Time Zone
[p0243-l0009] [14.4pt] If multiple time zones are involved, communicating clearly about time-zone and
[p0243-l0010] [14.4pt] working-hour boundaries becomes critical. These boundaries must be respected
[p0243-l0011] [14.4pt] for everyone, including the need for breaks. Encourage everyone to
[p0243-l0012] [14.4pt] communicate about issues with scheduling so that these can be addressed. When
[p0243-l0013] [14.4pt] everyone feels respected and listened to, you will have a happier and more
[p0243-l0014] [14.4pt] productive team.
[p0243-l0015] [14.4pt] When scheduling or discussing deadlines, all parties must understand exactly
[p0243-l0016] [14.4pt] when you are talking about. If you don’t specify a time zone, you may end up
[p0243-l0017] [14.4pt] with various assumptions. One person may think you mean their time zone,
[p0243-l0018] [14.4pt] another might think you mean the time zone you are currently in, and another
[p0243-l0019] [14.4pt] might think you mean UTC (coordinated universal time).
[p0243-l0020] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0243-l0021] [10.8pt] In some countries, it is far less common to use the 24-hour clock. To avoid confusion, specify
[p0243-l0022] [10.8pt] a.m. and p.m. and only the hours 1–12 (for example, 1 p.m. UTC rather than 13:00 UTC).
[p0243-l0023] [14.4pt] For an idea of how much confusion not specifying a time zone could cause, look
[p0243-l0024] [14.4pt] at Figure 13-1. The world has many time zones, and the implementation of zone
[p0243-l0025] [14.4pt] borders varies immensely (a person’s longitude can’t necessarily be used to work
[p0243-l0026] [14.4pt] out which time zone they are in).

=== PDF page 244 / printed label 244 ===
[p0244-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 13-1. Map of current de facto time zones, courtesy of Wikimedia
[p0244-l0002] [14.4pt] Make sure you specify the time zone (and, conversely, ask for clarification if
[p0244-l0003] [14.4pt] someone else doesn’t specify the time zone). It is good practice to do this even if
[p0244-l0004] [14.4pt] you know everyone involved is in the same time zone as you. It creates an
[p0244-l0005] [14.4pt] expectation and means you are less likely to forget to do it when needed. You
[p0244-l0006] [14.4pt] also never know when someone in another time zone might become involved in
[p0244-l0007] [14.4pt] the discussion.
[p0244-l0008] [14.4pt] To improve communication across time zones, specify the recipient’s time zone
[p0244-l0009] [14.4pt] rather than your own (or include both). When communicating with a group
[p0244-l0010] [14.4pt] covering more than one time zone, you can either specify each time zone (for
[p0244-l0011] [14.4pt] example, 9 a.m. PST/12 p.m. EST/5 p.m. GMT) or use one reference time zone
[p0244-l0012] [14.4pt] (such as 5 p.m. UTC).
[p0244-l0013] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0244-l0014] [10.8pt] You and your company should consider which reference time zone is the best standard to use
[p0244-l0015] [10.8pt] and review that decision as needed. UTC is a good option; it doesn’t change, and other time
[p0244-l0016] [10.8pt] zones are often expressed in terms of UTC (such as EST: UTC-5:00 and IST: UTC+5:30).
[p0244-l0017] [10.8pt] Some time zones observe summer time or daylight saving time, and don’t necessarily switch
[p0244-l0018] [10.8pt] over at the same time, adding to possible miscommunication twice per year.
[p0244-l0019] [14.4pt] Keeping time zones in mind helps avoid accidents like scheduling a stand-up

=== PDF page 245 / printed label 245 ===
[p0245-l0001] [14.4pt] meeting at 6:30 a.m. for your colleague that seems to be perfectly reasonable at
[p0245-l0002] [14.4pt] 9:30 a.m. in your time zone. Or scheduling a video call with a colleague when it
[p0245-l0003] [14.4pt] is 10 p.m. for them.
[p0245-l0004] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0245-l0005] [10.8pt] Identify your colleagues’ availability. You may think that 6:30 a.m. is not appropriate for your
[p0245-l0006] [10.8pt] colleague in another time zone, but they may have shifted their working hours and therefore be
[p0245-l0007] [10.8pt] working then and happy to meet. Don’t make assumptions.
[p0245-l0008] [14.4pt] Here are some useful techniques and tools for keeping time zones front-of-mind:
[p0245-l0009] [14.4pt] Add others’ time zones to your calendar application. Google Calendar
[p0245-l0010] [14.4pt] allows you to add a secondary time zone and edit the labels of your primary
[p0245-l0011] [14.4pt] and secondary time zones. Outlook Calendar allows you to add up to two
[p0245-l0012] [14.4pt] additional time zones and change their labels.
[p0245-l0013] [14.4pt] World Time Buddy is a website and mobile app that allows you to add
[p0245-l0014] [14.4pt] multiple time zones and easily compare times or periods of time between
[p0245-l0015] [14.4pt] them. You can view specific dates, which is useful for scheduling around
[p0245-l0016] [14.4pt] times of the year when time zones are switching to summer or back to
[p0245-l0017] [14.4pt] standard time. You can add your Google Calendar to see your events
[p0245-l0018] [14.4pt] alongside time zones and use tabs to create groups of time zones for easy
[p0245-l0019] [14.4pt] reference.
[p0245-l0020] [14.4pt] World Clock Meeting Planner is a website with lots of features to help you
[p0245-l0021] [14.4pt] organize events across multiple time zones. It uses colors to show morning,
[p0245-l0022] [14.4pt] daytime, evening, and nighttime in each time zone, including red to indicate
[p0245-l0023] [14.4pt] weekends and public holidays. You can create calendar invitations and polls
[p0245-l0024] [14.4pt] so attendees can vote on their preference from the times you select.
[p0245-l0025] [14.4pt] To avoid putting unintended pressure on colleagues to reply to emails or
[p0245-l0026] [14.4pt] accept meetings during nonworking hours, add a statement to your email
[p0245-l0027] [14.4pt] signature and the bottom of your meeting request indicating you do not
[p0245-l0028] [14.4pt] expect them to reply or accept the meeting outside of their normal working
[p0245-l0029] [14.4pt] hours. Ask for suggested times if they need to reject a meeting.

=== PDF page 246 / printed label 246 ===
[p0246-l0001] [14.4pt] Add your time zone(s), along with your working hours, to your email
[p0246-l0002] [14.4pt] signature so that others can use this information when communicating and
[p0246-l0003] [14.4pt] setting up meetings with you.
[p0246-l0004] [14.4pt] Set up office hours in any applications you use, such as Outlook, Google
[p0246-l0005] [14.4pt] Calendar, and Slack. Encourage colleagues to do the same. This will make
[p0246-l0006] [14.4pt] it less likely that you will try to schedule something outside their working
[p0246-l0007] [14.4pt] hours.
[p0246-l0008] [14.4pt] When looking for free time in a colleague’s calendar, check to make sure it
[p0246-l0009] [14.4pt] is an acceptable time in their time zone, in case they haven’t set up office
[p0246-l0010] [14.4pt] hours in their calendar, before sending the meeting request.
[p0246-l0011] [14.4pt] Instant messaging tools such as Teams or Slack show the status of each
[p0246-l0012] [14.4pt] user. Pay attention to these when sending messages to manage your
[p0246-l0013] [14.4pt] expectations, and state that you don’t expect a response outside of their
[p0246-l0014] [14.4pt] office hours if they are not online (as they still may be notified). Many apps
[p0246-l0015] [14.4pt] allow you to customize the status or message shown, so you can help others
[p0246-l0016] [14.4pt] manage their expectations when communicating with you.
[p0246-l0017] [14.4pt] Embrace and coordinate virtual tools for project management, processes,
[p0246-l0018] [14.4pt] and communication. Those working together should use the same tools in
[p0246-l0019] [14.4pt] these cases as far as possible. It is OK to use a different IDE to code in, or a
[p0246-l0020] [14.4pt] different app to draw diagrams, but for communication and ticket or task
[p0246-l0021] [14.4pt] management, it is critical to use the same apps to coordinate.
[p0246-l0022] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0246-l0023] [10.8pt] When setting up automatic alerts or other notifications from applications or cloud
[p0246-l0024] [10.8pt] infrastructure, you can take both time zone and urgency of the notification into account. If the
[p0246-l0025] [10.8pt] notification doesn’t need immediate attention, it could be scheduled to be sent during the
[p0246-l0026] [10.8pt] receiver’s working hours to avoid unnecessary disturbance. Email notifications could be sent
[p0246-l0027] [10.8pt] to a shared mailbox so that those who are online at the time can deal with the message.
[p0246-l0028] [14.4pt] If you commonly have meetings or calls in a particular time zone or group of
[p0246-l0029] [14.4pt] time zones, make a note of the best times to have those meetings. This can be a
[p0246-l0030] [14.4pt] reference for all those involved in scheduling and can prompt negotiations if
[p0246-l0031] [14.4pt] scheduling the meeting within everyone’s working hours is not possible.

=== PDF page 247 / printed label 247 ===
[p0247-l0001] [14.4pt] If time zones are far apart, communicate asynchronously as much as possible,
[p0247-l0002] [14.4pt] but bear in mind the pitfalls: this type of communication is not a silver bullet
[p0247-l0003] [14.4pt] (see “Async to Think”).
[p0247-l0004] [16.56pt] Empathy and Compromise
[p0247-l0005] [14.4pt] Empathy is hugely important when it comes to coordinating across time zones. If
[p0247-l0006] [14.4pt] a synchronous meeting is required and one or more people will be
[p0247-l0007] [14.4pt] inconvenienced, you can show empathy and increase fairness in the following
[p0247-l0008] [14.4pt] ways:
[p0247-l0009] [14.4pt] Compromise by taking turns. If one person or team ends up working late for
[p0247-l0010] [14.4pt] one meeting, another person or team could work early for the next meeting.
[p0247-l0011] [14.4pt] Inconvenience should be compensated, especially for company-wide or
[p0247-l0012] [14.4pt] company-mandated meetings, but also for regular meetings. This could be
[p0247-l0013] [14.4pt] paid overtime, time off in lieu/comp time,3 expensed meal, or another type
[p0247-l0014] [14.4pt] of compensation. Giving people more than one option for compensation is
[p0247-l0015] [14.4pt] another way to show empathy and create goodwill.
[p0247-l0016] [14.4pt] Record the meeting so that those who cannot attend can catch up during
[p0247-l0017] [14.4pt] their working hours.
[p0247-l0018] [14.4pt] If one or more people cannot attend, make sure that they have input into
[p0247-l0019] [14.4pt] any decisions that come out of the meeting.
[p0247-l0020] [14.4pt] Use asynchronous communication before and after the meeting to keep
[p0247-l0021] [14.4pt] everyone in the loop.
[p0247-l0022] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0247-l0023] [10.8pt] Plan ahead for meetings that involve two or more time zones, particularly if the time zones are
[p0247-l0024] [10.8pt] far apart. Take extra care to make sure everyone has the authorization to access any
[p0247-l0025] [10.8pt] documents, tools, or other resources. If someone can’t gain access, they may have to wait a
[p0247-l0026] [10.8pt] long time for you to be available to fix the problem, possibly delaying the meeting itself.
[p0247-l0027] [14.4pt] To show respect for others’ working hours, and to also protect your own,
[p0247-l0028] [14.4pt] automate messages and emails so that they arrive during the receivers’ office

=== PDF page 248 / printed label 248 ===
[p0248-l0001] [14.4pt] hours. This way you do not accidentally disturb your colleagues if they have left
[p0248-l0002] [14.4pt] their notifications on, or put pressure on them to reply outside of their working
[p0248-l0003] [14.4pt] hours.
[p0248-l0004] [14.4pt] Protect your own time by not logging in outside of your working hours to send a
[p0248-l0005] [14.4pt] message at a good time for your colleague, and set an example of how others
[p0248-l0006] [14.4pt] should respect your working hours. Outlook and Gmail both offer a scheduling
[p0248-l0007] [14.4pt] feature for email. Teams, Slack, Telegram, and Signal allow you to schedule
[p0248-l0008] [14.4pt] messages.
[p0248-l0009] [16.56pt] Split Shifts
[p0248-l0010] [14.4pt] Split shifts are an option to help with time-zone coverage. If you need someone
[p0248-l0011] [14.4pt] to be available for at least some of the time that others are working in another
[p0248-l0012] [14.4pt] time zone, a standard 9 a.m.–5 p.m. schedule may not work.
[p0248-l0013] [14.4pt] For some employees, a split shift for either some or all workdays could help
[p0248-l0014] [14.4pt] overcome time-zone challenges. It is important to be inclusive and remember
[p0248-l0015] [14.4pt] that some people have other commitments, such as children. A split shift might
[p0248-l0016] [14.4pt] work well for someone with a commitment within standard office hours for their
[p0248-l0017] [14.4pt] time zone. For example, a parent may benefit from working in the morning, then
[p0248-l0018] [14.4pt] caring for children until their bedtime, and then working their remaining hours in
[p0248-l0019] [14.4pt] the evening, at the same time as colleagues in another time zone.
[p0248-l0020] [14.4pt] A manager living and working on the US East Coast may have a team in India,
[p0248-l0021] [14.4pt] for example. Because of the 10.5-hour time difference, 5:30 p.m. for the team in
[p0248-l0022] [14.4pt] India is 7 a.m. for the manager in the US. Although most communication could
[p0248-l0023] [14.4pt] take place asynchronously, in this case, the manager might choose to work one
[p0248-l0024] [14.4pt] or two days per week beginning at 6 a.m. and schedule meetings with the team
[p0248-l0025] [14.4pt] in India from 6 to 7 a.m. US Eastern time.
[p0248-l0026] [14.4pt] When working a split shift, it is important to block out your nonworking hours in
[p0248-l0027] [14.4pt] your calendar on any split or shifted days. Then you won’t get booked into
[p0248-l0028] [14.4pt] meetings during your nonworking hours (which are working hours for others in
[p0248-l0029] [14.4pt] your time zone).
[p0248-l0030] [14.4pt] TIP

=== PDF page 249 / printed label 249 ===
[p0249-l0001] [10.8pt] If you need a quick reply from someone in another time zone, aim to contact them as early in
[p0249-l0002] [10.8pt] their working hours as possible so that they have more opportunity to see your message and
[p0249-l0003] [10.8pt] respond. It is also a good idea to mark the urgency in the subject line if sending an email, or
[p0249-l0004] [10.8pt] early in the message if instant messaging.
[p0249-l0005] [14.4pt] Regardless of time zone, showing respect, empathy, and inclusiveness when
[p0249-l0006] [14.4pt] communicating with others is important. A lack of work-life balance impairs
[p0249-l0007] [14.4pt] health and risks burnout, potentially affecting your own work and that of your
[p0249-l0008] [14.4pt] colleagues. To get the best contribution from all, create boundaries and establish
[p0249-l0009] [14.4pt] etiquette. Your teams will be welcoming and more diverse, and you and your
[p0249-l0010] [14.4pt] company will benefit.
[p0249-l0011] [14.4pt] DAYLIGHT SAVING TIME/SUMMER TIME
[p0249-l0012] [14.4pt] Some countries observe daylight saving time (DST), also known as summer
[p0249-l0013] [14.4pt] time, which means that relative time differences change when these
[p0249-l0014] [14.4pt] observances come into effect. The switch can be especially confusing
[p0249-l0015] [14.4pt] because some countries, such as Canada and the US, don’t uniformly
[p0249-l0016] [14.4pt] observe DST; some states or provinces remain in the same time zone year-
[p0249-l0017] [14.4pt] round. Increasing the complexity, different countries switch over on different
[p0249-l0018] [14.4pt] dates.
[p0249-l0019] [14.4pt] The best way to avoid confusion is to check the regions that you work with
[p0249-l0020] [14.4pt] to identify any times of the year that might become confusing and add a
[p0249-l0021] [14.4pt] recurring event to your calendar (like “US DST starts”) that will serve as a
[p0249-l0022] [14.4pt] reminder to you. Ensure that reminder does not mark you as unavailable or
[p0249-l0023] [14.4pt] out of office. Adding time zones to your calendar and using time-zone
[p0249-l0024] [14.4pt] comparison tools will also help.
[p0249-l0025] [14.4pt] Suppose you have teams working in the US and European Union (EU). Most
[p0249-l0026] [14.4pt] US states observe DST from the second Sunday in March at 2 a.m. until the
[p0249-l0027] [14.4pt] first Sunday in November at 2 a.m. The EU, UK, Norway, and Switzerland
[p0249-l0028] [14.4pt] observe DST from the last Sunday in March at 1 a.m. UTC until the last
[p0249-l0029] [14.4pt] Sunday in October at 1 a.m. UTC. Therefore, for several weeks each year,
[p0249-l0030] [14.4pt] the time difference between multiple time zones in the US and multiple time
[p0249-l0031] [14.4pt] zones in the EU varies.
[p0249-l0032] [14.4pt] For example, the Eastern US is four hours behind the UK when the UK is

=== PDF page 250 / printed label 250 ===
[p0250-l0001] [14.4pt] not yet observing DST in March, but the Eastern US is five hours behind
[p0250-l0002] [14.4pt] when the UK starts observing DST as well. The time difference between the
[p0250-l0003] [14.4pt] two time zones changes by one hour for the week spanning from the end of
[p0250-l0004] [14.4pt] October to the beginning of November also.
[p0250-l0005] [14.4pt] And remember that you can’t just rely on the time zone to work out whose
[p0250-l0006] [14.4pt] time will change relative to yours. If your team in London works with one
[p0250-l0007] [14.4pt] team in Salt Lake City, Utah, and one team in Phoenix, Arizona, the two US
[p0250-l0008] [14.4pt] teams will have an hour’s time difference for roughly half the year beacuse
[p0250-l0009] [14.4pt] Arizona does not observe DST (Figure 13-2).
[p0250-l0010] [14.4pt] Many countries that have observed DST in the past have since abolished the
[p0250-l0011] [14.4pt] observance, either settling for permanent standard time or permanent DST.
[p0250-l0012] [14.4pt] Countries that observe DST now could stop doing so in the future too.
[p0250-l0013] [14.4pt] All these factors underscore why it is best practice to include the time zone
[p0250-l0014] [14.4pt] when storing time and date in a data store.
[p0250-l0015] [10.8pt] Figure 13-2. Relative time differences between London, Phoenix, and Salt Lake City when London and
[p0250-l0016] [10.8pt] Salt Lake City are observing DST (World Time Buddy)
[p0250-l0017] [20.16pt] Respect Working Patterns
[p0250-l0018] [14.4pt] Your colleagues, whatever time zone they work in, may have a different working
[p0250-l0019] [14.4pt] pattern than you. This may be their preference, to fit around family
[p0250-l0020] [14.4pt] commitments, to align with colleagues working in another time zone, or to
[p0250-l0021] [14.4pt] accommodate business and customer needs. Working patterns can change daily
[p0250-l0022] [14.4pt] and are affected by leave and public holidays. Resist assuming that others are
[p0250-l0023] [14.4pt] available at the same times as you.
[p0250-l0024] [14.4pt] You and your colleagues have various commitments outside of work that may
[p0250-l0025] [14.4pt] mean planned or unplanned changes to working hours. Picking up children from
[p0250-l0026] [14.4pt] school could mean a regular break in afternoon hours, or an occasional need to

=== PDF page 251 / printed label 251 ===
[p0251-l0001] [14.4pt] take that break if a partner or regular childcare is not available. Plan for
[p0251-l0002] [14.4pt] schedules to change and you will not be disappointed.
[p0251-l0003] [16.56pt] Communicate Availability
[p0251-l0004] [14.4pt] As discussed earlier in this section, you should encourage everyone to block out
[p0251-l0005] [14.4pt] time in their calendars when they are not available and set office hours in
[p0251-l0006] [14.4pt] calendar and messaging apps. Simply marking the time as “Not Available” rather
[p0251-l0007] [14.4pt] than giving full details is perfectly acceptable and will make everyone more
[p0251-l0008] [14.4pt] comfortable.
[p0251-l0009] [14.4pt] Stating your working hours and days in the signature of your email, as
[p0251-l0010] [14.4pt] recommended, is extremely helpful if your working hours differ from the norm.
[p0251-l0011] [14.4pt] Example 13-1 shows how you can do this. Noting that you do not expect replies
[p0251-l0012] [14.4pt] outside of the recipient’s working hours implicitly communicates that they
[p0251-l0013] [14.4pt] should not expect a reply from you outside of your working hours.
[p0251-l0014] [14.4pt] Example 13-1. Email signature indicating working hours
[p0251-l0015] [10.8pt] ---
[p0251-l0016] [10.8pt] I understand that your working hours may differ from mine and I
[p0251-l0017] [10.8pt] do not expect a reply outside of your working hours.
[p0251-l0018] [10.8pt] My working hours (GMT/BST):
[p0251-l0019] [10.8pt] Mon-Thu 9 a.m. to 3 p.m. and 4.30 p.m. to 6.30 p.m.
[p0251-l0020] [10.8pt] Fri 10 a.m. to 2 p.m.
[p0251-l0021] [14.4pt] Think about key times in the day that could be hot spots for conflict and try to
[p0251-l0022] [14.4pt] avoid these for important synchronous meetings and events. Consider when your
[p0251-l0023] [14.4pt] colleagues might be dropping off or picking up from school, when they may
[p0251-l0024] [14.4pt] need to prepare lunch or dinner for a dependent, or when religious activities may
[p0251-l0025] [14.4pt] be happening (especially around religious holidays). This reduces the likelihood
[p0251-l0026] [14.4pt] that someone will decline a meeting because of a time conflict.
[p0251-l0027] [16.56pt] Defend Part-Time Hours
[p0251-l0028] [14.4pt] You, or some of your colleagues, may work part-time hours. Part-time workers
[p0251-l0029] [14.4pt] can feel left out if important meetings and decisions happen when they are not
[p0251-l0030] [14.4pt] working or feel pressured to attend and work more hours than they are paid for.
[p0251-l0031] [14.4pt] Diversity (as well as nondiscrimination) is key to a successful business. You
[p0251-l0032] [14.4pt] need the input of part-time workers and must plan to accommodate this.

=== PDF page 252 / printed label 252 ===
[p0252-l0001] [14.4pt] Aim to schedule most, or at least important, events and meetings within the
[p0252-l0002] [14.4pt] working hours of part-time employees. When you need to schedule events and
[p0252-l0003] [14.4pt] meetings outside of this time, try the following:
[p0252-l0004] [14.4pt] Make it clear they are not expected to attend during nonworking hours.
[p0252-l0005] [14.4pt] If they are happy to attend and want to do so, give time-in-lieu (comp time),
[p0252-l0006] [14.4pt] overtime payments, or similar compensation without the part-time worker
[p0252-l0007] [14.4pt] having to ask.
[p0252-l0008] [14.4pt] Record the meeting so they can catch up during their working hours. If
[p0252-l0009] [14.4pt] recording is not possible, carefully document important points and
[p0252-l0010] [14.4pt] decisions and send them to nonattendees.
[p0252-l0011] [14.4pt] If a decision is being made or feedback sought, ensure that nonattendees are
[p0252-l0012] [14.4pt] informed and have time, within their working hours, to respond.
[p0252-l0013] [16.56pt] Plan for Holidays
[p0252-l0014] [14.4pt] National holidays and observances vary from country to country, and even
[p0252-l0015] [14.4pt] between areas within a country. Knowing when your colleagues will not be
[p0252-l0016] [14.4pt] working, and informing them when you will not be working, will mean less
[p0252-l0017] [14.4pt] rescheduling and fewer no-shows at meetings and events. Encourage everyone to
[p0252-l0018] [14.4pt] put these days off into their calendar and consider having a shared calendar with
[p0252-l0019] [14.4pt] all holidays blocked out.
[p0252-l0020] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0252-l0021] [10.8pt] Don’t assume that people won’t be working on their national holidays or weekends.
[p0252-l0022] [10.8pt] Contractors in another country might work all or some of their national holidays to line up with
[p0252-l0023] [10.8pt] the national holidays of the main business. It is also common for customer-facing or site
[p0252-l0024] [10.8pt] reliability teams to supply some level of service during national holidays or weekends. Check
[p0252-l0025] [10.8pt] with your colleagues.
[p0252-l0026] [14.4pt] Remember, others may be working when you are not, so make sure they have
[p0252-l0027] [14.4pt] what they need from you as well as notice of your availability. You do not want a
[p0252-l0028] [14.4pt] development team in another country to be short of work or to have unanswered

=== PDF page 253 / printed label 253 ===
[p0253-l0001] [14.4pt] questions, and then discover that their manager or an architect in another country
[p0253-l0002] [14.4pt] is not working.
[p0253-l0003] [14.4pt] PUBLIC HOLIDAYS AND OBSERVANCES
[p0253-l0004] [14.4pt] Some holidays are celebrated internationally, and others are national or even
[p0253-l0005] [14.4pt] regional. Here are a few examples of differences you may come across:
[p0253-l0006] [14.4pt] Some regions within countries have extra or different holidays:
[p0253-l0007] [14.4pt] In the UK holidays may differ across regions. For example,
[p0253-l0008] [14.4pt] Scotland has a summer bank holiday on the first Monday in
[p0253-l0009] [14.4pt] August, which the rest of the UK observes on the last Monday in
[p0253-l0010] [14.4pt] August. Northern Ireland observes St. Patrick’s Day and the Battle
[p0253-l0011] [14.4pt] of the Boyne as public holidays.
[p0253-l0012] [14.4pt] In Spain many holidays are specific to autonomous communities.
[p0253-l0013] [14.4pt] For example, Shrove Tuesday is celebrated only in Extremadura,
[p0253-l0014] [14.4pt] and each autonomous community observes its national day at
[p0253-l0015] [14.4pt] different times of the year.
[p0253-l0016] [14.4pt] In Canada public holidays vary among provinces and territories.
[p0253-l0017] [14.4pt] For example, Good Friday is observed across Canada, but Easter
[p0253-l0018] [14.4pt] Monday is observed only in Quebec and the Northwest Territories.
[p0253-l0019] [14.4pt] When a holiday lands on a weekend, the observance day is often, but
[p0253-l0020] [14.4pt] not always, moved to the Monday or Friday.
[p0253-l0021] [14.4pt] Some holidays are on set dates (such as December 25), some on set
[p0253-l0022] [14.4pt] days (for example, the last Monday in May), and some change based on
[p0253-l0023] [14.4pt] the moon or other calendars (including Good Friday and Eid al-Fitr).
[p0253-l0024] [14.4pt] Plan ahead with all your colleagues so you know when everyone will be
[p0253-l0025] [14.4pt] available.
[p0253-l0026] [14.4pt] Besides public holidays, you and your colleagues are likely entitled to personal
[p0253-l0027] [14.4pt] leave or statutory leave. Some workers are entitled to a certain amount of leave
[p0253-l0028] [14.4pt] by law, and many companies contractually give more time off. A total of 20 to
[p0253-l0029] [14.4pt] 30 days of personal leave is typical in the EU, for example.

=== PDF page 254 / printed label 254 ===
[p0254-l0001] [14.4pt] Some companies specify unlimited leave but require the worker to put in enough
[p0254-l0002] [14.4pt] days to do their job. Some workers are entitled to statutory leave such as
[p0254-l0003] [14.4pt] maternity/paternity or adoption leave, which can last for more than a year.
[p0254-l0004] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0254-l0005] [10.8pt] Block out any leave you are taking in your calendar as soon as you plan to take it. You can
[p0254-l0006] [10.8pt] mark it as pending or to be confirmed (TBC) if you need approval or are unsure for some
[p0254-l0007] [10.8pt] reason, but put in the dates to give your colleagues advance notice of your unavailability.
[p0254-l0008] [14.4pt] Be aware of and plan around hot spots in the year where more people will be
[p0254-l0009] [14.4pt] taking leave (for example, around public holidays, school holidays, and better
[p0254-l0010] [14.4pt] weather). Also bear in mind regional patterns; for example, most leave in Spain
[p0254-l0011] [14.4pt] is typically taken in August.
[p0254-l0012] [14.4pt] Encourage everyone to put their leave into their calendar as far in advance as
[p0254-l0013] [14.4pt] possible and to discuss future plans openly, so that milestones, deadlines,
[p0254-l0014] [14.4pt] meetings, and events can be planned or adjusted.
[p0254-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0254-l0016] [10.8pt] Turn on out-of-office replies in your email, and set your status in chat applications to let
[p0254-l0017] [10.8pt] colleagues know you are unavailable, when you will be back, and who to contact in your
[p0254-l0018] [10.8pt] absence. You may also want to turn on this feature for people outside your organization if you
[p0254-l0019] [10.8pt] are customer-facing or work closely with business partners.
[p0254-l0020] [16.56pt] Account for Geography and Culture
[p0254-l0021] [14.4pt] Colleagues working in other countries or areas may also have different standard
[p0254-l0022] [14.4pt] working hours, which may also vary by industry. The actual times worked and
[p0254-l0023] [14.4pt] the number of hours worked per week may vary a little or a lot. Although the
[p0254-l0024] [14.4pt] number of working hours in a week may be specified by law, the actual
[p0254-l0025] [14.4pt] expectation of working hours may be different. In the EU, workers are protected
[p0254-l0026] [14.4pt] by the working time directive, which says they cannot work more than 48 hours
[p0254-l0027] [14.4pt] per week, although individuals can choose to opt out.
[p0254-l0028] [14.4pt] In many parts of the world, a typical full-time work week is 40 hours (8 hours

=== PDF page 255 / printed label 255 ===
[p0255-l0001] [14.4pt] per day over five days), but many employers now see the benefit of a good
[p0255-l0002] [14.4pt] work-life balance to their business and expect 37.5 or even a 35-hour week or
[p0255-l0003] [14.4pt] less.4 In some areas, a four-day week is becoming popular. This can look
[p0255-l0004] [14.4pt] different depending on the company but is another factor to take into account
[p0255-l0005] [14.4pt] when considering others’ availability.
[p0255-l0006] [14.4pt] Start and finish times are another element of schedules that can vary by country
[p0255-l0007] [14.4pt] or region. In many places, the standard day is 9 a.m. to 5 p.m. But in Spain, for
[p0255-l0008] [14.4pt] example, a typical workday can be from 8:30 a.m. to 1:30 p.m., and then from
[p0255-l0009] [14.4pt] 4:30 p.m. to 8 p.m., with a siesta break in between to avoid the hottest part of the
[p0255-l0010] [14.4pt] day. Those who work from home often vary their hours more than those who
[p0255-l0011] [14.4pt] work in an office.
[p0255-l0012] [14.4pt] Understanding the working patterns of your colleagues is key to effective
[p0255-l0013] [14.4pt] scheduling and to setting appropriate expectations around asynchronous
[p0255-l0014] [14.4pt] communication. Take the time to develop this understanding—in part, by
[p0255-l0015] [14.4pt] learning about cultural differences—and use that knowledge to save time and
[p0255-l0016] [14.4pt] reduce frustration (even increase happiness) in the long run. You may discover
[p0255-l0017] [14.4pt] that you have more working hours in common than you thought.
[p0255-l0018] [14.4pt] WORKING WITH OTHER CULTURES
[p0255-l0019] [14.4pt] You should be very clear on expectations and processes when working with
[p0255-l0020] [14.4pt] teams or individuals from other countries and cultures. Besides different
[p0255-l0021] [14.4pt] working practices, cultural differences may affect the implementation of
[p0255-l0022] [14.4pt] these practices and techniques.
[p0255-l0023] [14.4pt] If you expect to use test-driven development (TDD) or have a certain level of
[p0255-l0024] [14.4pt] code coverage for tests, make this explicit from the beginning. Make clear
[p0255-l0025] [14.4pt] any other practices or patterns you expect to be adhered to, such as factory
[p0255-l0026] [14.4pt] methods, DRY, and so on.
[p0255-l0027] [14.4pt] If your local colleagues are expected to automatically adhere to laws and
[p0255-l0028] [14.4pt] standards, communicate this to remote colleagues who may not be aware of
[p0255-l0029] [14.4pt] the same regulations, such as GDPR, HIPPA, OWASP, and NIST.
[p0255-l0030] [14.4pt] In some cultures, people will follow designs to the letter, which might or
[p0255-l0031] [14.4pt] might not be what you want. If you have included example or prototype
[p0255-l0032] [14.4pt] code, specify whether it should be followed exactly. People in other cultures

=== PDF page 256 / printed label 256 ===
[p0256-l0001] [14.4pt] may use designs as a starting point and then do their own thing. You need to
[p0256-l0002] [14.4pt] communicate how much they need to stick to the designs they have been
[p0256-l0003] [14.4pt] given.
[p0256-l0004] [14.4pt] Make sure to document your practices and expectations and share these with
[p0256-l0005] [14.4pt] local and remote colleagues. Having to redo work or to work around
[p0256-l0006] [14.4pt] problems later can be costly.
[p0256-l0007] [16.56pt] Recognize Real Working Capacity
[p0256-l0008] [14.4pt] It is important to know how much time each person you work with is available
[p0256-l0009] [14.4pt] and to take into account that the entire workday will not be spent on meaningful
[p0256-l0010] [14.4pt] work (tasks like meetings, emailing, and filling in time sheets have a larger
[p0256-l0011] [14.4pt] impact on capacity than you might think).
[p0256-l0012] [14.4pt] If you estimate tasks in hours for your sprints, you need to know how many
[p0256-l0013] [14.4pt] productive hours are available from developers working in that sprint. Don’t
[p0256-l0014] [14.4pt] assume an 8-hour working day. Communicate to work out productive hours per
[p0256-l0015] [14.4pt] day and use that in your calculations.

=== PDF page 257 / printed label 257 ===
[p0257-l0001] [14.4pt] BOOK MEETINGS EFFICIENTLY
[p0257-l0002] [14.4pt] When working with customers or partner businesses, use a service that
[p0257-l0003] [14.4pt] allows them to book a meeting with you based on your availability. This
[p0257-l0004] [14.4pt] eliminates the back-and-forth of trying to find a mutually convenient time.
[p0257-l0005] [14.4pt] Many versions of this type of service are available—including Cal.com,
[p0257-l0006] [14.4pt] Easy!Appointments, and Microsoft Bookings—that allow you to customize
[p0257-l0007] [14.4pt] the meeting length, minimum buffer time before and after the meeting, how
[p0257-l0008] [14.4pt] far into the future they can book, and the time periods they can book (for
[p0257-l0009] [14.4pt] example, any time in your standard office hours, or just Thursday mornings).
[p0257-l0010] [14.4pt] Give a link to the tech lead of a partner so that they can book up to an hour
[p0257-l0011] [14.4pt] with you to discuss an architecture query, or to a customer so they can book
[p0257-l0012] [14.4pt] an hour’s training session on your software or a 30-minute initial
[p0257-l0013] [14.4pt] consultation on a project.
[p0257-l0014] [14.4pt] To protect everyone’s personal time and mental health, you can set the
[p0257-l0015] [14.4pt] expectation that everyone will have notifications turned off outside of their work
[p0257-l0016] [14.4pt] hours (unless they’re on call or under a contractual requirement). This
