[p0257-l0017] [14.4pt] expectation sets a precedent for a more healthy work-life balance and respects
[p0257-l0018] [14.4pt] everyone’s boundaries—including for breaks, such as lunchtime.
[p0257-l0019] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0257-l0020] [10.8pt] When you’re communicating with others who may not share the same first language as you,
[p0257-l0021] [10.8pt] being clear and concise is even more important than usual. Use plain language and avoid
[p0257-l0022] [10.8pt] idioms and cultural references to avoid misunderstandings and back-and-forth clarifications
[p0257-l0023] [10.8pt] (see “Simple Language” for more details).
[p0257-l0024] [20.16pt] Improve Energy and Productivity
[p0257-l0025] [14.4pt] In addition to considering time zones and working hours in your
[p0257-l0026] [14.4pt] communications, you should take energy and productivity into account. These
[p0257-l0027] [14.4pt] vary with a person’s circadian rhythm but are also affected by the amount of
[p0257-l0028] [14.4pt] time they have been working, work-life balance, and the interruptions they

=== PDF page 258 / printed label 258 ===
[p0258-l0001] [14.4pt] receive.5
[p0258-l0002] [16.56pt] Control Notifications
[p0258-l0003] [14.4pt] Notifications are probably the most common form of communication because
[p0258-l0004] [14.4pt] you receive them to tell you about most other forms of communication. But they
[p0258-l0005] [14.4pt] are not necessarily useful or productive; they can distract you.
[p0258-l0006] [14.4pt] Turning off notifications could be one of your most effective productivity
[p0258-l0007] [14.4pt] improvements. You may be worried you will miss something important, but you
[p0258-l0008] [14.4pt] can put processes in place to avoid this. Instead of switching your attention to
[p0258-l0009] [14.4pt] every email notification, schedule times throughout the day to check for
[p0258-l0010] [14.4pt] important emails and times to process and respond.
[p0258-l0011] [14.4pt] You can combine switching off notifications with listening to your circadian
[p0258-l0012] [14.4pt] rhythm and energy levels. Email is a relatively low-energy task, so scheduling it
[p0258-l0013] [14.4pt] for a time when you have low energy will mean you can do high-energy tasks
[p0258-l0014] [14.4pt] when you have higher levels of energy.
[p0258-l0015] [14.4pt] You can apply the same pattern to instant messages and other forms of
[p0258-l0016] [14.4pt] communication, such as tasks being assigned to you. If you do have specific
[p0258-l0017] [14.4pt] communications that need instant responses, most tools will allow you to apply a
[p0258-l0018] [14.4pt] filter to notifications. Microsoft Teams, for example, allows you to still receive
[p0258-l0019] [14.4pt] notifications from specific people when other notifications are turned off, and it
[p0258-l0020] [14.4pt] also lets you mute specific chats or teams.
[p0258-l0021] [14.4pt] Turning off and customizing notifications allows you to take control of your
[p0258-l0022] [14.4pt] communication tools rather than letting the tools control you.
[p0258-l0023] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0258-l0024] [10.8pt] Reducing the number of tools you use for communication and understanding which tools to
[p0258-l0025] [10.8pt] use for which type of communication will decrease the mental load of your team. See “Remote
[p0258-l0026] [10.8pt] Tools and Governance” for more on governing tools.
[p0258-l0027] [16.56pt] Automate Tasks
[p0258-l0028] [14.4pt] Automating repetitive communication tasks is another way to make your

=== PDF page 259 / printed label 259 ===
[p0259-l0001] [14.4pt] communication more productive. Here are some ways to save time and reduce
[p0259-l0002] [14.4pt] distractions when communicating:
[p0259-l0003] [14.4pt] Set up auto-replies for common emails or messages (and for times that you
[p0259-l0004] [14.4pt] are out of the office or otherwise unavailable). Use the auto-reply to direct
[p0259-l0005] [14.4pt] people to other sources; for example, a FAQ page or point of contact.
[p0259-l0006] [14.4pt] Automating these replies enables people to help themselves or at least stay
[p0259-l0007] [14.4pt] informed of when you will be able to help them.
[p0259-l0008] [14.4pt] Many email programs allow you to set rules that can be applied to emails
[p0259-l0009] [14.4pt] when they arrive. Set these rules to make your life easier, such as flagging
[p0259-l0010] [14.4pt] emails that are important or moving newsletters to another folder to look at
[p0259-l0011] [14.4pt] later (making processing your inbox faster).
[p0259-l0012] [14.4pt] You may be able to automate many communication tasks by using a service
[p0259-l0013] [14.4pt] like IFTTT, Zapier, or Microsoft Power Automate. Examples include being
[p0259-l0014] [14.4pt] assigned a task in one app and automatically adding it to your list of to-dos
[p0259-l0015] [14.4pt] in another app, or automatically emailing a reminder to colleagues who
[p0259-l0016] [14.4pt] have yet to add their update to a team status page.
[p0259-l0017] [14.4pt] Messaging apps such as Teams and Slack allow you to set up bots to
[p0259-l0018] [14.4pt] automatically manage aspects of messaging. A bot could remind everyone
[p0259-l0019] [14.4pt] in a chat of an upcoming deadline or meeting. A bot could also answer a
[p0259-l0020] [14.4pt] query typed into the chat, such as “What is the app ID of the service named
[p0259-l0021] [14.4pt] Widget?”
[p0259-l0022] [14.4pt] Unsubscribe from all the emails that you don’t read. You’ve probably
[p0259-l0023] [14.4pt] signed up for many newsletters in good faith but never read them. You may
[p0259-l0024] [14.4pt] also end up on many email lists that you never really wanted to be
[p0259-l0025] [14.4pt] subscribed to. Unsubscribe via the link that should be in every email. If you
[p0259-l0026] [14.4pt] have trouble unsubscribing, set up a filter to send those emails straight to
[p0259-l0027] [14.4pt] junk or trash.
[p0259-l0028] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0259-l0029] [10.8pt] Don’t be tempted to sort all your read email into folders. This makes it harder to find what you
[p0259-l0030] [10.8pt] need in the future. Most read emails can be sent straight to Archive, where you know they will
[p0259-l0031] [10.8pt] be if you need them. You might have a few types of important emails that warrant their own
[p0259-l0032] [10.8pt] folder, but this should be the rare exception. Also, bear in mind that you should not use email

=== PDF page 260 / printed label 260 ===
[p0260-l0001] [10.8pt] as a storage system. If your company purges old email, you will lose it all. Move important
[p0260-l0002] [10.8pt] information to a wiki or personal notes.
[p0260-l0003] [16.56pt] Work with Others’ Rhythms
[p0260-l0004] [14.4pt] It can be hard to be patient when waiting on someone in another time zone or
[p0260-l0005] [14.4pt] with a working pattern different from yours. It can be even more frustrating
[p0260-l0006] [14.4pt] when waiting for a response from a person who works the same hours as you.
[p0260-l0007] [14.4pt] Keeping energy and productive times in mind can help. Many people are most
[p0260-l0008] [14.4pt] productive in the late morning, least productive around 3 p.m., and increasingly
[p0260-l0009] [14.4pt] productive again until about 6 p.m.
[p0260-l0010] [14.4pt] If you know that the response you need will require a higher amount of energy
[p0260-l0011] [14.4pt] from your colleague, allow them to respond during their more productive times
[p0260-l0012] [14.4pt] or prompt them just before these times. Conversely, if the response won’t take
[p0260-l0013] [14.4pt] much thought, you can prompt them at a lower energy time of day.
[p0260-l0014] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0260-l0015] [10.8pt] Synchronous remote communication requires more energy than asynchronous, so replace
[p0260-l0016] [10.8pt] meetings with asynchronous updates as much as possible. See Chapter 14 for more on
[p0260-l0017] [10.8pt] synchronous and asynchronous approaches.
[p0260-l0018] [16.56pt] Schedule for Energy
[p0260-l0019] [14.4pt] Another way you can use circadian rhythm to your advantage is when
[p0260-l0020] [14.4pt] scheduling meetings. Picking a low-energy time of day to discuss a problem or
[p0260-l0021] [14.4pt] generate ideas is going to be a disaster.
[p0260-l0022] [14.4pt] Time zones can put pressure on when you can schedule a meeting, but you can
[p0260-l0023] [14.4pt] focus on meeting at a higher energy time for key people or the majority of
[p0260-l0024] [14.4pt] people to improve your synchronous communication time.
[p0260-l0025] [14.4pt] Your and your colleagues’ energy levels are also affected by the amount of time
[p0260-l0026] [14.4pt] you have been doing a certain task. Consider this when planning effective
[p0260-l0027] [14.4pt] meetings and events. You should plan for a break if the meeting is more than 60
[p0260-l0028] [14.4pt] minutes, ideally at least every 45 minutes. Breaking up the time with different
[p0260-l0029] [14.4pt] activities, a change in visual focus (for example, a blank slide or something

=== PDF page 261 / printed label 261 ===
[p0261-l0001] [14.4pt] funny like a meme in a presentation), or some type of movement will also
[p0261-l0002] [14.4pt] improve your meeting outcomes.
[p0261-l0003] [14.4pt] As people approach the end of a meeting or the end of the day, they have less
[p0261-l0004] [14.4pt] energy. Plan to take this into account. Higher-energy activities should be
[p0261-l0005] [14.4pt] scheduled earlier in the agenda, or after a break, as much as possible.
[p0261-l0006] [14.4pt] Ensure that your agenda prioritizes time for eating (enough time for healthy food
[p0261-l0007] [14.4pt] rather than rushed fast food) and that your scheduling is not detrimental to
[p0261-l0008] [14.4pt] attendees’ sleep. All of these considerations put together maximize your
[p0261-l0009] [14.4pt] synchronous communication.
[p0261-l0010] [14.4pt] In addition, you can improve attendees’ concentration by providing a
[p0261-l0011] [14.4pt] comfortable and appropriately lit environment when meeting in-person. Provide
[p0261-l0012] [14.4pt] good equipment and encourage your colleagues to set up their desks
[p0261-l0013] [14.4pt] ergonomically and to move around during remote meetings.
[p0261-l0014] [14.4pt] COMMUNICATING FOCUS TIME
[p0261-l0015] [14.4pt] Focus time, time that you’ve blocked out in your calendar for uninterrupted
[p0261-l0016] [14.4pt] work, is an excellent tool for productivity. The best times to block out are
[p0261-l0017] [14.4pt] your most productive, high-energy times, but if that isn’t possible, go for
[p0261-l0018] [14.4pt] your least busy times. A two-hour block two or three times per week is
[p0261-l0019] [14.4pt] usually more doable than you think.
[p0261-l0020] [14.4pt] If you struggle to find time slots, block out one definite slot and others that
[p0261-l0021] [14.4pt] are marked as tentative and labeled “book over if necessary.” You are then
[p0261-l0022] [14.4pt] more available if need be, but most people will not book this time. You can
[p0261-l0023] [14.4pt] always swap your definite and tentative slots if something urgent comes up.
[p0261-l0024] [14.4pt] Turn off all notifications during this time. Your computer’s operating system
[p0261-l0025] [14.4pt] may have a focus setting that does this automatically for you, or you may be
[p0261-l0026] [14.4pt] able to install an app to manage this (at the very least, you can mute your
[p0261-l0027] [14.4pt] computer). You should also close tabs and apps that you aren’t using.
[p0261-l0028] [14.4pt] To communicate your focus time to others, try the following:
[p0261-l0029] [14.4pt] Block out the time in your calendar and mark it as busy or working
[p0261-l0030] [14.4pt] elsewhere.

=== PDF page 262 / printed label 262 ===
[p0262-l0001] [14.4pt] Having your time blocked out as busy should mean that messaging apps
[p0262-l0002] [14.4pt] such as Teams show your status as busy as well, but if not, set your
[p0262-l0003] [14.4pt] status at the beginning of a focus session.
[p0262-l0004] [14.4pt] Communicate the reasons for your focus time (actually getting the
[p0262-l0005] [14.4pt] things done that others want from you) so that they understand why you
[p0262-l0006] [14.4pt] are unavailable.
[p0262-l0007] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0262-l0008] [14.4pt] Time is highly important to coordinating remote and hybrid work, and even to
[p0262-l0009] [14.4pt] people’s happiness. When you consider that time is the one resource you can
[p0262-l0010] [14.4pt] never get back or make more of, you can start to appreciate how valuable it is.
[p0262-l0011] [14.4pt] These techniques around time lead neatly into the principles of communication.
[p0262-l0012] [14.4pt] In the next chapter, you will discover the appropriate use of asynchronous and
[p0262-l0013] [14.4pt] synchronous communications and how making the correct choice can determine
[p0262-l0014] [14.4pt] success.
[p0262-l0015] [10.8pt] 1 See Wikipedia for a breakdown of date format by country.
[p0262-l0016] [10.8pt] 2 The format year-day-month is not used anywhere, so if you see the year first, you can be sure it is
[p0262-l0017] [10.8pt] supposed to be year-month-day.
[p0262-l0018] [10.8pt] 3 Time off in lieu and comp time refer to taking overtime accrued as extra holiday or vacation time.
[p0262-l0019] [10.8pt] 4 Working hours should not be reduced to avoid legislation, such as employee benefit requirements.
[p0262-l0020] [10.8pt] Don’t take advantage; give the better work-life balance along with the full-time benefits, and you will
[p0262-l0021] [10.8pt] have happier and more productive employees.
[p0262-l0022] [10.8pt] 5 Circadian rhythms are the cycles of your internal body clock and run over a period of around 24
[p0262-l0023] [10.8pt] hours (such as the sleep-wake cycle). Not everyone’s rhythm is the same (for example, some people
[p0262-l0024] [10.8pt] prefer to work earlier in the day than others).

=== PDF page 263 / printed label 263 ===
[p0263-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 14. Remote Principles
[p0263-l0002] [14.4pt] Knowing when to use different types of communication is essential to achieve
[p0263-l0003] [14.4pt] your goals efficiently and with the best chance of success. To increase the
[p0263-l0004] [14.4pt] effectiveness of remote communication in fully remote and hybrid environments,
[p0263-l0005] [14.4pt] you can apply some overarching principles and the patterns within them.
[p0263-l0006] [14.4pt] This chapter explores how and when to use the various types of synchronous and
[p0263-l0007] [14.4pt] asynchronous communication to synchronize your expectations and workflows
[p0263-l0008] [14.4pt] and put all workers on an equal footing, wherever you are working.
[p0263-l0009] [20.16pt] Meetings to Sync
[p0263-l0010] [14.4pt] Meetings are often seen as a time-sink, taking time away from your actual work.
[p0263-l0011] [14.4pt] The act of synchronously coming together can be extremely productive for some
[p0263-l0012] [14.4pt] types of work, but when the wrong people are attending or the focus is on
[p0263-l0013] [14.4pt] activities that could be done much better asynchronously, it’s time to make
[p0263-l0014] [14.4pt] changes to meetings.
[p0263-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0263-l0016] [10.8pt] If you struggle to optimize the number of people invited to a meeting, think about how much
[p0263-l0017] [10.8pt] an hour of their time is worth and how much you want to spend on the meeting.
[p0263-l0018] [16.56pt] Synchronous Versus Asynchronous
[p0263-l0019] [14.4pt] Remote synchronous communication takes more energy than in-person. You
[p0263-l0020] [14.4pt] have probably felt this yourself, but it is also backed up by research from
[p0263-l0021] [14.4pt] Stanford University (see “Why Do You Get Zoom Fatigue?”).
[p0263-l0022] [14.4pt] When you are in the same room with colleagues, you can more easily hear their
[p0263-l0023] [14.4pt] tones of voice and see their body language and facial expressions. You
[p0263-l0024] [14.4pt] subconsciously read all these cues without thinking and use them to understand
[p0263-l0025] [14.4pt] what others are saying (or not saying).

=== PDF page 264 / printed label 264 ===
[p0264-l0001] [14.4pt] Remote meetings disrupt the normal flow of conversation and prevent you from
[p0264-l0002] [14.4pt] reading body language and facial expressions as easily, or at all. You have to
[p0264-l0003] [14.4pt] work much harder to understand one another.
[p0264-l0004] [14.4pt] In Thinking, Fast and Slow, Daniel Kahneman explores the idea that your brain
[p0264-l0005] [14.4pt] is split into system 1, which does 98% of your thinking and is involved in
[p0264-l0006] [14.4pt] reading body language and facial expressions, and system 2, which does just 2%
[p0264-l0007] [14.4pt] of your thinking and is used to make rational decisions. In person, your system 1
[p0264-l0008] [14.4pt] makes fast and cheap decisions about body language, tone of voice, and facial
[p0264-l0009] [14.4pt] expressions (using heuristics).1 In a remote situation, at least some decoding has
[p0264-l0010] [14.4pt] to be done by your system 2, which requires much more energy.
[p0264-l0011] [14.4pt] Synchronous meetings and events should occur only when needed,
[p0264-l0012] [14.4pt] communicating asynchronously as much as possible (see “Async to Think”).
[p0264-l0013] [14.4pt] This improves communication and productivity in several ways:
[p0264-l0014] [14.4pt] Finding a good time for everyone to meet can be difficult, especially when
[p0264-l0015] [14.4pt] attendees are in more than one time zone. With asynchronous
[p0264-l0016] [14.4pt] communication, everyone can process the message at a good time for them.
[p0264-l0017] [14.4pt] A lot of time is wasted in meetings because not everyone is engaged or
[p0264-l0018] [14.4pt] needed for the whole session. Ten people in a meeting for one hour is 10
[p0264-l0019] [14.4pt] hours of work time used in just one hour.
[p0264-l0020] [14.4pt] Meetings and other synchronous events are interruptions in your workday,
[p0264-l0021] [14.4pt] meaning you waste time switching context at the beginning of each meeting
[p0264-l0022] [14.4pt] and when you go back to your other work.
[p0264-l0023] [14.4pt] It is harder to keep in-person and remote attendees of a hybrid synchronous
[p0264-l0024] [14.4pt] meeting on an equal footing, with in-person attendees likely to dominate.
[p0264-l0025] [14.4pt] Unless a synchronous meeting is recorded, everything that is said can be
[p0264-l0026] [14.4pt] heard only once. Some discussion might be missed by all or some attendees
[p0264-l0027] [14.4pt] and is gone forever unless someone takes minutes. Asynchronous
[p0264-l0028] [14.4pt] communications are there for reference afterward.
[p0264-l0029] [14.4pt] Both synchronous and asynchronous approaches have benefits and drawbacks,
[p0264-l0030] [14.4pt] but which is best depends on the reason for your communication (and to a
[p0264-l0031] [14.4pt] degree, your team’s location).

=== PDF page 265 / printed label 265 ===
[p0265-l0001] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0265-l0002] [10.8pt] It is a commonly held misconception that communication needs to be either synchronous or
[p0265-l0003] [10.8pt] asynchronous. Mixing both can be efficient. You may decide that a synchronous meeting is the
[p0265-l0004] [10.8pt] best way to make a final decision, but use asynchronous communication to brief people on the
[p0265-l0005] [10.8pt] context and the need to make the decision, to brainstorm ideas, and to vote to narrow choices
[p0265-l0006] [10.8pt] before the meeting starts. You are also likely to use asynchronous communication after the
[p0265-l0007] [10.8pt] meeting to document and communicate the decision made.
[p0265-l0008] [14.4pt] Synchronous communication is typically better when communication aims to do
[p0265-l0009] [14.4pt] the following:
[p0265-l0010] [14.4pt] Build rapport, such as team-building or a project kickoff
[p0265-l0011] [14.4pt] Generate ideas, such as coming up with solutions to a problem, or new
[p0265-l0012] [14.4pt] ideas for a product
[p0265-l0013] [14.4pt] Asynchronous communication is typically better when communication aims to
[p0265-l0014] [14.4pt] accomplish one of these:
[p0265-l0015] [14.4pt] Report progress, such as a stand-up or project progress
[p0265-l0016] [14.4pt] Gather feedback, such as on a draft ADR
[p0265-l0017] [14.4pt] Disseminate information, such as announcing a change to team structure
[p0265-l0018] [10.8pt] EXAMPLE
[p0265-l0019] [14.4pt] SYNCHRONOUS INTERRUPTIONS AT POLYGLOT MEDIA
[p0265-l0020] [14.4pt] Sander, a product owner at Polyglot Media, has problems balancing the
[p0265-l0021] [14.4pt] expectations of the development team for his product with their actual
[p0265-l0022] [14.4pt] output. The development team responds that their working time is constantly
[p0265-l0023] [14.4pt] being interrupted and taken up by meetings. How are they supposed to get
[p0265-l0024] [14.4pt] any coding done?
[p0265-l0025] [14.4pt] When he looks at the team’s calendar, Sander can see they are right.
[p0265-l0026] [14.4pt] Synchronous meetings are dotted throughout their week, so they are
[p0265-l0027] [14.4pt] constantly switching between tasks and wasting time refocusing. Many of
[p0265-l0028] [14.4pt] these meetings are set by Sander and the project manager (PM). So Sander
[p0265-l0029] [14.4pt] and the PM remove, combine, or change nearly all the synchronous meetings

=== PDF page 266 / printed label 266 ===
[p0266-l0001] [14.4pt] to asynchronous. They leave the Monday morning stand-up meeting as
[p0266-l0002] [14.4pt] synchronous, but change all other progress reports to asynchronous updates
[p0266-l0003] [14.4pt] in the team’s project management software. The team is also given the power
[p0266-l0004] [14.4pt] to schedule a synchronous meeting if needed.
[p0266-l0005] [14.4pt] Retrospective meetings are changed to an asynchronous collection that the
[p0266-l0006] [14.4pt] team can add to as things arise and a shorter synchronous meeting. Sander
[p0266-l0007] [14.4pt] and the PM are notified when items are added so they can take action if
[p0266-l0008] [14.4pt] needed. If something urgent requires the team to come together to discuss or
[p0266-l0009] [14.4pt] fix, the team can schedule a synchronous meeting.
[p0266-l0010] [14.4pt] After a few weeks, the development team is a lot happier and a lot more
[p0266-l0011] [14.4pt] productive. Sander is happier because of their improved results and starts to
[p0266-l0012] [14.4pt] spread the word to other product owners and PMs at Polyglot Media.
[p0266-l0013] [16.56pt] Enhance Meetings
[p0266-l0014] [14.4pt] Getting the most out of a meeting and improving the outcomes starts before the
[p0266-l0015] [14.4pt] meeting even begins. Here are some ways to organize and structure a remote or
[p0266-l0016] [14.4pt] hybrid meeting for the best experience for all attendees:
[p0266-l0017] [14.4pt] Choose and state the goal(s) for the meeting carefully to get the outcomes
[p0266-l0018] [14.4pt] you desire. The goals for a stand-up are to remove roadblocks, receive help
[p0266-l0019] [14.4pt] with issues, and understand if you are on track. It is not just to hear
[p0266-l0020] [14.4pt] everyone’s progress report, which is one of the most common outcomes and
[p0266-l0021] [14.4pt] could be done more easily asynchronously.
[p0266-l0022] [14.4pt] Ensure that all meeting activities connect with these goals. You can explain
[p0266-l0023] [14.4pt] or point out the link between each activity and the goal(s) to encourage
[p0266-l0024] [14.4pt] interest and participation; for example, hearing everyone’s input at a
[p0266-l0025] [14.4pt] postmortem meeting connects to the goals of gaining a full understanding
[p0266-l0026] [14.4pt] of what went wrong, whether the event can be prevented from happening
[p0266-l0027] [14.4pt] again, and how disaster recovery from the event can be improved.
[p0266-l0028] [14.4pt] Set an agenda, including timings, and keep to it as much as possible. Your
[p0266-l0029] [14.4pt] agenda will help you define how to accomplish the meeting goal(s) and
[p0266-l0030] [14.4pt] bring tangents to a close quickly. Use a method to put aside items that need
[p0266-l0031] [14.4pt] attention but aren’t relevant to the meeting goal(s) for a later time, such as a

=== PDF page 267 / printed label 267 ===
[p0267-l0001] [14.4pt] car park or parking lot.2
[p0267-l0002] [14.4pt] Set expectations to support the meeting and create a safe space. Send these
[p0267-l0003] [14.4pt] out with the agenda so everyone is forewarned. If attendees aren’t normally
[p0267-l0004] [14.4pt] expected to have their video on, you may want to specify that you would
[p0267-l0005] [14.4pt] like everyone to turn on their video to support goals like team-building or
[p0267-l0006] [14.4pt] onboarding new members. Other expectations could include not
[p0267-l0007] [14.4pt] interrupting and keeping information discussed confidential.
[p0267-l0008] [14.4pt] Make sure you invite only the correct attendees to achieve the meeting
[p0267-l0009] [14.4pt] goal(s). If a decision needs to be made in the meeting, a person with
[p0267-l0010] [14.4pt] authority to make it needs to be there. If a technical opinion is required,
[p0267-l0011] [14.4pt] someone with the appropriate technical background should be there. Don’t
[p0267-l0012] [14.4pt] invite people just in case; it is a waste of their time. Instead, ask if they can
[p0267-l0013] [14.4pt] be available to be called into the meeting on short notice or to answer a
[p0267-l0014] [14.4pt] question via messaging.
[p0267-l0015] [14.4pt] If someone is presenting or facilitating a part of the meeting, brief them
[p0267-l0016] [14.4pt] beforehand on how much time they have. Don’t rely on them to check the
[p0267-l0017] [14.4pt] timing in the agenda; specify the time period clearly so they can prepare
[p0267-l0018] [14.4pt] appropriately.
[p0267-l0019] [14.4pt] Plan and put in place any asynchronous communications needed for before
[p0267-l0020] [14.4pt] or after the meeting. If supporting documents are needed, send links to
[p0267-l0021] [14.4pt] these with the agenda and specify whether they should be consumed before
[p0267-l0022] [14.4pt] the meeting starts. Make sure that all attendees have access to any
[p0267-l0023] [14.4pt] documents or other reference material you link to by asking them to check.
[p0267-l0024] [14.4pt] Document decisions and actions from the meeting and send them out to all
[p0267-l0025] [14.4pt] relevant stakeholders as well as attendees after the meeting. This is a useful
[p0267-l0026] [14.4pt] way to keep anyone who couldn’t attend in the loop, and it makes sure
[p0267-l0027] [14.4pt] everyone is aware of actions they need to take and any other outcomes.
[p0267-l0028] [14.4pt] Manage attendees’ energy by planning breaks every 45–60 minutes and
[p0267-l0029] [14.4pt] planning tasks that require more energy early on or soon after a break.
[p0267-l0030] [14.4pt] REDUCING SYNCHRONOUS MEETINGS
[p0267-l0031] [14.4pt] If you have too many synchronous meetings right now, you may be thinking

=== PDF page 268 / printed label 268 ===
[p0268-l0001] [14.4pt] it will be impossible to get people to cancel their precious meetings and
[p0268-l0002] [14.4pt] change completely or partially to asynchronous communication. Reducing
[p0268-l0003] [14.4pt] synchronous and increasing asynchronous communication is about behavior
[p0268-l0004] [14.4pt] change. Don’t give up quickly; it will take time.
[p0268-l0005] [14.4pt] Here are some ideas to help you convince others:
[p0268-l0006] [14.4pt] Meetings are often called because someone feels they don’t know
[p0268-l0007] [14.4pt] enough about what is happening. Put in place asynchronous methods,
[p0268-l0008] [14.4pt] such as status updates in a project or knowledge management app or a
[p0268-l0009] [14.4pt] dashboard for a project, and give the person access. That person can
[p0268-l0010] [14.4pt] then likely be persuaded to cancel or reduce the duration of the meeting
[p0268-l0011] [14.4pt] once they have the information they want by other means.
[p0268-l0012] [14.4pt] Meetings are waterfall. If you hold a stand-up at 9 a.m. each day, by 10
[p0268-l0013] [14.4pt] a.m. the situation could have changed. Introduce a place for new
[p0268-l0014] [14.4pt] updates, such as a project or knowledge management app. Once this is
[p0268-l0015] [14.4pt] being used, you can tackle reducing the stand-up meetings themselves.
[p0268-l0016] [14.4pt] The asynchronous updates fit much better with Agile and happen as and
[p0268-l0017] [14.4pt] when you have the information. Your remaining meetings can then be
[p0268-l0018] [14.4pt] used for what they are more optimal for: bidirectional communication.
[p0268-l0019] [14.4pt] You will likely need more bidirectional communication in a team with
[p0268-l0020] [14.4pt] less-experienced members.
[p0268-l0021] [14.4pt] Asynchronous communication enables those who can’t attend a
[p0268-l0022] [14.4pt] synchronous meeting to add their update or information when they are
[p0268-l0023] [14.4pt] available. For example, when someone is going on leave, they can
[p0268-l0024] [14.4pt] make sure to contribute before they go. If updates and information are
[p0268-l0025] [14.4pt] added as and when they occur, it is more likely that someone who has
[p0268-l0026] [14.4pt] fallen sick has still contributed.
[p0268-l0027] [14.4pt] Trialing or implementing no-meeting blocks or days is a good way to
[p0268-l0028] [14.4pt] allow people to focus on their work. When the overall time available for
[p0268-l0029] [14.4pt] meetings is reduced, people are more likely to push back on meetings
[p0268-l0030] [14.4pt] they don’t think are productive and to reduce the time a meeting runs.
[p0268-l0031] [14.4pt] There will be enough time for the meetings that are required. Block out
[p0268-l0032] [14.4pt] nonmeeting periods on everyone’s calendar to help everyone respect
[p0268-l0033] [14.4pt] this focus time.

=== PDF page 269 / printed label 269 ===
[p0269-l0001] [14.4pt] Even necessary meetings can be redesigned. The duration of the
[p0269-l0002] [14.4pt] meeting can often be shortened without other changes, or some of the
[p0269-l0003] [14.4pt] meeting content can be shifted to asynchronous communication before
[p0269-l0004] [14.4pt] or after the meeting. Try ending the meeting early and telling attendees
[p0269-l0005] [14.4pt] that this is the aim. When this works, you can change the meeting
[p0269-l0006] [14.4pt] length in everyone’s calendars to make it official. You may find you can
[p0269-l0007] [14.4pt] reduce the meeting length even further.
[p0269-l0008] [14.4pt] Stress that the content is still important but that the synchronous
[p0269-l0009] [14.4pt] meeting format is not the best approach. Once people have been shown
[p0269-l0010] [14.4pt] they can get the same or better results from asynchronous
[p0269-l0011] [14.4pt] communication, they will be happier to move away from meetings.
[p0269-l0012] [14.4pt] You can employ the following practices during a remote or hybrid meeting to
[p0269-l0013] [14.4pt] improve it and its outcomes:
[p0269-l0014] [14.4pt] Prompt those who have not contributed as much by asking them open-ended
[p0269-l0015] [14.4pt] questions
[p0269-l0016] [14.4pt] This can help even up participation so that people who are colocated (in the
[p0269-l0017] [14.4pt] same room) don’t dominate the meeting, and can result in a diversity of input
[p0269-l0018] [14.4pt] from those who otherwise may not feel they can speak up.
[p0269-l0019] [14.4pt] Encourage use of the chat feature
[p0269-l0020] [14.4pt] This is another way to foster equal contribution because those who may not
[p0269-l0021] [14.4pt] feel they can speak (or get a word in!) can use the chat to give their input. Be
[p0269-l0022] [14.4pt] aware that the chat feature can also replace the side conversations that would
[p0269-l0023] [14.4pt] naturally happen if everyone were in the same room, which may become a
[p0269-l0024] [14.4pt] distraction.
[p0269-l0025] [14.4pt] Monitor the chat and Q&A
[p0269-l0026] [14.4pt] You, or someone on your behalf, should be monitoring the chat and Q&A
[p0269-l0027] [14.4pt] features to make sure that nothing pertinent is missed.
[p0269-l0028] [14.4pt] Encourage everyone to interrupt if they cannot hear something
[p0269-l0029] [14.4pt] Set this expectation at the beginning of the meeting so that everyone can hear

=== PDF page 270 / printed label 270 ===
[p0270-l0001] [14.4pt] all contributions and have their contributions heard.
[p0270-l0002] [14.4pt] Use breakout rooms in larger meetings so that attendees can participate in
[p0270-l0003] [14.4pt] activities in smaller groups
[p0270-l0004] [14.4pt] In one large online group, some people’s opinions likely won’t be heard.
[p0270-l0005] [14.4pt] Break-out rooms could be used to simultaneously work on the same task or
[p0270-l0006] [14.4pt] different tasks before coming back together to consolidate or report back on
[p0270-l0007] [14.4pt] outputs.
[p0270-l0008] [14.4pt] Use interactivity tools
[p0270-l0009] [14.4pt] Polls, votes, whiteboard activities, and other exercises will encourage
[p0270-l0010] [14.4pt] contribution and keep participants’ attention.
[p0270-l0011] [14.4pt] Keep to time as much as possible
[p0270-l0012] [14.4pt] Monitor time against your agenda and use tools such as a parking lot to stop
[p0270-l0013] [14.4pt] tangents before they go too far and without upsetting anyone. On-screen
[p0270-l0014] [14.4pt] countdown times are another useful tool for managing time.
[p0270-l0015] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0270-l0016] [10.8pt] The time of day can affect how well an activity goes in your meeting. Because of different
[p0270-l0017] [10.8pt] time zones, you may not be able to optimize the time for everyone, but thinking and decision-
[p0270-l0018] [10.8pt] based activities are usually done best early in the day.
[p0270-l0019] [14.4pt] In addition, the following are ways you can improve your contribution to a
[p0270-l0020] [14.4pt] remote meeting while also boosting your credibility:
[p0270-l0021] [14.4pt] Put energy into your voice and make it bright. You are not trying to go as
[p0270-l0022] [14.4pt] far as a children’s TV presenter, but the way you speak will affect the
[p0270-l0023] [14.4pt] amount of attention your colleagues give you.
[p0270-l0024] [14.4pt] Turn on your camera and use purposeful and exaggerated gestures and
[p0270-l0025] [14.4pt] facial expressions. Make it as easy as possible for others to understand you
[p0270-l0026] [14.4pt] by emphasizing your body language, which is harder to understand
[p0270-l0027] [14.4pt] remotely compared to in person.

=== PDF page 271 / printed label 271 ===
[p0271-l0001] [14.4pt] WHY DO YOU GET ZOOM FATIGUE?
[p0271-l0002] [14.4pt] Professor Jeremy Bailenson, founding director of the Stanford Virtual
[p0271-l0003] [14.4pt] Human Interaction Lab (VHIL), has been studying the effects of spending
[p0271-l0004] [14.4pt] hours on platforms like Zoom.3 Rather than say you shouldn’t use video
[p0271-l0005] [14.4pt] conferencing, he hopes his research will enable video conferencing
[p0271-l0006] [14.4pt] organizations to improve workflows and interfaces.
[p0271-l0007] [14.4pt] Bailenson summarizes four main reasons video conferencing drains your
[p0271-l0008] [14.4pt] energy more than in-person interaction:
[p0271-l0009] [14.4pt] The amount of close-up eye contact is intense and unnatural
[p0271-l0010] [14.4pt] Everyone is looking at everyone all the time, and a close and large face is
[p0271-l0011] [14.4pt] like having someone in your immediate personal space.
[p0271-l0012] [14.4pt] Seeing yourself constantly is fatiguing
[p0271-l0013] [14.4pt] He cites a study that concludes that you are more critical of yourself
[p0271-l0014] [14.4pt] when you see your reflection.
[p0271-l0015] [14.4pt] Video calls drastically reduce your mobility
[p0271-l0016] [14.4pt] In person and on the phone, you move around, but the narrow camera
[p0271-l0017] [14.4pt] view limits your movement.
[p0271-l0018] [14.4pt] Cognitive load is much higher
[p0271-l0019] [14.4pt] Nonverbal communication is picked up easily and subconsciously in
[p0271-l0020] [14.4pt] person, but you have to work hard to both send and receive signals
[p0271-l0021] [14.4pt] online.
[p0271-l0022] [14.4pt] To counteract these challenges, Bailenson suggests the following:
[p0271-l0023] [14.4pt] Reducing the size of others’ faces by coming out of full-screen.
[p0271-l0024] [14.4pt] Increasing the space between you and the screen by, for example, using
[p0271-l0025] [14.4pt] an external keyboard and mouse when using a laptop.
[p0271-l0026] [14.4pt] Turning off the self-view after you have checked you are centered and
[p0271-l0027] [14.4pt] in focus.

=== PDF page 272 / printed label 272 ===
[p0272-l0001] [14.4pt] Using an external camera, farther away when possible, to increase the
[p0272-l0002] [14.4pt] field of view and then aiming to move around.
[p0272-l0003] [14.4pt] Turning away from the screen for a break from sending and receiving
[p0272-l0004] [14.4pt] visual cues.
[p0272-l0005] [20.16pt] Async to Think
[p0272-l0006] [14.4pt] Asynchronous communication has many benefits, but you should also look out
[p0272-l0007] [14.4pt] for its pitfalls. Every type of communication has trade-offs.
[p0272-l0008] [14.4pt] Whether communication is asynchronous does not depend on the tool used but
[p0272-l0009] [14.4pt] rather on the expectations of the way the tool is used. Instant messaging can be
[p0272-l0010] [14.4pt] asynchronous, but if people are chatting back and forth or expect that messages
[p0272-l0011] [14.4pt] will be replied to right away, then it is synchronous.
[p0272-l0012] [16.56pt] Async Advantages
[p0272-l0013] [14.4pt] The most obvious benefit of asynchronous communication is that the recipient
[p0272-l0014] [14.4pt] chooses when to consume the message (and respond if required). Related to this
[p0272-l0015] [14.4pt] is the built-in silence, or time to think, before responding. This can lead to a
[p0272-l0016] [14.4pt] higher caliber of response, likely better thought through or more researched.
[p0272-l0017] [14.4pt] Because the recipient chooses when to consume the message, they are not
[p0272-l0018] [14.4pt] interrupted at the time the message is received. This leads to higher levels of
[p0272-l0019] [14.4pt] focus on work and means the recipient can focus better on the response to the
[p0272-l0020] [14.4pt] message as well. During this focus time, the recipient can put all their effort and
[p0272-l0021] [14.4pt] attention into what they are working on, and focus can lead to deep work.4
[p0272-l0022] [14.4pt] In addition, communicating with a colleague in a different time zone is much
[p0272-l0023] [14.4pt] easier with asynchronous communication. You do not need to worry about what
[p0272-l0024] [14.4pt] time it is for them or find mutual free time. However, it is important to bear in
[p0272-l0025] [14.4pt] mind time differences so that you don’t have unreasonable expectations of when
[p0272-l0026] [14.4pt] the recipient will act on your message.
[p0272-l0027] [14.4pt] TIP

=== PDF page 273 / printed label 273 ===
[p0273-l0001] [10.8pt] When sending asynchronous communications, make it clear that you do not expect a response
[p0273-l0002] [10.8pt] right away, or outside of the recipient’s working hours. This can be done with shared
[p0273-l0003] [10.8pt] expectations or communication agreements (for example, notifications expected to be off out
[p0273-l0004] [10.8pt] of work hours), and via specific notices (such as a note in your email signature).
[p0273-l0005] [16.56pt] Async Obstacles
[p0273-l0006] [14.4pt] Beware of jumping into asynchronous communication without knowing the
[p0273-l0007] [14.4pt] problems that can occur. Many people blame synchronous meetings for all their
[p0273-l0008] [14.4pt] productivity problems, but the cost of bad asynchronous communication is
[p0273-l0009] [14.4pt] harder to see.
[p0273-l0010] [14.4pt] As with synchronous communication, make sure that you are communicating
[p0273-l0011] [14.4pt] with the right people. It is still possible to include people who should not be
[p0273-l0012] [14.4pt] included in asynchronous communications, which can add confusion and waste
[p0273-l0013] [14.4pt] their time (and yours when they ask you questions). Make sure to include the
[p0273-l0014] [14.4pt] people required for the goal of your communication.
[p0273-l0015] [14.4pt] Online tools can cause more friction than in-person communication. You and
[p0273-l0016] [14.4pt] your colleagues may now all be conversant in your choice of online whiteboard,
[p0273-l0017] [14.4pt] project management software, and instant messaging, but bear in mind that these
[p0273-l0018] [14.4pt] tools often take more mental energy than the equivalent in-person
[p0273-l0019] [14.4pt] communication.
[p0273-l0020] [14.4pt] A remote conversation in Slack or Teams would likely be a face-to-face
[p0273-l0021] [14.4pt] conversation in an office. In person, it is much simpler to speak and to read each
[p0273-l0022] [14.4pt] other’s body language. Over chat, you need to type (which can be much more
[p0273-l0023] [14.4pt] cumbersome than speech), and all the body language and tone of voice are lost.
[p0273-l0024] [14.4pt] It is easy to misunderstand a typed message, which requires a lot more mental
[p0273-l0025] [14.4pt] energy to decode.
[p0273-l0026] [14.4pt] The internet and instant messaging have been flooded with emojis for a long
[p0273-l0027] [14.4pt] time, but remote work has made emojis first-class citizens in business
[p0273-l0028] [14.4pt] communications too. One reason is the loss of body language and tone of voice
[p0273-l0029] [14.4pt] in written communication. Although emojis can help communicate, be aware
[p0273-l0030] [14.4pt] that not everyone will interpret them in the same way. If you encounter problems
[p0273-l0031] [14.4pt] with this, you may want to develop an emoji dictionary together as a team. This
[p0273-l0032] [14.4pt] will help avoid miscommunication and is also a good team-building activity that
[p0273-l0033] [14.4pt] can be done asynchronously.

=== PDF page 274 / printed label 274 ===
[p0274-l0001] [16.56pt] Direction Matters
[p0274-l0002] [14.4pt] Communication can be classified into two general categories: one-way
[p0274-l0003] [14.4pt] (unidirectional) and two-way (bidirectional). Both of these can be from one
[p0274-l0004] [14.4pt] person or system to many, from one to one, from many to one, or from many to
[p0274-l0005] [14.4pt] many (see Figure 14-1).
[p0274-l0006] [14.4pt] Unidirectional communication includes personal or progress updates,
[p0274-l0007] [14.4pt] announcements, and other information sharing. Unidirectional communication
[p0274-l0008] [14.4pt] typically works well asynchronously and has problems when done
[p0274-l0009] [14.4pt] synchronously. A positive or neutral company-wide announcement, for example,
[p0274-l0010] [14.4pt] works well asynchronously. Bringing everyone together at the same time to
[p0274-l0011] [14.4pt] make this type of announcement can have huge downsides, including people not
[p0274-l0012] [14.4pt] having time to digest the information.
[p0274-l0013] [14.4pt] Progress updates from your team are often also best done asynchronously to
[p0274-l0014] [14.4pt] avoid interrupting everyone and trying to find time. Dedicate synchronous time
[p0274-l0015] [14.4pt] for getting instant feedback (bidirectional), like help with the task you are
[p0274-l0016] [14.4pt] working on.

=== PDF page 275 / printed label 275 ===
[p0275-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 14-1. Communication relationships
[p0275-l0002] [14.4pt] In bidirectional communication, you expect a response to a message, whether
[p0275-l0003] [14.4pt] that is a quick confirmation, a long-form answer, or an artifact. Bidirectional
[p0275-l0004] [14.4pt] communication varies more as to whether asynchronous, synchronous, or a
[p0275-l0005] [14.4pt] mixture works best. Here are some examples:
[p0275-l0006] [14.4pt] When you need feedback or comments on something, such as an ADR or
[p0275-l0007] [14.4pt] documentation, asynchronous works well. Many tools, such as Google
[p0275-l0008] [14.4pt] Docs, Microsoft Word, and wikis have a built-in commenting features.
[p0275-l0009] [14.4pt] Linking to the document and asking for feedback is easy. Make sure
[p0275-l0010] [14.4pt] contributors have access, the ability to comment or edit, and reasonable
[p0275-l0011] [14.4pt] deadlines based on working hours to ensure you get the widest and most
[p0275-l0012] [14.4pt] diverse set of feedback.
[p0275-l0013] [14.4pt] Getting an answer to a question is usually not a planned activity that can be
[p0275-l0014] [14.4pt] put off until later. If the person or people you need information from are in
[p0275-l0015] [14.4pt] another time zone or have a working pattern different from your own,
[p0275-l0016] [14.4pt] asynchronous is likely the best way to communicate. But sometimes quick
[p0275-l0017] [14.4pt] questions can balloon into many messages back and forth, whereas a simple
[p0275-l0018] [14.4pt] synchronous audio or video call would have been much quicker.
[p0275-l0019] [14.4pt] Discussions are another form of bidirectional communication that can get
[p0275-l0020] [14.4pt] out of hand if not managed well asynchronously. When team members
[p0275-l0021] [14.4pt] cannot all be present at the same time, you may have no choice, but
[p0275-l0022] [14.4pt] consider whether a synchronous discussion, such as a meeting, could work
[p0275-l0023] [14.4pt] better for the goal and the content. A mixture of gathering information and
[p0275-l0024] [14.4pt] ideas asynchronously and then meeting synchronously to discuss may work
[p0275-l0025] [14.4pt] best.
[p0275-l0026] [14.4pt] Synchronous communication is the better option for a project kick-off
[p0275-l0027] [14.4pt] meeting if its goal is for team members to become familiar with one
[p0275-l0028] [14.4pt] another. That doesn’t mean that some of the communication involved can’t
[p0275-l0029] [14.4pt] be done asynchronously before or after the meeting. Creating templates and
[p0275-l0030] [14.4pt] standards that support the planning processes you use can enable more of
[p0275-l0031] [14.4pt] your planning activities to be done asynchronously. Prework, such as
[p0275-l0032] [14.4pt] adding ideas to an online whiteboard or reading a document or spreadsheet,
[p0275-l0033] [14.4pt] can be asynchronous so that time in the meeting is not used waiting for

=== PDF page 276 / printed label 276 ===
[p0276-l0001] [14.4pt] people to complete these tasks.
[p0276-l0002] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0276-l0003] [10.8pt] When using purely asynchronous communication, make sure to give everyone time to
[p0276-l0004] [10.8pt] contribute and encourage those who have not contributed as any deadline approaches. It is easy
[p0276-l0005] [10.8pt] to end up with the same people dominating asynchronous communication, which lowers
[p0276-l0006] [10.8pt] diversity and innovation. The highest paid person’s opinion (HiPPO) or the loudest person’s
[p0276-l0007] [10.8pt] opinion is not the only thing you want to hear.
[p0276-l0008] [16.56pt] Async Methods
[p0276-l0009] [14.4pt] You can communicate asynchronously in many ways. Here are some examples:
[p0276-l0010] [14.4pt] Email
[p0276-l0011] [14.4pt] This is one of the most-used communication methods, often used within
[p0276-l0012] [14.4pt] companies and for business to business (B2B) and business to customer
[p0276-l0013] [14.4pt] (B2C) transactions. Use email when you don’t have an alternative method
[p0276-l0014] [14.4pt] for messages of any length with suppliers or external teams. To communicate
[p0276-l0015] [14.4pt] with colleagues, it is generally best to use email only for longer text-based
[p0276-l0016] [14.4pt] messages. Meeting invites are usually sent via email. See “Symmetrical
[p0276-l0017] [14.4pt] Email” for more on using email effectively.
[p0276-l0018] [14.4pt] Instant messaging
[p0276-l0019] [14.4pt] This comes in two main forms: walled-garden options, such as Slack and
[p0276-l0020] [14.4pt] Teams (which are often extensible and integratable with other apps), and
[p0276-l0021] [14.4pt] messaging apps based on phone numbers, such as WhatsApp, Signal, and
[p0276-l0022] [14.4pt] plain old text messages (which are limited to—or started life as apps on—
[p0276-l0023] [14.4pt] mobile devices, used to contact anyone with the app or a mobile phone
[p0276-l0024] [14.4pt] number). Professionally, you are much more likely to use a walled-garden
[p0276-l0025] [14.4pt] option, which is useful for communication within specific teams or on a 1:1
[p0276-l0026] [14.4pt] basis with colleagues. Many also have the option of adding external users,
[p0276-l0027] [14.4pt] which is useful for adding external development teams or other partners. The
[p0276-l0028] [14.4pt] ability to create a channel or team to communicate with multiple people
[p0276-l0029] [14.4pt] makes it easy to include only those who need to be included, but this feature
[p0276-l0030] [14.4pt] should be managed so that you don’t end up with an endless proliferation of

=== PDF page 277 / printed label 277 ===
[p0277-l0001] [14.4pt] what are essentially multiple inboxes.5
[p0277-l0002] [14.4pt] Prerecorded video and audio
[p0277-l0003] [14.4pt] These can capture a live event, such as a meeting or training session, and
[p0277-l0004] [14.4pt] then be shared afterward, or created specifically for sharing asynchronously.
[p0277-l0005] [14.4pt] Podcasts, vlogs, and prerecorded videos on platforms such as Vimeo and
[p0277-l0006] [14.4pt] YouTube are all asynchronous. These are great for sharing an update or
[p0277-l0007] [14.4pt] making an announcement, especially when you want to convey things via
[p0277-l0008] [14.4pt] your body language and tone of voice in an asynchronous way. You can also
[p0277-l0009] [14.4pt] create videos or training material for colleagues or customers.
[p0277-l0010] [14.4pt] Forums and Q&A platforms
[p0277-l0011] [14.4pt] These can be internal and private to the company, external with controlled
[p0277-l0012] [14.4pt] access, or public. One of the most common uses of forums is to ask for help
[p0277-l0013] [14.4pt] or share tips on a particular topic. Internally, you can use a tool like
[p0277-l0014] [14.4pt] Microsoft Viva Engage (formerly Yammer). This tool is often used as a more
[p0277-l0015] [14.4pt] informal method of communication within organizations, usually on
[p0277-l0016] [14.4pt] nonwork-related topics. These types of environments help create a sense of
[p0277-l0017] [14.4pt] belonging and alleviate loneliness, which can be a problem with remote
[p0277-l0018] [14.4pt] work. An example of a Q&A platform is Stack Overflow, a public platform
[p0277-l0019] [14.4pt] for people to ask and answer technical questions. You can also set up a
[p0277-l0020] [14.4pt] private Q&A platform for your company (other options are available, but
[p0277-l0021] [14.4pt] Stack Overflow offers a software as a service [SaaS] version), for content
[p0277-l0022] [14.4pt] including company and product-specific information, or sensitive private
[p0277-l0023] [14.4pt] information. The main advantage of these platforms is that knowledge is
[p0277-l0024] [14.4pt] preserved and searchable, whereas the answer to a question in an email or
[p0277-l0025] [14.4pt] chat is easily lost.
[p0277-l0026] [14.4pt] Project management tools
[p0277-l0027] [14.4pt] These tools come in many forms, including open source options like
[p0277-l0028] [14.4pt] Redmine, Taiga, and WeKan, and proprietary options like Asana and Jira.
[p0277-l0029] [14.4pt] The best use for these tools is managing communication of status updates,
[p0277-l0030] [14.4pt] assignment of tasks, and other project- or product-related communications. If
[p0277-l0031] [14.4pt] you use a Kanban or Scrum board, your team can update items on these
[p0277-l0032] [14.4pt] boards so that everyone can see the status of each item at a time that suits

=== PDF page 278 / printed label 278 ===
[p0278-l0001] [14.4pt] them.
[p0278-l0002] [14.4pt] Wikis and knowledge management systems
[p0278-l0003] [14.4pt] Information needs to be accessible and searchable to those with authority to
[p0278-l0004] [14.4pt] read it and editable to those with permission to add to or update it. Storing
[p0278-l0005] [14.4pt] information in digital documents (such as slide decks or word processor
[p0278-l0006] [14.4pt] documents) is not an efficient way to do this, but wikis and knowledge
[p0278-l0007] [14.4pt] management systems can provide the required access and permissions.
[p0278-l0008] [14.4pt] Policies and a technology radar (discussed further in “Remote Tools and
[p0278-l0009] [14.4pt] Governance”) can be communicated in this way, so they are always
[p0278-l0010] [14.4pt] accessible when needed. Open source options include AppFlowy,
[p0278-l0011] [14.4pt] MediaWiki, XWiki, and BookStack. Proprietary options include Notion,
[p0278-l0012] [14.4pt] Confluence, and Nuclino. These tools are good options for asynchronous
[p0278-l0013] [14.4pt] status and progress updates. Besides adding your update, often you can
[p0278-l0014] [14.4pt] comment on or react to an update, such as with an emoji.
[p0278-l0015] [14.4pt] Surveys and polls
[p0278-l0016] [14.4pt] An often overlooked form of communication, surveys and polls are good for
[p0278-l0017] [14.4pt] gaining information in a more structured way. You can include open-ended
[p0278-l0018] [14.4pt] questions (the answer will be free-form text) as well as closed questions (the
[p0278-l0019] [14.4pt] answer will be a score, single choice, multiple choices, and so on). These are
[p0278-l0020] [14.4pt] particularly good for eliciting information from those who may not speak up
[p0278-l0021] [14.4pt] or contribute in a synchronous discussion or even in an asynchronous chat,
[p0278-l0022] [14.4pt] especially if the answers are anonymous. You can use surveys and polls to
[p0278-l0023] [14.4pt] collect information from stakeholders or to ask customers for feedback on an
[p0278-l0024] [14.4pt] existing or proposed product.
[p0278-l0025] [14.4pt] Whiteboards and collaborative drawing tools
[p0278-l0026] [14.4pt] Digital versions of office whiteboards have benefits compared to their
[p0278-l0027] [14.4pt] physical counterparts: the digital versions have effectively infinite space,
[p0278-l0028] [14.4pt] allow many more people to view them at the same time, and enable items to
[p0278-l0029] [14.4pt] be moved around easily. Once everyone is familiar with using the tool, it can
[p0278-l0030] [14.4pt] be used either synchronously or asynchronously. Drawing tools such as
[p0278-l0031] [14.4pt] draw.io and Excalidraw can be shared and used similarly. These tools can be
[p0278-l0032] [14.4pt] employed in many ways, with the most common being to collect ideas or

=== PDF page 279 / printed label 279 ===
[p0279-l0001] [14.4pt] information, create and get feedback on diagrams, and allow voting and
[p0279-l0002] [14.4pt] other interaction (such as collaborative modeling).
[p0279-l0003] [14.4pt] Files, feedback, and comments
[p0279-l0004] [14.4pt] Collecting feedback and comments on a document or diagram lends itself
[p0279-l0005] [14.4pt] well to asynchronous communication. The document or diagram itself can
[p0279-l0006] [14.4pt] also be created collaboratively, either synchronously or asynchronously. Both
[p0279-l0007] [14.4pt] Google and Microsoft provide these features in their document tools and
[p0279-l0008] [14.4pt] similar products.
[p0279-l0009] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0279-l0010] [10.8pt] To avoid wasting time on asynchronous communication that is going nowhere fast, develop
[p0279-l0011] [10.8pt] rules for switching to synchronous communication and then use these as a team. For example,
[p0279-l0012] [10.8pt] if an email or instant message has gone back and forth four or more times, switch to a video or
[p0279-l0013] [10.8pt] audio call to clarify.
[p0279-l0014] [16.56pt] Enhance Async
[p0279-l0015] [14.4pt] Asynchronous communication can be improved by automating as much as
[p0279-l0016] [14.4pt] possible. There are many ways to do this, including:
[p0279-l0017] [14.4pt] Automate access to documents and resources by using single-sign-on
[p0279-l0018] [14.4pt] options, security groups, or built-in permissions models in the software you
[p0279-l0019] [14.4pt] use.
[p0279-l0020] [14.4pt] Prepopulate a person’s status update with information from their last update
[p0279-l0021] [14.4pt] so that they can make changes rather than having to write it from scratch.
[p0279-l0022] [14.4pt] Use tools such as Power Automate, Zapier, and IFTTT to automatically
[p0279-l0023] [14.4pt] populate information among your tools.
[p0279-l0024] [14.4pt] Many activities that need to be done synchronously can be improved by moving
[p0279-l0025] [14.4pt] some of the communication to asynchronous options before and after the
[p0279-l0026] [14.4pt] synchronous event. This creates an asynchronous sandwich: the asynchronous
[p0279-l0027] [14.4pt] activities are like the bread, and the synchronous activity is the filling.6

=== PDF page 280 / printed label 280 ===
[p0280-l0001] [14.4pt] SETTING AND HANDLING EXPECTATIONS FOR ASYNC
[p0280-l0002] [14.4pt] COMMUNICATION
[p0280-l0003] [14.4pt] To avoid or minimize problems that occur with asynchronous
[p0280-l0004] [14.4pt] communication, it is important to set expectations. In their book Remote
[p0280-l0005] [14.4pt] Works (Berrett-Koehler, 2023), Ali Greene and Tamara Sanderson describe a
[p0280-l0006] [14.4pt] useful model to help with this: the 4 Ws. Here is an overview of their 4 Ws:
[p0280-l0007] [14.4pt] Who?
[p0280-l0008] [14.4pt] Who should and shouldn’t be included in the communication? Don’t
[p0280-l0009] [14.4pt] overburden people with unnecessary information or leave out the
[p0280-l0010] [14.4pt] person who has the authority to make the decision.
[p0280-l0011] [14.4pt] Who is responsible for responding? Is a response needed, and if so,
[p0280-l0012] [14.4pt] from whom? Explicitly state what you need from each person
[p0280-l0013] [14.4pt] involved.
[p0280-l0014] [14.4pt] Ensure that the discussion or decision doesn’t move forward until the
[p0280-l0015] [14.4pt] person or people you need a response from have responded.
[p0280-l0016] [14.4pt] What?
[p0280-l0017] [14.4pt] What are you expecting in return? If you are expecting a response,
[p0280-l0018] [14.4pt] what form are you expecting that response to take? Explicitly state
[p0280-l0019] [14.4pt] this in your communication.
[p0280-l0020] [14.4pt] What tool and workflow should be used? Do you have a
[p0280-l0021] [14.4pt] communication agreement that specifies this? Ensure that the tool
[p0280-l0022] [14.4pt] and workflow meet the goals of the communication.
[p0280-l0023] [14.4pt] When?
[p0280-l0024] [14.4pt] Specify the date, time, and time zone every time. Using statements
[p0280-l0025] [14.4pt] such as end of day tomorrow or asap is meaningless, especially when
[p0280-l0026] [14.4pt] people have different work hours or are in different time zones.
[p0280-l0027] [14.4pt] Set a deadline for responses that takes into account recipients’
[p0280-l0028] [14.4pt] working hours and time zones. Hold people accountable and make

=== PDF page 281 / printed label 281 ===
[p0281-l0001] [14.4pt] sure the communication doesn’t stall.
[p0281-l0002] [14.4pt] Wah-wah!
[p0281-l0003] [14.4pt] What happens if you receive no response? State what will happen if
[p0281-l0004] [14.4pt] the deadline is missed.
[p0281-l0005] [14.4pt] Make the reasons for response clear. State the impact on the
[p0281-l0006] [14.4pt] team/solution/project, so that everyone knows why they are putting
[p0281-l0007] [14.4pt] effort into the response. For example: “If I don’t hear back from you
[p0281-l0008] [14.4pt] by the date and time above, I will assume no changes need to be
[p0281-l0009] [14.4pt] made and send the document to the client as is.”
[p0281-l0010] [14.4pt] To these 4 Ws, I will add a 5th: Why? Why are you sending the
[p0281-l0011] [14.4pt] communication? What are the goals? Understanding why will help you
[p0281-l0012] [14.4pt] answer the other 4 Ws. Why will help you to do the following:
[p0281-l0013] [14.4pt] Select the people who are needed to meet the goals (who).
[p0281-l0014] [14.4pt] Work out what response you need (what).
[p0281-l0015] [14.4pt] Decide on an appropriate deadline (when).
[p0281-l0016] [14.4pt] Understand the effects of missing the deadline (wah-wah).
[p0281-l0017] [20.16pt] Remote-First Working
[p0281-l0018] [14.4pt] In this section, you’ll learn all about remote-first working, but first let’s untangle
[p0281-l0019] [14.4pt] some of the terminology. Although the terms remote-first and remote-friendly are
[p0281-l0020] [14.4pt] often used interchangeably, the outcomes of these approaches differ
[p0281-l0021] [14.4pt] significantly.
[p0281-l0022] [16.56pt] Remote-First Versus Remote-Friendly
[p0281-l0023] [14.4pt] Both remote-first and remote-friendly are hybrid approaches to working,
[p0281-l0024] [14.4pt] meaning an organization’s workforce may be working remotely or in the
[p0281-l0025] [14.4pt] organization’s offices. In a remote-first approach, processes and decisions are

=== PDF page 282 / printed label 282 ===
[p0282-l0001] [14.4pt] based on optimizing remote work, and all workers are given the same chance of
[p0282-l0002] [14.4pt] success. It doesn’t mean remote-only or fully distributed but has the same
[p0282-l0003] [14.4pt] principles.
[p0282-l0004] [14.4pt] In a remote-friendly approach, the business allows remote work but does not
[p0282-l0005] [14.4pt] optimize for it. The organization is office-centric and sees remote working as a
[p0282-l0006] [14.4pt] benefit to workers.
[p0282-l0007] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0282-l0008] [10.8pt] One of the key differences is that remote-first values outcomes over hours worked, and
[p0282-l0009] [10.8pt] remote-friendly values hours worked over outcomes.
[p0282-l0010] [14.4pt] The remote-first model is characterized by the following:
[p0282-l0011] [14.4pt] Remote work is not just supported but encouraged, with managers also
[p0282-l0012] [14.4pt] encouraged to work remotely.
[p0282-l0013] [14.4pt] Organizational processes and decisions are based on optimizing for remote
[p0282-l0014] [14.4pt] work, such as taking different time zones into account, with everything
[p0282-l0015] [14.4pt] designed around supporting remote working.
[p0282-l0016] [14.4pt] Asynchronous communication is emphasized, and key decisions are made
[p0282-l0017] [14.4pt] and communicated this way.
[p0282-l0018] [14.4pt] Synchronous meetings are kept to a minimum and recorded, with
[p0282-l0019] [14.4pt] attendance being optional (based on the assumption that some people
[p0282-l0020] [14.4pt] cannot attend).
[p0282-l0021] [14.4pt] Virtual team-building is given a high priority. The difficulty of bonding
[p0282-l0022] [14.4pt] virtually is taken into account, and more effort is put into this activity to
[p0282-l0023] [14.4pt] compensate.
[p0282-l0024] [14.4pt] All employee voices are equal, regardless of where they are working; for
[p0282-l0025] [14.4pt] example, all attendees of synchronous meetings are expected to attend
[p0282-l0026] [14.4pt] virtually, whether they are working remotely or happen to be colocated in
[p0282-l0027] [14.4pt] an office.
[p0282-l0028] [14.4pt] All employees have the same flexibility of schedule, whether working

=== PDF page 283 / printed label 283 ===
[p0283-l0001] [14.4pt] remotely or in an office, which is supported by asynchronous
[p0283-l0002] [14.4pt] communication.
[p0283-l0003] [14.4pt] Praise and promotions are awarded based on contribution and not on
[p0283-l0004] [14.4pt] proximity to the office.
[p0283-l0005] [14.4pt] People, information, and resources are equally accessible whether someone
[p0283-l0006] [14.4pt] is working remotely or in an office.
[p0283-l0007] [14.4pt] Recruitment does not focus on where a potential employee lives, but
[p0283-l0008] [14.4pt] whether they are right for the role and company.
[p0283-l0009] [14.4pt] Output is valued over hours worked. Employees do not gain points with
[p0283-l0010] [14.4pt] management for being seen working late in the office. The output of their
[p0283-l0011] [14.4pt] work is what matters.
[p0283-l0012] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0283-l0013] [10.8pt] Employees’ work-life balance must be maintained via explicit expectations from the
[p0283-l0014] [10.8pt] organization and managers, such as that notifications will be turned off outside working hours.
[p0283-l0015] [10.8pt] It is easy for work and home to blur when your office is in your home. Remote workers need to
[p0283-l0016] [10.8pt] be protected from burnout.
[p0283-l0017] [14.4pt] The remote-friendly model is quite different, being more of a supplement to
[p0283-l0018] [14.4pt] traditional office-based work, and brings with it more collaboration challenges
[p0283-l0019] [14.4pt] when it comes to remote workers:
[p0283-l0020] [14.4pt] Remote work is allowed, such as for certain roles or a specified number of
[p0283-l0021] [14.4pt] days per week, and is seen as an employee benefit rather than the
[p0283-l0022] [14.4pt] organization actively seeking to employ remote workers.
[p0283-l0023] [14.4pt] The organization is office-centric, with managers typically working in the
[p0283-l0024] [14.4pt] office full-time or rarely remotely.
[p0283-l0025] [14.4pt] The business relies heavily on synchronous communication, such as team
[p0283-l0026] [14.4pt] or project meetings, and attendance is expected.
[p0283-l0027] [14.4pt] When synchronous meetings take place, those who are colocated attend
[p0283-l0028] [14.4pt] together in a meeting room, while remote workers attend virtually.

=== PDF page 284 / printed label 284 ===
[p0284-l0001] [14.4pt] Key decisions are made synchronously and in person, without taking
[p0284-l0002] [14.4pt] remote working into account much, if at all.
[p0284-l0003] [14.4pt] The business usually provides little to no support for remote workers in
[p0284-l0004] [14.4pt] other time zones.
[p0284-l0005] [14.4pt] Remote workers end up on a lower tier than office-based employees, with
[p0284-l0006] [14.4pt] office-based employees praised and promoted over remote employees.
[p0284-l0007] [14.4pt] Hours worked are valued over output. Value is given to those seen to work
[p0284-l0008] [14.4pt] overtime, whether they are recompensed for this or not.
[p0284-l0009] [16.56pt] Remote-First Benefits
[p0284-l0010] [14.4pt] Remote-first has many benefits, even to organizations that have a minority of
[p0284-l0011] [14.4pt] employees working remotely, with some benefits coming from the ability to
[p0284-l0012] [14.4pt] employ a workforce that is not dependent on the availability of office space or
[p0284-l0013] [14.4pt] location. Benefits of remote-first include the following:
[p0284-l0014] [14.4pt] Long-term flexibility
[p0284-l0015] [14.4pt] The organization can scale up or down without worrying about having to
[p0284-l0016] [14.4pt] also scale office space or physical infrastructure.
[p0284-l0017] [14.4pt] Business continuity planning
[p0284-l0018] [14.4pt] A distributed workforce decreases the likelihood that a large percentage of
[p0284-l0019] [14.4pt] employees would be affected by an issue that would stop them from
[p0284-l0020] [14.4pt] working, such as a power cut, lost internet access, weather conditions, or
[p0284-l0021] [14.4pt] public transport strikes.
[p0284-l0022] [14.4pt] People and productivity are put first
[p0284-l0023] [14.4pt] Irrespective of location, time zone, or work pattern, people are enabled to do
[p0284-l0024] [14.4pt] their best work via processes, tools, and so on. Everyone is enabled to work
[p0284-l0025] [14.4pt] effectively, without wasting the potential of remote employees.
[p0284-l0026] [14.4pt] A larger talent pool is available
[p0284-l0027] [14.4pt] The organization is not restricted to people who live within commuting
[p0284-l0028] [14.4pt] distance of an office and can find the best person for the job regardless of

=== PDF page 285 / printed label 285 ===
[p0285-l0001] [14.4pt] location.7 Many companies now employ people who don’t live in a fixed
[p0285-l0002] [14.4pt] location or country, commonly known as digital nomads.
[p0285-l0003] [14.4pt] Costs are reduced
[p0285-l0004] [14.4pt] Less office space is needed, along with less lighting, heating, and cooling.
[p0285-l0005] [14.4pt] There is less need for a travel subsidy for commuters, and employees also
[p0285-l0006] [14.4pt] reduce or eliminate their commuting costs.
[p0285-l0007] [14.4pt] A reduced carbon footprint
[p0285-l0008] [14.4pt] Less commuting, office space, lighting, heating, and cooling mean lower
[p0285-l0009] [14.4pt] emissions.
[p0285-l0010] [14.4pt] Increased employee productivity and engagement
[p0285-l0011] [14.4pt] All employees feel valued, enjoy a better work-life balance, and experience
[p0285-l0012] [14.4pt] fewer interruptions because they have less synchronous communication and
[p0285-l0013] [14.4pt] are not in an office environment.
[p0285-l0014] [14.4pt] More and better documentation
[p0285-l0015] [14.4pt] Asynchronous communication promotes documentation by default, which
[p0285-l0016] [14.4pt] supports onboarding new people and enables everyone to find the
[p0285-l0017] [14.4pt] information they need independently.
[p0285-l0018] [14.4pt] Improved personal management skills
[p0285-l0019] [14.4pt] Because employees know they might not receive a response to their
[p0285-l0020] [14.4pt] asynchronous communication within a few minutes, they become better at
[p0285-l0021] [14.4pt] managing their work and planning ahead.
[p0285-l0022] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0285-l0023] [10.8pt] Some of the benefits of remote-first are similar to the benefits of moving from on-premises
[p0285-l0024] [10.8pt] servers to the cloud. Not having to worry about scaling office space up or down is similar to
[p0285-l0025] [10.8pt] moving servers and services onto the cloud provider. A distributed workforce is similar to
[p0285-l0026] [10.8pt] using multiregion or zonal options in the cloud for high availability and business continuity.

=== PDF page 286 / printed label 286 ===
[p0286-l0001] [14.4pt] In person work has some advantages over remote working, although these
[p0286-l0002] [14.4pt] benefits are only fully realized in a remote-first or fully in-person environment.
[p0286-l0003] [14.4pt] Some of the benefits of in person working can be adjusted for remote working:
[p0286-l0004] [14.4pt] Building rapport or team-building is much easier in person. Remote-first
[p0286-l0005] [14.4pt] and fully remote organizations need to focus on virtual team-building
[p0286-l0006] [14.4pt] and/or fund retreats to support these activities.
[p0286-l0007] [14.4pt] Social accountability is built in when working in person. It needs to be
[p0286-l0008] [14.4pt] planned and built remotely.
[p0286-l0009] [14.4pt] Chance encounters and conversations can lead to new ideas or fixing
[p0286-l0010] [14.4pt] problems when working in person. This is very hard to replicate remotely.
[p0286-l0011] [14.4pt] Some employees need or want to work in an office environment. Remote-
[p0286-l0012] [14.4pt] first or fully remote companies would need to have flexible workspace near
[p0286-l0013] [14.4pt] the employee or offer a stipend for coworking.
[p0286-l0014] [14.4pt] When working in person, collaborating and communicating requires less
[p0286-l0015] [14.4pt] reliance on technology. Coworkers can still communicate face-to-face if
[p0286-l0016] [14.4pt] there were a technology issue in an office (although they might not be able
[p0286-l0017] [14.4pt] to do their jobs without it).
[p0286-l0018] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0286-l0019] [10.8pt] Personal user manuals, or readme files, are an effective tool for remote team-building. Each
[p0286-l0020] [10.8pt] team member’s user manual, based on a template, is shared asynchronously among the team
[p0286-l0021] [10.8pt] and other colleagues. It should include how you like to work, a bit about yourself, your goals,
[p0286-l0022] [10.8pt] and so on.
[p0286-l0023] [16.56pt] Evolving to Remote-First
[p0286-l0024] [14.4pt] Becoming a remote-first organization (also known as an anywhere organization)
[p0286-l0025] [14.4pt] makes sense. It supports short- and long-term business goals, such as scalability,
[p0286-l0026] [14.4pt] business continuity, and reducing costs, while supporting employee wellness and
[p0286-l0027] [14.4pt] productivity. As with many adoptions, the hardest part is often culture change
[p0286-l0028] [14.4pt] and convincing people of the need for change.
[p0286-l0029] [14.4pt] The change required will be a mixture of business and technical aspects. From

=== PDF page 287 / printed label 287 ===
[p0287-l0001] [14.4pt] your perspective as a technical professional, enterprise architecture (EA) will
[p0287-l0002] [14.4pt] play a key role (even if you don’t have anyone with the role of enterprise
[p0287-l0003] [14.4pt] architect). Approach the change from an enterprise architecture perspective,
[p0287-l0004] [14.4pt] considering both business and technology. Here are some ways EA will support
[p0287-l0005] [14.4pt] you in evolving to remote-first:
[p0287-l0006] [14.4pt] Impact analysis
[p0287-l0007] [14.4pt] Identifies potential impacts on the wide-ranging aspects of the organization
[p0287-l0008] [14.4pt] (for example, data, systems, HR, and finance). You can use it to analyze the
[p0287-l0009] [14.4pt] benefits and risks to enable or mitigate them.
[p0287-l0010] [14.4pt] Business process modeling
[p0287-l0011] [14.4pt] Models the current processes to help identify the changes needed and design
[p0287-l0012] [14.4pt] the future state of these processes.
[p0287-l0013] [14.4pt] Gap analysis
[p0287-l0014] [14.4pt] Works out what needs to be implemented, migrated, and deprecated when
[p0287-l0015] [14.4pt] moving from the current state to the planned future state or milestone.
[p0287-l0016] [14.4pt] Capability mapping
[p0287-l0017] [14.4pt] Helps you identify the capabilities that need to change or be created to
[p0287-l0018] [14.4pt] support remote working as a priority.
[p0287-l0019] [14.4pt] EventStorming
[p0287-l0020] [14.4pt] Facilitates assessing your current state, planning, and moving toward remote-
[p0287-l0021] [14.4pt] first using the process-modeling level.
[p0287-l0022] [14.4pt] Domain storytelling
[p0287-l0023] [14.4pt] Elicits the high-level current state of processes and systems and to design
[p0287-l0024] [14.4pt] how these need to change.
[p0287-l0025] [14.4pt] The following are some business- and process-centered ways to implement
[p0287-l0026] [14.4pt] remote-first in your organization or team and can be applied to the architecture
[p0287-l0027] [14.4pt] techniques:

=== PDF page 288 / printed label 288 ===
[p0288-l0001] [14.4pt] Consider every employee to be a remote worker
[p0288-l0002] [14.4pt] Those working in an office just happen to be colocated.
[p0288-l0003] [14.4pt] Plan and create support systems for remote work
[p0288-l0004] [14.4pt] You need to understand the challenges faced by remote workers. Work with
[p0288-l0005] [14.4pt] them (and their office-based colleagues) to identify challenges and
[p0288-l0006] [14.4pt] compensate for them. This might involve changing expectations or providing
[p0288-l0007] [14.4pt] software or hardware tools.
[p0288-l0008] [14.4pt] Adopt asynchronous communication
[p0288-l0009] [14.4pt] Put in place expectations and agreements on how communication will
[p0288-l0010] [14.4pt] happen and the tools that will be used (see “Async to Think”).
[p0288-l0011] [14.4pt] Make key decisions asynchronously
[p0288-l0012] [14.4pt] Allow time for everyone involved to contribute on an equal footing.
[p0288-l0013] [14.4pt] Underlay these decisions with a remote-first bias.8
[p0288-l0014] [14.4pt] Encourage those who work in offices to work remotely
[p0288-l0015] [14.4pt] When everyone works remotely, at least occasionally, empathy is built for
[p0288-l0016] [14.4pt] those who usually work remotely.
[p0288-l0017] [14.4pt] Give the same flexibility to all
[p0288-l0018] [14.4pt] Those working in an office should be given the same flexibility as remote
[p0288-l0019] [14.4pt] workers; for example, if remote workers can choose their start and finish
[p0288-l0020] [14.4pt] time, so should office workers.
[p0288-l0021] [14.4pt] Treat all employees equally, but not the same
[p0288-l0022] [14.4pt] Take into account time zones, personal circumstances, disabilities, and so
[p0288-l0023] [14.4pt] forth, to put everyone on an equal footing.
[p0288-l0024] [14.4pt] Tailor remuneration to whether the role is remote or not
[p0288-l0025] [14.4pt] Benefits offered to employees who work in an office are often useless to a
[p0288-l0026] [14.4pt] remote employee, such as free lunch, on-site gym, or travel subsidies.

=== PDF page 289 / printed label 289 ===
[p0289-l0001] [14.4pt] Remote benefits could include a stipend for coworking space, a gym
[p0289-l0002] [14.4pt] membership, or a certain amount of expensed groceries. Where it is difficult
[p0289-l0003] [14.4pt] to offer benefits such as healthcare and pensions to employees in other
[p0289-l0004] [14.4pt] countries, a stipend toward these can work well.
[p0289-l0005] [14.4pt] Embrace documentation by default and invest time and money into knowledge
[p0289-l0006] [14.4pt] management
[p0289-l0007] [14.4pt] Asynchronous communication means that many things are documented
[p0289-l0008] [14.4pt] automatically, but this documentation needs to be managed so that it is
[p0289-l0009] [14.4pt] accessible and efficient. See Part III for more on knowledge management.
[p0289-l0010] [14.4pt] Consider creating a head of remote9
[p0289-l0011] [14.4pt] A head of remote or similar position within the company can improve
[p0289-l0012] [14.4pt] internal adoption of remote-first and/or promote the practice outside of your
[p0289-l0013] [14.4pt] company (marketing and promotion).
[p0289-l0014] [14.4pt] Move away from traditional KPIs and OKRs
[p0289-l0015] [14.4pt] Key performance indicators (KPIs) and objectives and key results (OKRs)
[p0289-l0016] [14.4pt] typically measure performance over time and value hours worked over
[p0289-l0017] [14.4pt] output. Change to managing by output and productivity.
[p0289-l0018] [14.4pt] Plan for and put into action digital team- and rapport-building activities
[p0289-l0019] [14.4pt] Those joining these activities from an office should join in the same way as
[p0289-l0020] [14.4pt] those joining remotely.
[p0289-l0021] [14.4pt] Remember that remote working can increase loneliness
[p0289-l0022] [14.4pt] Remind remote employees that camaraderie does not have to come from
[p0289-l0023] [14.4pt] colleagues alone and that they should take social opportunities. You can help
[p0289-l0024] [14.4pt] by paying for remote employees to visit offices or each other, to attend
[p0289-l0025] [14.4pt] retreats for all employees, or to go to in-person conferences and training.
[p0289-l0026] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0289-l0027] [10.8pt] SaaS tools support remote work well, as they can be accessed from anywhere and usually via
[p0289-l0028] [10.8pt] single sign-on (SSO). This simplifies access management and removes the need to allow

=== PDF page 290 / printed label 290 ===
[p0290-l0001] [10.8pt] external access to an on-prem or cloud-based resource (reducing attack vectors on your
[p0290-l0002] [10.8pt] systems).
[p0290-l0003] [14.4pt] In Remote Works, Greene and Sanderson write, “In the future, we believe the
[p0290-l0004] [14.4pt] lines we draw today—in person, remote, or hybrid—will blur, and all knowledge
