[p0290-l0005] [14.4pt] work will be digital-first.” The future does look to be moving toward a digital-
[p0290-l0006] [14.4pt] first, asynchronous, and global business world.
[p0290-l0007] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0290-l0008] [14.4pt] You have now seen that successful communication is, in part, about accessibility
[p0290-l0009] [14.4pt] and inclusion. Leveling the playing field for all colleagues, and diagrams that
[p0290-l0010] [14.4pt] don’t shut out part of your audience (see Chapter 3), contribute to a better
[p0290-l0011] [14.4pt] working environment and better communication overall.
[p0290-l0012] [14.4pt] In this chapter, you saw how using asynchronous or synchronous communication
[p0290-l0013] [14.4pt] appropriately can make your colleagues happier by improving their work life
[p0290-l0014] [14.4pt] and helping them to communicate. In the previous chapter, you saw how
[p0290-l0015] [14.4pt] respecting people’s time also improves contentment and work-life balance. I bet
[p0290-l0016] [14.4pt] you didn’t think this was a book about happiness.
[p0290-l0017] [14.4pt] To round out Part IV, you will now learn how to more effectively use email and
[p0290-l0018] [14.4pt] presentations (some of the most common channels of communication), as well as
[p0290-l0019] [14.4pt] govern remote communication and the tools your company uses.
[p0290-l0020] [10.8pt] 1 Heuristics are practical methods that are not guaranteed to be optimal.
[p0290-l0021] [10.8pt] 2 The car park or parking lot technique is an efficient tool to keep meetings on track, placing topics
[p0290-l0022] [10.8pt] that are not on the agenda aside so that they can be discussed later. Participants interested in the topics
[p0290-l0023] [10.8pt] should be content to go back to the meeting agenda, knowing their topic will not be forgotten.
[p0290-l0024] [10.8pt] 3 See “Nonverbal Overload: A Theoretical Argument for the Causes of Zoom Fatigue” by Jeremy
[p0290-l0025] [10.8pt] Bailenson.
[p0290-l0026] [10.8pt] 4 For more on deep work, see Cal Newport’s Deep Work: Rules for Focused Success in a Distracted
[p0290-l0027] [10.8pt] World (Grand Central Publishing, 2016).
[p0290-l0028] [10.8pt] 5 I know of one organization that had a greater number of Microsoft Teams teams than people
[p0290-l0029] [10.8pt] working in the whole organization.

=== PDF page 291 / printed label 291 ===
[p0291-l0001] [10.8pt] 6 This is similar to the async-sync-async sandwich in coding, but the second async element in code is
[p0291-l0002] [10.8pt] processing the response to the first async element, which may not be the case for meetings and pre-
[p0291-l0003] [10.8pt] and postwork.
[p0291-l0004] [10.8pt] 7 Legal or other restrictions might limit where employees of a company can live.
[p0291-l0005] [10.8pt] 8 Note this is a remote-first bias, not a bias toward remote workers.
[p0291-l0006] [10.8pt] 9 A head-of-remote role was originally suggested by Andreas Klinger, who was Head of Remote at
[p0291-l0007] [10.8pt] AngelList.

=== PDF page 292 / printed label 292 ===
[p0292-l0001] [28.8pt] Chapter 15. Remote Channels
[p0292-l0002] [14.4pt] Methods of communication can be classified as asynchronous, synchronous, or a
[p0292-l0003] [14.4pt] combination of both. Identifying the best method in a particular situation can be
[p0292-l0004] [14.4pt] hard, especially if you don’t know the pros and cons of each.
[p0292-l0005] [14.4pt] This chapter explores how to get the best out of email communication,
[p0292-l0006] [14.4pt] presenting and screen-sharing online, and communication tools you can use with
[p0292-l0007] [14.4pt] remote and hybrid teams.
[p0292-l0008] [20.16pt] Symmetrical Email
[p0292-l0009] [14.4pt] Many see email as a necessary evil, but it doesn’t have to be that way.
[p0292-l0010] [14.4pt] Symmetrical email is a pattern to help govern the process of sending and
[p0292-l0011] [14.4pt] responding to emails and address the problems and inequalities email can cause.
[p0292-l0012] [14.4pt] Here are the three main problems with email that this pattern addresses:
[p0292-l0013] [14.4pt] Different people deal with and respond to emails in different ways.
[p0292-l0014] [14.4pt] When to use email and when to use another form of communication, such
[p0292-l0015] [14.4pt] as instant messaging, isn’t always clear.
[p0292-l0016] [14.4pt] Expectations and clarity are usually missing from the emails themselves.
[p0292-l0017] [14.4pt] Your company or team may or may not have set expectations for email, but some
[p0292-l0018] [14.4pt] of your colleagues likely check their email constantly, while others check it once
[p0292-l0019] [14.4pt] a day or less. You also receive many types of communications in email form:
[p0292-l0020] [14.4pt] newsletters, notifications, requests, tasks, and so on.
[p0292-l0021] [14.4pt] Without shared and respected expectations and processes around email, no one
[p0292-l0022] [14.4pt] can reliably communicate. You need to know when to use email over another
[p0292-l0023] [14.4pt] method of communication, how quickly you are expected to respond to different
[p0292-l0024] [14.4pt] types of email, and how to communicate effectively via email using shared
[p0292-l0025] [14.4pt] standards.
[p0292-l0026] [14.4pt] You can solve these problems by structuring and augmenting email based around

=== PDF page 293 / printed label 293 ===
[p0293-l0001] [14.4pt] the reasons for sending emails, setting expectations for how email is used, and
[p0293-l0002] [14.4pt] improving clarity in email messages and subjects.
[p0293-l0003] [16.56pt] Email Reasons
[p0293-l0004] [14.4pt] A shared communication agreement within your team, department, or company
[p0293-l0005] [14.4pt] is a good way to enact shared standards. You may find it useful to set up
[p0293-l0006] [14.4pt] agreements at all these levels. You can even share the agreement with other
[p0293-l0007] [14.4pt] businesses you work with so that they are aware and can hopefully reciprocate,
[p0293-l0008] [14.4pt] or create formal internal or external service level agreements.
[p0293-l0009] [14.4pt] Decide what email should be used for. Some uses might include sending updates
[p0293-l0010] [14.4pt] about projects to stakeholders or communicating with people outside of your
[p0293-l0011] [14.4pt] business. Your communication agreement should state which types of
[p0293-l0012] [14.4pt] communication should be emailed and which types should be conveyed in
[p0293-l0013] [14.4pt] another way.
[p0293-l0014] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0293-l0015] [10.8pt] To decide which types of communication to email, it is useful to first create a list of all the
[p0293-l0016] [10.8pt] typical types of (and reasons for) communication within your team, department, or company.
[p0293-l0017] [10.8pt] Then work out which are better suited to synchronous or asynchronous methods and use that to
[p0293-l0018] [10.8pt] help decide which channel of communication to use, such as email, instant message, wiki page
[p0293-l0019] [10.8pt] update, and so forth.
[p0293-l0020] [16.56pt] Email Expectations
[p0293-l0021] [14.4pt] Once you have decided the reasons for using email, create a shared standard for
[p0293-l0022] [14.4pt] efficiently communicating the urgency of a message and the response you
[p0293-l0023] [14.4pt] expect. The subject line of the email is the best place to communicate this
[p0293-l0024] [14.4pt] information so that it is front and center for the receiver.
[p0293-l0025] [14.4pt] For an urgent email, a subject line might be “[URGENT, response required by
[p0293-l0026] [14.4pt] 1 p.m. CET today, 24th July] Request for telemetry data.” If an email requires no
[p0293-l0027] [14.4pt] response and is only for information, you might use “[FYI] Minor update to
[p0293-l0028] [14.4pt] project budget,” and if a response is required by one recipient only you might
[p0293-l0029] [14.4pt] include something like “[Response required - Libby] Architecture meeting
[p0293-l0030] [14.4pt] update.”

=== PDF page 294 / printed label 294 ===
[p0294-l0001] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0294-l0002] [10.8pt] Don’t overuse phrases like urgent. If messages are marked urgent that are not urgent, people
[p0294-l0003] [10.8pt] will stop reacting quickly to messages that really are urgent. This is the same advice as the
[p0294-l0004] [10.8pt] fable “The Boy Who Cried Wolf”.
[p0294-l0005] [14.4pt] Add examples to your communication agreement and ensure that the agreement
[p0294-l0006] [14.4pt] is part of onboarding so that new team members understand emails sent to them
[p0294-l0007] [14.4pt] and can use the standard themselves. The examples just mentioned are clear to
[p0294-l0008] [14.4pt] anyone who reads them and so can be sent to people outside of your
[p0294-l0009] [14.4pt] communication agreement too.
[p0294-l0010] [14.4pt] It is a good idea to review your communication agreement around 2 to 4 weeks
[p0294-l0011] [14.4pt] after it is first created, when issues arise, and regularly, for example, every 6 to
[p0294-l0012] [14.4pt] 12 months.
[p0294-l0013] [16.56pt] Email Clarity
[p0294-l0014] [14.4pt] Problems arise in email communication in part because email is easy to send
[p0294-l0015] [14.4pt] (even to multiple recipients) but is not always quick to read and respond to.
[p0294-l0016] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0294-l0017] [10.8pt] The symmetrical email pattern can be applied to automated emails and notifications to ensure
[p0294-l0018] [10.8pt] that recipients are clear about the urgency and response required from them.
[p0294-l0019] [14.4pt] If it is not clear what is required of the recipient, they need to spend time and
[p0294-l0020] [14.4pt] mental energy working out what they need to do and on what time scale. This
[p0294-l0021] [14.4pt] happens every time there is a reply to the email thread, which may happen many
[p0294-l0022] [14.4pt] times, especially if more than two people are involved. Part of this can be solved
[p0294-l0023] [14.4pt] with the subject line examples mentioned earlier in this chapter, but you can go
[p0294-l0024] [14.4pt] further. Spending a little more time and thought on the creation of an email can
[p0294-l0025] [14.4pt] save hours for everyone involved, including the original author.
[p0294-l0026] [14.4pt] It is good practice to include any specifics from the subject line in the email
[p0294-l0027] [14.4pt] body also, such as the time and date a response is required. Then the recipient

=== PDF page 295 / printed label 295 ===
[p0295-l0001] [14.4pt] doesn’t need to refer back to the subject line at the top when they finish reading,
[p0295-l0002] [14.4pt] and you have made the response requirement obvious.
[p0295-l0003] [14.4pt] When you send an email to multiple recipients, explicitly state your expectations
[p0295-l0004] [14.4pt] for each one. Don’t expect recipients to work this out based on whether they are
[p0295-l0005] [14.4pt] in the To, Cc, or Bcc field. Indicate who you expect a response from and who
[p0295-l0006] [14.4pt] you don’t, and what you expect in the response, as shown in Example 15-1.
[p0295-l0007] [14.4pt] Example 15-1. Email with clear expectations for all recipients
[p0295-l0008] [10.8pt] from: kim@polyglotmedia.com
[p0295-l0009] [10.8pt] to: gino@polyglotmedia.com
[p0295-l0010] [10.8pt] cc: sander@polyglotmedia.com, elissa@glidani.com
[p0295-l0011] [10.8pt] subject: [Response required by Friday - Gino] Change of plan for kick-off meeting
[p0295-l0012] [10.8pt] Hi Gino, Elissa, and Sander,
[p0295-l0013] [10.8pt] The kick-off meeting arranged for next Thursday needs to be moved. Elissa has
[p0295-l0014] [10.8pt] suggested Friday at 10 a.m.-12 p.m. CET or 2 p.m.-4 p.m. CET.
[p0295-l0015] [10.8pt] @Gino, can you please let me know by 4 p.m. (CET) this Friday which one of
[p0295-l0016] [10.8pt] Elissa's suggestions is preferred by you and your team, or any days and times your
[p0295-l0017] [10.8pt] team can make it the following Monday or Tuesday if Friday is not possible.
[p0295-l0018] [10.8pt] @Sander, this is FYI but please let me know if you have any concerns.
[p0295-l0019] [10.8pt] @Elissa please let me know if the availability you gave me for the following Monday
[p0295-l0020] [10.8pt] and Tuesday changes.
[p0295-l0021] [10.8pt] Kind regards,
[p0295-l0022] [10.8pt] Kim
[p0295-l0023] [14.4pt] Making your expectations of recipients clear is useful for helping you decide
[p0295-l0024] [14.4pt] who should be included and who shouldn’t be. Excluding people who would not
[p0295-l0025] [14.4pt] benefit from receiving the email will save you both time.
[p0295-l0026] [14.4pt] When you ask someone to spend time on something, you should specify the
[p0295-l0027] [14.4pt] amount of time or at least an estimate:
[p0295-l0028] [14.4pt] If you ask someone to meet with you, specify a duration. For example,
[p0295-l0029] [14.4pt] “Can we please meet for 30 minutes?”
[p0295-l0030] [14.4pt] If you are asking them to complete a survey or similar task, give them an
[p0295-l0031] [14.4pt] estimate. For example, “The form should take no longer than 5–7 mins to
[p0295-l0032] [14.4pt] complete.”

=== PDF page 296 / printed label 296 ===
[p0296-l0001] [14.4pt] Or when asking for a review of a document or something similar, specify a
[p0296-l0002] [14.4pt] limit on their time. For example, “Please spend no longer than 30 minutes
[p0296-l0003] [14.4pt] reviewing the draft architecture decision.”
[p0296-l0004] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0296-l0005] [10.8pt] Email is not the only option for communicating with customers and people outside of your
[p0296-l0006] [10.8pt] company. Many tools have B2B options for inviting external users, such as adding them to a
[p0296-l0007] [10.8pt] chat or team in a messaging app. Online forums and web-based chat are other options for
[p0296-l0008] [10.8pt] communicating with customers. Pick the communication channel that best fits the purpose of
[p0296-l0009] [10.8pt] your message.
[p0296-l0010] [16.56pt] Email Tips
[p0296-l0011] [14.4pt] Here are some extra tips to improve your email communication:
[p0296-l0012] [14.4pt] Start with your main point
[p0296-l0013] [14.4pt] This makes sure that all recipients see the most important point, even if they
[p0296-l0014] [14.4pt] don’t read the whole email (see “Structured Writing” for more details).
[p0296-l0015] [14.4pt] Stick to one topic per email
[p0296-l0016] [14.4pt] Send separate emails for separate reasons or requirements to avoid
[p0296-l0017] [14.4pt] miscommunication.
[p0296-l0018] [14.4pt] Use hyperlinks
[p0296-l0019] [14.4pt] Link to any documents, web pages, or other resources you reference;
[p0296-l0020] [14.4pt] otherwise, your recipients will waste time trying to find the content you are
[p0296-l0021] [14.4pt] referring to.
[p0296-l0022] [14.4pt] Link to documents rather than attach copies
[p0296-l0023] [14.4pt] Every time you attach a document to an email, it creates a copy for every
[p0296-l0024] [14.4pt] recipient. Linking to a document as your single source of truth can help
[p0296-l0025] [14.4pt] avoid confusion and having to merge copies with updates.
[p0296-l0026] [14.4pt] Default to Reply instead of Reply All
[p0296-l0027] [14.4pt] Change the default in your email program to Reply so that you have to make

=== PDF page 297 / printed label 297 ===
[p0297-l0001] [14.4pt] a conscious effort to reply to everyone included in an email. Typically, you
[p0297-l0002] [14.4pt] need to reply only to the sender.
[p0297-l0003] [14.4pt] Turn on Undo Send
[p0297-l0004] [14.4pt] Many email programs, including Gmail and Outlook, have a feature that
[p0297-l0005] [14.4pt] delays sending the email for 10 to 20 seconds so that you can cancel the send
[p0297-l0006] [14.4pt] and make changes if you change your mind in that time frame.
[p0297-l0007] [14.4pt] Be polite and assume good intent
[p0297-l0008] [14.4pt] When writing an email to others, be polite. If an email you receive seems to
[p0297-l0009] [14.4pt] be impolite, assume that it was sent with good intent and has come across
[p0297-l0010] [14.4pt] wrong. It is easy to seem brash or impolite in writing.
[p0297-l0011] [14.4pt] Be as concise as possible
[p0297-l0012] [14.4pt] Get to the point quickly.
[p0297-l0013] [14.4pt] Proofread before hitting Send
[p0297-l0014] [14.4pt] Get into the habit of at least scanning through your email before sending to
[p0297-l0015] [14.4pt] avoid the easy-to-spot errors.
[p0297-l0016] [14.4pt] AVOIDING ROBOTIC LANGUAGE
[p0297-l0017] [14.4pt] Emails written by humans often contain robotic and unnatural language,
[p0297-l0018] [14.4pt] which can be jarring to the recipient. Consider the following when writing
[p0297-l0019] [14.4pt] emails or crafting templates for automatic notifications in emails and
[p0297-l0020] [14.4pt] software:
[p0297-l0021] [14.4pt] Make it personal
[p0297-l0022] [14.4pt] Use you and I to make the message more personal and easy to
[p0297-l0023] [14.4pt] understand. Write “You should be proud of the effort you put into the
[p0297-l0024] [14.4pt] design” rather than “The design is an effort to be proud of.”
[p0297-l0025] [14.4pt] Write as you speak
[p0297-l0026] [14.4pt] Imagine you are talking to someone you know and think about the

=== PDF page 298 / printed label 298 ===
[p0298-l0001] [14.4pt] language you would use to express your message: “Can you please give
[p0298-l0002] [14.4pt] me feedback on the context diagram by 4 p.m. IST?” rather than
[p0298-l0003] [14.4pt] “Feedback on the context diagram should be returned by 4 p.m. IST,
[p0298-l0004] [14.4pt] please.”
[p0298-l0005] [14.4pt] A good way to check your message is to read it aloud before clicking Send.
[p0298-l0006] [20.16pt] Online Presentations
[p0298-l0007] [14.4pt] Presenting online, whether using a slide deck or sharing your screen, is different
[p0298-l0008] [14.4pt] from presenting in person and can be much harder. This section covers patterns
[p0298-l0009] [14.4pt] and techniques to enhance online presenting and screensharing. It also shows
[p0298-l0010] [14.4pt] how to update in-person presentations for an online audience.
[p0298-l0011] [16.56pt] Audience Engagement
[p0298-l0012] [14.4pt] In a remote presentation, engaging with your audience is difficult. You don’t
[p0298-l0013] [14.4pt] tend to see them, which makes gauging their reactions and feelings tricky. You
[p0298-l0014] [14.4pt] also get the added complication of having to keep an eye on a chat or Q&A
[p0298-l0015] [14.4pt] feature for questions and possibly trying to spot a digitally raised hand. Your
[p0298-l0016] [14.4pt] audience is much less likely to call out a question than they would in person if
[p0298-l0017] [14.4pt] you had missed their raised hand.
[p0298-l0018] [14.4pt] You can use a few techniques to help with these challenges. First, ask your
[p0298-l0019] [14.4pt] audience to offer questions in the way you want to receive them. If you want to
[p0298-l0020] [14.4pt] control when you answer questions, using the Q&A or chat is the best idea. If
[p0298-l0021] [14.4pt] you are happy to answer questions as they come, raising virtual hands may be
[p0298-l0022] [14.4pt] more appropriate. If possible, ask someone else to keep an eye out for questions
[p0298-l0023] [14.4pt] and alert you or read them out so you can focus on your presentation. Even if
[p0298-l0024] [14.4pt] you ask your audience to use only the Q&A, it’s a good idea to look for raised
[p0298-l0025] [14.4pt] hands or other questions because someone will miss or ignore your instruction.
[p0298-l0026] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0298-l0027] [10.8pt] You aren’t likely to impress or influence anyone as an icon on a screen. Turn on your camera
[p0298-l0028] [10.8pt] to better engage with your audience.

=== PDF page 299 / printed label 299 ===
[p0299-l0001] [14.4pt] In addition to dealing with questions, you need to read audience reactions. Many
[p0299-l0002] [14.4pt] presenting tools have a feature that allows your audience to react to you in real-
[p0299-l0003] [14.4pt] time, usually with emojis. Ask your audience to use these throughout and keep
[p0299-l0004] [14.4pt] an eye out for them so that you can respond to them as you would people’s facial
[p0299-l0005] [14.4pt] expressions in a room. You can also ask your audience to give a thumbs up or
[p0299-l0006] [14.4pt] down, in answer to a question you ask them, to gauge understanding or conduct
[p0299-l0007] [14.4pt] a vote.
[p0299-l0008] [14.4pt] Getting and keeping your audience’s attention is more difficult because online
[p0299-l0009] [14.4pt] interactions take more of your audience’s energy and each audience member has
[p0299-l0010] [14.4pt] their own set of distractions. Here are some ways to help you gain and keep your
[p0299-l0011] [14.4pt] audience’s attention:
[p0299-l0012] [14.4pt] If you are using a slide deck, use more slides than you would in person.
[p0299-l0013] [14.4pt] Advancing through slides more quickly helps keep attention. Spreading
[p0299-l0014] [14.4pt] your information across more slides has other advantages as well, such as
[p0299-l0015] [14.4pt] allowing you to make content larger and clearer, and more negative space is
[p0299-l0016] [14.4pt] easier for your audience to look at (see Figure 15-1 for an overview). Slides
[p0299-l0017] [14.4pt] are free, so add as many as you need.
[p0299-l0018] [14.4pt] Aim to split your message into shorter segments and add visual breaks.
[p0299-l0019] [14.4pt] These might be a blank slide or an image such as a photo, meme, or
[p0299-l0020] [14.4pt] cartoon. It is hard to sustain concentration on one subject for long (the
[p0299-l0021] [14.4pt] average attention span is no more than 20 minutes), so breaking up the
[p0299-l0022] [14.4pt] content gives your audience a rest, which they need for online interaction.
[p0299-l0023] [14.4pt] Use a presenter mode (picture-in-picture), if available, when showing slides
[p0299-l0024] [14.4pt] or sharing your screen so that your audience can see you. Make sure to use
[p0299-l0025] [14.4pt] exaggerated facial expressions and gestures so they are easier to see in the
[p0299-l0026] [14.4pt] small view you will be providing. Seeing your facial expressions and
[p0299-l0027] [14.4pt] gestures will make it much easier for your audience to understand you.
[p0299-l0028] [14.4pt] Besides visual breaks, give your audience actual breaks at least every 45 to
[p0299-l0029] [14.4pt] 60 minutes. This will help them concentrate better afterward.
[p0299-l0030] [14.4pt] Enable your audience to participate by using polls and similar tools such as
[p0299-l0031] [14.4pt] creating a word cloud using live responses. Many third-party options offer

=== PDF page 300 / printed label 300 ===
[p0300-l0001] [14.4pt] this functionality for a presentation, such as Claper and Mentimeter, and
[p0300-l0002] [14.4pt] simple polls are often built into the presentation tool, such as Microsoft
[p0300-l0003] [14.4pt] Teams or Zoom.
[p0300-l0004] [14.4pt] Try to minimize presentation time and maximize discussion, group
[p0300-l0005] [14.4pt] activities, and interactions. Asynchronous communication before the
[p0300-l0006] [14.4pt] presentation can provide some of the information to reduce the presentation
[p0300-l0007] [14.4pt] time during the event.
[p0300-l0008] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0300-l0009] [10.8pt] Remember that your audience’s bandwidth and screen size will probably vary. Keep this in
[p0300-l0010] [10.8pt] mind if you are using media such as video. When designing your slides, remember that many
[p0300-l0011] [10.8pt] of your audience may be viewing them on a small laptop screen.
[p0300-l0012] [10.8pt] Figure 15-1. Improving a slide deck by splitting up slides
[p0300-l0013] [16.56pt] Presentation Content
[p0300-l0014] [14.4pt] A common fallacy of presenting is that an agenda slide is required. This comes
[p0300-l0015] [14.4pt] from the premise that you should tell your audience what you are going to tell
[p0300-l0016] [14.4pt] them, tell them, and then tell them what you told them. You can follow this
[p0300-l0017] [14.4pt] premise, but telling your audience what you are going to tell them rarely
[p0300-l0018] [14.4pt] translates to an agenda slide, which is usually boring and unnecessary. You may
[p0300-l0019] [14.4pt] want to include an overview if you are teaching a training course or workshop,
[p0300-l0020] [14.4pt] but you should not spend much time on this. In most cases, you should focus on
[p0300-l0021] [14.4pt] the challenge or pain point you are addressing and let your audience know that
[p0300-l0022] [14.4pt] you are going to talk about solving this for them.

=== PDF page 301 / printed label 301 ===
[p0301-l0001] [14.4pt] Good ways to begin your presentation include showing an image, bold statement
[p0301-l0002] [14.4pt] or statistic, or starting with a story. Use these techniques to frame your main
[p0301-l0003] [14.4pt] message or takeaway. You can also end with that same main message or
[p0301-l0004] [14.4pt] takeaway for emphasis.
[p0301-l0005] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0301-l0006] [10.8pt] You may think that you don’t need to use techniques like this when you are presenting to your
[p0301-l0007] [10.8pt] architecture review board, but you will find that you get your message across much better by
[p0301-l0008] [10.8pt] getting and keeping your audience’s attention and interest. An agenda slide will not do that.
[p0301-l0009] [14.4pt] And finally, at the end of a presentation, consider the next steps or actions that
[p0301-l0010] [14.4pt] you want your audience to take. This is where a remote presentation has some
[p0301-l0011] [14.4pt] benefits as it is easy for you to share a link that you want the audience to click,
[p0301-l0012] [14.4pt] or ask for feedback or some sort of input via the chat or poll feature.
[p0301-l0013] [14.4pt] You can send asynchronous communication afterward with more information,
[p0301-l0014] [14.4pt] follow-up actions, and so forth. Use these techniques to meet the goals(s) of your
[p0301-l0015] [14.4pt] presentation.
[p0301-l0016] [14.4pt] INFODECKS VERSUS SLIDE DECKS VERSUS
[p0301-l0017] [14.4pt] SLIDEUMENTS
[p0301-l0018] [14.4pt] When is a slide deck not a slide deck? When it contains all the information
[p0301-l0019] [14.4pt] that the presenter is going to say. This sort of slide deck, when delivered by a
[p0301-l0020] [14.4pt] presenter, is called a slideument. It is a cross between a slide deck and a
[p0301-l0021] [14.4pt] document. The audience spends most of the presentation reading the slides
[p0301-l0022] [14.4pt] rather than listening to the presenter and not giving enough attention to
[p0301-l0023] [14.4pt] either.
[p0301-l0024] [14.4pt] Slideuments often happen when the same deck is used for the slides and as a
[p0301-l0025] [14.4pt] hand-out to the audience, or when the presenter doesn’t know their material
[p0301-l0026] [14.4pt] and needs to read it off the slides. This never works as a presentation.
[p0301-l0027] [14.4pt] Infodecks are slide decks that are created specifically to communicate
[p0301-l0028] [14.4pt] information and not to use alongside a presenter. They are stand-alone.
[p0301-l0029] [14.4pt] Infodecks can be more useful than a document, allowing diagrams to be

=== PDF page 302 / printed label 302 ===
[p0302-l0001] [14.4pt] shown full-screen and in landscape and giving you more control over layout.
[p0302-l0002] [14.4pt] They also encourage a multimedia approach with less text and so are easier
[p0302-l0003] [14.4pt] to read than long paragraphs in a document. When distributing an infodeck,
[p0302-l0004] [14.4pt] it is best to convert it to a PDF document, or distribute it within a web page,
[p0302-l0005] [14.4pt] so that more people can easily access it.
[p0302-l0006] [14.4pt] The slide deck that you create to be used by a presenter should be considered
[p0302-l0007] [14.4pt] the visual element of your presentation. The presenter provides the audio
[p0302-l0008] [14.4pt] element. One doesn’t work without the other, and together they make the
[p0302-l0009] [14.4pt] presentation. The presenter controls time during the presentation, using this
[p0302-l0010] [14.4pt] to gain and keep attention, emphasize, and allow the message to sink in.
[p0302-l0011] [16.56pt] Screen Shares
[p0302-l0012] [14.4pt] When collaborating with colleagues, you can share information visually by
[p0302-l0013] [14.4pt] sharing various applications on your screen, not just slides. For presenting
[p0302-l0014] [14.4pt] finished work, you will often share full-screen and in presentation mode so that
[p0302-l0015] [14.4pt] everyone is focused on your content and sees it at its largest.
[p0302-l0016] [14.4pt] When you want input and collaboration, a good technique is to share your
[p0302-l0017] [14.4pt] content in editing mode. This emphasizes that you are happy to make changes.
[p0302-l0018] [14.4pt] To make sure everyone can still see the content, you can hide toolbars and fit the
[p0302-l0019] [14.4pt] content to the window.
[p0302-l0020] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0302-l0021] [10.8pt] When sharing your screen, bear in mind that your audience may find it hard to see your cursor.
[p0302-l0022] [10.8pt] If you use your cursor to indicate anything on your screen, move it slowly and keep it pointed
[p0302-l0023] [10.8pt] at your focus for at least 5 seconds to help anyone experiencing lag. You can also set your
[p0302-l0024] [10.8pt] cursor to be larger and a bright color.
[p0302-l0025] [20.16pt] Remote Tools and Governance
[p0302-l0026] [14.4pt] The tools needed for remote work differ from those needed in person. You must
[p0302-l0027] [14.4pt] compensate for the lack of proximity with the tools you use in a hybrid or
[p0302-l0028] [14.4pt] remote environment.

=== PDF page 303 / printed label 303 ===
[p0303-l0001] [14.4pt] Physical in-person tools and resources such as sticky notes and whiteboards have
[p0303-l0002] [14.4pt] online counterparts that can offer more benefits (such as the ability to move an
[p0303-l0003] [14.4pt] item around a digital whiteboard). It is important to understand when tools for
[p0303-l0004] [14.4pt] remote work may be a better alternative for in-person working too.
[p0303-l0005] [14.4pt] In this section, you will find patterns and techniques to augment and strengthen
[p0303-l0006] [14.4pt] your use and governance of remote tools. By considering tool selection, data,
[p0303-l0007] [14.4pt] security, and efficiency, you can enhance your remote and hybrid working
[p0303-l0008] [14.4pt] practices.
[p0303-l0009] [16.56pt] Selection Techniques
[p0303-l0010] [14.4pt] When it comes to selecting tools, you have so many options offering so many
[p0303-l0011] [14.4pt] features that the choice can be overwhelming. Requirements are generally used
[p0303-l0012] [14.4pt] to assess whether a tool meets the business, technical, and security needs (often
[p0303-l0013] [14.4pt] using MoSCoW prioritization: must, should, could, and won’t), along with initial
[p0303-l0014] [14.4pt] and ongoing costs. Some software architecture techniques are also useful in this
[p0303-l0015] [14.4pt] situation.
[p0303-l0016] [14.4pt] Architectural characteristics are useful to help prioritize functional requirements
[p0303-l0017] [14.4pt] (such as assigning must, should, or could) and for forming nonfunctional
[p0303-l0018] [14.4pt] requirements for the tool (see “Architecture Characteristics”). This prioritization
[p0303-l0019] [14.4pt] can make the difference between selecting one tool over another and getting the
[p0303-l0020] [14.4pt] tool that fits best with the company’s priorities.
[p0303-l0021] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0303-l0022] [10.8pt] Functional and nonfunctional requirements that support your architectural characteristics
[p0303-l0023] [10.8pt] should generally be prioritized higher than those that don’t. If you have selected a top 3
[p0303-l0024] [10.8pt] architecture characteristics, the requirements supporting these should be given the highest
[p0303-l0025] [10.8pt] priority.
[p0303-l0026] [14.4pt] ADRs enable you to structure the process of choosing a software tool.
[p0303-l0027] [14.4pt] Documenting the context along with the reasons you need the software, the
[p0303-l0028] [14.4pt] decision drivers, and any assumptions or constraints, all contribute to the
[p0303-l0029] [14.4pt] software requirements.
[p0303-l0030] [14.4pt] The Evaluation Criteria section of an ADR documents all the requirements for

=== PDF page 304 / printed label 304 ===
[p0304-l0001] [14.4pt] the software, and the Options section contains each option’s scores against that
[p0304-l0002] [14.4pt] evaluation criteria. Also note any other trade-offs, pros, or cons for each option
[p0304-l0003] [14.4pt] that could significantly affect the final decision.
[p0304-l0004] [14.4pt] Some tools are like a Swiss army knife, offering lots of features. At the other end
[p0304-l0005] [14.4pt] of the spectrum, other tools do one thing (and hopefully do it well). There is no
[p0304-l0006] [14.4pt] clear-cut answer as to which will best fit your needs, but when choosing a tool, it
[p0304-l0007] [14.4pt] is prudent to consider all its features; some functions might either overlap with
[p0304-l0008] [14.4pt] other tools you use or fill a gap in your tool set. Follow the YAGNI rule of
[p0304-l0009] [14.4pt] coding (you aren’t gonna need it) for features that you don’t need, but tools with
[p0304-l0010] [14.4pt] overlapping features might be best consolidated.
[p0304-l0011] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0304-l0012] [10.8pt] Just as the style of your diagram can affect how your audience perceives it (see “Style
[p0304-l0013] [10.8pt] Communicates”), so too can the tool you choose affect what your audience thinks of the
[p0304-l0014] [10.8pt] output. A shared digital whiteboard would likely be perceived as containing transitory material,
[p0304-l0015] [10.8pt] and the more heavyweight tools (such as Enterprise Architect) can put off a lot of people even
[p0304-l0016] [10.8pt] though the content may be valuable.
[p0304-l0017] [10.8pt] EXAMPLE
[p0304-l0018] [14.4pt] SELECTING FOR CULTURAL FIT
[p0304-l0019] [14.4pt] One of Gino’s development teams maintains a custom documentation system
[p0304-l0020] [14.4pt] (PolyDocs), that all the development teams and the architects at Polyglot
[p0304-l0021] [14.4pt] Media use. The team pours a lot of effort into PolyDocs, but it causes a great
[p0304-l0022] [14.4pt] deal of conflict. Other teams feel that the team maintaining PolyDocs
[p0304-l0023] [14.4pt] prioritizes its own needs for the tool over other teams’ requests. It also takes
[p0304-l0024] [14.4pt] more development time than Gino wants to dedicate to it, with broken builds
[p0304-l0025] [14.4pt] and deployment issues wasting further time.
[p0304-l0026] [14.4pt] Gino asks the architect team members for their thoughts and finds he and the
[p0304-l0027] [14.4pt] team agree that PolyDocs is not the right fit for the company. Overall it
[p0304-l0028] [14.4pt] fulfilled its purpose, but the trade-off is unhappy developers.
[p0304-l0029] [14.4pt] The problem is cultural conflict, not technical disagreement, and so Gino and
[p0304-l0030] [14.4pt] the architecture team work with the developers and others to define
[p0304-l0031] [14.4pt] architectural characteristics for the documentation system. The architectural

=== PDF page 305 / printed label 305 ===
[p0305-l0001] [14.4pt] characteristics are then used as evaluation criteria in an ADR to assess
[p0305-l0002] [14.4pt] commercial off-the-shelf options, with stakeholders supplying input.
[p0305-l0003] [14.4pt] The replacement for PolyDocs increases happiness among the development
[p0305-l0004] [14.4pt] teams and other users. An open source option is selected, and any developer
[p0305-l0005] [14.4pt] can contribute to the project if they want to make a change, subject to Gino’s
[p0305-l0006] [14.4pt] approval for the time spent.
[p0305-l0007] [14.4pt] The architecture team adds cultural fit to its list of architecture
[p0305-l0008] [14.4pt] characteristics for assessing software procurement.
[p0305-l0009] [16.56pt] Remote Tools
[p0305-l0010] [14.4pt] Developers and architects use many types of tools when working or
[p0305-l0011] [14.4pt] collaborating remotely, including instant messaging, documents, and wikis. Here
[p0305-l0012] [14.4pt] is a fairly comprehensive list (including some of the less obvious) of the types of
[p0305-l0013] [14.4pt] tools and how they can be useful to you and the people you work with:
[p0305-l0014] [14.4pt] Online diagramming
[p0305-l0015] [14.4pt] Digital diagramming tools have now moved toward a SaaS or online
[p0305-l0016] [14.4pt] approach, and offer collaboration options to fit with remote work. Some offer
[p0305-l0017] [14.4pt] built-in collaboration, and others utilize file-sharing services such as
[p0305-l0018] [14.4pt] OneDrive and Google Drive. These tools are essential for getting feedback
[p0305-l0019] [14.4pt] on or collaboratively creating diagrams and other visuals for your software
[p0305-l0020] [14.4pt] architecture. Examples include draw.io, Excalidraw, and Lucidchart.
[p0305-l0021] [14.4pt] Q&A platforms
[p0305-l0022] [14.4pt] Documentation is even more important when it comes to remote working,
[p0305-l0023] [14.4pt] and one version uses questions and answers. By using a specific Q&A
[p0305-l0024] [14.4pt] platform, useful answers don’t get lost in chat or email conversations. Public
[p0305-l0025] [14.4pt] versions exist, but you will want a private version for Q&A related to
[p0305-l0026] [14.4pt] internal software and processes or containing sensitive information.
[p0305-l0027] [14.4pt] Examples include Codidact, Question2Answer, and Stack Overflow.
[p0305-l0028] [14.4pt] Wikis and knowledge management
[p0305-l0029] [14.4pt] Wikis and similar forms of knowledge management are particularly

=== PDF page 306 / printed label 306 ===
[p0306-l0001] [14.4pt] important for a distributed workforce. Asynchronous communication creates
[p0306-l0002] [14.4pt] documentation naturally because the information is sent in durable forms
[p0306-l0003] [14.4pt] (unlike discussions in a synchronous meeting, unless minutes are taken). Use
[p0306-l0004] [14.4pt] these tools to organize the information from asynchronous communication
[p0306-l0005] [14.4pt] and make it accessible. Document processes and synchronous meetings so
[p0306-l0006] [14.4pt] that everything is easy to find and in one place. Examples include
[p0306-l0007] [14.4pt] AppFlowy, TiddlyWiki, Obsidian, XWiki, Notion, and Confluence.
[p0306-l0008] [14.4pt] Forums and social tools
[p0306-l0009] [14.4pt] Private social networks and forums are good ways to encourage rapport
[p0306-l0010] [14.4pt] building among colleagues who might never see each other face-to-face.
[p0306-l0011] [14.4pt] Topics are typically not about work (such as sports, hobbies, and family), or
[p0306-l0012] [14.4pt] focus on professional subjects not necessarily related a work task or project
[p0306-l0013] [14.4pt] (for example, cloud technology, event sourcing, and DDD). Examples of
[p0306-l0014] [14.4pt] these tools include HumHub, Viva Engage, and Whaller.
[p0306-l0015] [14.4pt] Video conferencing
[p0306-l0016] [14.4pt] These tools are often used daily for virtual face-to-face conversations and
[p0306-l0017] [14.4pt] meetings, presenting slides or sharing your screen, and interactivity in virtual
[p0306-l0018] [14.4pt] meetings (for example, polls, and break-out rooms). Use these tools for
[p0306-l0019] [14.4pt] synchronous communication. Examples include Jitsi Meet, Zoom, Google
[p0306-l0020] [14.4pt] Meet, Skype, and Teams.
[p0306-l0021] [14.4pt] Instant messaging
[p0306-l0022] [14.4pt] Chat applications usually allow other media as well as plain text, such as
[p0306-l0023] [14.4pt] images or links to files, to be shared among individuals and groups. Instant
[p0306-l0024] [14.4pt] messaging can be synchronous or asynchronous, so make sure to set and
[p0306-l0025] [14.4pt] share expectations for using these tools. Examples include Mattermost,
[p0306-l0026] [14.4pt] Slack, Teams, and Google Chat.
[p0306-l0027] [14.4pt] Email
[p0306-l0028] [14.4pt] Often used for external communication (with customers or other businesses),
[p0306-l0029] [14.4pt] long-form internal communication, and publishing newsletters, email is
[p0306-l0030] [14.4pt] flexible but also prone to creating time sinks. Follow the advice in
[p0306-l0031] [14.4pt] “Symmetrical Email”. Examples include Mozilla Thunderbird, Mailspring,

=== PDF page 307 / printed label 307 ===
[p0307-l0001] [14.4pt] Gmail, and Microsoft Outlook.
[p0307-l0002] [14.4pt] Digital whiteboards
[p0307-l0003] [14.4pt] Online whiteboards aren’t often used for free-hand drawing (probably
[p0307-l0004] [14.4pt] because drawing with a mouse is hard), but they have many other features,
[p0307-l0005] [14.4pt] including the ability to add sticky notes, images, icons, and so on, and to
[p0307-l0006] [14.4pt] move these about and link them together with arrows or by drawing a box or
[p0307-l0007] [14.4pt] other shape around them. Another benefit compared to a standard whiteboard
[p0307-l0008] [14.4pt] is the size of the online canvas. It is much larger or effectively infinite, with
[p0307-l0009] [14.4pt] benefits such as being able to include multiple EventStorming perspectives
[p0307-l0010] [14.4pt] on one canvas and show progression. Outcomes are also automatically
[p0307-l0011] [14.4pt] documented, rather than rubbed off a standard whiteboard. Examples include
[p0307-l0012] [14.4pt] OpenBoard, Mural, Miro, and Microsoft Whiteboard.
[p0307-l0013] [14.4pt] Project management
[p0307-l0014] [14.4pt] Project management tools have come a long way since the first days of
[p0307-l0015] [14.4pt] products such as Microsoft Project. Waterfall planning has been largely
[p0307-l0016] [14.4pt] superseded by Agile methods, with many offerings now being SaaS. These
[p0307-l0017] [14.4pt] tools are great for asynchronous communication, including assigning tasks
[p0307-l0018] [14.4pt] and project updates. Examples include Redmine, Taiga, Basecamp, and Jira.
[p0307-l0019] [14.4pt] File sharing
[p0307-l0020] [14.4pt] Attaching a document to an email or other communication is well known to
[p0307-l0021] [14.4pt] be an antipattern, creating another version of the file every time for every
[p0307-l0022] [14.4pt] recipient. This makes updating everyone with any changes a nightmare.
[p0307-l0023] [14.4pt] Sharing a link to the file, with appropriate access rights, is the pattern to
[p0307-l0024] [14.4pt] follow. Examples include Seafile, Dropbox, Google Drive, and OneDrive.
[p0307-l0025] [14.4pt] Collaborative documents
[p0307-l0026] [14.4pt] Those working remotely may need to collaborate, or ask for feedback, on
[p0307-l0027] [14.4pt] documents, spreadsheets, slide decks, and so on. Most online document
[p0307-l0028] [14.4pt] applications offer live collaboration features if you want to work
[p0307-l0029] [14.4pt] synchronously on a document and comments for asynchronous collaboration.
[p0307-l0030] [14.4pt] Examples include ONLYOFFICE; Microsoft 365, which was formerly Office
[p0307-l0031] [14.4pt] 365; and Google Drive, which offers Docs, Sheets, and Slides.

=== PDF page 308 / printed label 308 ===
[p0308-l0001] [16.56pt] Data Proliferation
[p0308-l0002] [14.4pt] For many, the move to remote working was sudden and unplanned. The need to
[p0308-l0003] [14.4pt] maintain business continuity, avoid working in silos, and give people the
[p0308-l0004] [14.4pt] authority to make purchasing decisions has led to a proliferation of software
[p0308-l0005] [14.4pt] tools for remote working. In addition, entropy has increased applications used
[p0308-l0006] [14.4pt] within businesses. Everything tends toward chaos if left uncontrolled, so the use
[p0308-l0007] [14.4pt] of software must be managed within a business to avoid the problems that chaos
[p0308-l0008] [14.4pt] causes.
[p0308-l0009] [14.4pt] One thing often forgotten is that the proliferation of software tools also means
[p0308-l0010] [14.4pt] the proliferation of data. Each tool contains its own unique data, but also data
[p0308-l0011] [14.4pt] that is shared with other tools, and data that is a copy of or similar to data in
[p0308-l0012] [14.4pt] other apps (for example, user accounts and metadata like a list of departments in
[p0308-l0013] [14.4pt] the business).
[p0308-l0014] [14.4pt] Figure 15-2 illustrates the proliferation of data as apps multiply. Some apps share
[p0308-l0015] [14.4pt] data, some store their own data, and some data may be duplicated. Read on for
[p0308-l0016] [14.4pt] an explanation of these situations and their advantages and trade-offs.
[p0308-l0017] [14.4pt] Data that is similar or replicated can cause several issues. For each software tool
[p0308-l0018] [14.4pt] that manages its own users’ authentication and authorization, a user has to create
[p0308-l0019] [14.4pt] a new user account and may not follow good practices for creating a password.
[p0308-l0020] [14.4pt] This creates a new attack vector.
[p0308-l0021] [14.4pt] If a user changes roles or leaves the company, the user accounts in all the tools
[p0308-l0022] [14.4pt] they use must be updated to remove or change their permissions. One or more
[p0308-l0023] [14.4pt] accounts can easily slip through the cracks in this process. One of these accounts
[p0308-l0024] [14.4pt] with an insecure password is an excellent opportunity for hacking.

=== PDF page 309 / printed label 309 ===
[p0309-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 15-2. Proliferation and sharing of data
[p0309-l0002] [14.4pt] Copies of data can easily go out of sync. A list of departments in the business
[p0309-l0003] [14.4pt] could end up out-of-date if a new department is added or departments merge. A
[p0309-l0004] [14.4pt] list of current projects or tools would become out-of-date even quicker.
[p0309-l0005] [14.4pt] Tools needing to use the same data often lead to several versions of that data,
[p0309-l0006] [14.4pt] which may need to be kept in sync somehow. As the number of tools using data
[p0309-l0007] [14.4pt] increases, synchronization and maintaining a golden copy (a single authoritative
[p0309-l0008] [14.4pt] source of truth, or master copy) becomes harder and harder. Even when the
[p0309-l0009] [14.4pt] synchronization of data is automated, such as using APIs in the tools or a shared
[p0309-l0010] [14.4pt] data store, more tools mean more complexity and the likelihood of errors or
[p0309-l0011] [14.4pt] other problems. Inconsistent or unreliable data can cripple a company.
[p0309-l0012] [14.4pt] Then there is the consideration of security and compliance. Is the data
[p0309-l0013] [14.4pt] transferred securely? When and how is it encrypted? How is access to the data
[p0309-l0014] [14.4pt] controlled? Where is the data stored? These are all important questions for
[p0309-l0015] [14.4pt] protecting business intellectual property but also for complying with laws and
[p0309-l0016] [14.4pt] standards such as GDPR, HIPAA, PCI, and ISO.

=== PDF page 310 / printed label 310 ===
[p0310-l0001] [14.4pt] Any personally identifiable information contained in the data adds a huge
[p0310-l0002] [14.4pt] amount of complexity to data storage. You’ll need to figure out whether the
[p0310-l0003] [14.4pt] storage of the data (including geographical location and encryption) complies
[p0310-l0004] [14.4pt] with laws around this type of data, or even if you are allowed to use that data in
[p0310-l0005] [14.4pt] a particular software tool at all.
[p0310-l0006] [16.56pt] Security
[p0310-l0007] [14.4pt] Remote teams need the ability to access tools and data from anywhere, meaning
[p0310-l0008] [14.4pt] a shift toward SaaS products (hosted by the vendor) and the need to control
[p0310-l0009] [14.4pt] access to on-premises or cloud-based resources. For all its benefits, remote
[p0310-l0010] [14.4pt] working introduces more attack vectors to an organization, which need to be
[p0310-l0011] [14.4pt] mitigated by security policies such as using SSO, approving vendors, and
[p0310-l0012] [14.4pt] checking licenses and terms of service, among others.
[p0310-l0013] [14.4pt] Data security is not the only problem when managing software tools within a
[p0310-l0014] [14.4pt] company. An attacker could move laterally through company systems once they
[p0310-l0015] [14.4pt] have breached one software application. Organizations with 500 to 2,000
[p0310-l0016] [14.4pt] employees report using on average just over 800 cloud apps each month, with
[p0310-l0017] [14.4pt] 97% of those being shadow IT apps, according to the July 2021 Netskope cloud
[p0310-l0018] [14.4pt] and threat report.
[p0310-l0019] [14.4pt] Shadow IT is the software in use that the company is not aware of or in control
[p0310-l0020] [14.4pt] of. Even the software that is known poses a risk, but having around 780
[p0310-l0021] [14.4pt] unknown apps that might be malware or have vulnerabilities is a security
[p0310-l0022] [14.4pt] nightmare.
[p0310-l0023] [16.56pt] Tool Efficiency
[p0310-l0024] [14.4pt] The proliferation of software tools often leads to two or more tools that overlap
[p0310-l0025] [14.4pt] or even provide exactly the same features. Departments or teams that are
[p0310-l0026] [14.4pt] working in silos may invest in different software to meet their needs, when they
[p0310-l0027] [14.4pt] could use something already in use in the company.
[p0310-l0028] [14.4pt] If software capabilities don’t overlap, incompatabilities could arise when the
[p0310-l0029] [14.4pt] systems need to communicate at a later date. This lack of oversight of software
[p0310-l0030] [14.4pt] tools leads to wasting money on buying duplicate tools or migrating to another
[p0310-l0031] [14.4pt] compatible system.

=== PDF page 311 / printed label 311 ===
[p0311-l0001] [14.4pt] Costs can easily spiral with many SaaS and other remote working software tools
[p0311-l0002] [14.4pt] charging per user, per month. The cost looks innocent when viewed individually,
[p0311-l0003] [14.4pt] but having many users and many tools can easily lead to thousands of wasted
[p0311-l0004] [14.4pt] dollars per month.
[p0311-l0005] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0311-l0006] [10.8pt] When tools are underutilized, either because they aren’t needed or because they are duplicates,
[p0311-l0007] [10.8pt] the company also misses out on cost savings from bulk discounts and cost-effectiveness across
[p0311-l0008] [10.8pt] the business.
[p0311-l0009] [14.4pt] The efficiency of users and processes is also hampered by having large numbers
[p0311-l0010] [14.4pt] of software tools. Employees need to know which software they should use for
[p0311-l0011] [14.4pt] each task or process and where to find information, but having too many options
[p0311-l0012] [14.4pt] can be confusing.
[p0311-l0013] [14.4pt] Each new tool an employee is exposed to also has a learning curve. If an
[p0311-l0014] [14.4pt] employee is used to using one whiteboard tool but joins a meeting to find a
[p0311-l0015] [14.4pt] different one is being used, they will need to learn this new tool and cope with
[p0311-l0016] [14.4pt] switching between the two.
[p0311-l0017] [14.4pt] When you identify a need for new software but don’t know whether the business
[p0311-l0018] [14.4pt] already has other software that meets this need, the business could waste time
[p0311-l0019] [14.4pt] and effort in acquiring the new software and training employees. Identifying
[p0311-l0020] [14.4pt] who is responsible for the software is also difficult when there are so many
[p0311-l0021] [14.4pt] software tools. When responsibility is known, it is easy for that information to be
[p0311-l0022] [14.4pt] lost as people leave the company or change roles.
[p0311-l0023] [14.4pt] At a certain threshold, the number of tools slows employees down rather than
[p0311-l0024] [14.4pt] helps or streamlines their work. This threshold will vary, but it is probably lower
[p0311-l0025] [14.4pt] than you think.
[p0311-l0026] [16.56pt] Tool Governance
[p0311-l0027] [14.4pt] Remote work has accelerated the increase in software tools used by businesses,
[p0311-l0028] [14.4pt] but governance and stewardship can steer the situation back and away from
[p0311-l0029] [14.4pt] chaos. Using asynchronous options to both communicate and make decisions

=== PDF page 312 / printed label 312 ===
[p0312-l0001] [14.4pt] about this governance is key for a distributed workforce.
[p0312-l0002] [14.4pt] An audit of the current tool landscape is an important place to begin the
[p0312-l0003] [14.4pt] governance of software tools. Ask what apps are being used, what they are being
[p0312-l0004] [14.4pt] used for, who uses them, how many people use them, how many licenses the
[p0312-l0005] [14.4pt] company owns, what the initial and ongoing costs are, and whether the ROI is
[p0312-l0006] [14.4pt] known.
[p0312-l0007] [14.4pt] Then one important question remains, for which the answer is typically lost to
[p0312-l0008] [14.4pt] time: why is this particular tool being used? If you are lucky, the answer will
[p0312-l0009] [14.4pt] have been recorded. This information is useful when deciding whether a tool
[p0312-l0010] [14.4pt] should be kept.
[p0312-l0011] [14.4pt] Retaining information about tools is also useful, like the renewal dates for
[p0312-l0012] [14.4pt] licenses, especially when the renewal period is long, so that you don’t need to
[p0312-l0013] [14.4pt] make a rushed decision on whether to renew. If you know when the vendor will
[p0312-l0014] [14.4pt] stop supporting the tool, the date should also be recorded so plans for a
[p0312-l0015] [14.4pt] replacement or upgrade can be made.
[p0312-l0016] [14.4pt] Information about each tool can be used to create an application portfolio. Tools
[p0312-l0017] [14.4pt] in the portfolio should be assessed against business policies to make sure they
[p0312-l0018] [14.4pt] are compliant. This should include security and compliance, such as HIPAA or
[p0312-l0019] [14.4pt] GDPR, or with a policy such as all data being encrypted at rest and in transit.
[p0312-l0020] [14.4pt] See Table 15-1 for an extract from Polyglot Media’s application portfolio
[p0312-l0021] [14.4pt] summary (which can link to more detailed information).
[p0312-l0022] [14.4pt] Table 15-1. Extract from Polyglot Media’s application portfolio summary

=== PDF page 313 / printed label 313 ===
[p0313-l0001] [14.4pt] Mattermost
[p0313-l0002] [14.4pt] TBD (by end
[p0313-l0003] [14.4pt] Q4 2023)
[p0313-l0004] [14.4pt] Polyglot
[p0313-l0005] [14.4pt] Media
[p0313-l0006] [14.4pt] Enterprise
[p0313-l0007] [14.4pt] 148 users
[p0313-l0008] [14.4pt] 2023)
[p0313-l0009] [14.4pt] Inter-tool dependencies should also be added to the application portfolio. These
[p0313-l0010] [14.4pt] are important when working out which tools will be kept (creating an impact
[p0313-l0011] [14.4pt] analysis) and for determining data lineage and security. Business data flows
[p0313-l0012] [14.4pt] should be mapped for all tools, showing integrations and their implementation
[p0313-l0013] [14.4pt] (for example, an API).
[p0313-l0014] [14.4pt] All tools should be evaluated against policies, business objectives, and their pros
[p0313-l0015] [14.4pt] and cons (for example, pricing and pricing model, effort to use and manage,
[p0313-l0016] [14.4pt] training availability and learning curve, and so on). The other criteria to evaluate
[p0313-l0017] [14.4pt] against are the requirements of the tool, bearing in mind that these may have
[p0313-l0018] [14.4pt] changed over time (such as a platform now needing to provide scalability for
[p0313-l0019] [14.4pt] products built on top of it).
[p0313-l0020] [14.4pt] Once you have evaluated the tools in your portfolio, you can move on to
[p0313-l0021] [14.4pt] consolidation. You’ll use the results of your analysis to identify tools with the
[p0313-l0022] [14.4pt] following characteristics:
[p0313-l0023] [14.4pt] Duplicate features without good reason
[p0313-l0024] [14.4pt] For these, a decision should be made about which tool to keep and which to
[p0313-l0025] [14.4pt] migrate away from.
[p0313-l0026] [14.4pt] Can be consolidated into another tool you already use
[p0313-l0027] [14.4pt] Doing this will mean reducing the number of tools and vendors, and
[p0313-l0028] [14.4pt] therefore reducing your attack surface. You may also save money and will
[p0313-l0029] [14.4pt] have less management overhead.
[p0313-l0030] [14.4pt] Don’t meet the needs and goals of the business
[p0313-l0031] [14.4pt] These tools may have met business needs when they were originally licensed
[p0313-l0032] [14.4pt] or may have not taken some business needs into account. They should be
[p0313-l0033] [14.4pt] marked for replacement or phasing out.
[p0313-l0034] [14.4pt] Don’t comply with policies or laws

=== PDF page 314 / printed label 314 ===
[p0314-l0001] [14.4pt] These tools should be marked for replacement or phasing out as a priority,
[p0314-l0002] [14.4pt] unless a change to the tool can be made so that it does comply, such as
[p0314-l0003] [14.4pt] changing where data is stored.
[p0314-l0004] [14.4pt] Have a negative ROI or are not used enough to justify managing
[p0314-l0005] [14.4pt] Mark these for phasing out and migrating users to other options if needed.
[p0314-l0006] [14.4pt] Have too many licenses for the number of users
[p0314-l0007] [14.4pt] Change the licensing levels on these tools as soon as possible to save money.
[p0314-l0008] [14.4pt] When making these decisions, you should also take the following into account:
[p0314-l0009] [14.4pt] The cost of increasing the number of licenses
[p0314-l0010] [14.4pt] If you are consolidating users to use one tool, you should take into account
[p0314-l0011] [14.4pt] the new license numbers for each tool to evaluate the price.
[p0314-l0012] [14.4pt] The license models for each tool
[p0314-l0013] [14.4pt] You may find that one tool is much more cost-effective overall, because of
[p0314-l0014] [14.4pt] its licensing model, or that using two tools with different models is more
[p0314-l0015] [14.4pt] cost-effective (for example, one repository hosting service charges per
[p0314-l0016] [14.4pt] repository, and another service charges per user).
[p0314-l0017] [14.4pt] How and whether migration of data is possible
[p0314-l0018] [14.4pt] For tools that are being considered for phasing out, find out how data can be
[p0314-l0019] [14.4pt] migrated. If it is not possible, the migration may need to be done manually.
[p0314-l0020] [14.4pt] The skill sets of your employees
[p0314-l0021] [14.4pt] Which tools do they know how to use already? What is the learning curve for
[p0314-l0022] [14.4pt] tools you are considering migrating users to? What training is available for
[p0314-l0023] [14.4pt] tools, and how much does it cost?
[p0314-l0024] [14.4pt] Compatibility and integration requirements
[p0314-l0025] [14.4pt] Does the tool need to integrate or be compatible with another tool? This
[p0314-l0026] [14.4pt] could be a deal breaker when it comes to selecting a new tool or choosing

=== PDF page 315 / printed label 315 ===
[p0315-l0001] [14.4pt] which tool to keep.
[p0315-l0002] [14.4pt] Further analysis can then be done on the tools that you plan to keep, and tools
[p0315-l0003] [14.4pt] available on the market, to identify tools with these characteristics:
[p0315-l0004] [14.4pt] Have a cheaper option available
[p0315-l0005] [14.4pt] This may mean changing the licensing tier that you pay for or migrating to a
[p0315-l0006] [14.4pt] new tool.
[p0315-l0007] [14.4pt] Could be consolidated by replacing several tools with one new tool
[p0315-l0008] [14.4pt] As with consolidating to a tool you already use, this will reduce attack
[p0315-l0009] [14.4pt] vectors, management overhead, and probably costs as well.
[p0315-l0010] [14.4pt] Have a better option available
[p0315-l0011] [14.4pt] Assessing the current market may bring up options that better fit business
[p0315-l0012] [14.4pt] needs and goals, or are otherwise more efficient. These can be investigated
[p0315-l0013] [14.4pt] as replacements.
[p0315-l0014] [14.4pt] When it comes to implementing these changes, it is usually best to go for a
[p0315-l0015] [14.4pt] phased approach and something like the strangler fig pattern.
[p0315-l0016] [14.4pt] NOTE
[p0315-l0017] [10.8pt] The strangler fig pattern (sometimes known as the Ship of Theseus) is used to replace a legacy
[p0315-l0018] [10.8pt] system by gradually replacing legacy functionality with code in a new system. The new system
[p0315-l0019] [10.8pt] eventually replaces the legacy, which can then be decommissioned. Think of the tools you are
[p0315-l0020] [10.8pt] replacing or phasing out as features of the legacy system you are deciding whether to migrate.
[p0315-l0021] [14.4pt] Migrating users and data in phases allows you to iron out any issues before
[p0315-l0022] [14.4pt] affecting large numbers of people. Select the people who will be most happy to
[p0315-l0023] [14.4pt] run into and report issues as your early adopters.
[p0315-l0024] [14.4pt] With a plan for how your software tool landscape will look when you are done,
[p0315-l0025] [14.4pt] you can also consider optimizations such as integrating both upstream and
[p0315-l0026] [14.4pt] downstream services and tools. Many tools have APIs that you can exploit to get

=== PDF page 316 / printed label 316 ===
[p0316-l0001] [14.4pt] better value for money and increase efficiency.
[p0316-l0002] [14.4pt] Implementing oversight and governance of your application portfolio is an
[p0316-l0003] [14.4pt] important step that will keep you from falling back toward chaos. Processes and
[p0316-l0004] [14.4pt] policies need to be put in place to manage the lifecycle of the software tools used
[p0316-l0005] [14.4pt] in your company, whether they are used for remote work or not. Training should
[p0316-l0006] [14.4pt] complement these processes so that everyone knows where to find information
[p0316-l0007] [14.4pt] on the current tools and the process for requesting that a new tool be added to the
[p0316-l0008] [14.4pt] portfolio.
[p0316-l0009] [14.4pt] The processes you implement will feed back into your application portfolio,
[p0316-l0010] [14.4pt] reducing the need to perform audits (although it is good to perform these to
[p0316-l0011] [14.4pt] identify shadow IT). Processes and policies should also have a lifecycle and be
[p0316-l0012] [14.4pt] reviewed and updated as needed.
[p0316-l0013] [14.4pt] Your application portfolio, processes, and policies can be communicated in
[p0316-l0014] [14.4pt] various ways, including by the following:
[p0316-l0015] [14.4pt] Technology radar
[p0316-l0016] [14.4pt] A technology radar describes tools and technologies and categorizes them for
[p0316-l0017] [14.4pt] use in your organization. A typical visualization looks like a target (or radar),
[p0316-l0018] [14.4pt] with technology near the center being targeted for use and technology at the
[p0316-l0019] [14.4pt] edges being on hold, banned, or retired. Technologies and tools are
[p0316-l0020] [14.4pt] categorized into quadrants on the target. It is good to provide a text-based or
[p0316-l0021] [14.4pt] searchable version, as well as the visual, for easy access. Figures 15-3 and
[p0316-l0022] [14.4pt] 15-4 show some layouts from the Thoughtworks technology radar.
[p0316-l0023] [14.4pt] Technical/technology reference model
[p0316-l0024] [14.4pt] A technical reference model (TRM) is similar to a technology radar but
[p0316-l0025] [14.4pt] categorizes and displays tools differently. You can implement your TRM as
[p0316-l0026] [14.4pt] specified in the TOGAF standard or in a way that better meets your needs.
[p0316-l0027] [14.4pt] Technologies and tools are classified into a taxonomy. This is a much heavier
[p0316-l0028] [14.4pt] option than a technology radar.
[p0316-l0029] [14.4pt] Searchable and accessible application catalog
[p0316-l0030] [14.4pt] Make sure that all those who should have access do have access to the
[p0316-l0031] [14.4pt] application catalog. Tagging tools by their features and reasons for use will

=== PDF page 317 / printed label 317 ===
[p0317-l0001] [14.4pt] help those looking for existing tools that meet their needs. Including feature
[p0317-l0002] [14.4pt] information that may not be currently used will allow you to identify an
[p0317-l0003] [14.4pt] existing tool for another use.
[p0317-l0004] [14.4pt] Existing wiki and knowledge management tools
[p0317-l0005] [14.4pt] Define and describe processes and policies in the same place that other
[p0317-l0006] [14.4pt] business and technical information is documented. Link to any tools, such as
[p0317-l0007] [14.4pt] a technology radar, from here as well.
[p0317-l0008] [14.4pt] Bring your own device (BYOD) management
[p0317-l0009] [14.4pt] Use a tool like Microsoft Intune, so that company data and security are
[p0317-l0010] [14.4pt] controlled on devices that are not fully managed by the company.

=== PDF page 318 / printed label 318 ===
[p0318-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 15-3. Thoughtworks technology radar overview1
[p0318-l0002] [14.4pt] When creating policies and processes, it is important to communicate
[p0318-l0003] [14.4pt] expectations and exceptions for the lifecycle handling of tools. In general, when
[p0318-l0004] [14.4pt] a new need is identified that is fulfilled by an existing tool, you should expect
[p0318-l0005] [14.4pt] existing tools to be used, but exceptions will arise and a process should exist for
[p0318-l0006] [14.4pt] granting these.

=== PDF page 319 / printed label 319 ===
[p0319-l0001] [10.8pt] Figure 15-4. Techniques quadrant of the Thoughtworks technology radar2
[p0319-l0002] [14.4pt] TIP
[p0319-l0003] [10.8pt] If you see a pattern of exceptions being granted, it may be time to review the policies or the
[p0319-l0004] [10.8pt] use of software that is causing the exceptions to be requested.
[p0319-l0005] [14.4pt] When exceptions are granted, they should be documented as exceptions in your
[p0319-l0006] [14.4pt] application portfolio so that others know whether to use that tool or the existing
[p0319-l0007] [14.4pt] tool(s) that could not be used in the case of that exception. An exception may
[p0319-l0008] [14.4pt] need to be granted each time someone else wants to use any tools added to the
[p0319-l0009] [14.4pt] portfolio as an exception.
[p0319-l0010] [14.4pt] A balance needs to be reached between creating too much red tape and making it
[p0319-l0011] [14.4pt] too easy to add another tool to the application portfolio. Both extremes will
[p0319-l0012] [14.4pt] negatively affect the business. Make the process as quick and efficient as
[p0319-l0013] [14.4pt] possible, while still having checks for policy and process compliance. Automate

=== PDF page 320 / printed label 320 ===
[p0320-l0001] [14.4pt] as much as possible and make everything else self-service.
[p0320-l0002] [14.4pt] WARNING
[p0320-l0003] [10.8pt] If your processes for adding a new tool to the application portfolio are too restrictive, you will
[p0320-l0004] [10.8pt] find that shadow IT creeps in, with people circumnavigating the governance around software
[p0320-l0005] [10.8pt] tools.
[p0320-l0006] [14.4pt] The application portfolio can be used to track application ownership, making one
[p0320-l0007] [14.4pt] person responsible for the software tool. To make the process of changing the
[p0320-l0008] [14.4pt] owner as smooth as possible (for example, if the owner leaves the company),
[p0320-l0009] [14.4pt] assign the ownership to a role, such as Owner of App X, and assign that role to a
[p0320-l0010] [14.4pt] person. That way, reassigning that role to any other person is easy, whatever
[p0320-l0011] [14.4pt] their actual job role might be.
[p0320-l0012] [14.4pt] The whole governance and management process of software tools should be
[p0320-l0013] [14.4pt] agile. Auditing, analysis, evaluating, consolidating, and managing are all
[p0320-l0014] [14.4pt] ongoing processes that need to be repeated and reviewed regularly. In addition to
[p0320-l0015] [14.4pt] this ongoing loop, the processes and governance around the application catalog
[p0320-l0016] [14.4pt] need to feed back into the application catalog, such as updating the number of
[p0320-l0017] [14.4pt] licenses when it changes, or adding a new tool with all the required information.
[p0320-l0018] [14.4pt] Overall, the processes you put in place should deliver pertinent information to
[p0320-l0019] [14.4pt] the people who are paying for the tools in the application portfolio. Only then
[p0320-l0020] [14.4pt] can they make the necessary informed decisions about the case for any tool in
[p0320-l0021] [14.4pt] the portfolio. Every tool should be proving itself, either directly or indirectly, to
[p0320-l0022] [14.4pt] be granted space in your portfolio and management processes.
[p0320-l0023] [20.16pt] Summary
[p0320-l0024] [14.4pt] Now that your email communication is improved (and will hopefully improve
[p0320-l0025] [14.4pt] that of others by example) and you are communicating more effectively via
[p0320-l0026] [14.4pt] remote presentations, you are well on your way to getting your point across in
[p0320-l0027] [14.4pt] whatever medium you are using.
[p0320-l0028] [14.4pt] The governance of your communication and other tools should be the glue that
[p0320-l0029] [14.4pt] holds together your new framework for communication in your team, company,

=== PDF page 321 / printed label 321 ===
[p0321-l0001] [14.4pt] and beyond.
[p0321-l0002] [10.8pt] 1 “Technology Radar,” Thoughtworks, volume 28, 2023, https://oreil.ly/lGru8.
[p0321-l0003] [10.8pt] 2 Thoughtworks, “Technology Radar.”

=== PDF page 322 / printed label 322 ===
[p0322-l0001] [28.8pt] Epilogue
[p0322-l0002] [14.4pt] Now we come to the end of this narrative. I hope that I have managed to “get the
[p0322-l0003] [14.4pt] ball over the net,” to borrow a phrase from Michel Thomas that I quoted in the
[p0322-l0004] [14.4pt] Preface, in conveying the true importance of successful communication in the
[p0322-l0005] [14.4pt] technology arena and beyond.
[p0322-l0006] [14.4pt] You have learned that communication is not simply about getting someone to
[p0322-l0007] [14.4pt] understand you. Communication is building relationships, fostering trust, solving
[p0322-l0008] [14.4pt] problems, motivating people, promoting efficiency, and (possibly most
[p0322-l0009] [14.4pt] important) making people feel seen and respected.
[p0322-l0010] [14.4pt] In this book, I have brought together teachings from many diverse areas, mixed
[p0322-l0011] [14.4pt] them with my own ideas and experience, and applied them to software
[p0322-l0012] [14.4pt] architecture and business communication. I mentioned that diversity in people
[p0322-l0013] [14.4pt] and teams has benefits. Learning from diverse areas also has benefits, including
[p0322-l0014] [14.4pt] finding new understanding and making connections that others cannot see, and I
[p0322-l0015] [14.4pt] have passed that on to you in this book.
[p0322-l0016] [14.4pt] Communication is a part of every job, and in the technology arena, we have
[p0322-l0017] [14.4pt] perhaps the most diverse set of tools and methods for communication. This wide
[p0322-l0018] [14.4pt] selection has advantages and disadvantages, and what you have learned in this
[p0322-l0019] [14.4pt] book will help you maximize the advantages and mitigate the disadvantages.
[p0322-l0020] [14.4pt] As we close this book together, remember that communication is a dynamic,
[p0322-l0021] [14.4pt] ever-evolving process. Our journey here may be ending, but yours is just
[p0322-l0022] [14.4pt] beginning. Take the lessons from this book, apply them in your work, and
[p0322-l0023] [14.4pt] observe the transformation unfold in your professional and personal life.
[p0322-l0024] [14.4pt] Put into practice the communication patterns discussed, remain mindful of the
[p0322-l0025] [14.4pt] antipatterns, and most important, don’t be afraid to experiment and explore.
[p0322-l0026] [14.4pt] Communication has no one-size-fits-all approach—your unique experiences and
[p0322-l0027] [14.4pt] context will dictate your path.
[p0322-l0028] [14.4pt] Share your knowledge and your journey with others. You have a unique
[p0322-l0029] [14.4pt] perspective and experience that can benefit those around you, and in sharing,
[p0322-l0030] [14.4pt] you strengthen your understanding and create a positive ripple effect.

=== PDF page 323 / printed label 323 ===
[p0323-l0001] [14.4pt] Remember to revisit the essentials in Chapter 1 whenever you need a refresher
[p0323-l0002] [14.4pt] or find yourself off track. The fundamentals are your guiding light in the realm
[p0323-l0003] [14.4pt] of effective communication.
[p0323-l0004] [14.4pt] I encourage you to reach out with your experiences, insights, and stories. Your
[p0323-l0005] [14.4pt] journey matters, and hearing about your progress and the changes you’re
[p0323-l0006] [14.4pt] fostering is genuinely exciting. Be part of the conversation, contribute to the
[p0323-l0007] [14.4pt] community, and let’s continue learning together.
[p0323-l0008] [14.4pt] Wishing you successful communication,
[p0323-l0009] [14.4pt] —Jacqui Read

[p0324-l0001] [28.8pt] Appendix A. ADR Templates
[p0324-l0002] [14.4pt] Use these templates to structure your architecture decision records (ADRs).
[p0324-l0003] [14.4pt] They are also available on the companion website.
[p0324-l0004] [20.16pt] ADR Structure
[p0324-l0005] [14.4pt] See “ADR Structure” for instructions on how to use each section. See Figures
[p0324-l0006] [14.4pt] 12-3, 12-4, and 12-5 for examples of how to use this template.
[p0324-l0007] [16.56pt] Identifier and Title: A Statement of the Decision Made
[p0324-l0008] [14.4pt] Status
[p0324-l0009] [14.4pt] Draft/Decided/Superseded by ADR-XXX
[p0324-l0010] [14.4pt] Context
[p0324-l0011] [14.4pt] Why you need to make the decision. Assumptions, constraints, and decision
[p0324-l0012] [14.4pt] drivers.
[p0324-l0013] [14.4pt] Evaluation Criteria
[p0324-l0014] [14.4pt] What is important to you in making this decision? Which of your architectural
[p0324-l0015] [14.4pt] characteristics apply to making this decision? Should any constraints or decision
[p0324-l0016] [14.4pt] drivers become a criterion?
[p0324-l0017] [14.4pt] Options
[p0324-l0018] [14.4pt] Outlines of the options considered against the evaluation criteria (usually using a
[p0324-l0019] [14.4pt] score or rating), and trade-offs outside of the evaluation criteria. See “ADR
[p0324-l0020] [14.4pt] Options” for a template.
[p0324-l0021] [14.4pt] Decision
[p0324-l0022] [14.4pt] The choice that was made and why.

=== PDF page 325 / printed label 325 ===
[p0325-l0001] [14.4pt] Implications
[p0325-l0002] [14.4pt] The positive and negative consequences of the decision made.
[p0325-l0003] [14.4pt] Consultation
[p0325-l0004] [14.4pt] If taking input from others, document it here. Although consultation takes place
[p0325-l0005] [14.4pt] before a decision is made, it is documented at the end because it can become
[p0325-l0006] [14.4pt] long and obscure the decision itself.
[p0325-l0007] [20.16pt] ADR Options
[p0325-l0008] [14.4pt] Use the ADR Options table (Table A-1) in the Options section of your ADR. For
[p0325-l0009] [14.4pt] an example of how this table can be used, see Figures 12-3 and 12-4. To use
[p0325-l0010] [14.4pt] Table A-1:
[p0325-l0011] [14.4pt] Create one table per option considered.
[p0325-l0012] [14.4pt] Add a row for each of your evaluation criteria.
[p0325-l0013] [14.4pt] Give a score out of 5 and represent this visually using stars or a Harvey
[p0325-l0014] [14.4pt] ball.
[p0325-l0015] [14.4pt] Place notes justifying the score given in the Rationale column.
[p0325-l0016] [14.4pt] Total the score for comparison with other options.
[p0325-l0017] [14.4pt] Add other trade-offs, outside of the evaluation criteria, in the “Other trade-
[p0325-l0018] [14.4pt] offs” section.
[p0325-l0019] [14.4pt] Table A-1. ADR Options
[p0325-l0020] [14.4pt] Criteria
[p0325-l0021] [14.4pt] Score
[p0325-l0022] [14.4pt] Rationale
[p0325-l0023] [14.4pt] ☆☆☆☆☆ 0/5
[p0325-l0024] [14.4pt] ☆☆☆☆☆ 0/5
[p0325-l0025] [14.4pt] ☆☆☆☆☆ 0/5

[p0393-l0001] [14.4pt] About the Author
[p0393-l0002] [14.4pt] Jacqui Read is an internationally recognized solution and enterprise architect
[p0393-l0003] [14.4pt] with hands-on experience and expertise coding and architecting software
[p0393-l0004] [14.4pt] systems. She specializes in assisting businesses to create and enhance
[p0393-l0005] [14.4pt] architecture practices, construct evolutionary architectures, and untangle and
[p0393-l0006] [14.4pt] extract value from data and knowledge. Alongside consulting, Jacqui teaches
[p0393-l0007] [14.4pt] public and private workshops and speaks at international conferences on topics
[p0393-l0008] [14.4pt] such as architecture practices, technical communication, and architecture
[p0393-l0009] [14.4pt] decisions. Her professional interests include collaborative modeling, knowledge
[p0393-l0010] [14.4pt] management, domain driven design, sociotechnical architecture, and
[p0393-l0011] [14.4pt] modernizing enterprise architecture practices. Outside of work she enjoys
[p0393-l0012] [14.4pt] gardening and attempting to strum her ukulele and sing at the same time. Her
[p0393-l0013] [14.4pt] website is jacquiread.com.

=== PDF page 394 / printed label 394 ===
[p0394-l0001] [14.4pt] Colophon
[p0394-l0002] [14.4pt] The animal on the cover of Communication Patterns is a cinnamon-headed
[p0394-l0003] [14.4pt] green pigeon (Treron fulvicollis). This rare bird can be found in subtropical and
[p0394-l0004] [14.4pt] tropical mangrove forests, swamps, and moist scrublands in Indonesia, Malaysia,
[p0394-l0005] [14.4pt] Myanmar, Singapore, and Thailand. You might also spot them in rural gardens.
[p0394-l0006] [14.4pt] The male cinnamon-headed green pigeon gives the bird its name, sporting green
[p0394-l0007] [14.4pt] plumage with a pinkish-orange to chestnut colored head and breast. The females
[p0394-l0008] [14.4pt] have green heads and breasts. Both sexes have reddish and white bills and white-
[p0394-l0009] [14.4pt] fringed wing feathers.
[p0394-l0010] [14.4pt] The population of cinnamon-headed green pigeons is Vulnerable at the time of
[p0394-l0011] [14.4pt] this writing, according to the IUCN; their population is decreasing. Many of the
[p0394-l0012] [14.4pt] animals on O’Reilly covers are endangered; all of them are important to the
[p0394-l0013] [14.4pt] world.
[p0394-l0014] [14.4pt] The cover illustration is by Karen Montgomery, based on an antique line
[p0394-l0015] [14.4pt] engraving from Shaw’s General Zoology. The cover fonts are Gilroy Semibold
[p0394-l0016] [14.4pt] and Guardian Sans. The text font is Adobe Minion Pro; the heading font is
[p0394-l0017] [14.4pt] Adobe Myriad Condensed; and the code font is Dalton Maag’s Ubuntu Mono.
